# Sūrah al-Kahf (Chapter 18) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Read Once a Week by the Whole Ummah**

Al-Kahf, "The Cave," is the eighteenth chapter of the Qur'an, consisting of 110 verses, revealed in Makkah in the later Makkan period — a minority view transmitted by al-Qurṭubī holds the first eight verses to be Madinan, but the overwhelming majority place the entire sūrah in Makkah. It occupies the fifteenth and the opening of the sixteenth juzʾ of the standard thirty-part division, and it holds a position in Muslim devotional life shared by no other long chapter except al-Fātiḥah: it is recited, by an unbroken practice, on Friday. Al-Ḥākim records in his *Mustadrak* the Prophet's ﷺ words:

> *"Whoever recites Sūrat al-Kahf on the day of Jumuʿah, a light shall shine for him between the two Fridays."* (al-Ḥākim, *al-Mustadrak* 3392; al-Bayhaqī, *al-Sunan al-Kubrā*; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī in *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Jāmiʿ* 6470)

And in the version recorded by al-Nasāʾī and al-Bayhaqī from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī, the same reciter is promised *a light between him and the Ancient House* — the Kaʿbah. This weekly rhythm means that a Muslim household which keeps the practice hears, between every pair of Fridays, the whole of the cave, the whole of Moses and Khiḍr, the whole of Dhu'l-Qarnayn, and the whole of the two-garden parable. No other chapter of comparable length has this repetition built into it, and the effect on the Muslim imagination has been enormous: these four stories are among the most deeply known narratives in the Islamic world, known not from books but from the tongue.

**The Three Questions That Produced This Chapter**

The sūrah has an occasion of revelation that is unusually well documented, and it is a dramatic one. As Ibn Isḥāq records in the *Sīrah*, and as Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, al-Suyūṭī, and al-Qurṭubī transmit in their commentaries: the Quraysh, uncertain how to evaluate the man claiming prophethood among them, sent two of their own — al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith and ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ — to the Jewish scholars of Yathrib to ask what test might settle the matter. The scholars gave them three questions:

> *"Ask him about some young men who lived in ancient times and had an extraordinary affair; ask him about a man who travelled until he reached the east and the west of the earth; and ask him about the Spirit. If he answers you about these, he is a prophet — follow him; if he does not, then he is a man speaking by conjecture."*

The delegation returned and put the questions. The Prophet ﷺ, confident that revelation would arrive, answered: *"Tomorrow I will tell you"* — and did not add *inshāʾ Allāh*. For fifteen days nothing came. The reports describe his distress and the glee of the Quraysh. Then the revelation descended: Sūrah al-Kahf answered the first two questions and Sūrah al-Isrāʾ (17:85) answered the third. And within the answer came a correction addressed to the Prophet himself:

> *"And say not of anything, 'Surely, I shall do it tomorrow,' save that God wills."* (Qur'an 18:23–24)

This is one of the Qur'an's most remarkable structural features: a chapter delivered as a *defence* of the Messenger contains inside it a *rebuke* of the Messenger. Nothing but an authentic revelation behaves this way. A forger writing to vindicate himself would not embed his own lapse in the vindicating text.

**Four Trials and One Sūrah**

Read together, the chapter's four great units form a systematic survey of the trials that destroy human beings. The Companions of the Cave face the trial of **religion** — persecution for belief — and save themselves by leaving home. The owner of the two gardens faces the trial of **wealth** — prosperity without gratitude — and loses himself inside his own estate. Moses faces the trial of **knowledge** — the humbling discovery that a prophet can be surpassed in understanding by a servant no one has heard of. Dhu'l-Qarnayn faces the trial of **power** — sovereignty over the known world — and passes it by refusing payment and building a wall for the weak. Between these four, the chapter places the story of Satan's refusal before Adam (v. 50), the reminder that this world is water-vegetation-chaff (v. 45), and the scene of the Book being set down on the Day of Judgment (v. 49). The sūrah is, in effect, a curriculum: here are the four things that ruin people, here is how each is survived.

This is why the Prophetic reports connect the chapter to al-Dajjāl, the false messiah, who is the concentration of all four trials at once — demanding worship, displaying wealth, claiming knowledge, wielding power. Muslim records from Abū al-Dardāʾ:

> *"Whoever memorizes the first ten verses of Sūrat al-Kahf will be protected from al-Dajjāl."* (Muslim 809)

And in a variant in the same collection, *"the last ten verses."* The protection is not magical; it is educative. The one who has internalized these four stories carries a mental immune system against the deceiver, because he has already seen, in narrative form, exactly the four temptations the deceiver will present.

**The Barzakh: The Chapter's Hidden Architecture**

A structural reading that the classical commentators noted in scattered remarks and that later scholars developed systematically is that all three narratives turn on *intermediate states* — the Qur'anic concept of *barzakh*, which means both a meeting-point of opposites and a barrier between them. The Qur'an uses the word of the partition between the two seas:

> *"And He it is Who has let the two seas flow freely, this one sweet and fresh and this one salt and bitter, and has placed between them a barrier and a forbidden partition."* (Qur'an 25:53)

The sleepers of the cave exist in a *barzakh* between waking and sleeping, life and death, this world and the next — which is precisely the state Islamic theology assigns to the grave. Moses meets Khiḍr only at the *majmaʿ al-baḥrayn*, the junction of the two seas — the meeting-place of exoteric and esoteric knowledge, of the law and the hidden wisdom beneath it. Dhu'l-Qarnayn returns to a *barzakh* between two mountains, an opening he then seals into a barrier that will hold until the Hour. Each story is a variation on one theme: reality is not flat; it has seams, and at the seams strange things are possible.

**How to Read What Follows**

The commentary that follows treats each of the 110 verses as an independent discourse, in strict order, exactly as mandated. Where the source material groups verses — as it does with 2–3, 4–5, 7–8, 13–14, 19–21, 25–26, 27–28, 32–34, 60–64, 65–70, 83–86, 89–90, 103–105, 107–108 — each verse is here separated and given its own full treatment. Nothing has been compressed because the chapter is long. From *"Praise be to God, Who sent down the Book unto His servant"* to *"Say: I am only a human being like you, unto whom it is revealed that your God is one God,"* the whole chapter is taken up verse by verse, without omission and without haste.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:1

> **Praise be to God, Who sent down the Book unto His servant, and placed no crookedness therein,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Al-Ḥamd That Opens a Chapter About Escape**

The sūrah opens with *al-ḥamdu lillāh* — "Praise belongs to God" — the same three-word formula that opens the Qur'an itself, and five other sūrahs besides (6, 34, 35, 45). Grammatically the sentence is *nominal*, not verbal: it does not say "I praise God" or "We praise God" but states praise as a settled fact belonging to God whether or not anyone utters it. The commentators note the difference carefully: *ḥamd* is praise given to one who deserves it, whether or not he has done you a favour; *shukr* is gratitude for a specific benefit received. By using *ḥamd*, the verse places God's praiseworthiness outside the fluctuations of the servant's circumstances.

The connection to what follows is immediate and instructive. God is praised *for sending the Book* — *alladhī anzala ʿalā ʿabdihi al-kitāb*. Ibn Kathīr observes that this is the greatest of God's favours upon humanity, because the Book is what takes human beings *"out of the darknesses into the light"* (2:257). Every other blessing preserves the body; this one preserves the soul. And the timing matters: this is a chapter revealed when a handful of believers were being tortured in Makkah, when the Prophet ﷺ had no political standing, when the community possessed nothing but words. To open such a chapter with praise for a *Book* is to declare that the possession of revelation is already a possession of everything.

**"His Servant": The Highest Title in the Sentence**

Notice what the verse does not say. It does not say "unto His messenger," or "unto His prophet," or "unto Muḥammad." It says *ʿalā ʿabdihi* — "unto His servant." The commentators — al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī, al-Rāzī, Ibn Kathīr — agree that *ʿabd* (servant, slave) is used here deliberately, and that it carries the highest honour rather than a diminishment. The reasoning is logical: servitude to God is the only relationship in which the servant loses nothing and gains everything, because the Master's generosity is infinite and His need of the servant is nil. Every other master–servant relation in the world is a negotiation between two needs. This one is a pure gift.

There is also a polemical edge that Makkan ears would catch. The Quraysh called Muḥammad ﷺ a madman, a poet, a sorcerer, a man possessed. The verse answers by giving him a title they cannot dispute and cannot improve upon: he is God's servant. Later, in the Night Journey, the same word will open Sūrah al-Isrāʾ — *"Glory be to Him Who took His servant by night"* (17:1) — and the tradition will read both together as establishing that servitude is the Prophet's ﷺ defining rank, so much so that in the *tashahhud* of every prayer the Muslim testifies: *wa ashhadu anna Muḥammadan ʿabduhu wa rasūluh* — "servant" before "messenger."

**"No Crookedness Therein": The ʿIwaj That Is Absent**

The phrase *wa lam yajʿal lahu ʿiwajan* — "and placed no crookedness in it" — uses a precise and unusual word. *ʿIwaj* (with a kasrah on the ʿayn) is not general crookedness; the commentators specify that it denotes a deviation *in something that ought to be straight*, particularly in matters of meaning, speech, and conduct — as against *ʿawaj* (with a fathah), used for physical bending in objects. A road that curves is not blameworthy; a statement that curves when it ought to be straight is a defect.

The commentators — al-Suyūṭī and al-Zamakhsharī among them — unpack this absence of *ʿiwaj* in three layers. First, **doctrinal**: no contradiction within the Book, no statement that cancels another. The Qur'an supplies its own verification test a few chapters later:

> *"Do they not then contemplate the Qur'an? Had it been from other than God, they would surely have found within it much discrepancy."* (Qur'an 4:82)

Second, **moral**: no falsehood, no corruption, nothing that would lead a reader into error. Third, **functional**: no obsolescence — the Book remains continuously beneficial, never ceasing to guide toward the straight path. Ibn Kathīr connects this third layer to the *ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm* of al-Fātiḥah (1:6): a text with no *ʿiwaj* is by definition a text that can produce a straight road, because a crooked ruler cannot draw a straight line.

The logical point is worth pausing on. Any human document produced across twenty-three years, in conditions of war and migration and legislation and bereavement, will show its seams — revisions, tensions, abandoned positions. The verse asserts the absence of seams *in advance*, as a falsifiable claim addressed to readers of every century. It is an invitation to inspect, not a demand for surrender.

**A Negative That Performs a Positive Work**

Rhetorically, the verse describes the Book by what is *not* in it. This is characteristic of Qur'anic self-description, which often proceeds by negation: *"there is no doubt therein"* (2:2), *"no falsehood approaches it from before it or behind it"* (41:42), *"We have made it easy"* (54:17). The reason is pedagogical. The human mind approaches any new text carrying prior anxieties — is this reliable, is this consistent, will this contradict itself tomorrow? By naming and removing the anxieties one by one, revelation clears the ground so that the positive content can land.

Verse 2 will supply the positive: the Book is *qayyim*, upright. Verse 1 removes the obstacle; verse 2 states the quality. The order is deliberate, and it mirrors how a physician works — first the differential diagnosis ruling out the dangerous possibilities, then the treatment plan.

**The Servant and the Book: Two Nouns, One Mission**

Everything in this opening verse reduces to a relation between three terms: God who sends, the servant who receives, the Book that is sent. The sūrah that follows will dramatize precisely this relation under pressure. The youths of the cave have the Book of a previous messenger and no protection but God. The owner of the garden has protection and wealth but no Book. Khiḍr has knowledge given directly by God. Dhu'l-Qarnayn has power given directly by God. In each case the chapter asks: what do you do when what you hold from God meets what the world offers? The answer the verse implies is already complete: praise the One who sent the Book, because the Book has no crookedness in it, and everything else does.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:2

> **upright, that He may warn of a great might coming from His Presence and give glad tidings unto the believers who perform righteous deeds that theirs shall be a beautiful reward,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Qayyim: A Word Doing Four Jobs at Once**

The single word *qayyimā* — "upright" — is grammatically attached to verse 1 as a description of the Book, and it is one of the densest adjectives in the Qur'an. The root *q-w-m* carries the sense of standing, of rising to a task, of being established and straight. From it come *qiyām* (standing in prayer), *qawwām* (one who maintains and provides), and *qīmah* (worth, value). Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Suyūṭī note that the Qur'an usually applies *qayyim* to religion itself —

> *"Say: Truly my Lord has guided me unto a straight path, an upright religion, the creed of Abraham the upright."* (Qur'an 6:161)

and also at 9:36, 12:40, 30:30, 30:43, 98:5 — but here applies it to the *Book*. The transfer is significant: the Book is upright in itself, and it is what makes religion upright in the one who follows it.

The commentators extract four distinct meanings, and the word sustains all of them simultaneously. **First**, upright means *without crookedness* — the direct complement of verse 1's negation, so that the pair forms a single affirmation from two directions. **Second**, upright means *balanced and harmonious* — al-Suyūṭī's reading: a Book whose demands are not excessive, whose promises are not inflated, whose severity is proportioned to its mercy. **Third**, upright means *custodial*: *qayyim* can describe one who maintains and oversees a thing, and on this reading the Qur'an is the custodian (*muhaymin*) over the earlier scriptures — confirming their truth, witnessing over their transmission, correcting what human hands altered in them, and superseding certain of their laws. This is the meaning of 5:48:

> *"And We have sent down unto thee the Book with truth, confirming that which preceded it of the Book and as a guardian over it."* (Qur'an 5:48)

**Fourth**, and most comprehensively, al-Rāzī's formulation: the Qur'an is called *qayyim* because it is *perfect in itself and perfects something else*. A thing that is merely complete is a monument; a thing that completes others is an instrument. The Qur'an claims to be both.

**Warn and Give Glad Tidings: The Twin Vocation**

The verse then states the Book's purpose in the two verbs that define prophetic office in the Qur'an: *li-yundhira* — "that he may warn" — and *wa yubashshira* — "and give glad tidings." This pairing recurs throughout the revelation as a fixed formula for the prophetic mission: 2:119, 2:213, 4:165, 6:48, 7:188, 11:2, 17:105, 25:56, 33:45, 34:28, 48:8, and again in this very sūrah at 18:56. Al-Ṭabarī treats it as structural: a messenger who only warns is a threat, and a messenger who only promises good news is a flatterer. Neither can produce a morally serious human being.

The psychology is exact. Fear alone produces evasion — the person who is only frightened looks for loopholes, for intercessors, for the belief that the danger will not actually materialize. Hope alone produces presumption — the person who is only reassured sees no reason to discipline himself. The combination produces *taqwā*, which is neither terror nor comfort but a settled awareness that one's actions have consequences and that the One who holds the consequences is merciful. This is the same balance the Prophet ﷺ described in the ḥadīth qudsī recorded by Muslim (2737): God's mercy preceded His wrath in His own self-description, but both were written.

**"A Great Might Coming From His Presence"**

The object of the warning is *baʾsan shadīdan min ladunhu* — "a severe punishment from His Presence." Two features of the phrase deserve attention. The word *baʾs* denotes punishment that arrives with force and suddenness, often in the Qur'an with the sense of an affliction that cannot be deflected once it begins (6:65, 7:4, 17:68). And the words *min ladunhu* — "from His Presence" — attach the punishment directly to God rather than to some intermediary mechanism.

Al-Rāzī draws out the implication: the severest aspect of the punishment is not its heat but its *source*. A harm that comes from an impersonal process can be endured philosophically; a harm that comes from the One you were made for, and turned away from, carries the weight of a broken relationship. The commentators also note that the phrase answers the disbelievers' implicit objection — that divine punishment is a remote abstraction administered at a distance. No: it issues *from His Presence*, from the very nearness that was offered and refused.

**"A Beautiful Reward": Not Merely Large, But Beautiful**

The promise to believers is *ajran ḥasanan* — "a beautiful reward." Al-Suyūṭī and al-Zamakhsharī identify this as the Garden itself, and the choice of *ḥasan* (beautiful, good, excellent) over a word denoting mere magnitude is telling. The Qur'an consistently describes Paradise in aesthetic as well as material terms: rivers, shade, couches, silk, brocade, reclining, greeting, peace. This is not decorative. It corrects a conception of reward as compensation — as though the believer had endured deprivation and will be reimbursed in kind.

The logic runs deeper. What the believer gave up in this world was *beauty* misused — pleasure detached from gratitude, wealth detached from generosity, status detached from service. What is returned is beauty *restored to its source*. The reward is described as beautiful because the believer's whole discipline was the redirection of his love of beauty toward the Beautiful. Verse 31 will make this concrete with gold bracelets and green silk — the very luxuries the owner of the two gardens hoarded without thankfulness.

**Belief Joined to Deed, Inseparably**

The recipients are *alladhīna āmanū wa ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt* — "those who believe and perform righteous deeds." The Qur'an yokes these two terms so consistently — dozens of times across the revelation — that the commentators treat the conjunction as deliberate theology rather than stylistic repetition. Belief without righteous action is inert; righteous action without belief is, in the Qur'anic scheme of value, weightless on the Scale, because it was not offered to God. The pairing forecloses both the quietist error (faith alone suffices) and the moralist error (conduct alone suffices).

Al-Rāzī adds a subtle reading of the phrase *ajran ḥasanan*: it may mean not only "a beautiful reward" but "a reward for the most beautiful of deeds" — and the most beautiful deed, he suggests, is the testimony of God's Oneness. The reward is beautiful because the deed that earns it is beautiful. Nothing in the verse suggests a transaction between strangers.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:3

> **wherein they shall remain forever,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Short Words Carrying Eternity**

The whole verse is four words in Arabic: *mākhthīna fīhi abadan* — "remaining therein forever." Coming immediately after the promise of "a beautiful reward," it performs the single operation that makes the promise intelligible. Without permanence, no reward can be called a reward in the full sense, because the anticipation of loss poisons the enjoyment. A paradise with an exit is not paradise; it is a lease.

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Suyūṭī connect *mākithīna* — "remaining, dwelling, abiding" — to the Qur'anic vocabulary of settled habitation, the same family that produces *mukth* (a period of tarrying). The word is used elsewhere of people who dwell somewhere by choice and at leisure (20:40, 28:45), not of people trapped. The abiding of the Garden is a *residence*, not a confinement. The Qur'an will name it *Jannāt ʿAdn* — Gardens of Eden, Gardens of Perpetual Residence — at verse 31.

**Why Permanence Is a Theological Necessity, Not a Bonus**

The reasoning deserves articulation because the modern reader often experiences the promise of eternity as an add-on, a bonus feature. Classical theology treats it differently. If the Hereafter were finite, then the moral universe would ultimately reduce to this one: finite pleasures, finite pains, and then nothing — in which case the tyrant who dies comfortably in old age and the martyr who dies at twenty-five would have made equivalent wagers. Justice requires that outcomes be proportionate to choices, and human choices about ultimate matters have an infinite shape — the person who rejects God rejects Him *forever*, not for a term.

The Qur'an makes this argument explicitly elsewhere:

> *"Indeed, those who disbelieve and die while they are disbelievers — of them is the curse of God and of the angels and of the people, all together. Abiding therein eternally; the punishment will not be lightened for them, nor will they be granted respite."* (Qur'an 2:161–162)

Symmetry is preserved: permanent rejection meets permanent consequence; permanent faith meets permanent reward. Verse 3 is the hinge on which the whole moral architecture of the sūrah turns.

**The Answer to the Sūrah's Own Central Anxiety**

Read in the flow of the chapter, this tiny verse answers the anxiety the chapter will dramatize at length. The owner of the two gardens says at verse 35, *"I think not that this will ever perish,"* and at verse 36, *"if I am brought back to my Lord, I shall surely find something better than this in the Hereafter."* His error is precisely a confusion about *which* possession is permanent. He assumes the garden is permanent and doubts the Hereafter; then, hedging, he assumes that even the Hereafter will be better *for him*. Verse 3 places the fact of permanence at the outset, before the parable, so that the reader knows where to look for the durable thing.

The practical instruction is direct. Human beings allocate attention according to perceived duration: nobody maintains a rented apartment as carefully as a home he owns. The verse is telling the reader that the reward promised in verse 2 is the property, and everything else is the rental.

**The Grammar of Never-Ending**

*Abadan* is the Qur'an's standard adverb of unbroken duration, used both of the Garden's permanence and of Hell's. Its force is reinforced in the Qur'an by constructions like *khālidīna fīhā abadan* — "abiding therein forever" — where two markers of permanence are stacked. Linguists of the Qur'an note that this doubling is not redundancy but emphasis, because the concept being expressed exceeds what any single Arabic word can carry: a duration with no terminus is not a *long* time, and any single term for length can be misread as finite.

There is a further subtlety. The verse attaches permanence to *the reward*, not to the recipients as such — *fīhi*, "therein." The Qur'an's eschatology is careful about attributing intrinsic eternity to creatures, who are contingent in their very being. Eternal life in the Garden is a *sustained gift*, continuously upheld by God, not a property the believer owns independently. Al-Rāzī makes this point in his treatment of the Garden's descriptions: every blessing of the Hereafter, including its permanence, is a fresh act of mercy. The believer does not possess eternity; he is held in it.

**The Contrast With Verse 35**

The commentators note that the phrase *khālidīna fīhā abadan* is the chapter's positive counterpart to the garden owner's claim at verse 35 — *"I think not that this will ever perish."* Both statements are about permanence, and the chapter is concerned to show that only one of them can be true.

The garden owner asserted permanence for a thing that had no power over itself; the verse grants permanence to a reward that belongs to the One who does. The difference is not in the word *abadan* but in who is speaking about what. The commentators have drawn the lesson that the desire for permanence is legitimate — it is what verse 3 promises to satisfy — but that it is satisfied only in the direction of God, never in the direction of one's own possessions.

The reader who wants something that will not end is not mistaken in wanting it. He is mistaken only about where to look for it.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:4

> **and that He may warn those who say, "God has taken a child."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Warning, Aimed at a Specific Claim**

Verse 2 stated the warning generally — "a severe punishment from His Presence." Verse 4 narrows it to a particular assertion: *wa yundhira alladhīna qālū ittakhadha Allāhu waladan* — "and that He may warn those who say, 'God has taken a child.'" The grammatical structure is deliberate: the general warning of verse 2 covers all who reject the message; this one is directed at a doctrine. Ibn Kathīr notes that the verse identifies the specific theological crime that the chapter is answering.

The choice of verb matters enormously, and the commentators — al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī, al-Rāzī — all draw attention to it. The word is *ittakhadha*, from *a-kh-dh*, "to take." This is not the language of biological begetting (*walada*). It is the idiom of adoption, of taking someone into one's care and granting him a special standing — as in 28:9, where Pharaoh's wife says of Moses, *"He may be a comfort to me and to thee; slay him not. Perhaps he will be of benefit to us, or we may take him as a son (nattakhidhuhu waladan)."*

The distinction is theologically productive. What the verse rejects is not merely a claim about divine biology — which would be crude and which nobody seriously holds — but the claim that God singles out any being for a *unique filial relationship*, a status that no other creature shares, entailing privilege, inheritance, and access. The critique lands on the logic of exclusivity, not on anatomy.

**Who Is Being Answered**

Three groups in the Prophet's ﷺ world made claims the verse addresses. The Makkan polytheists held that the angels were God's daughters — a belief the Qur'an rebukes repeatedly and with evident exasperation:

> *"And they ascribe unto God daughters — glory be to Him! — and for themselves what they desire."* (Qur'an 16:57)

Because this is a Makkan sūrah, Ibn Kathīr judges that this is the primary referent. Christians held that Jesus is the son of God; Jews held that ʿUzayr is the son of God, as 9:30 records:

> *"The Jews say ʿUzayr is the son of God, and the Christians say the Messiah is the son of God. That is their saying with their mouths, imitating the saying of those who disbelieved before. God's curse be upon them! How they are perverted!"* (Qur'an 9:30)

The Qur'an's response to all three is identical in structure: God is One, self-sufficient, without need; a child implies need, similarity, and succession; therefore the claim is not merely false but incoherent. Sūrah al-Ikhlāṣ states the argument in four verses:

> *"Say: He is God, One. God, the Eternally Sufficient. He begets not, nor is He begotten. And there is none comparable unto Him."* (Qur'an 112:1–4)

**Why the Charge Is Treated as Gravely as It Is**

The modern reader may wonder why the Qur'an treats this claim with such severity, given that many who make it intend it as the highest possible honour for God and for the person they revere. The Qur'anic answer is that the claim does not honour God but *reduces* Him. To say God has taken a son is to say that God needs an heir, a representative, a deputy — that His governance is delegated, His presence mediated, His authority shared. This dismantles the entire moral logic of the Qur'an, in which every human being stands before God directly, without intermediary, and is accountable in his own person:

> *"And no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another."* (Qur'an 35:18)

If a divine son exists, the direct relationship collapses into a hierarchy of access, and the poor believer with no connection stands further from God than the one who does. The Qur'an's insistence on divine Oneness is, among other things, an insistence on the *equality of access*.

**The Warning, Not the Condemnation, Comes First**

Observe the verb: *yundhira* — "that He may warn." The verse does not say "that He may punish those who say." It says the Book is sent to *warn* them. The warning precedes the consequence and exists precisely so that the consequence may be avoided. This is the structure of divine justice throughout the Qur'an: no punishment without prior notification.

> *"And We never punish until We have sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

The verse therefore contains its own mercy. The people holding this belief are being addressed, personally, by a Book sent to them — the fact of address is itself an invitation to reconsider. Verse 5 will characterize the claim's intellectual standing; verse 4 preserves the fact that the speaker is still being spoken to.

**Why the Child-Claim Is Named Separately**

The commentators have asked why the verse singles out the claim that *"God has taken a child"* rather than subsuming it under general disbelief. The answer the classical exegetes give is historical and theological at once.

Historically, the claim was live in the Prophet's ﷺ environment: the Quraysh said the angels were God's daughters (cf. 53:19–23), and the verse answers a claim actually being made. Theologically, the claim is not one error among many but a specific denial of God's self-sufficiency — *al-Ṣamad*, the One upon whom all depend and who depends on none (112:2). To say God takes a child is to say He has a need, and need is the definition of a created thing.

The Qur'an answers the claim directly at 112:3–4:

> *"He begets not, nor is He begotten; and there is none comparable unto Him."* (Qur'an 112:3–4)

**The Warning Comes Before the Verdict**

The commentators note the verse's order: it warns rather than pronounces. The word *yundhira* — to warn, to give notice of danger — presupposes that the one warned can still act. A verdict closes the matter; a warning leaves it open.

The chapter opens with a warning, and the reader should note that every warning in the Qur'an is addressed to someone who is not yet condemned. The verse is therefore an act of mercy toward the very people it names, and the commentators have read its severity as evidence of its seriousness rather than of its finality.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:5

> **They have no knowledge thereof, nor do their fathers. A monstrous word it is that issues from their mouths. They speak naught but a lie.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Diagnosis: Not Error, But Absence**

Verse 5 answers the claim of verse 4 with an epistemological verdict before a theological one: *mā lahum bihi min ʿilm* — "they have no knowledge of it." The construction is emphatic in Arabic: *mā* (negation), *lahum* (prepositional phrase fronted), *min* (the particle that intensifies negation), and *ʿilm* (indefinite, so "any knowledge at all"). Read plainly: they do not possess a shred of knowledge concerning this claim.

The commentators — al-Suyūṭī and al-Zamakhsharī — note that the verse identifies the *source* of the error rather than merely labelling it. The claim is not derived from revelation, nor from demonstration, nor from observation. It has no root. This is important because it tells the reader what kind of correction is required: not a better argument, since there is no argument to answer, but the acquisition of a source.

**"Nor Do Their Fathers": The Anatomy of Inherited Belief**

The second clause — *wa lā li-ābāʾihim* — "nor to their fathers" — cuts off the escape route. When challenged, the natural defence of an inherited belief is that it was received from one's forefathers, and that the forefathers must have had reasons. The verse removes the appeal: the fathers did not know either. What was transmitted was not knowledge but a phrase.

The Qur'an attacks this pattern of unexamined inheritance with remarkable persistence — 2:170, 5:104, 7:70–71, 21:53–54, 37:68–72, 43:23–24. Its most famous formulation:

> *"And when it is said unto them, 'Follow that which God has sent down,' they say, 'Nay, we follow that which we found our fathers upon.' What! Even though their fathers understood nothing and were not guided?"* (Qur'an 2:170)

The logic is worth stating explicitly, because it applies well beyond religious belief. A belief held without a source is not thereby false — but it is not *known*, and a person who holds it cannot distinguish it from a falsehood he might equally have inherited. The verse is not insulting the fathers; it is observing that ignorance transmitted is still ignorance. Every generation that repeats a claim without checking it converts a possibly-tested proposition into an untested slogan.

The practical force is considerable. The single most common reason adults hold the views they hold is that their parents held them — political views, national prejudices, religious identities, assumptions about who is trustworthy. The verse asks the reader to locate the moment at which each of his own convictions was actually verified, and it is candid that for many there was no such moment.

**"A Monstrous Word": The Weight of the Term**

*Kaburat kalimatan takhruju min afwāhihim* — "Enormous is the word that comes out of their mouths." The verb *kabura* here functions exclamatorily — "how enormous!" — and the commentators note that the enormity is moral as well as intellectual. Al-Suyūṭī reads *kaburat* as expressing the gravity of the claim; al-Zamakhsharī reads it as expressing the shock such a claim should provoke in anyone who hears it.

The word appears in the same construction at 17:40, where the Qur'an responds to the same claim. Its use signals that this is not a minor doctrinal variation but a categorical error about the nature of God. The commentators reason as follows: every other theological mistake concerns a detail within a framework; this one concerns the framework itself. To assign offspring to God is to misidentify what God is.

**"Issues From Their Mouths": Locating the Fault in the Organ of Speech**

The phrase *takhruju min afwāhihim* — "comes out of their mouths" — is, as Ibn Kathīr observes, a way of saying that the claim has no origin beyond the mouth that utters it. It does not come from a Book, or from an angel, or from a prophet, or from the mind's examination. It comes from lips.

The Qur'an frequently locates falsehood in the organs of speech to emphasize its groundlessness — *"that is their saying with their mouths"* (9:30), *"they say with their mouths what is not in their hearts"* (3:167). The imagery is diagnostic: a statement that exists only at the level of the tongue has not passed through the heart, where conviction lives, nor through the intellect, where evidence is weighed. It is a sound, produced socially, maintained by repetition.

This is precisely the mechanism by which false claims survive for centuries. The claim about God's child was not held by most of its holders as a worked-out position; it was recited. Recited claims are extremely durable because they are never examined — examination would end them.

**"They Speak Naught But a Lie"**

The verse closes: *in yaqūlūna illā kadhiban* — "they say nothing but a lie." The restrictive construction *in ... illā* — "nothing but" — is absolute. Having established that there is no knowledge behind the claim, that the fathers had none either, that the enormity is moral, and that the utterance has no source but the mouth, the verse pronounces the conclusion: what remains is *kadhib*, falsehood.

Al-Rāzī raises an obvious question at this point: many who make the claim are sincere, devout, morally upright people. How can they be speaking "nothing but a lie"? His answer distinguishes between *sincerity* and *truth*. Sincerity is a property of the speaker's intention; truth is a property of the statement. A person may be entirely sincere in asserting something false, and his sincerity neither makes it true nor excuses him from the duty to seek its verification. The verse condemns the *statement*, and the ignorance that sustains it — not the moral worth of everyone who repeats it.

The two verses together (4 and 5) therefore form a complete epistemology in miniature: identify the claim, ask for its source, trace it to those who transmitted it, evaluate its moral gravity, locate where it actually lives (in mouths), and name what it is. That sequence is a method, and the Qur'an intends it to be applied far beyond this single doctrine.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:6

> **Yet perhaps thou wouldst destroy thyself with grief for their sake, should they believe not in this account.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Pivot Verse of the Whole Chapter**

Verse 6 turns the address directly to the Prophet ﷺ: *fa-laʿallaka bākhiʿun nafsaka ʿalā āthārihim in lam yuʾminū bi-hādhā al-ḥadīthi asafā* — "Then perhaps you would kill yourself with grief, following in their footsteps, if they do not believe in this message." The word *bākhiʿ* is from *b-kh-ʿ*, meaning to slaughter an animal so thoroughly that the knife reaches the vertebrae — one of the most violent words for killing in Arabic. Al-Rāzī renders the sense as "to expend yourself" or "to consume yourself"; the commentators agree the image is of a person destroying himself through the intensity of his grief.

The verse is almost identical to 26:3 — *"Perhaps you would kill yourself with grief that they do not become believers"* — and its placement here is structurally decisive. It sits between the theological prologue (vv. 1–5) and the parable of the earth's adornment (vv. 7–8), immediately before the cave narrative begins. Al-Suyūṭī and Ibn Kathīr note the function: the chapter is about to tell the story of a small group of believers preserved through persecution, and this verse prepares it by addressing the Prophet's ﷺ anguish over the persecution of his own.

**A Portrait of the Prophet ﷺ in a Single Word**

What this verse discloses about the Prophet ﷺ is remarkable, and the commentators dwell on it. Here is a man whose people have called him a liar, a madman, a poet; who have boycotted his clan into a ravine for three years; who have thrown camel entrails on his back while he prayed; who will have his teeth broken and his uncle mutilated. And his overwhelming emotion toward them is not resentment but *grief that they will be lost*.

The Qur'an testifies to this disposition repeatedly:

> *"We know well that what they say grieves thee. Yet it is not thee that they deny. Rather, it is the signs of God that the wrongdoers reject."* (Qur'an 6:33)

> *"So let not thyself go to grief over them. Truly God is the Knower of what they do."* (Qur'an 35:8)

And the sūrah that describes him to his community names mercy, not severity, as his defining quality:

> *"It is by the mercy of God that thou wast gentle with them. Hadst thou been harsh and hard-hearted, they would surely have scattered from around thee."* (Qur'an 3:159)

And 9:128 — *"There has come unto you a Messenger from among yourselves. Grievous unto him is your suffering; he is solicitous for you, and unto the believers compassionate and merciful."* The word *ʿazīz* there — "grievous" — describes the pain your suffering causes him.

**The Consolation Embedded in a Rebuke**

Read carefully, the verse does something unusual: it corrects the Prophet ﷺ while honouring the feeling that needs correcting. It does not say his grief is misplaced or unworthy; it says it may *destroy* him, and that this is beyond what is required of him. The theological principle involved is the limit of human responsibility. A messenger's duty is *balāgh* — transmission, delivery — not *hidāyah*, the granting of guidance, which belongs to God alone:

> *"Truly thou guidest not whom thou lovest; but God guideth whom He will. And He knows best those who are guided."* (Qur'an 28:56)

> *"So remind. Thou art only a reminder. Thou art not a warden over them."* (Qur'an 88:21–22)

This principle is one of the most liberating in the Qur'an, and it applies to every person who has ever grieved over someone else's choices — a parent over a child, a teacher over a student, a friend over a friend. You are responsible for delivering the truth clearly and kindly. You are not responsible for the outcome, and the attempt to carry responsibility for the outcome will destroy you without helping them. The verse locates a genuine boundary in human agency.

**"In Their Footsteps": Grief as a Kind of Attachment**

The phrase *ʿalā āthārihim* — literally "upon their traces, following their footsteps" — is a striking image that the commentators gloss in a revealing way. Al-Rāzī reads it as the posture of one who has departed: the person stands watching the tracks left in the sand by people walking away from him. The grief is not only for their fate but for their *departure* — the felt experience of losing people.

This gives the verse a psychological precision that general consolations lack. What wounds the caller to truth is not primarily the arguments of opponents but the sight of people he loves walking away. The image validates the pain — it is the pain of love — while diagnosing its danger: standing over footprints accomplishes nothing for the walkers.

**Why This Comes Before the Cave Story**

The placement is not accidental, and the commentators make the connection explicit. Al-Suyūṭī and Ibn Kathīr both note that the cave narrative follows this verse as a *practical consolation*: just as God preserved that small band of believers in the teeth of a tyrant's persecution, so He will preserve this one. The chapter's own occasion of revelation reinforces the point — the sūrah came after fifteen days of prophetic anxiety and Qurayshi triumphalism. Verse 6 acknowledges the anxiety; verses 9 onward answer it with a story.

There is also an implicit rebalancing. The Prophet ﷺ is being consumed by grief over people who reject the message; the chapter is about to show him that God's care for the few who accept it is more operative in history than the obstinacy of the many who refuse it. Seven young men in a cave outlasted an empire. The proportion matters.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:7

> **Surely We made whatsoever is on the earth an adornment for it, that We may try them as to which of them is most virtuous in deed.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Zīnah: The World Presented as Decoration, Not as Home**

The word at the centre of this verse is *zīnah* — adornment, ornament, finery. *Innā jaʿalnā mā ʿalā al-arḍi zīnatan lahā* — "We have made whatever is upon the earth an adornment for it." The choice of noun is theologically loaded and the commentators press it. An adornment is something that beautifies a thing without being the thing itself: jewellery adorns a person but is not the person; paint adorns a house but is not the house. To call the contents of the earth *zīnah* is to classify the entire material world as decoration upon a substrate, which is what the verse goes on to say — verse 8 will strip the decoration off and reveal bare ground.

Al-Rāzī enumerates what the adornment comprises: the flora, the fauna, the rivers and mountains, the gold and minerals beneath the surface. He adds a striking suggestion — that the noblest of the earth's adornments is *humanity itself*, so that the verse quietly includes the reader among the decorations. Al-Ghazzālī, in the *Iḥyāʾ ʿUlūm al-Dīn* (Beirut ed., 5:340), draws the ascetic conclusion the verse is designed to produce: the adornment exists precisely so that the human being may be tested on whether he can hold it without being held by it.

**Why God Made It Beautiful**

The verse is emphatic that the beautification is divine: *jaʿalnā* — "We made." This forecloses the ascetic error that runs through many religious traditions, in which matter is treated as inherently base or as a corruption of spirit. The Qur'an's position is the opposite: the world is beautiful *because God made it so*, and its beauty is a sign of Him:

> *"Say: Who has forbidden the adornment of God which He has brought forth for His servants, and the good things of provision?"* (Qur'an 7:32)

The beauty of the world is not the problem. The problem is what the human being does with it. And here the verse supplies its purpose clause: *li-nabluwahum* — "that We may try them."

**The Test Is Not Whether You Have It, But What You Do**

The trial named in the verse is precise and worth examining closely: *ayyuhum aḥsanu ʿamalā* — "which of them is best in deed." Not *which of them has most*, not *which of them abstains most*, but *which is best in action*. The commentators — Ibn Kathīr among them — draw on the well-known exposition of this phrase attributed to the early scholars, in two dimensions: a deed is *aḥsan* when it is **sincere** (done for God) and **correct** (conforming to what God has legislated). Beauty in action is a combination of interior intention and exterior form.

This framing has practical consequences that the ordinary language of "trial" obscures. A trial in which abstention automatically scores highest would be trivially gamed — one simply acquires nothing. The Qur'an's trial is harder: you may have the garden, the wealth, the beauty, and still win — provided you handle it with gratitude, generosity, and awareness that it is decoration. The chapter will dramatize both outcomes within a few verses: the owner of the two gardens has the adornment and fails; the poor companion has almost none and succeeds.

**"Asfan" and the Psychology of Detachment**

The verse quietly establishes the mental posture Islam calls *zuhd*. The commentators define *zuhd* not as poverty but as the removal of the world from the heart rather than from the hand. ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib is reported to have said: *"Zuhd is not that you own nothing, but that nothing owns you."* The verse's logic supports this: since the contents of the earth are *zīnah* — decoration, temporary, external — the person who has correctly perceived their nature can enjoy them without mistaking them for the substance of his life.

A useful analogy is a hotel room. A traveller who understands he is in a hotel keeps the room tidy, uses its amenities, appreciates its comfort — and does not begin remodelling the bathroom. The person who forgets he is in a hotel invests in the wrong things and is devastated when he is checked out. The world is the room; *zīnah* is the classification; the test is whether you remember.

**Directly Answering the Prophet's Grief**

Read after verse 6, this verse performs a therapeutic function. The Prophet ﷺ is grieving over people who reject him. The verse responds by re-classifying what those people are rejecting him *for*. They reject him because they want the adornment, and he is calling them away from it. Verse 7 tells him — and the reader — that the adornment was made *as a test*, which means that those who fail the test are not victims of fate but participants in an examination they chose to fail by refusing the paper.

The chapter will return to this classification at verse 46, in what is effectively this verse's conclusion: *"Wealth and children are the adornment of the life of this world, but the enduring righteous deeds are better with thy Lord in reward, and better as a hope."* The frame is closed: v. 7 states the adornment as test; v. 46 states the adornment as contrast. Between them lie the cave, the garden, the sea, and the wall.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:8

> **And surely We shall make whatsoever is upon it a barren plain!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ṣaʿīdan Juruzā: The Earth Stripped Bare**

The verse is short and its two nouns are severe. *Ṣaʿīd* denotes the bare, level surface of the earth — the ground itself, exposed, with nothing upon it. *Juruz* denotes land stripped of vegetation, barren, unable to grow, as in the Qur'an's use of the same root at 32:27:

> *"Have they not seen that We drive water unto the barren land and bring forth thereby crops whereof they eat, and their cattle, and they themselves? Will they not then see?"* (Qur'an 32:27)

There, *juruz* is the state *before* God's mercy arrives; here, at 18:8, it is the state to which the earth *returns*. Ibn Kathīr makes the point directly: none of the earth's adornments will endure; all of it will come to an end and return to God. The Qur'an states the same elsewhere with equal finality:

> *"All that is upon it passes away, and the Face of thy Lord endures, full of majesty and bounty."* (Qur'an 55:26–27)

**The Rhetorical Shock of the Sequence**

Verse 7 says *We made*; verse 8 says *We shall make*. The same divine act of making, applied twice, produces opposite results. This is not carelessness but the point. The God who decorated the earth is the same God who will strip it, and both are described with the same verb of making. Nothing in the world's fate depends on the world.

The commentators observe the deliberate contrast in imagery: verse 7's *zīnah* — colour, variety, ornament — against verse 8's *ṣaʿīd juruz* — flat, bare, monochrome. Between the two lies the entire span of human history, and the verse asks the reader to hold both images simultaneously. Al-Ṭabarī notes the rhetorical effect: the second verse destroys the emotional investment the first verse creates. The reader's eye has just been given a beautiful world; two lines later it is a scoured plain.

**The Eschatological Confirmation Elsewhere**

The Qur'an describes this stripping repeatedly, in language that leaves no room for a symbolic reading:

> *"And on the Day We set the mountains in motion, and thou seest the earth an open plain, and We gather them and leave not one of them behind."* (Qur'an 18:47)

> *"And they ask thee concerning the mountains. Say: My Lord will grind them to powder and scatter them as dust, and leave them a level empty plain, wherein thou seest neither crookedness nor curve."* (Qur'an 20:105–107)

Verse 47 of this same chapter will return to the image within a few pages — *kāna* in the earth a *qāʿan ṣafṣafā*, "an open plain." The chapter states its cosmology twice, and states it identically.

**Why the Announcement Is a Mercy, Not Only a Threat**

It would be a mistake to read verse 8 as pure menace. Its function is corrective, and the correction is generous. Human beings systematically overestimate the permanence of their surroundings; this is close to a cognitive default. We plan as though the current arrangement will last, invest as though it will last, grieve as though its ending were an aberration. The verse announces the ending in advance so that the reader's expectations can be recalibrated *before* the loss, rather than shattered by it.

The practical instruction is the one every mature person eventually learns through bereavement: everything you love in this world will be taken from you or you from it, and knowing this in advance is what allows you to love it properly — with open hands, gratefully, without the desperate grip that turns love into anxiety. The Sufi commentators read verse 7–8 together as the ground of *tawakkul*: if the world is decoration that will be stripped, then reliance must be placed on the One who does not pass away.

**The Answer to the Garden-Owner Before the Parable Begins**

The structural function is now clear. Verses 7–8 form a two-line prologue: the earth is decorated as a test; the decoration will be removed. Verse 32 will introduce a man with two gardens of vines and palms and a stream running between them — the adornment at full display. Verses 40–42 will show God sending a calamity from the sky and turning the garden into a flattened, barren plain — *ṣaʿīdān zalaqā*, then the water sinking so deep it cannot be reached. The parable is verses 7–8 dramatized at the scale of one man's life. Everything the chapter will teach about wealth is already stated in these two verses; the story exists so that the reader feels it rather than merely assents to it.

**Juruzā and the Agricultural Vocabulary**

The commentators note that *juruza* is agricultural language: land that has been stripped of vegetation, whether by being cut, by drought, or by overgrazing. The word implies that the land once produced. It is not the vocabulary of desert but of loss.

This matters for the verse's argument. Verse 7 described the earth's contents as an *adornment* — *zīnah*, decoration, that which makes a thing attractive. Verse 8 describes the same earth stripped of the adornment, and the word chosen is one that a farmer would use of a field that has been cut back to nothing.

The pairing of *zīnah* and *juruza* gives the chapter's clearest statement of the relation between the world and the test: what is attractive is also what will be removed, and the test is precisely whether the person treats the adornment as the thing itself. Verse 46 will state the comparison explicitly — *"wealth and children are the adornment of the life of this world"* — using the same word the chapter introduced here.

The commentators have drawn the instruction that the world's attractiveness is not denied but framed. It is real, and it is temporary, and both facts are stated in the same breath.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:9

> **Dost thou reckon that the Companions of the Cave and the Inscription are a marvel among Our signs?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Is Also a Reprimand of Astonishment**

The verse opens with a rhetorical question to the Prophet ﷺ: *am ḥasibta anna aṣḥāba al-kahfi wa'l-raqīmi kānū min āyātinā ʿajabā* — "Or do you suppose that the Companions of the Cave and the Inscription were a wonder among Our signs?" The commentators — Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī — converge on the sense: the story is indeed wondrous, but it is *not* the most wondrous of God's signs. Greater wonders surround it in plain sight.

Al-Rāzī develops the argument: the creation of the heavens and the earth, the alternation of night and day, the growth of a seed, the formation of a human being from a drop of fluid — these are miracles of a far higher order than the sleep of seven young men, because they are continuous, universal, and inexhaustible. A miracle that suspends the order of nature once is less astounding, on reflection, than the order itself, which never suspends. This is a profound epistemological point, and it is the reason the Qur'an so often directs attention from the spectacular to the ordinary: the spectacular is a footnote; the ordinary is the text.

**Al-Kahf: A Specific Kind of Cave**

The word *kahf* is not the general word for cave (*ghār*, used at 9:40 for the cave where the Prophet ﷺ sheltered with Abū Bakr). Al-Rāzī, al-Suyūṭī, and al-Zamakhsharī specify that *kahf* denotes a spacious cavern within a mountain — a large enough interior to be a dwelling. The word's specificity matters for the narrative: this is not a crevice but a chamber, and the Qur'an later describes it as having a broad opening (v. 17, *fajwah*) and a threshold (v. 18, *waṣīd*).

**Al-Raqīm: The Word the Commentators Debated**

*Al-raqīm* derives from *r-q-m*, to write, to mark, to inscribe — the same root that produces *raqm* (a number, a digit) and *murqim*. The commentators offer a striking spread of readings, and the honesty with which they present alternatives is itself instructive:

- **An inscribed tablet.** The majority — al-Ṭabarī, al-Suyūṭī, al-Zamakhsharī, al-Qurṭubī — hold that it was a tablet of lead or stone placed at the cave's entrance, or an inscription carved into the mountain beside it, recording the youths' names, their story, and their fate. Some reports add that the townspeople later recovered this tablet from the cave (al-Ṭabarī).
- **A place-name.** The valley, the mountain, or the region in which the cave stood; Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī report suggestions locating it between northwest Arabia and southern Palestine, while others proposed Ephesus in present-day Turkey or a site near Amman in Jordan.
- **A building.** The commemorative structure raised over the cave — connecting to verse 21's dispute about erecting a building or a *masjid*.
- **A book of scripture.** Al-Qurṭubī reports the suggestion that *raqīm* refers to a written book of Christian law and teaching, since the youths were Christians persecuted for their religion.
- **A person.** A small minority suggested it was the name of the youths' leader, or even of their dog.

Ibn ʿAbbās is famously reported to have said that he did not know what *al-raqīm* meant — a remark preserved precisely because it models intellectual honesty in a tradition that could easily have invented a confident answer. The Qur'an leaves the word unexplained, and the commentators leave it uncertain, and the uncertainty costs the story nothing.

**Why the Story Was Told at All: The Occasion**

The chapter's reason for being is recorded in the sources Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī transmit. The Quraysh sent al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith and ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ to the Jewish scholars of Yathrib, who advised them to test the Prophet ﷺ with three questions — the young men of ancient times, the traveller who reached the east and west, and the Spirit. The Prophet ﷺ answered that he would respond the next day, without adding *inshāʾ Allāh*, and fifteen days passed in silence while Makkah concluded that his claim had collapsed. Then the chapter came.

The lesson embedded in the delay is the subject of verses 23–24, and it is severe: the Messenger of God was corrected, in a chapter revealed to defend him, for omitting the phrase *inshāʾ Allāh*. Al-Qurṭubī draws the ruling that this is a permanent instruction, not a historical footnote.

**"A Marvel Among Our Signs" — The Consolation Beneath the Question**

Al-Suyūṭī notes the pastoral function of the verse following verse 6. The Prophet ﷺ has just been told not to destroy himself with grief. He is now given a story whose moral is that God preserves a tiny number of believers against an empire. Ibn Kathīr and al-Suyūṭī both draw the parallel: as God delivered those youths, so He will deliver this community.

There is a further layer. The youths achieved their preservation by *leaving*. The Quraysh were demanding miraculous proof while remaining exactly where they were; the believers were being asked to leave their homes, and eventually would. The story validates migration as a religious act — which, given that the Hijrah lay a short time ahead, is not incidental.

**Karāmah and the Question of Non-Prophetic Miracles**

The commentators raise a doctrinal question prompted by this story: the Companions of the Cave were not prophets, yet they received what is plainly a supernatural intervention. Al-Rāzī at verse 12 and al-Qushayrī at verse 17 use the narrative as evidence for *karāmāt* — miracles granted to the *awliyāʾ* (the friends of God) — as distinct from *muʿjizāt*, which God grants only to prophets and which function as credentials for a public claim. The distinction is standard in Sunni theology: a *muʿjizah* is accompanied by a prophetic challenge; a *karāmah* is not claimed by its recipient and is never used as proof of authority. The youths asked for nothing spectacular; they asked for mercy.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:10

> **When the youths took refuge in the cave, they said, "Our Lord! Grant us mercy from Thy Presence, and make us incline to sound judgment concerning our affair."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fatawā and the Choice of Word for Young Men**

The Qur'an calls them *al-fityah* — plural of *fatā*, a young man. The commentators note that the word carries more than an age reference; it denotes youth in the sense of vigour, courage, and unhardened conscience. Ibn Kathīr makes a sociological observation the Qur'an supports throughout: it is typically the young who respond to the prophets, while the elders — the *malaʾ*, the notables — resist, because the elders have positions, wealth, and reputations to defend. The Qur'an records the pattern explicitly in the case of Abraham:

> *"They said: We heard a youth (fatan) mention them; he is called Abraham."* (Qur'an 21:60)

And 21:59–60 has the elders' response to a *fatā*. The classical tradition took the connection seriously enough that the word *fityah* was linked to *futuwwah* — spiritual chivalry — a concept al-Qurṭubī and al-Suyūṭī develop: generosity, restraint from the forbidden, never complaining of one's lot, keeping one's word, freedom from hypocrisy, detachment from worldly attachments. The term later gave its name to artisanal guilds across the Islamic world precisely because it described a *character*, not an age.

**"Took Refuge in the Cave": The Word Istawā and Its Meaning**

The verb is *awā* — *idh awā al-fityatu ilā al-kahf* — "when the youths took refuge in the cave." The root *a-w-y* means to seek shelter, to betake oneself to a place of safety, and it is used in the Qur'an of God's sheltering of His servants. The youths did not merely *enter* the cave; they *took refuge* in it. The preposition *ilā* — "toward" — reinforces the directionality: they moved toward safety, and the commentators (Ibn Kathīr, al-Suyūṭī) specify that the refuge they sought was from persecution for their religion.

The historical setting transmitted by al-Ṭabarī and al-Ṭabrisī names the persecutor as Daqyānūs (or Daqyūs) — an Arabization of Decius, the Roman emperor whose persecution of Christians in the mid-third century matches the Christian tradition of the Seven Sleepers of Ephesus. Al-Ṭabrisī also transmits the possibility, from some Christian sources, of a Zoroastrian king persecuting Christian subjects. The Qur'an itself names no king, no city, and no date — a deliberate economy that the commentators have long remarked upon.

**The Prayer's First Petition: Mercy From Thy Presence**

*Rabbanā ātinā min ladunka raḥmah* — "Our Lord, grant us mercy from Thy Presence." The phrase *min ladunka* — "from Thy Presence, from with Thee" — is one of the Qur'an's most weighty expressions, used of what comes directly from God rather than through ordinary channels: the knowledge given to Khiḍr is *ʿilman min ladunnā* (18:65), and the punishment warned of in verse 2 is *min ladunhu*.

Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī read the petition as a request for mercy *from God's own storehouse* — not the mediated mercy that comes through favourable circumstances, allies, or resources, but the unmediated mercy that arrives when all channels are closed. Al-Rāzī unpacks what they are asking for in five parts: guidance and knowledge; provision; patience in the trial; security from their enemies; and eventual deliverance. Note the sequence: knowledge and patience come *before* safety.

There is a psychological precision here that repays attention. The youths are asking for the right things in the right order. A person in danger instinctively asks for escape. These young men ask first for mercy — for the interior condition that will make any outcome bearable — and only then for the outcome. Al-Suyūṭī connects the petition to the Prophet's ﷺ reported supplication, cited by Ibn Kathīr: *"O God, set right for me the whole of my affair"* — and to the phrase *"and do not leave me to myself for the blink of an eye."*

**The Second Petition: "Make Our Affair Right for Us"**

*Wa hayyiʾ lanā min amrinā rashadā* — "and prepare for us, from our affair, right guidance." The verb *hayyaʾa* means to prepare, to make ready, to set in order. The noun *rashad* is right guidance, sound judgment, correctness of course — from the same root as *rāshid*, and closely connected to the Qur'anic vocabulary of *rushd*, mature soundness of judgment (as at 2:256, *"there is no compulsion in religion; guidance has become clear from error"* uses *al-rushd*).

The commentators — al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Suyūṭī, al-Zamakhsharī — explain the petition as a request that the *end* of their affair be sound: that whatever happens, it issue in guidance rather than ruin. Some read it as a prayer for a safe exit from the cave. Both readings converge on a single insight: they are not asking to be rescued from the situation, but for the situation itself to be made *right*.

This is the prayer of a person who has understood that he cannot control circumstances but can ask that circumstances be arranged toward a good end. It is, in modern terms, the difference between demanding a specific outcome and requesting a good outcome whatever the specific form. Al-Qushayrī notes the Sufi reading: their plea for mercy was a plea to be preserved *in the remembrance of God* — and since the person wholly absorbed in God's remembrance stands outside ordinary time, their centuries of sleep become an image of that absorption.

**What They Left Behind**

The commentators supply the context that gives the prayer its weight. The youths were, according to the transmitted reports, sons of noble families or members of the king's court — al-Suyūṭī and al-Ṭabrisī both record this — who had come to belief in One God and were concealing it. Some reports describe them as having met independently and discovered their shared faith by chance outside the city, the eldest speaking first (Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī). Others describe them as Christians attempting to preserve true belief in a period of corruption and idolatry.

The king, on learning of their refuge, had the cave's entrance sealed, expecting them to die of hunger and thirst. What the verse records is their last free act: they walked into a sealed space and prayed. The prayer is the only action available to them, and the Qur'an makes it the pivot of the entire story — everything that follows, including the three centuries of sleep, is the answer to these two petitions.

**The Cave as Retreat and as Heart**

Al-Qushayrī's reading, preserved by al-Qurṭubī and the later commentators, identifies the cave with the heart: the place to which the believer withdraws from a world that has become hostile to faith, and in which God shelters him. The reports add that the youths spent their time in the cave in prayer and praise, wholly devoted to God.

The practical application is not merely mystical. Every tradition of serious spiritual life has recognised the need for withdrawal — the *khalwah*, the retreat, the cell — not as escape from responsibility but as the condition of returning to it intact. The verse legitimises withdrawal when remaining means losing one's religion, and it makes clear that withdrawal is not passive: the youths withdrew and *prayed*, and the prayer was an act.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:11

> **So We placed [a veil] over their ears in the cave for a number of years.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Struck Upon Their Ears": The Idiom of Deep Sleep**

The Arabic is *fa-ḍarabnā ʿalā ādhānihim fī al-kahfi sinīna ʿadadā* — literally, "So We struck upon their ears in the cave for a number of years." The idiom *ḍaraba ʿalā al-udhun* — to strike upon the ear — is not used anywhere else in the Qur'an in exactly this form, and the commentators explain it consistently: al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī read it as rendering them incapable of hearing anything that might wake them; Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabrisī, and al-Suyūṭī read it as putting them into a very deep sleep.

The image is exact. The ear is the sense organ that continues to function during sleep — the sleeper's eyes are closed but his ears remain open to the world, which is why a mother wakes at her infant's cry and not at the traffic outside. To "strike upon the ears" is therefore to close the *last remaining aperture* between the sleeper and the world. The verse is describing a suspension more complete than ordinary sleep: the senses sealed at the point where they are ordinarily most vigilant.

**"A Number of Years": Deliberate Vagueness**

The phrase *sinīna ʿadadā* — "years, a number [of them]" — is, as al-Zamakhsharī notes, an expression that can denote either a large or a small quantity, and is deliberately unspecified. Verse 25 will supply the figure — three hundred years, plus nine — and verse 26 will immediately subordinate that figure to God's knowledge: *"Say: God knows best how long they tarried."*

The commentators observe the literary effect of the vagueness here. The reader is given a duration without a number, so that the emphasis falls on the *fact* of long duration rather than its arithmetic. Al-Suyūṭī notes that the youths themselves experienced this long span as "a day or part of a day" (v. 19) — the disparity between objective and experienced time being one of the story's central themes, and one that verse 11 prepares by refusing to quantify.

**They Did Not Die**

A crucial point the commentators make explicitly (al-Suyūṭī, al-Ṭabrisī): the description indicates that the youths *did not die* but remained in a deep sleep. This matters for the story's function. If they had died and been resurrected, the account would be a resurrection narrative; because they slept and woke, it is a *barzakh* narrative — an account of an intermediate state that mirrors the state of the grave without being death.

The Qur'an's own vocabulary supports the parallel. Sleep is repeatedly called a kind of death:

> *"And He it is Who takes your souls by night, and knows what you commit by day. Then He raises you up by day, that a term appointed may be fulfilled."* (Qur'an 6:60)

> *"God takes the souls at the time of their death, and those that die not during their sleep. Then He keeps those for which He has ordained death, and sends the others back for a term appointed."* (Qur'an 39:42)

The well-known saying attributed to the Prophet ﷺ and to ʿAlī — *"People are asleep; when they die they wake"* — expresses the same hierarchy. Verse 11's sealing of the ears is the story's way of describing a soul held in suspension: alive, but withdrawn from the world, exactly as the soul in the *barzakh* is described in Islamic eschatology.

**The Divine Act Behind the Preservation**

Notice the verb: *ḍarabnā* — "We struck." The preservation is God's act, not the youths'. They did not achieve three centuries of survival through endurance, technique, or luck; it was done *to* them. This is consistent with the chapter's theology of grace: the youths contributed the faith and the flight and the prayer, and God contributed everything else.

Al-Rāzī draws the doctrinal conclusion at verse 12: this is a *karāmah*, a miracle granted to non-prophets, and it establishes that God's miraculous power is not restricted to the prophetic office. The distinction from a *muʿjizah* is preserved because the youths claimed no authority on the basis of what happened to them — they were, indeed, unaware of it while it happened.

**Why Sleep, and Not Escape or Victory**

The choice of miracle is instructive. God could have destroyed the king, blinded the pursuers, opened a back exit from the cave, or made the youths invisible. Instead He put them to sleep. The commentators offer reasons that go to the heart of the story's message.

First, sleep is *hidden*: no observer sees a miracle, so no political legend accumulates around the youths and they do not become a cult. Second, sleep is *passive*: the youths are not required to perform anything; the entire work is God's, which prevents the miracle from being read as a reward for their prowess. Third, sleep is *time-spanning*: it demonstrates power over duration, not merely over an event, which is precisely the power at issue in the resurrection — the raising of bodies after centuries. Fourth, sleep is *reversible in a way that mirrors resurrection*: they wake, they ask how long they have been there, they are told God knows best — the exact sequence the Qur'an describes for the resurrected at 10:45, 20:104, 23:112–13, 30:55, 79:46.

The modern reader can add a fifth observation: sleep is the one miracle every human being experiences nightly and never finds astonishing. The story takes the most ordinary phenomenon in human life and stretches it to three centuries, which is the chapter's method throughout — the wondrous is not the suspension of the ordinary but the ordinary at an impossible scale.

**The Sealed Cave and the Irony of the King**

The transmitted reports add a detail that gives the verse its irony: the king, having found the cave, sealed its entrance so that the youths would die of hunger and thirst. He intended the cave as a tomb. Verse 11 records that inside the tomb he built, God placed a veil upon their ears and they slept. The instrument of their execution became the instrument of their preservation — a pattern the Qur'an returns to throughout, most famously in the story of Moses, whose mother cast him into the river precisely because the river was the site of Pharaoh's slaughter of infants.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:12

> **Then We raised them up again, that We might know which of the two parties had best calculated how long they had tarried.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Raised Them": The Resurrection Verb Applied to Sleepers**

The verb is *baʿathnāhum* — "We raised them up." This is the same verb the Qur'an uses throughout for the raising of the dead on the Day of Judgment: *baʿth*, resurrection. Al-Rāzī and al-Suyūṭī note the deliberate usage: the youths are *raised*, not merely woken. The word choice turns the narrative into a enacted demonstration of the resurrection, and verse 21 will state the purpose in exactly these terms — *"that they might know that God's Promise is true and that there is no doubt about the Hour."*

This is the theological hinge of the cave story. The Makkan disbelievers' central objection was not to God's existence but to bodily resurrection — how can decayed bones be reconstituted? The Qur'an's method of answering is not argument but exhibited instance: here are bodies preserved unchanged across three centuries, and here they are standing.

**The Apparent Problem: "That We Might Know"**

The verse continues: *li-naʿlama ayyu al-ḥizbayni aḥṣā limā labithū amadā* — "that We might know which of the two parties had best reckoned the extent of their tarrying." Read literally, this suggests that God did not know and needed the event to occur in order to find out — which would contradict the Qur'an's absolute affirmation of divine knowledge:

> *"And with Him are the keys of the Unseen; none knows them save Him."* (Qur'an 6:59)

The commentators — al-Rāzī at length, al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr — address this directly, and their solution is a standard principle of Qur'anic interpretation that recurs at 2:143 and 47:31. The principle: God's testing of creatures is not to acquire knowledge He lacks but to bring into external existence what He already knows, so that it may be *seen*, *evidenced*, and *accountable*. Al-Rāzī formulates it as the distinction between God's knowledge of what will occur and the occurrence itself: the knowledge is eternal and complete; the occurrence is what makes the matter manifest to the creatures concerned.

A familiar analogy is an examination. The teacher who sets an exam usually knows how each student will perform. The exam does not inform the teacher; it informs the students, and it produces a public record that can be acted upon fairly. A grade awarded without an examination would be unaccountable, however accurate. God's *ʿilm* is not increased by events; the events are the arena in which His knowledge becomes justice.

**"Which of the Two Parties": Identifying the Disputants**

Who are the *ḥizbayn* — the two parties — who differed about the length of the tarrying? The commentators offer three readings. Al-Rāzī and al-Suyūṭī suggest two groups among the youths themselves — some estimating "a day," others "part of a day" (v. 19). Al-Rāzī also proposes the youths against the kings who reigned over the city across the intervening centuries. Ibn Kathīr offers the broadest reading: all who differed on the matter, including the later generations who disputed the number of the sleepers (v. 22) and the duration.

The word *aḥṣā* — from *ḥ-ṣ-y*, to count, to enumerate precisely, to take exact account — is significant. It is the verb of exact reckoning, and the verse asks which party reckoned *best*. The commentators note the implication: the answer is that the party who said *"Your Lord knows best how long you have tarried"* (v. 19) reckoned best of all, because the most accurate calculation of an unknown quantity is the acknowledgment that only God knows it. This is a subtle and beautiful epistemological point: precision is not only a matter of getting the number right but of knowing what you are entitled to claim.

**Karāmah: The Doctrinal Inference**

Al-Rāzī explicitly notes that the Sufis and the theologians take this verse as proof that beyond the *muʿjizāt* granted to prophets as credentials of their mission, God bestows other supernatural gifts — *karāmāt* — upon those who attain exceptional nearness to Him, the *awliyāʾ Allāh*. The two categories are distinguished in the classical texts along clear lines: a *muʿjizah* is accompanied by a public claim of prophethood and a challenge to opponents; a *karāmah* is not claimed, is not used to establish authority, and typically occurs unknown to its recipient.

The doctrine was contested — the Muʿtazilah generally denied *karāmāt*, since they threatened the exclusive evidentiary function of the prophetic miracle — but the mainstream position, articulated by al-Qushayrī, al-Baghdādī, and Ibn Taymiyyah among others, affirms them while insisting that a *karāmah* never contradicts revelation and never elevates its recipient above the Prophet ﷺ. Ibn Taymiyyah's formulation in the *ʿAqīdah Wāsiṭiyyah* is characteristic: the *karāmāt* of the saints are real, but they are not a mark of superiority, since the greatest of God's friends is the one most obedient to the Messenger.

**Why the Story Turns on a Question About Time**

It is worth asking why the resurrection demonstration is framed around *duration* rather than around the miracle of preservation itself. The answer lies in the specific objection being answered. The disbelievers did not doubt that God could keep bodies intact; they doubted that God would raise the dead *after long ages*, when bones had scattered and memory had faded. Their objection was about the reach of divine power across time. The youths' three centuries answer exactly that: not merely power over bodies but power over centuries.
