This is why verse 12's focus on the *reckoning* of the years is not a digression but the point. The party that reckons best is the party that understands that time itself belongs to God — which is the underlying conviction the whole sūrah will confirm, from the garden that perishes to the wall that crumbles at the Hour.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:13

> **We recount their story unto thee in truth. Verily they were youths who believed in their Lord, and We increased them in guidance.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"In Truth": The Guarantee Prefixed to the Narrative**

The verse opens with *naḥnu naquṣṣu ʿalayka nabaʾahum bi'l-ḥaqq* — "We relate to you their account in truth." The commentators note that the Qur'an frequently prefaces or closes its narratives with an assertion of truthfulness, and the reason is contextual. The story the Prophet ﷺ was about to tell was already known to the Jewish scholars of Yathrib and, in a variant form, to Christians across the Near East. Any discrepancy would have been seized upon. By announcing *bi'l-ḥaqq*, the verse stakes the revelation's credibility on the accuracy of the account, while simultaneously asserting that the Qur'anic version corrects the distortions the story had accumulated.

The verb *naquṣṣ* — from *q-ṣ-ṣ*, to narrate, to recount in sequence — is the root of *qiṣaṣ* (narratives) and *qaṣaṣ*. The Qur'an uses it of its own storytelling at 12:3: *"We relate unto thee the most beautiful of narratives (aḥsan al-qaṣaṣ)"* — and at 3:62: *"Truly this is the true account."* The choice signals that what follows is *revelation narrating*, not legend repeating.

**They Were Youths: The Age That Answers First**

*Innahum fityatun āmanū bi-rabbihim* — "they were youths who believed in their Lord." Ibn Kathīr's observation about the sociology of belief is one of the most durable in the commentary tradition: the young respond to the prophets; the elders resist. The Qur'an documents the pattern across every prophetic narrative. When Abraham destroyed the idols, the response was:

> *"They said: We heard a youth (fatan) mention them; he is called Abraham."* (Qur'an 21:60)

And in the accounts of Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, and Shuʿayb, it is the *malaʾ* — the notables, the senior figures — who lead the rejection: 7:60, 7:66, 7:75, 7:88, 7:90, 7:103, 11:27, 11:38, 23:24, 23:33, 34:34, 43:23.

The reason is structural rather than moral. The elders of any society hold positions within it — property, office, religious authority, social standing. A call that overturns the society's foundations threatens those positions directly. The young hold little and therefore risk little, and they retain the capacity for outrage at inconsistency that experience tends to anaesthetize. The verse honours this: it names their youth as part of their merit, not as a mitigating circumstance.

**"Believed in Their Lord": The Specific Object of Faith**

The phrase *āmanū bi-rabbihim* — "believed in their Lord" — uses *Rabb*, not *Allāh*. The commentators note the significance: *Rabb* is the Name denoting Lordship, sustenance, nurture, and governance — the God who *raises* and *provides for*. The youths' faith is therefore specified as faith in the One who nurtures them, which is precisely the faith that sustains people in a sealed cave. They did not merely believe God exists; they believed God was *their* Lord, actively engaged in their affairs.

Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabrisī report the circumstance: these were young men who had come to belief independently, and — in one widely transmitted account — met by chance outside the city, whereupon the eldest confessed his belief and discovered that the others shared it (Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī). Their faith preceded their fellowship. This ordering matters: the Qur'an is describing conviction arrived at individually, then confirmed communally — not group conformity producing belief.

**"We Increased Them in Guidance": The Law of Spiritual Increment**

*Wa zidnāhum hudā* — "and We increased them in guidance." This is one of the Qur'an's clearest statements of a principle it returns to constantly: guidance is not a binary state but a growing one, and God increases it in those who have already turned toward Him. The parallel verses are explicit:

> *"And God increases in guidance those who are guided. And the enduring good deeds are better with thy Lord in reward, and better in recourse."* (Qur'an 19:76)

> *"But as for those who are guided, He increases them in guidance and gives them their reverence."* (Qur'an 47:17)

> *"He it is Who sent down tranquillity into the hearts of the believers, that they might increase in faith along with their faith."* (Qur'an 48:4)

The same law operates in the negative direction, and the Qur'an states it with equal clarity:

> *"And as for those in whose hearts is a disease, it increases them in filth upon their filth, and they die while they are disbelievers."* (Qur'an 9:125)

The principle is that divine action responds to human orientation. The one who turns toward God is given more of what he turned toward; the one who turns away is left to the consequences of his turning. This is not arbitrary favouritism but the operation of a moral universe in which direction compounds. Al-Rāzī formulates it as: the initial act of faith is the servant's; the increase is God's, and the increase is proportionate to the sincerity of the initial act.

The practical consequence is the most encouraging thing in Islamic spirituality: nobody has to arrive at certainty in one step. The youths believed, and were *then* given more. The person who takes the first honest step finds the second step made available — which is the meaning of the ḥadīth qudsī preserved in al-Bukhārī (7405) and Muslim (2675):

> *"I am as My servant thinks I am... If he draws near to Me a handspan, I draw near to him an arm's length; if he draws near to Me an arm's length, I draw near to him a fathom; and if he comes to Me walking, I come to him running."*

**What the Increase Consisted In**

The commentators specify what "increased in guidance" meant for these particular youths. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Suyūṭī mention: an increase of faith itself; greater understanding and discernment of the truth; and — al-Ṭabarī's addition — the patience to endure separation from their people. The third is the most telling. Guidance in the Qur'an is not only intellectual illumination but the capacity to *act* on what one knows, and the hardest action is often the one that costs you your community.

The verse is therefore a complete account of how the youths came to be in that cave: they were young, they believed individually, God increased their guidance, and the increase included the patience that made the flight possible. Verse 14 will describe the next increment — the fortification of their hearts — and the public declaration that followed it.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:14

> **And We fortified their hearts. When they arose, they said, "Our Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth. We shall call upon no god apart from Him, for then we would have certainly uttered an outrage.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Rabaṭnā ʿalā Qulūbihim: The Verb of Binding**

The verb *rabaṭa* means to bind, to tie fast, to secure — the same root that produces *ribāṭ*, the term for a frontier garrison, and *murābaṭah*, the Qur'anic command at 3:200 to "be steadfast" (*wa rābiṭū*). Al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Zamakhsharī read *rabaṭnā ʿalā qulūbihim* — "We bound their hearts" — as God's strengthening of them in patience and faith; al-Qushayrī adds *yaqīn*, certitude.

The image is precise and worth dwelling on. A heart is not fortified by being enlarged but by being *tied down*. The word pictures a thing that would otherwise move — a tent flapping in wind, a boat swinging at anchor — made fast. The commentators' reading implies that the youths' hearts were, in fact, liable to move: they were frightened young men facing an emperor, and the natural movement of the heart in such circumstances is panic, vacillation, and capitulation. God's gift was not the absence of fear but the binding of the heart so that fear could not carry it away.

This is the Qur'an's consistent account of courage. It is never the absence of fear; it is the fixing of the heart despite fear. The same verb appears in the story of Moses's mother:

> *"And the heart of the mother of Moses became empty. She was indeed on the point of disclosing him had We not fortified her heart (rabaṭnā ʿalā qalbihā), that she might be among the believers."* (Qur'an 28:10)

And at 8:11, of the believers at Badr: *"that He might fortify (li-yarbiṭa) the hearts thereby."* In every instance the binding is divine and the recipient's own capacity is insufficient.

**"When They Arose": The Moment of Public Declaration**

*Idh qāmū fa-qālū* — "when they arose and said." The commentators differ on what the arising was. Al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Zamakhsharī read it as the youths' open defiance of the tyrant Daqyānūs — they stood before him and refused his demand. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī transmit the alternative: they had each secretly held belief, met by chance outside the city, and the eldest declared his faith; finding agreement, they uttered this proclamation together, either there or upon returning to the king's court.

Both readings share a structure: an interior conviction becoming a public statement at a moment of maximum cost. The verb *qāma* — to stand, to rise — is the Qur'an's standard word for the transition from private belief to public testimony. This is why *qiyām* is the standing posture of prayer and why the word carries the sense of undertaking a duty: to stand is to commit.

**"Our Lord Is the Lord of the Heavens and the Earth"**

*Rabbunā rabbu al-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* — the declaration is a single equation that demolishes the tyrant's claim. Al-Ṭabarī draws out the logic: the youths assert that their Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth, which means that the false deities Daqyānūs commands them to worship are themselves *subjects* of the one Lord. An idol is a creature; the emperor who demands worship of it is a creature; both are inside the domain the declaration names.

This is the Qur'an's characteristic argumentative move, and it is worth naming because it is so economical. Rather than arguing against each particular idol, the Qur'an expands the frame until the idol is visibly contained within it. Once "the Lord of the heavens and the earth" is established, every rival is automatically demoted to a thing within the system. The argument does not need to examine the rival; it only needs to establish the frame. Abraham uses the identical move with the star, the moon, and the sun at 6:76–78: he does not disprove them individually, he observes that each *sets*, and anything that sets is inside the order rather than above it.

**"We Shall Call Upon No God Apart From Him"**

*Lan nadʿuwa min dūnihi ilāhan* — the resolve is stated in the strongest available form: *lan*, the particle of emphatic future negation, meaning "never, under no circumstances." The youths are not saying they currently do not worship other gods; they are foreclosing the possibility permanently.

The commentators note that the verse places the declaration in the first person plural and in the future tense, which makes it a *covenant* rather than a description. They are binding themselves. And they immediately state the consequence of breach: *la-qad qulnā idhan shataṭā* — "then we would indeed have uttered an outrage." The word *shataṭ* denotes excess, extravagance, a statement that goes far beyond the bounds — from the same root as *shāṭṭ*, distant, remote. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī read it as a gross falsehood, an enormity in speech.

There is a moral insight here that deserves attention. The youths characterise idolatry not primarily as a *mistake* but as an *outrage* — a transgression against what is owed to God. Modern religious language tends to frame unbelief as an intellectual error, something one is simply wrong about. The Qur'an frames it as an injustice, because worship is a debt owed to the One who created and sustains, and diverting it elsewhere is not a miscalculation but a misappropriation. The same logic underlies the Qur'anic term for idolatry, *shirk*, which it calls *ẓulmun ʿaẓīm* — "a tremendous wrong" (31:13).

**Why the Fortification Precedes the Speech**

The verse's order is instructive: first the binding of the hearts, then the standing, then the words. The commentators do not comment on the sequence explicitly, but its logic is evident and it corresponds to the Qur'an's whole account of speech. Words spoken from an unbound heart are unreliable — they will be retracted under pressure. The Qur'an is repeatedly severe about the discrepancy between tongue and heart:

> *"They say with their mouths what is not in their hearts."* (Qur'an 3:167)

The youths' declaration carries weight because God bound their hearts first. This is why Islamic spiritual teaching has always treated the interior condition as prior to the exterior statement, and why the tradition holds that the testimony of faith is only fully real when the heart has been made fast. The verse gives the sequence in a single sentence: *We fortified their hearts; when they arose, they said.*

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:15

> **These, our people, have taken gods apart from Him. Why do they not bring a clear authority concerning them? For who does greater wrong than one who fabricates a lie against God?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand for Evidence, Spoken by Teenagers**

The youths' declaration in verse 14 established *who* God is. Verse 15 turns to *their people*, and does something remarkable: it demands documentation. *Lawlā yaʾtūna ʿalayhim bi-sulṭānin bayyin* — "Why do they not produce a clear authority concerning them?" The word *sulṭān* is from *s-l-ṭ*, power, authority, dominion — and in Qur'anic usage it consistently means a demonstrable warrant, a proof that compels. The adjective *bayyin* means manifest, evident, self-disclosing. Together: a proof that speaks for itself.

Al-Rāzī, al-Suyūṭī, and al-Zamakhsharī all note the jurisprudential significance: this verse is cited in support of the principle that religious belief must rest on intellectual demonstration and not on *taqlīd* — the blind imitation of predecessors. The Qur'an is here not merely permitting inquiry; it is *demanding* it, and it puts the demand in the mouths of young men addressing an empire.

The logic is worth isolating because it is a complete epistemology in one sentence. A claim that some beings are worthy of worship is a claim about reality. Claims about reality require warrants. The youths do not argue that the idols are powerless, or ugly, or traditional — they ask for the warrant, and note that none has ever been produced. A claim with no warrant is not refuted by counter-argument; it is dissolved by the absence of support.

**The Connection to Verse 5**

Readers of the sūrah's opening will recognise the pattern. Verse 5 said of those who claim God has a child: *"They have no knowledge thereof, nor do their fathers."* Verse 15 asks the same question of the idolaters: where is the proof? The chapter is systematically applying one epistemological standard across all its subjects — theological claims, ancestral traditions, and the number of sleepers in a cave (v. 22).

This consistency is what makes the Qur'an's critique of idolatry different from mere sectarian polemic. It is not "our gods are real and yours are not"; it is "no one on either side of this dispute should assert what he cannot warrant." The demand applies to the Muslims too, which is why the Qur'an says at 2:111:

> *"Say: Bring your proof, if you are truthful."* (Qur'an 2:111)

and at 27:64: *"Say: Is there any of your partners who originates creation, then brings it back again? Say: Bring your proof, if you are truthful."*

**"Who Does Greater Wrong Than One Who Fabricates a Lie Against God?"**

*Fa-man aẓlamu mimmani iftarā ʿalā Allāhi kadhiban* — the interrogative of superlative: nobody is more unjust. The Qur'an repeats this formula at 6:21, 7:37, 10:17, 11:18, 29:68, 61:7, always in connection with fabricating against God. The verb *iftarā* — from *f-r-y*, to cut, to split — denotes fabrication, the manufacture of a falsehood, and it is stronger than merely speaking untruly: it implies invention.

Why is this the *greatest* injustice? Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr reason it out. Injustice (*ẓulm*) is the placing of a thing where it does not belong. All lesser injustices harm a creature: theft harms the owner, slander harms the reputation, oppression harms the subject. Fabricating against God corrupts the very framework by which justice is defined — because if God has said something He did not say, then the moral map is false, and every subsequent action guided by it goes wrong. It is the poisoning of the well from which all other judgments are drawn.

There is a further dimension the commentators draw out: to fabricate against God is to make other human beings accountable to a rule God never gave, which is to usurp divine authority over other people's consciences. This is why the Qur'an reserves its harshest language not for the sinner but for the religious innovator — the one who legislates in God's name.

**The Idolaters' Actual Position**

The verse's rhetorical force depends on a fact the Qur'an states elsewhere: the Makkan idolaters did not claim to have a warrant. They claimed ancestry. When asked for justification, their standard reply was:

> *"And when it is said unto them, 'Follow that which God has sent down,' they say, 'Nay, we follow that which we found our fathers upon.'"* (Qur'an 2:170)

The youths' question therefore has no possible answer, and everyone in the audience knew it. This is why the verse does not proceed to refute idolatry: the demand for a *sulṭān bayyin* is itself the refutation. A question that cannot be answered in principle is more decisive than any argument that could be answered.

**The Practical Reach of the Demand**

The principle extends well beyond idolatry, and the commentators' invocation of *taqlīd* shows they understood this. Every religious community contains claims sustained only by repetition — about who is saved, what God requires, which practices are obligatory, which groups are condemned. The verse supplies a test that any believer can apply to his own inherited convictions: *what is the warrant?* Not "who said it," not "how long it has been said," not "how many say it" — but what demonstrable authority stands behind it.

This is not a licence for every individual to reconstruct religion from scratch; the Islamic tradition developed sophisticated structures of transmitted evidence precisely to make warrants available. But it does establish that the *ideal* of the tradition is warranted belief, and that a claim which cannot name its warrant has, in the Qur'an's own framing, the weakest possible standing.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:16

> **And when you have withdrawn from them and all that they worship save God, then take refuge in the cave. Your Lord will spread forth something of His Mercy for you, and make you incline to ease in your affair."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Speaker the Qur'an Does Not Name**

The verse shifts into direct speech without identifying the speaker, and the commentators supply the identification from transmitted reports: al-Suyūṭī and al-Ṭabrisī name him Tamlīkhā (or Yamlīkhā — in the Christian account, Iamblichus), said to be the leader of the youths. The Qur'an's silence about his name is characteristic of its narrative method throughout this chapter: it withholds names, cities, kings, and dates, and supplies only what serves the moral purpose. The commentators have transmitted the names; the revelation has not fixed them.

The effect of the anonymity is important. What matters is not who spoke but what was said, and the speech itself is the theological core of the whole cave narrative: an instruction to withdraw, and a promise that God's mercy will follow the withdrawal.

**"When You Have Withdrawn From Them": The Condition of Separation**

*Wa idh iʿtazaltumūhum wa mā yaʿbudūna illā Allāh* — "and when you have withdrawn from them and from what they worship, except God." The verb *iʿtazala* — from *ʿ-z-l*, to separate, to set apart, to withdraw — is the root of *iʿtizāl*, withdrawal, and gave its name to the *Muʿtazilah*, the theological school that took separation as its defining posture.

The verse establishes a sequence, and the sequence is theologically significant: separation *precedes* shelter. The youths are not told to hide in the cave and there maintain a private faith while continuing to participate in public idolatry. They are told to withdraw from the people *and from what they worship*, and only then to enter the cave. The commentators read the two withdrawals as inseparable: physical distance from a community that is meaningless without doctrinal distance from its worship.

This is the Qur'an's consistent teaching about environments. A person's belief is sustained or eroded by the company he keeps, and the Qur'an takes this seriously enough to command physical separation when the environment becomes religiously lethal:

> *"And when thou seest those who engage in vain discourse about Our signs, turn away from them until they engage in other discourse. And if Satan causes thee to forget, then after remembering, sit not with the wrongdoing people."* (Qur'an 6:68)

**"Except God": The Exception the Commentators Explain**

The phrase *illā Allāh* has been read in two ways. The majority read it as attached to the object of worship: withdraw from them and from whatever they worship — except God, Whom you continue to worship. Others read it as an exception to the withdrawal itself: withdraw from them except in matters where God's right is concerned, i.e. do not let withdrawal become a withdrawal from every obligation. Both readings converge on the practical point: the separation is from *shirk*, not from humanity as such.

**"Your Lord Will Spread Forth Mercy From His Mercy"**

*Ya bsuṭ lakum rabbukum min raḥmatihi* — literally, "your Lord will spread out for you from His mercy." The verb *basṭ* denotes spreading, extending, opening out — the same verb used at 2:245 of God's expansion of provision (*yabasuṭu*). The commentators note the preposition *min* — "from" His mercy — indicating that what the youths will receive is a portion drawn from an inexhaustible store, not the store itself. This is the same *min ladunhu* language as verse 10.

Al-Suyūṭī connects the promise directly to the youths' prayer in verse 10: they asked for mercy from God's Presence, and here their leader announces that the mercy will come. The verse is, structurally, the first instalment of the answer — spoken by a young man who had no way of knowing it, which is itself the reason the commentators treat the speech as inspired.

**"And Make For You Your Affair Easy"**

*Wa yuhayyiʾ lakum min amrikum mirfaqā* — "and prepare for you, from your affair, a means of ease." The noun *mirfaq* is from *r-f-q*, gentleness, kindness, ease — the same root as *rafīq* (companion) and *rifq* (gentleness), and the source of the word for elbow, the joint on which one rests. A *mirfaq* is thus something one leans on, a support that makes a burden bearable.

Compare the youths' own prayer in verse 10: *wa hayyiʾ lanā min amrinā rashadā* — "prepare for us, from our affair, right guidance." The same verb *hayyaʾa* returns, and the same construction *min amrinā*. The leader's promise answers their prayer in its own grammatical terms: they asked for *rashad*, soundness of course; he promises *mirfaq*, ease of circumstance. Al-Suyūṭī and al-Ṭabrisī both mark this correspondence explicitly.

The pairing is theologically exact. The believer asks for guidance — that his affair be *right*. God answers with ease — that his affair be *bearable*. Not necessarily the outcome he requested, but a support within the outcome he receives. The cave itself turns out to be the *mirfaq*: three centuries of sheltered sleep, sunlight deflected, bodies preserved.

**The Cave and the Hijrah: The Parallel the Commentators Draw**

Ibn Kathīr draws the connection that a Makkan audience would have made immediately: this is the second cave in the Qur'an. The first is at 9:40 —

> *"If you help him not — God helped him when those who disbelieved drove him out, the second of two, when they were in the cave, and he said to his companion, 'Grieve not; truly God is with us.'"* (Qur'an 9:40)

In both accounts a small group takes refuge in a cave from pursuers, and in both God's protection is complete and unobserved. Ibn Kathīr notes the resonance, and the Sufi commentators (al-Qushayrī, and the tradition al-Qurṭubī transmits) add the interior reading: the cave is the heart, and the withdrawal is the retreat into God that every believer must make when the world becomes a place where faith cannot be lived openly.

For the first Muslims hearing this chapter in Makkah, with the Hijrah approaching, the parallel was not literary. It was an announcement. The chapter that told them how God had preserved seven young men in a cave was preparing them for the night their Prophet ﷺ would be preserved in one.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:17

> **Thou wouldst have seen the sun when it rose, slanting away from their cave to the right; and when it set, turning away from them to the left, while they were in an open space within it. That is among the signs of God. Whomsoever God guides, he is rightly guided; and whomsoever He leads astray, thou wilt find no protector to lead him aright.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Astronomy of Providence**

The verse describes a physical arrangement with extraordinary precision: at sunrise the light inclines away from the cave to the right; at sunset it withdraws to the left; the sleepers lie in a *fajwah* — an open space — within it. Al-Ṭabarī notes that although the two phrases differ, the motions are parallel, describing a cave oriented so that direct sun never enters its interior. Ibn Kathīr, al-Jalālayn, and al-Zamakhsharī explain the *fajwah* as a spacious cavity with a broad opening, allowing air and cool breeze to reach the sleepers while excluding direct heat.

The commentators' point is that the preservation was not a suspension of nature but a *disposition* of nature. The sun continued to rise and set on its ordinary course; the cave's orientation did the work. This is characteristic of the Qur'an's account of providence, which consistently prefers the arrangement of ordinary causes to the interruption of them. God did not extinguish the sun; He placed the youths where the sun would not touch them.

**Shade as a Divine Blessing**

The commentators observe that shade is one of the Qur'an's recurring images of divine care. In this world:

> *"And We caused the cloud to shade you, and sent down upon you manna and quails: 'Eat of the good things We have provided you.'"* (Qur'an 2:57)

> *"Hast thou not seen thy Lord, how He stretches out the shadow? Had He willed, He could have made it still. Then We made the sun its guide."* (Qur'an 25:45)

And in the Hereafter, shade is among the first blessings promised: 4:57, 13:35, 36:56 — and 16:81, where shade and refuges in the mountains are named together among God's favours, a verse that reads almost as a caption to this story.

The theological weight of shade is easily missed by readers in temperate climates. In the Arabian desert, shade is not comfort but survival; the difference between sun and shade is the difference between life and death within hours. To say that God kept the sun off the youths is to say that God kept them alive, by the most basic means available.

**"That Is Among the Signs of God"**

*dhālika min āyāti Allāh* — the verse pauses to label what it has just described. The commentators note the significance: the arrangement of sunlight in a cave is called a *sign*, the same word used for the Qur'anic verses themselves (*āyāt*) and for the great phenomena of creation. This is the chapter's epistemological claim in miniature: providence is legible. The person who looks at how things are arranged can read God's care in the arrangement.

Al-Qushayrī's comment, transmitted by al-Qurṭubī, adds the Sufi layer: the sleepers were preserved from the sun's heat as the *awliyāʾ* are preserved from the burning of worldly attachment. The commentators who use this story as evidence for *karāmāt* (see v. 12) take this verse as further support.

**The Abrupt Turn to Guidance and Misguidance**

The verse then shifts without transition: *man yahdi Allāhu fa-huwa al-muhtadī, wa man yuḍlil fa-lan tajida lahu waliyyan murshidā* — "Whomsoever God guides, he is the rightly guided; and whomsoever He leads astray, you will find for him no protecting guide." Al-Ṭabarī takes the address as directed to the Prophet ﷺ; others, including al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī, read it as addressed to an unspecified hearer.

The juxtaposition is deliberate and its logic is clear. The story just described is an instance of God's guidance reaching a handful of young men against an empire, and of the empire being left in its error. The verse generalises: guidance is God's, and where God does not guide, no human advocate can supply the deficiency. The word *walī* denotes a protector, an ally, one who takes charge of another's affairs; *murshid*, one who directs to the right path. The verse denies that either function can be performed for a person God has left astray.

The classical commentators are careful here, and their care is essential to reading the verse correctly. The Qur'an's statements that God leads astray are consistently read in light of the Qur'an's equally consistent statements that misguidance follows a human choice:

> *"But God does not guide the wrongdoing people."* (Qur'an 5:51)

> *"And when they turned away, God turned their hearts away. And God guides not the transgressing people."* (Qur'an 61:5)

The standard Sunni position, articulated by al-Ṭabarī and by Ibn Taymiyyah, is that God's leading astray is a *withdrawal* of guidance from one who has already refused it — a judicial act, not an arbitrary one. The parallel passage for interpretation is 7:178 and 17:97, to which the source commentary refers the reader. The person "led astray" is not an innocent victim of divine decree; he is a person whose repeated refusals God has ratified.

**Why This Comes at the End of the Sleepers' Preservation**

The placement of the general statement here, rather than at the beginning, is instructive. The reader has just watched God preserve seven believers through three centuries by deflecting sunlight. The verse then says: this is what guidance looks like when God gives it, and there is no substitute when He withholds it. The lesson is not about caves. It is about the fact that the entire apparatus of the empire — its soldiers, its sealed doors, its determination — could not reach seven sleeping young men, while the empire itself remained, awake and powerful, in the dark.

The practical application is the one the Prophet ﷺ was being taught in verse 6: you cannot guide anyone. Grieve for them, warn them, deliver the message — but the outcome belongs to God, and no amount of your distress will supply what He has withheld.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:18

> **Thou wouldst have thought them awake, though they were asleep. And We turned them to the right and to the left, with their dog stretching forth his paws at the threshold. Hadst thou come upon them, thou wouldst have turned away in flight and been filled with terror on their account.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Asleep and Seeming Awake: The Description of the Barzakh**

*Wa taḥsabuhum ayqāẓan wa hum ruqūd* — "you would reckon them awake, while they were sleeping." Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī offer two explanations: they slept with their eyes open, or their periodic turning gave the appearance of wakefulness — a turning some reports place once or twice a year. Either way the verse describes a state between the two ordinary conditions, and the commentators treat this as theologically significant.

The reason is that sleep is the Qur'an's own image for death. At 6:60 God *"takes your souls by night"* and returns them by day; at 39:42 He *"takes the souls at the time of their death, and those that die not during their sleep."* The well-known saying transmitted from ʿAlī and attributed by some to the Prophet ﷺ — *"People are asleep; when they die they wake"* — rests on this identification. The sleepers are therefore in a state that is simultaneously sleep and a figure of death: alive, but withdrawn; intact, but absent.

This is the *barzakh* — the intermediate realm — and the commentators connect it explicitly. The cave narrative is, on this reading, not only a proof of resurrection but a dramatization of the grave: bodies preserved, souls held, time experienced as nothing, waking deferred until a term appointed. When the youths wake at verse 19 and ask *"how long have you tarried?"*, and answer *"a day or part of a day"*, they enact exactly what the Qur'an says the resurrected will say at 10:45, 20:104, 23:112–13, 30:55, 79:46.

**"We Turned Them to the Right and to the Left"**

*Wa nuqallibuhum dhāta al-yamīni wa dhāta al-shimāl* — the verb *qallaba* is intensive and frequentative: not merely turning but turning repeatedly. Al-Ṭabarī gives the practical reason: to prevent the earth from consuming them, i.e. to prevent the pressure sores and tissue decay that would come from centuries of immobility. The verse is describing, in the language of revelation, what any nurse knows: a body that is not turned develops bedsores.

The detail is one of the Qur'an's characteristic touches of realism. A less careful account would have described a miraculous suspension of all physical process. This one describes a *mechanism* — periodic repositioning — which is exactly how a body would in fact have to be managed. The commentators do not labour the point, but it is there: the miracle operates through a care regimen.

Al-Qushayrī's Sufi reading, recorded in the tradition, sees the turning as the alternation between *fanāʾ* (annihilation in God) and *baqāʾ* (subsistence in God) — the two states between which the spiritual traveller moves. The reading is homiletic rather than exegetical, but it captures something true about the verse: the sleepers are not inert; they are being *tended*.

**The Dog at the Threshold**

*Wa kalbuhum bāsiṭun dhiraʿayhi bi'l-waṣīd* — "and their dog stretching out his forepaws at the threshold." The image is one of the most vivid in the Qur'an, and the commentators have much to say about it.

The transmitted reports, gathered by al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and al-Ṭabrisī, offer several accounts: the dog was a hunting animal that served them; the youths encountered it while fleeing and tried to drive it away for fear it would betray their hiding place, whereupon — in one remarkable report — it was given speech and said, *"I love the lovers of God; sleep, and I will keep watch"*; or it was a shepherd's dog, and the shepherd himself followed the youths (al-Rāzī). A minority reading suggests that *kalb* here was not a dog at all but the name of a companion — their cook (al-Ṭabarī) or their dogkeeper, on the reading *kālib* rather than *kalb* (al-Zamakhsharī) — in which case *dhiraʿayh* would be "his arms" rather than "his paws."

*Waṣīd* is itself disputed: al-Ṭabarī notes it can mean the door or entrance of the cave, or the earth or dust before it.

What the verse does *not* do is explain the dog's presence. It simply places him there, forepaws stretched, at the entrance — the posture of a guard who has settled in for a long watch. The commentators who take the story as evidence for the honour of the righteous note the implication: even an animal that kept company with the lovers of God was preserved with them and memorialised in the Qur'an. There is a homiletic point here that the tradition has drawn consistently: association with the righteous benefits even the irrational.

The dog's presence also serves a narrative function the commentators observe: it confirms the *reality* of the sleep. A dog stretched at a threshold is an image of ordinary domestic life, and its inclusion in a miraculous scene anchors the miracle in the physical world. The Qur'an's narratives consistently work this way — the miracle is surrounded by concrete, verifiable detail.

**"Thou Wouldst Have Fled in Terror"**

*Law iṭṭalaʿta ʿalayhim la-wallayta minhum firāran wa la-muliʾta minhum ruʿbā* — "had you looked upon them, you would surely have turned away in flight, and been filled with terror of them." Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī explain that God had clothed them in a fearsome aspect so that no one would touch or disturb them. The protection was not only physical but psychological: the appearance itself repelled intruders.

The verse addresses the Prophet ﷺ directly — *"hadst thou come upon them"* — which the commentators read as a rhetorical device establishing the reader as an imagined witness. The reader is placed at the cave mouth, looking in, and told what he would feel. This is unusual in Qur'anic narrative, which generally reports rather than immerses.

The terror is not arbitrary. The commentators note that awe (*haybah*) is the appropriate response to what has been removed from the ordinary order. The youths have been held outside time for three centuries; they are, in a real sense, not of this world, and the instinctive reaction to them is the instinctive reaction to anything that has crossed a boundary. The same response is described for the angels' appearance to the prophets, and for Moses at the burning bush.

**The Verse as a Whole: Ordinary Detail Around a Miracle**

Taken together, the verse is a remarkable composition. It contains: an ambiguous state of consciousness, a care routine, a dog, a threshold, and an emotional response. Every element is concrete and domestic. There is no thunder, no light, no voice. The miracle is described entirely through the physical arrangements that sustain it.

This is the Qur'an's method throughout, and it is why its narratives remain credible across fourteen centuries: the miraculous is embedded in the ordinary, and the reader is given enough physical detail to picture the scene. A legend accumulates wonders; a testimony accumulates details. This verse has details.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:19

> **And in this way We raised them up again, that they might question one another. One of them said, "How long have you tarried?" They said, "We have tarried a day or part of a day." They said, "Your Lord knows best how long you have tarried. So send one of you with this money of yours into the city, and let him observe which of them has the purest food and bring you some provision therefrom. Let him be discreet and make no one aware of you.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"That They Might Question One Another": The Purpose of the Waking**

*Wa kadhālika baʿathnāhum li-yatasāʾalū baynahum* — "and thus We raised them, that they might question one another." The stated purpose of the resurrection-miracle is *conversation*. Al-Rāzī and al-Suyūṭī note the significance: the youths are woken not primarily to be rescued, or to be shown to the world, but to *talk among themselves* — to establish, by their own mutual testimony, how long they had been there.

This is important because it establishes the evidential structure of the story. The fact of the long sleep is not asserted by a narrator alone; it is *discovered by the participants*, who compare notes and find themselves unable to account for the elapsed time. Verse 12 said God raised them *"that We might know which of the two parties had best reckoned the extent of their tarrying"*; verse 19 now shows the reckoning in progress. The story's proof of resurrection is built out of the sleepers' own confusion.

**"A Day or Part of a Day": The Epistemology of the Newly Risen**

*Qāla qāʾilun minhum kam labithtum, qālū labithnā yawman aw baʿḍa yawm* — "one of them said, how long have you tarried? They said, we have tarried a day or part of a day." Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Zamakhsharī explain the estimate: they had entered the cave in the morning and woke with the sun low, so the natural inference was that a day had passed. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī add the detail that only upon noticing the length of their hair and nails did they revise the estimate — and revise it not into a number but into a surrender.

The parallel is exact and deliberate. At 2:259 the Qur'an tells of a man who passed by a ruined town and asked how God could bring it back to life after its death; God caused him to die for a hundred years and then raised him, and asked him how long he had tarried. He said, *"A day or part of a day."* God said, *"Nay, thou hast tarried a hundred years."* The same estimate, in the same circumstances, from a different person, in a different chapter.

The Qur'an's point is a general law of consciousness: the dead, and the sleeping, have no access to elapsed duration. Which is why the resurrected will make the same estimate on the Last Day — 10:45, *"as though they had tarried but an hour of a day"*; 20:104, *"you tarried but ten"*; 23:112–13, *"we tarried a day or part of a day"*; 30:55; 46:35; 79:46. The youths' confusion is not a narrative detail; it is a rehearsal of the resurrection.

**"Your Lord Knows Best How Long You Have Tarried"**

*Qālū rabbukum aʿlamu bi-mā labithtum* — the third statement in the exchange, and the one verse 12 identifies as the *best reckoning*. The commentators — al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī — note the epistemological elegance: faced with an unknowable quantity, the most accurate response is to identify who knows it. This is not a surrender of inquiry; it is its correct completion.

The principle generalises. There is a class of questions to which the honest answer is not a guess but an attribution: *God knows best*. The Qur'an uses the formula *Allāhu aʿlam* repeatedly in exactly this function, and verse 22 will use it again about the number of sleepers, and verse 26 about the length of their stay. The chapter is teaching a discipline of intellectual humility that is not the opposite of knowledge but its boundary-mark.

**The Errand: Coin, City, and the Purest Food**

*Fa-bʿathū aḥadakum bi-wariqikum hādhihi ilā al-madīnah fa-l-yanẓur ayyuhā azkā ṭaʿāman fa-l-yaʾtikum bi-rizqin minhu* — "so send one of you with this coin of yours to the city, and let him see which of them has the purest food, and let him bring you provision from it."

*Wariq* is silver coinage — the commentators specify a silver *dirham* bearing the stamp of the oppressive king of their own time (al-Ṭabarī). The detail is narratively decisive: the coin is what gives him away. When he presents currency three centuries out of date, the townspeople recognise immediately that something extraordinary has occurred. Al-Ṭabarī's account continues that they informed him that centuries had passed and that they were now ruled by a righteous king, Tīdhusīs — identified with Theodosius — during whose reign people had been disputing whether the resurrection is bodily or spiritual only. The king, who believed in bodily resurrection and had prayed for a sign, took the youths' story as the answer.

*Azkā ṭaʿāman* — "the purest food" — carries a double sense that the commentators draw out: food that is *ṭayyib* in itself, and food that is lawful in its acquisition, i.e. not from an idolatrous sacrifice. Al-Jalālayn and al-Zamakhsharī read it as referring to food that is both wholesome and ritually clean. Even in extremity, after centuries of miraculous preservation, the youths' concern is that their provision be lawful. The commentators have noted the instruction: necessity does not automatically dissolve religious scruple about what one consumes.

**"Let Him Be Discreet, and Let Him Not Make Anyone Aware of You"**

*Wa l-yatalaṭṭaf wa lā yushʿiranna bikum aḥadā* — "and let him be gentle/discreet, and let him by no means make anyone aware of you." The verb *talaṭṭafa* means to act with subtlety, tact, careful gentleness — to move without attracting notice. The prohibition *lā yushʿiranna* is in the emphatic energetic form: "let him absolutely not cause anyone to perceive you."

There is an irony the commentators do not miss: the plan fails. The coin betrays him, and the whole city learns. But the instruction was correct, and its correctness is not undone by its failure. The verse establishes a principle of conduct — take precautions, act discreetly, do not expose yourselves — while the narrative demonstrates that precautions do not control outcomes. Verse 21 will state that God *caused* them to be discovered, for a purpose the youths could not have anticipated.

The pairing is the Qur'an's standard account of human agency: do everything required of you, and know that the result is God's. The precaution is not a lack of trust; the failure of the precaution is not a defeat.

**What the Errand Reveals About the Sleepers**

The whole exchange is psychologically exact. Seven men wake from three centuries of supernatural sleep, and what do they do? They ask the time, admit they do not know it, refer the matter to God, and send someone to buy lunch. There is no ecstasy, no vision, no theological reflection. They are hungry.

The commentators take this as evidence of the story's realism, and the tradition has drawn a further lesson: the righteous are not exempt from ordinary needs, and holiness does not consist in rising above the body. The youths' first act on waking is a practical errand, conducted with discretion and religious care about the food's lawfulness. In a chapter full of miracles, this is perhaps the most instructive detail.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:20

> **Verily, if they come to know of you, they will stone you, or make you revert to their creed, and then you will never prosper."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Realistic Assessment of Risk**

The speaker — one of the youths, continuing the conversation — states the danger in two options: *innahum in yaẓharū ʿalaykum yarjumūkum aw yuʿīdūkum fī millatihim wa lan tufliḥū idhan abadā* — "if they overcome you / come to know of you, they will stone you, or return you to their creed, and then you will never prosper, ever."

The commentators note the verb *yaẓharū ʿalaykum*, which carries both senses — "if they gain the upper hand over you" and "if they discover you." Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī read it as discovery followed by overpowering. The speaker is not speculating about abstract persecution; he is describing what this particular regime does to people like them.

**The Two Fates, and Their Order**

The sequence of the two threats is significant, and the commentators have drawn attention to it. The speaker names death first — stoning — and forced apostasy second. The ordering reveals the underlying hierarchy of value: death is the lesser evil. The second outcome is worse, because it ends in *falāḥ* forfeited forever: *wa lan tufliḥū idhan abadā* — "you will never prosper, ever."

*Falāḥ* is one of the Qur'an's key terms — success, thriving, attainment of what one seeks, salvation. It is the word that opens the description of the believers at 23:1: *"Successful indeed are the believers"* — and it is the word the muezzin calls across every Muslim city five times daily in *ḥayya ʿalā al-falāḥ*, "come to success." To lose *falāḥ* is to lose everything the religious life is for.

The verse therefore states a ranking that the Qur'an maintains throughout: physical survival is not the highest good, and there are conditions under which death is preferable. The same ranking appears in the Qur'an's account of the believers who fight *"in the cause of God"* and in the numerous verses preferring death to apostasy. This is not a glorification of death; it is a statement that the value of a life is determined by what it is lived for, and that a life purchased at the price of its purpose is not a purchase but a forfeiture.

**Why Forced Apostasy Is Worse Than Execution**

The logic deserves articulation because it is often misunderstood. Stoning ends a life that has already been lived in faith; the person dies a believer, and in the Qur'anic scheme this is the best of endings. Forced return to *millah* — creed, religious community — corrupts the person before ending him, and if he dies in that state, the loss is permanent.

There is a further consideration the commentators note: apostasy under coercion is not, in Islamic law, actual apostasy. The Qur'an explicitly permits dissimulation under compulsion:

> *"Whoso disbelieves in God after his belief — save him who is forced while his heart is at rest in faith — but whoso opens his breast to disbelief, upon them is wrath from God, and theirs shall be a tremendous punishment."* (Qur'an 16:106)

The verse's speaker is not, therefore, describing a legal trap; he is describing a *moral* danger — that prolonged exposure to the idolatrous community will erode what the heart once held firm. This is the Qur'an's recurring concern about environments, which is why the youths withdrew in the first place (v. 16). The speaker's fear is not of a moment of weakness under torture but of a slow return.

**The Verse as the Reason for the Cave**

Read structurally, verse 20 explains why the youths are in the cave at all, and why they cannot simply go home. The cave was not a retreat for contemplation; it was the only available option between two unacceptable outcomes. The chapter thereby legitimises a category of action that religious communities often struggle to justify: withdrawal from a society that has become religiously lethal.

The parallel to the first Muslims is immediate and the commentators make it. The Meccan believers faced precisely these two options — death or forced reversion to the creed of Quraysh — and the Qur'an's answer was the cave, and later the Hijrah. The story of the sleepers was, for them, a precedent: God has preserved people who made this choice before.

**"Never Prosper" — The Absolute Term**

The final phrase uses *abadan* — "forever" — the same word that verse 3 applied to the believers' reward: *mākithīna fīhi abadā*. The chapter thus sets two eternities against each other within seventeen verses. The reward of the faithful is *abadā*; the loss of the apostate is *abadā*. There is no middle term, and the verse's force depends on this. The speaker is not exaggerating; he is using the chapter's own eschatological vocabulary to describe what is actually at stake.

This is why the youths' decision, however strange it appears from a comfortable distance, is presented as rational. They are weighing a finite loss against an infinite one. The chapter asks the reader to do the same calculation about his own compromises — which are usually far cheaper than stoning, and far less visible, and for that very reason far more likely to be made.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:21

> **And in this way We caused them to be discovered, that they might know that God's Promise is true, and that there is no doubt about the Hour. When they were disputing among themselves about their affair, they said, "Erect a building over them; their Lord knows them best." Those who prevailed over their affair said, "We shall build a place of worship over them."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**God Caused the Discovery: The Purpose Clause**

*Kādhālika aʿtharnā ʿalayhim li-yaʿlamū anna waʿda Allāhi ḥaqqun wa anna al-sāʿata lā rayba fīhā* — "and thus We caused [others] to gain knowledge of them, that they might know that God's Promise is true and that there is no doubt about the Hour." The verb *aʿthara* means to cause to be known, to reveal, to bring to light. The subject is God.

The point is decisive for reading the narrative. The youths' plan in verse 19 was discretion — *"let him be discreet, and let him not make anyone aware of you."* The plan failed, and the verse tells us why: God intended the discovery. Al-Ṭabarī explains the mechanism through the transmitted account: a shepherd seeking shelter for his flock from the rain cleared the entrance of the cave, and the youths woke.

The theological principle is the one the chapter has been building: human precaution is obligatory and human outcomes are God's. The youths did the right thing by hiding; God had another purpose for their being found. Neither the precaution nor its failure was pointless.

**The Resurrection Argument Made Visible**

The stated purpose of the discovery is that people *"might know that God's Promise is true and that there is no doubt about the Hour."* Al-Ṭabarī states the argument plainly: One who can raise sleepers unchanged after many centuries can certainly raise the dead. The youths are a *demonstration*, and their demonstration is addressed to a specific doubt — the doubt about bodily resurrection.

The transmitted context makes this concrete. Al-Ṭabarī reports that during the reign of the righteous king, the people were disputing *"their affair"* — that is, whether the resurrection includes the body or the spirit alone. The king believed in bodily resurrection and prayed for a sign. The discovery of the sleepers, intact in body after three centuries, was the sign. This is why the Qur'an emphasises that the townspeople entered the cave and found them *"in a state of bodily perfection, without any sign of decomposition."*

The chapter's whole cave narrative is therefore an argument in narrative form. Its conclusion is stated in the verse: resurrection is bodily, and God's promise is reliable.

**"Erect a Building Over Them": The First Proposal**

*Idh yatanāzaʿūna baynahum amrahum, qālu-bnū ʿalayhim bunyānan, rabbuhum aʿlamu bihim* — "when they disputed among themselves about their affair, they said, 'Build over them a building; their Lord knows best about them.'"

The commentators differ on who disputes and about what. Al-Jalālayn and al-Rāzī suggest the dispute was between the disbelievers, who proposed a building, and the believers, who proposed a place of prayer. Others read it as a dispute about the youths themselves — whether they were dead or sleeping, how long they had tarried, how many there were (v. 22). Al-Ṭabarī's reading is that they built a structure which later became a place of prayer.

The clause *rabbuhum aʿlamu bihim* — "their Lord knows them best" — is the third occurrence of the formula in this chapter (vv. 19, 22, 26), and it functions here as the pious conclusion of an unresolvable dispute. When the disputants could not settle the matter, they referred it to God. The commentators have praised this as the correct posture, and the chapter's repetition of the formula is clearly pedagogical.

**"We Shall Build a Place of Worship Over Them": The Verse the Tradition Reads as a Warning**

*Qāla alladhīna ghalabū ʿalā amrihim la-nattakhidhanna ʿalayhim masjidā* — "those who prevailed in their affair said, 'We will surely take a place of worship over them.'" The emphatic construction *la-nattakhidhanna* — with both the *lām* of oath and the *nūn* of emphasis — marks a determined resolution.

The commentators read this in connection with a well-known Prophetic warning. Al-Bukhārī (435) and Muslim (529) record from ʿĀʾishah and Ibn ʿAbbās:

> *"When death approached the Messenger of God ﷺ, he began to cast a covering over his face, and when he was distressed he removed it, and he said: 'The curse of God be upon the Jews and the Christians — they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.' He was warning against what they had done."* (al-Bukhārī 435; Muslim 529)

And Muslim (532) records from Jundub ibn ʿAbdullāh:

> *"I heard the Prophet ﷺ, five days before his death, say: 'Behold, those before you took the graves of their prophets and their righteous as places of worship. Beware — do not take graves as places of worship. I forbid you that.'"*

Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī both cite these reports in commenting on this verse, and they read the Qur'anic statement as a *recounting with disapproval* rather than an endorsement: the Qur'an reports what the prevailing party said, and the Sunnah explains why the practice is forbidden. Al-Qurṭubī distinguishes carefully: building a mosque *near* a grave with the intention of venerating the occupant is what is prohibited, whereas a place of prayer built for ordinary worship without such veneration is a different matter — though the schools differ on the details, and the dominant position in the Ḥanbalī and Shāfiʿī schools is severe.

This is one of the clearest instances of the Qur'an and Sunnah functioning together: the verse records the fact, the ḥadīth supplies the ruling, and the reader who consults only one of them will misread the passage.

**Why the Chapter Ends the Story Here**

The cave narrative closes not with the youths' fate but with a dispute over their memorial. The choice is instructive. The chapter refuses to satisfy the reader's curiosity — it does not tell us whether they died or returned to sleep, how many they were, or where the cave is — and instead leaves us with the behaviour of later generations toward them. The commentators note the transmitted reports that some say the youth who went to the city spoke briefly with the king and townspeople, returned to the cave, rejoined his companions in sleep, and died.

The lesson the chapter chooses to end on is about what communities *do* with their saints. The youths themselves asked only for mercy and right guidance; their successors built structures. The Qur'an's silence about the youths' end, combined with its report of the building dispute, directs attention away from veneration of the dead and toward the faith that made them worth remembering.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:22

> **They say, "[They were] three, and the fourth was their dog." And they say, "Five, and the sixth was their dog"—guessing at the unseen. And they say, "Seven, and the eighth was their dog." Say, "My Lord knows best their number; none know them save a few." So dispute not concerning them, save with clear argument, nor consult any of them about them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Refuses to Answer**

Verse 22 is one of the most methodologically important passages in the Qur'an, and its importance lies in what it declines to do. The Quraysh's question, transmitted through the Jewish scholars of Yathrib, was precisely a question of detail — a story of ancient youths with an extraordinary affair. The chapter tells the story, and then, at the moment the reader expects the specifics, states that the specifics are unknowable and instructs the Prophet ﷺ to say so.

*sayaqūlūna thalāthatun rābiʿuhum kalbuhum, wa yaqūlūna khamsatun sādisuhum kalbuhum rajman bi'l-ghayb, wa yaqūlūna sabʿatun wa thāminuhum kalbuhum, qul rabbī aʿlamu bi-ʿidlatihim, mā yaʿlamuhum illā qalīl* — the three estimates are given, and then the divine instruction: *qul rabbī aʿlamu bi-ʿiddatihim* — "Say: My Lord knows best their number; none knows them save a few."

**The Mark That Identifies the Guess**

The Qur'an does not treat the three claims identically, and the commentators have noticed the difference with care. The phrase *rajman bi'l-ghayb* — "guessing at the unseen" — is inserted after the *second* claim only: *"Five, and the sixth was their dog" — guessing at the unseen.*

Al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī, and Ibn Kathīr draw the inference that has dominated the commentary tradition: the insertion of the disparaging phrase after the second claim, and its omission after the third, indicates that the first and third are not being dismissed in the same way. Some commentators go further and read the omission after "seven, and the eighth was their dog" as an indication that this is the correct number. Others, more cautiously, hold that the phrase attaches to the whole procedure of conjecture rather than to a particular figure.

There is also a stylistic observation the grammarians make: the three claims are stated in ascending order, and the third is introduced without the disparaging clause and with a slight change of construction — *wa thāminuhum kalbuhum* (using *wa*) rather than *rābiʿuhum* and *sādisuhum* (without it). Al-Zamakhsharī treats this as a signal.

Whether or not one accepts the inference that there were seven, the verse's *method* is unmistakable, and it is the reason the passage matters more than its arithmetic: when confronted with competing unverifiable claims, the correct response is not to choose one but to identify the question as one only God can answer.

**"None Knows Them Save a Few"**

*mā yaʿlamuhum illā qalīl* — "none knows them save a few." Al-Ṭabarī reports that some identified the "few" as the People of the Book — those who possessed the earlier tradition. Others read it as a small number of people in each generation who preserved the account. The verse does not specify, which is consistent with its purpose.

The phrase is remarkable in that it admits partial human knowledge while refusing to name its holders. The commentators have drawn a rule from it: there are matters on which knowledge exists but is not accessible to the enquirer, and the honest response to such matters is not speculation but acknowledgment.

**"Dispute Not Concerning Them, Save With Clear Argument"**

*Wa lā tumāri fīhim illā mirāʾan ẓāhiran* — "and do not argue about them except with an apparent/clear argument." The verb *mārā* means to dispute, to wrangle — and the Qur'an elsewhere warns against it: *"and do not dispute about the Qur'an"*; the noun *mirāʾ* carries a pejorative sense of contentious argument.

Al-Ṭabarī explains the instruction: the Prophet ﷺ is told not to debate the People of the Book about the details of the sleepers, but to let what has been revealed to him suffice. Al-Zamakhsharī reads *mirāʾan ẓāhiran* as an argument limited to what is outwardly revealed — i.e. tell them what the Qur'an says and go no further.

The ruling has been applied broadly in the Islamic tradition. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr both cite this verse in discussions of *Isrāʾīliyyāt* — the transmitted material from Jewish and Christian sources that entered Qur'anic commentary. The Prophetic guidance on the matter, recorded by al-Bukhārī (4485), is explicit:

> *"Do not believe the People of the Book nor disbelieve them, but say: 'We believe in God and what has been sent down to us.'"* (al-Bukhārī 4485, from Abū Hurayrah)

And in the version at al-Bukhārī (7362): *"If the People of the Book tell you something, do not confirm it and do not deny it."* The principle is that material which can neither be verified nor refuted should be reported, if at all, without endorsement — and should never be made the basis of doctrine or dispute.

**"Nor Consult Any of Them About Them"**

*Wa lā tastafti fīhim minhum aḥadan* — "and do not seek a ruling/opinion from any of them concerning them." The verb *istafā* means to ask for a *fatwā*, a formal determination. Al-Ṭabarī reads the instruction as a prohibition on questioning the People of the Book further about this matter.

This is the sharpest practical ruling in the verse, and it addresses a specific historical situation: the Prophet ﷺ and the early Muslims naturally consulted the People of the Book on scriptural matters where the Qur'an was silent. The verse closes that door on this subject, and by extension establishes a limit on the consultation. Ibn Kathīr draws the general principle: where revelation has spoken, further consultation of earlier traditions is not merely unnecessary but potentially corrupting, because it introduces unverifiable material into what God has deliberately left general.

**The Discipline the Verse Teaches**

Taken as a whole, verse 22 is a lesson in intellectual economy. The chapter has spent thirteen verses on a story of profound theological significance — faith under persecution, divine preservation, the resurrection demonstrated. It then refuses to spend a single verse on the number of sleepers. The implied instruction is about where religious attention should be directed: the *meaning* of the story is revelation; the *details* are curiosities, and curiosity is not a religious duty.

The modern reader recognises the phenomenon immediately. Every religious tradition contains a class of questions that generate enormous debate and no spiritual benefit — who was there, how many, where exactly, what did they look like. The verse names the activity (*rajm bi'l-ghayb*, throwing stones at the unseen), forbids the dispute, forbids the consultation, and supplies the correct formula: *My Lord knows best their number.* It is one of the Qur'an most efficient pieces of spiritual housekeeping.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:23

> **And say not of anything, "Surely, I shall do it tomorrow."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Correction at the Heart of the Chapter's Occasion**

This verse returns to the event that produced the sūrah. Al-Ṭabarī reports the circumstance: the Prophet ﷺ had told the Quraysh delegation that he would answer their three questions the following day, expecting revelation to arrive overnight. Fifteen days passed in silence. Makkah concluded that he had no answers and therefore no prophethood.

The verse is addressed to him directly: *wa lā taqūlanna li-shayʾin innī fāʿilun dhālika ghadā* — "and do not say of anything, 'I will surely do that tomorrow.'" The grammatical form is emphatic: *lā taqūlanna* combines the prohibitive *lā* with the *nūn* of emphasis, making it the strongest available prohibition. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī note that *tomorrow* here stands for the future generally, not the literal next day.

**Why the Rebuke Falls Here**

The reader might expect a chapter revealed to vindicate the Prophet ﷺ to omit this episode. It does the opposite: it inserts the rebuke into the vindication, at the exact point where the narrative of the sleepers ends. The commentators have drawn the obvious conclusion — and it is one of the classical arguments for the Qur'an's divine origin. A man composing a text to defend himself does not embed his own lapse in the defence. The presence of the rebuke is evidence about the text.

There is a second function. The delay itself was pedagogical: it taught the Prophet ﷺ, and the community after him, that revelation is not on tap. Even the Messenger could not summon it. Al-Ṭabarī treats the fifteen days as a period of instruction in dependence.

**The Theology of the Future Tense**

The prohibition rests on a claim about the nature of the future. A statement of the form "I will do X tomorrow" asserts, implicitly, three things: that I will still exist, that the conditions will remain in place, and that no intervening obstacle will arise. None of the three is within the speaker's control. The utterance is therefore not a report but an unauthorised prediction.

The Qur'an makes the point elsewhere with equal directness:

> *"O you who believe! Why do you say that which you do not do? Greatly hateful in the sight of God is that you say what you do not do."* (Qur'an 61:2–3)

And the Prophet ﷺ taught the matter in the ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī (6610) and Muslim (2674) concerning the man who swore an oath: the tradition's lesson is that oaths about the future require the qualification. Al-Bukhārī also records (7477) the account of Solomon, who said he would go around to seventy wives in one night, each bearing a warrior, and did not say *inshāʾ Allāh* — and none of them conceived except one who bore half a man. The Prophet ﷺ added: *"Had he said 'inshāʾ Allāh,' they would all have borne warriors fighting in God's cause."*

**The Psychological Mechanism**

There is a practical dimension the commentators touch on and modern experience confirms. Unqualified declarations about the future do two kinds of damage. They damage the speaker, who becomes invested in an outcome he cannot control and suffers when it fails — a small-scale version of the grief verse 6 warned against. And they damage the hearer, whose expectations have been raised and who experiences the failure as a breach of promise.

The qualification *inshāʾ Allāh* does not weaken the intention. The Muslim who says "I will come tomorrow, God willing" intends to come exactly as firmly as the one who omits the phrase. What the qualification alters is the *claim about reality* embedded in the statement: from "this will happen" to "I intend this, and its happening rests with God." That is a more accurate description of the world, and accuracy about the world is a religious duty.

**The Practice That Followed**

The verse generated one of the most widespread speech habits in human history. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī both note that the instruction is to add the conditional clause to any promise of future action, and the practice became universal in Muslim speech — in Arabic and in every language Islam reached, from Persian *inshāʾ Allāh* to Malay *insya-Allah* to Urdu *in shāʾ Allāh*. Al-Qurṭubī records the juristic discussion: the phrase must be uttered in the same breath, connected to the statement, for it to have its effect.

The practice is a *dhikr* embedded in ordinary conversation. Every time a Muslim says he will do something tomorrow, he rehearses the doctrine of divine will. The theology is not learned from a book; it is said, dozens of times a day, without thinking — which is precisely how a worldview is transmitted across generations.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:24

> **Save that God wills. And remember thy Lord when thou dost forget, and say, "It may be that my Lord will guide me nearer than this to rectitude."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Illā An Yashāʾa Allāh: The Exception That Governs All Speech**

*Illā an yashāʾa Allāh* — "unless God wills." Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī read the exception as attached to the prohibition: do not say "I will do this tomorrow" *except* with the acknowledgment that its fulfilment depends on God's will. The clause converts a bare prediction into a conditional intention.

The grammarians have discussed whether the exception is attached to the saying or to the doing. The majority read it as governing the speech — the utterance is permitted when qualified. The practical result is identical, and the Muslim practice derived from it is to attach the phrase to the statement immediately.

**"And Remember Thy Lord When Thou Dost Forget"**

*Wa-dhkur rabbaka idhā nasīta* — "and remember your Lord when you forget." The verse anticipates the failure of the instruction: people will forget to say *inshāʾ Allāh*. The remedy prescribed is not self-recrimination but *remembrance*. Al-Jalālayn and al-Zamakhsharī note the gentleness of the prescription, and the source material compares it to 6:68 — *"and if Satan causes thee to forget, then after remembering, sit not with the wrongdoing people"* — where the same structure appears: forgetfulness is expected, and the response is correction rather than despair.

This is a distinctive feature of Qur'anic spirituality that deserves emphasis. Many religious systems treat a lapse as a catastrophic event requiring elaborate expiation. The Qur'an's default response to forgetfulness is simply: remember. The ḥadīth in Muslim (675) records the Prophet ﷺ sleeping through the dawn prayer on a journey, and saying on waking:

> *"There is no negligence in sleep; negligence is only in waking. So if one of you forgets a prayer or sleeps through it, let him pray it when he remembers it."* (Muslim 681; al-Bukhārī 597)

The structure is identical: the lapse is acknowledged, the remedy is immediate, no guilt-cascade is authorised.

The commentators differ on the timing. Al-Jalālayn and al-Zamakhsharī report that it is best to correct oneself while still in the same place and situation in which the statement was made. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī allow the formula to be uttered much later, though — al-Ṭabarī adds — one may still be liable for expiation if an oath goes unfulfilled, the ruling on oaths being at 5:89 and 16:91–92.

**"It May Be That My Lord Will Guide Me Nearer Than This to Rectitude"**

*Wa qul ʿasā an yahdiyani rabbī li-aqraba min hādhā rashadā* — the commentators offer two readings, both preserved by al-Zamakhsharī, al-Bayḍāwī, and al-Jalālayn.

The first: this is the *content* of the remembrance. When one realises one has forgotten to acknowledge God's will, one says: "It may be that my Lord will guide me to something closer to right guidance than this." The formula acknowledges that the plan one had just announced may not be the best plan, and opens the matter to divine direction.

The second, transmitted from al-Bayḍāwī, al-Jalālayn, and al-Zamakhsharī: it is the Prophet's ﷺ prayer that God grant him a sign *greater* than the account of the sleepers. The Quraysh had challenged him to produce evidence; the cave story was one piece of it; and the Prophet ﷺ is instructed to say that God may well give him something nearer to the truth still. This reading fits the occasion: the revelation was still descending, and further evidences were to come.

The word *rashad* returns from verse 10 — the youths asked God to *"prepare for us, from our affair, right guidance (rashadā)."* The chapter thus links the Prophet's ﷺ prayer to the sleepers': both ask for *rushd*, and both receive more than they asked.

**What the Two Verses Together Establish**

Verses 23–24 form a complete practical doctrine. First: never assert the future without qualification. Second: when you forget, remember — and the remembering is itself a form of correction. Third: the correction includes a prayer for better guidance than the plan you had in mind.

The third element is the deepest. It asks the speaker not only to hedge his prediction but to doubt the wisdom of his intention. The person who says *inshāʾ Allāh* acknowledges that he does not control the outcome; the person who adds *"it may be that my Lord will guide me nearer than this to rectitude"* acknowledges that he may not even know what outcome is best. This is the difference between recognising divine power and recognising divine wisdom, and the verse requires both.

**The Practice of Istithnāʾ in the Tradition**

The commentators connect the verse to a specific Prophetic instruction recorded in the ḥadīth literature. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim record the report concerning Sulaymān:

> *"Sulaymān ibn Dāwūd said: I will go round tonight to ninety (or seventy, or ninety-nine) women, each of whom will give birth to a horseman who will fight in God's cause. But he did not say 'if God wills,' and he went round to them, and none of them gave birth except one, who gave birth to half a man. By the One in Whose Hand is my soul, had he said 'if God wills,' they would all have fought as horsemen in God's cause."* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 2819; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1654)

The commentators read the report as the practical exposition of this verse. The exception is not a formula of politeness but a correction of the underlying assumption — that the future is one's own to allocate.

The instruction is available daily. The person who says *"I will do this tomorrow"* has already made a claim about a matter outside his knowledge, and the verse requires the qualification not as etiquette but as accuracy. The tradition's practice of adding *in shāʾa Allāh* is therefore a discipline of truthful speech rather than a verbal habit.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:25

> **And they tarried in their cave three hundred years, plus nine more.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Only Number the Chapter Gives, Given Twice Over**

*Wa labithū fī kahfihim thalātha miʾatin sinīna wa-zdādū tisʿā* — "and they remained in their cave three hundred years, and they increased by nine." After verse 22's refusal to specify a number, verse 25 supplies one — for the duration rather than the persons — and verse 26 immediately qualifies it. The pairing is one of the Qur'an's most instructive rhetorical structures.

The commentators offer three readings of the figure. **First** (al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī): it reports the number the People of the Book themselves give, so that the Qur'an is transmitting their tradition rather than asserting a fact. **Second** (al-Ṭabarī): it is the actual figure for the sleep, and *"God knows best how long they tarried"* in verse 26 refers to the time they remained in the cave *after* their death, which the transmitted accounts place shortly after their discovery. **Third** (al-Zamakhsharī): it is the actual figure, and it settles an existing dispute, which is why the following verse affirms that God's knowledge is best.

**Why "Three Hundred, Plus Nine"**

The arithmetic has attracted attention from the earliest commentators, and Ibn Kathīr, al-Jalālayn, and al-Qurṭubī all record the explanation: three hundred years by the **solar** reckoning corresponds to three hundred and nine by the **lunar** reckoning, because the lunar year is approximately eleven days shorter. The Arabs, and the Islamic calendar the Prophet ﷺ established, follow the lunar year; the Christian tradition from which the story derives follows the solar.

The reading is elegant and it accounts for the odd construction. The verse does not say "three hundred and nine years"; it says "three hundred years, *and they added nine*." The addition is presented as an increment to a base figure, which is exactly how a solar-to-lunar conversion would appear. Ibn Kathīr, al-Jalālayn, and al-Qurṭubī present this as the reason for the phrasing.

If the reading is correct, the verse performs a small act of calendrical reconciliation between two religious communities whose accounts of the same event differ by nine years — and does so without announcing that it is doing so.

**The Number in Relation to the Historical Tradition**

The Christian account of the Seven Sleepers of Ephesus places their sleep under the emperor Decius (reigned 249–251 CE) and their waking under Theodosius II (reigned 402–450 CE), a span of roughly 150–200 years in some versions and considerably longer in others. The Qur'anic figure of 300/309 does not match the standard Christian chronology exactly, and the commentators have not been troubled by this, for the reason verse 26 supplies.

What matters for the chapter's purpose is not the precise figure but its *order of magnitude*: several centuries. The miracle is the preservation of bodies across generations, and whether the span is 150, 200, or 309 years does not alter the demonstration. The Qur'an's narrative economy — no names, no city, no emperor, and a number immediately qualified — is consistent with a text that is teaching rather than chronicling.

**The Qur'an's Habit of Naming and Then Qualifying**

The structure deserves notice because it recurs. The Qur'an states a figure and then subordinates it to divine knowledge: *"They tarried three hundred years, plus nine. Say: God knows best how long they tarried."* The same movement occurs at verse 22 — three numbers are given, and then *"My Lord knows best their number."*

The effect is to give the reader something concrete while refusing to make the concrete the point. A reader who fixes on 309 has missed the passage; a reader who refuses any figure at all has missed it too. The chapter wants both the fact and the acknowledgment that the fact is not the reader's to master.

**Three Centuries as a Theological Statement**

The duration itself carries meaning. The Qur'an elsewhere uses long spans to make the same point about divine power over time:
