> *"Or like the one who passed by a town while it was fallen upon its roofs. He said: How will God bring this to life after its death? So God caused him to die for a hundred years, then raised him."* (Qur'an 2:259)

And of Noah: *"And We sent Noah to his people, and he remained among them a thousand years less fifty."* (29:14). The Qur'an's use of large numbers is deliberate and rare; when it names a span, the span is doing theological work. Three centuries is long enough that no living memory connects the sleepers to their world — long enough that the coin in the youth's pocket is an antiquity, and the king who sealed the cave is a name in a chronicle.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:26

> **Say, "God knows best how long they tarried. Unto Him belongs the Unseen of the heavens and the earth. How well He sees, how well He hears! They have no protector apart from Him, and He makes no one a partner unto Him in His Judgment."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Instruction to Speak, Not to Argue**

*qul Allāhu aʿlamu bi-mā labithū* — "Say: God knows best how long they tarried." The verse does not resolve the figure of verse 25; it instructs the Prophet ﷺ on what to *say* when asked. Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr note the significance: the Qur'an is teaching a form of response, not merely a doctrine. When asked about matters belonging to the Unseen, the believer's answer is a *sentence* — and the sentence is provided.

This is the third use of the *aʿlam* formula in the chapter (vv. 19, 22, 26), and the repetition is plainly instructional. The chapter wants the phrase learned.

**"Unto Him Belongs the Unseen of the Heavens and the Earth"**

*la-hu ghaybu al-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* — the Qur'an's standard formulation, repeated at 10:20, 11:123, 16:77. The *ghayb* — the Unseen — is the whole domain of what is hidden from creatures: the future, the interior of hearts, the events of the past unrecorded, the realities of the Hereafter. The Qur'an's insistence on divine exclusivity over this domain is absolute:

> *"And with Him are the keys of the Unseen; none knows them save Him. And He knows whatever is on land and in sea; and no leaf falls but that He knows it, nor a seed in the darknesses of the earth, nor anything green or dry, but that it is in a clear Book."* (Qur'an 6:59)

> *"Say: None in the heavens or the earth knows the Unseen save God."* (Qur'an 27:65)

> *"Knower of the Unseen; He reveals not His Unseen to anyone, save a messenger whom He approves."* (Qur'an 72:26–27)

The last of these is important for the cave story: the Prophet ﷺ *was* told about the sleepers, which means the disclosure was a prophetic disclosure under 72:27, not a discovery. His knowledge of them is exactly as extensive as God made it — which is why he can narrate the story and cannot state the number.

**"How Well He Sees, How Well He Hears"**

*fa-abṣir bihi wa asmiʿ* — an exclamatory construction: "How seeing is He, and how hearing!" Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī explain it as indicating that none sees or hears as God sees and hears. The two faculties named are the two by which knowledge of particular events is acquired, and their perfection in God means that nothing in the *ghayb* is hidden from Him either.

The Qur'an pairs divine seeing and hearing constantly as the two Names *al-Baṣīr* and *al-Samīʿ*, and it grounds moral seriousness in them:

> *"Sight perceives Him not, but He perceives all sight; and He is the Subtle, the Aware."* (Qur'an 6:103)

The verse's placement here is significant. The story has just described seven sleepers hidden in a mountain cave, sealed behind a wall, unknown to their city for three centuries — the most complete concealment imaginable. And the verse responds: *how well He sees, how well He hears.* Nothing was hidden. God saw them throughout, and the "hiding" was itself His arrangement.

**"They Have No Protector Apart From Him"**

*mā lahum min dūnihi min waliyy* — al-Rāzī reads the reference as either the youths or those who disputed about them. The phrase *min dūnihi* — "apart from Him" — combined with the doubled *min* of emphasis, states total exclusion. The Qur'an repeats the formula at 6:70, 11:20, 29:22, 33:18, 42:32, 46:32.

For the youths specifically, the statement is a summary of their story. They had no protector: not their families, not their king, not their city, not their own strength. They had a cave and a prayer. The verse names what actually protected them.

**"He Makes No One a Partner in His Judgment"**

*wa lā yushriku fī ḥukmihi aḥadā* — "and He associates none in His judgment." Ibn Kathīr reads this as the finality of divine judgment: there is no appeal, no revision, no higher court. Al-Rāzī applies it to the immediate context: God's statement about the sleepers and their duration is the last word on the matter, and no further inquiry will improve on it.

Both readings close the discussion that verses 22 and 25 opened. The chapter has given the reader the story, refused the details, supplied a number, qualified the number, and now declared that no one else has standing to adjudicate. The reader is left with the moral and released from the arithmetic — which is precisely the outcome verse 22 aimed at.

**The Verse as a Summary of the Chapter's Theology**

Read as a whole, verse 26 is a compact statement of the doctrines the rest of the chapter will dramatize: God alone knows the Unseen (the sleepers' centuries, Khiḍr's hidden wisdom, the timing of the Hour); God sees and hears perfectly (the sealed cave, the garden's owner's private boast, the whispered complaint); there is no protector besides Him (the seven youths, the poor man facing the rich one, the weak people before Gog and Magog); and His judgment admits no partner (the Book set down at verse 49, the wall crumbling at verse 98).

It is placed at the end of the first narrative and before the second, functioning as the hinge on which the chapter turns from story to story.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:27

> **Recite that which has been revealed unto thee from the Book of thy Lord. None alters His Words. And thou wilt find no refuge apart from Him.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer to a Demand Made in Makkah**

*Wa-tlu mā ūḥiya ilayka min kitābi rabbik* — "and recite what has been revealed to you from the Book of your Lord." Al-Zamakhsharī identifies the occasion: the Makkans, displeased with the Qur'an, asked the Prophet ﷺ to bring a different Qur'an or to change it to suit their preferences. The Qur'an records the demand at 10:15:

> *"And when Our clear signs are recited unto them, those who do not expect to meet Us say, 'Bring a Qur'an other than this, or change it.' Say: It is not for me to change it of my own accord. I follow only what is revealed unto me. Truly I fear, were I to disobey my Lord, the punishment of a tremendous Day."* (Qur'an 10:15)

Verse 27 is the corresponding command: recite what was revealed. Not a version of it, not a selection from it, not an adaptation of it.

**"None Alters His Words"**

*lā mubaddila li-kalimātihi* — "there is none who can alter His words." The Qur'an repeats the assertion at 6:115 and 10:64:

> *"And the Word of thy Lord is fulfilled in truth and in justice. There is none who can alter His Words, and He is the Hearing, the Knowing."* (Qur'an 6:115)

The commentators — al-Zamakhsharī and others — clarify a point of doctrine: only God alters His revelations, replacing one with another, which is the mechanism of *naskh* described at 16:101:

> *"And when We exchange a sign for a sign — and God knows best what He sends down — they say, 'Thou art but a fabricator.' Nay, but most of them do not know."* (Qur'an 16:101)

The distinction matters: abrogation within the revelation is God's prerogative exercised through the revelation; alteration of the revelation by human beings is impossible. The verse's assertion is the doctrinal foundation of the Muslim claim that the Qur'anic text is unchanged — a claim that rests not on the diligence of scribes but on this verse and 15:9:

> *"Truly We have sent down the Remembrance, and truly We are its guardians."* (Qur'an 15:9)

**What the Command Assumes About the Messenger**

The verse addresses the Prophet ﷺ as a *reciter*, not an author. The verb *talā* — to recite, to rehearse — is the verb from which *tilāwah* comes, and the Qur'an uses it consistently of the Prophet's ﷺ relation to the text: he recites what is given. This is the structural claim of the whole revelation, and the verse restates it at a moment of pressure.

The demand to alter the Qur'an was not frivolous. The Makkans' objections were specific: the Qur'an attacked their ancestors, undermined their religious economy, elevated the poor over the notables, and insisted on a resurrection they found absurd. A negotiable text would have solved everything. The verse refuses, and the refusal is the reason the message survived — a revelation that can be edited by its audience is not a revelation.

**"Thou Wilt Find No Refuge Apart From Him"**

*wa lan tajida min dūnihi multaḥadā* — "and you will find, apart from Him, no place of refuge." The noun *multaḥad* is from *l-ḥ-d*, the root of *laḥad*, the niche cut into the side of a grave — a place one leans into, a hollow of shelter. The image is of someone looking for somewhere to hide and finding none.

Al-Zamakhsharī connects the clause to the preceding command: if you alter what was revealed, there is nowhere you can go. The commentators also read it more broadly as the general truth that the Prophet ﷺ — and every believer — has no recourse except God.

The placement is rhetorically powerful. Verse 26 ended with *"they have no protector apart from Him"*; verse 27 ends with *"you will find no refuge apart from Him."* The chapter says the same thing to the sleepers and to the Prophet ﷺ. Both are in the same position: dependent, exposed, with nowhere to go but God. The cave was not an exception to this condition; it was an illustration of it.

**The Verse's Function in the Chapter's Structure**

Verse 27 sits between the cave narrative (vv. 9–26) and the parable of the two gardens (vv. 32–43). It re-establishes the frame: this is a Book being recited, its reciter has no authority to modify it, and its contents — the cave, the garden, Khiḍr, Dhu'l-Qarnayn — are what God chose to reveal. The reader is being told, before the second story begins, that he is not reading legend but recitation.

**The Command Extended to the Reader**

The commentators note that the imperative *utlu* is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ, and that the tradition nonetheless reads it as establishing an obligation on the community. The reasoning is recorded in the exegetical literature: a command to recite what has been revealed is not discharged by the recitation of one man, since the purpose of recitation is that it be heard.

The ḥadīth literature treats recitation as an act of worship in its own right. Al-Tirmidhī records:

> *"Whoever recites a letter from the Book of God, for him is a good deed, and a good deed is tenfold. I do not say that *Alif-Lām-Mīm* is a letter, but *Alif* is a letter, *Lām* is a letter, and *Mīm* is a letter."* (Jāmiʿ al-Tirmidhī 2910)

The commentators have drawn from the verse and the ḥadīth together that recitation is not a preliminary to understanding but part of what is required. The reader who studies the chapter without reciting it, and the one who recites it without attending to it, have each performed half of what the verse describes.

This is why the chapter has entered the practice of the community as a text recited weekly rather than only consulted. The commentators note that the sūrah's own instruction — recite what has been revealed — is fulfilled by the recitation itself, and that the reader who does so is performing the command rather than merely learning about it.
---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:28

> **Make thy soul patient with those who call upon their Lord morning and evening, desiring His Face. Turn not thine eyes away from them, desiring the adornment of the life of this world, nor obeying one whose heart We have made heedless of the remembrance of Us and who follows his caprice and whose affair exceeds the bounds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Occasion: A Negotiation About Seating**

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Wāḥidī report the circumstance, which is also discussed in the commentary on 6:52. Certain prominent Makkans — the sources name ʿUyaynah ibn Ḥiṣn, al-Aqraʿ ibn Ḥābis, and others of the Qurayshi notables — indicated that they were prepared to sit with the Prophet ﷺ and listen to his teaching, on one condition: that his poorer followers be sent away, so that they would not have to sit alongside people of low social standing.

The demand was not a refusal. It was a *deal* — the conversion of the leading families of Makkah, which would have transformed the movement's political standing overnight. And the verse refuses it.

**"Make Thy Soul Patient With Those Who Call Upon Their Lord"**

*Wa-ṣbir nafsaka maʿa alladhīna yadʿūna rabbahum bi'l-ghadāti wa'l-ʿashiyyi yurīdūna wajhah* — "and keep your soul patiently with those who call upon their Lord morning and evening, seeking His Face." The verb *ṣabbara* is the intensive form: not merely to be patient but to *hold* oneself, to bind oneself to a thing. The object is *nafsaka* — your own soul. The Prophet ﷺ is being told to hold himself in place.

Al-Ṭabarī explains *yadʿūna* as encompassing invocation, praise, glorification, supplication, and the prayers. *Bi'l-ghadāti wa'l-ʿashiyy* — "morning and evening" — is read either as specifying those two times or, more commonly, as an idiom for constancy; the same formula appears at 33:42, 48:9, and 76:25.

*Yurīdūna wajhah* — "desiring His Face" — is one of the Qur'an's highest descriptions of sincerity. Al-Ṭabarī reads it as seeking God's pleasure and reward rather than worldly benefit; the parallel verses are 2:272, 13:22, 30:38–39, 76:9, 92:18–20. The Sufi commentators push it further: at its highest, seeking the Face is seeking the vision of God.

**"Turn Not Thine Eyes Away From Them"**

*Wa lā taʿdu ʿaynāka ʿanhum turīdu zīnata al-ḥayāti al-dunyā* — "and let not your eyes pass beyond them, desiring the adornment of the life of this world." Al-Ṭabarī explains the two possible readings: not to look past the poor believers toward potential followers who are wealthier or more influential; and not to prefer the company of the wealthy and prominent to that of the lowly who are pious and sincere.

The word *zīnah* returns from verse 7 — "We made whatever is upon the earth an adornment for it." The chapter is closing a loop: the adornment is the test, and here is the test being applied to the Prophet ﷺ himself, in the form of a business proposal.

The reported Prophetic response, recorded by Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Wāḥidī, is worth quoting:

> *"Praise be to God, Who did not let me die before commanding me to be patient with certain men of my community."*

The parallel incident in 80:1–10 — where the Prophet ﷺ frowned because a blind man, Ibn Umm Maktūm, interrupted his conversation with a Qurayshi chief — produced a revelation of similar severity. The Qur'an polices this boundary with remarkable consistency: the religious community may not be organised by social rank.

**"Nor Obey One Whose Heart We Have Made Heedless"**

*Wa lā tuṭiʿ man aghfalnā qalbahu ʿan dhikrinā* — al-Ṭabarī identifies the referent as the Makkans who wished the Prophet to drive away his poor followers. The verb *aghfalnā* — "We have made heedless" — is another instance of the divine attribution the commentators discuss at verse 17: God's "making heedless" is the withdrawal of remembrance from a heart that has turned away from it, not an arbitrary imposition.

The verse then gives the threefold diagnosis of such a person: his heart is heedless of God's remembrance; *wa-ttabaʿa hawāhu* — "he follows his caprice"; *wa kāna amruhu furuṭā* — "his affair exceeds the bounds." Al-Ṭabarī and the commentators explain *furuṭ* as prodigality, wastefulness, negligence — putting petty desires before the truth.

This is a complete moral psychology in three clauses. The sequence is causal: remembrance is abandoned, desire takes the vacant seat, and conduct loses its limits. The Qur'an states the same progression at 25:43: *"Hast thou seen him who takes his own desire as his god?"* The verse is not condemning a man for wanting to sit comfortably; it is diagnosing a condition in which the heart has no reference point beyond preference.

**Why the Verse Matters Beyond Its Occasion**

The incident could have been treated as a minor point of protocol. The Qur'an made it a standing command. Every religious community faces the same negotiation: the influential will engage if the community makes itself presentable, and the price of presentability is always the least presentable members. The verse forbids the trade absolutely, and forbids it to the Prophet ﷺ — the person with the strongest possible prudential reason to accept.

The practical instruction is uncomfortable and clear. A community's spiritual health is measured not by its most impressive adherents but by how it treats its least impressive ones. The people who "call upon their Lord morning and evening, desiring His Face" are, in the verse's own valuation, worth more than the notables whose conversion was on offer — and the Prophet ﷺ was commanded to sit with them, in full view, and be patient with the cost.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:29

> **And say, "It is the truth from your Lord! So whosoever will, let him believe, and whosoever will, let him disbelieve." Verily We have prepared for the wrongdoers a Fire whose canopies will encompass them. And if they plead for relief, they will be showered with water like molten lead that will scald faces—an evil drink indeed, and how evil a resting place!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"It Is the Truth From Your Lord": No Bargaining Left**

*Wa qul al-ḥaqqu min rabbikum* — "and say: the truth is from your Lord." The verse returns to the negotiation of verse 28 and closes it. There is no modified version of the message, no accommodation of social rank, and no adjustment of content. What is offered is the truth, and it comes from God rather than from the messenger, which is why it cannot be negotiated by the messenger.

**"Whosoever Will, Let Him Believe": The Most Misread Line in the Chapter**

*fa-man shāʾa fa-l-yuʾmin wa man shāʾa fa-l-yakfur* — "so whoever wills, let him believe, and whoever wills, let him disbelieve." Read in isolation, this appears to be a statement of indifference — believe or not, as you like. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī are emphatic that it is not: the statement is a **warning**, not a permission. Al-Zamakhsharī makes the same point from the human side: the individual must choose, and the choice has consequences.

Three features of the verse establish the reading. First, the second half of the verse describes the Fire prepared for the wrongdoers, which is hardly the tone of indifference. Second, the construction *fa-l-yuʾmin* uses the *lām* of command — "let him believe" — which is imperative in form even where the sense is declarative. Third, the Qur'an uses the same idiom elsewhere in contexts of unmistakable threat:

> *"Say: O disbelievers! I worship not what you worship... Unto you your religion, and unto me my religion."* (Qur'an 109:1–6)

The rhetorical form is that of a person who has exhausted argument: the terms are stated, the choice is yours, the outcome is fixed. It is the language of a final offer.

**The Principle It Establishes**

The verse articulates the limit of prophetic responsibility, which the chapter has been building toward since verse 6. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both note that the Prophet's ﷺ duty is *declaration*; the determination of who accepts and who rejects belongs to God — or, from the human side, to the individual. This is the same doctrine as 88:21–22 (*"So remind; you are only a reminder; you are not a warden over them"*) and 28:56 (*"Truly you guide not whom you love"*).

The corollary is the Qur'an's insistence that faith cannot be coerced, stated at 2:256 (*"there is no compulsion in religion"*) and 10:99 (*"Had your Lord willed, all who are on earth would have believed. Will you then compel people until they become believers?"*). Verse 29 belongs to this family. The choice is real, the choice is the individual's, and the choice has an issue.

**"A Fire Whose Canopies Will Encompass Them"**

*innā aʿtadnā li'l-ẓālimīna nāran aḥāṭa bihim surādiquhā* — *surādiq* denotes the enclosing wall or canopy of a tent — the cloth that surrounds a space on all sides. The commentators (al-Zamakhsharī, and the parallel verses at 29:55 and 39:16) explain that the Fire surrounds them from every direction, above and below. The image is of total enclosure with no exit, and it reverses the image of the Garden, where the believers are *surrounded* by shade and rivers.

**"Water Like Molten Lead"**

*wa in yastaghīthū yughāthū bi-māʾin ka'l-muhli yashwī al-wujūh* — "and if they cry for relief, they will be relieved with water like molten lead, which scalds the faces." *Muhl* is variously glossed as molten metal, the dregs of oil, or molten lead; the commentators note that the Qur'an uses the same word at 44:45 of the tree of Zaqqūm:

> *"Truly the tree of Zaqqūm is the food of the sinner, like molten brass boiling in the bellies, like the boiling of scalding water."* (Qur'an 44:43–46)

Al-Zamakhsharī describes the image: a wall of water poured into the Fire, and when its inhabitants seek to drink from it for relief, its heat burns their faces before it reaches their mouths. The verse specifies that the liquid functions in both ways at once — poured upon them *and* offered as a drink. The Qur'an lists a range of such drinks at 6:70, 10:4, 22:19, 37:67, 38:57, 40:72, 44:48, 47:15, 56:42, 56:54, 56:93, 78:25, 88:5.

The rhetorical structure is precise: they ask for *ghiyāth* — relief, succour — and they are given *ghiyāth*, in a form that increases the torment. The request is answered; the answer is the punishment. This is the Qur'an's characteristic eschatological irony, which it also employs in describing Hell as a *"welcome"* (*nuzul*, v. 102) and in *"giving glad tidings"* of painful punishment (31:7, 45:8).

**"That Will Scald Faces"**

*yashwī al-wujūh* — the verb *shawā* means to roast, to scorch. The commentators note that the Qur'an consistently attaches eschatological punishment and honour to *faces*: 3:106, 4:47, 8:50, 10:27, 14:50, 17:97, 21:39, 23:104, 25:34, 27:90, 33:66, 39:60, 47:27, 54:48, 67:27, 80:40–41. The face is the person — the seat of identity, the part turned toward others, the organ by which one is known.

The point is not anatomical but personal. The punishment reaches the *self*, not merely the body. And the symmetry with the Garden is exact: *"On that Day, faces shall be radiant, looking upon their Lord"* (75:22–23). The face that turned away from God in this world is the face that meets the consequence; the face that sought His Face (v. 28) is the face that beholds Him.

**"An Evil Drink, and How Evil a Resting Place"**

*biʾsa al-sharāb, wa sāʾat murtafaqā* — *murtafaq* is from *r-f-q*, the same root as verse 16's *mirfaq* (a support to lean on, a means of ease) and the source of *rafīq*, companion. A *murtafaq* is a place of reclining, of resting in company. The verse names Hell a *murtafaq* and calls it evil — and verse 31 will name the Garden a *murtafaq* and call it beautiful.

The chapter's two destinations are thus described with the same word, and the difference between them is the whole of the sūrah's argument.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:30

> **As for those who believe and perform righteous deeds, surely We neglect not the reward of one who is virtuous in deed.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counterweight, Given in a Single Sentence**

After the Fire of verse 29, the verse turns to the believers: *inna lā nuḍīʿu ajra man aḥsana ʿamalā* — "indeed, We do not waste the reward of one who does good in his deed." The verb *aḍāʿa* means to cause to perish, to lose, to squander — to let a thing go to waste. The negation is God's own, in the royal plural.

The commentators — al-Zamakhsharī, Ibn Kathīr — note the parallel affirmations elsewhere: 9:120, 11:115, 12:56, 12:89 for the doers of good; 3:171 for the believers; 7:170 for the righteous. The Qur'an repeats this guarantee because it addresses a specific human anxiety: that goodness goes unnoticed, that sacrifice counts for nothing, that the honest person finishes last.

**"Faith and Righteous Deeds": The Inseparable Pair**

The verse opens with *alladhīna āmanū wa ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt*, the formula that recurs throughout the Qur'an — 2:25, 3:57, 4:57, 5:69, 7:42, 17:9, and 98:7:

> *"Truly those who believe and perform righteous deeds — they are the best of creation."* (Qur'an 98:7)

The commentators treat the conjunction as theology rather than style. Belief supplies the direction; deeds supply the movement; neither functions alone.

**Aḥsana ʿAmalā and the Three Components of Religion**

The verse's closing phrase — *man aḥsana ʿamalā*, "the one who has made his deed good/beautiful" — recalls verse 7, where the purpose of the earth's adornment was *"that We may test them as to which of them is best in deed (ayyuhum aḥsanu ʿamalā)."* The chapter states the test in verse 7 and the reward for passing it in verse 30, using the same phrase.

The commentators connect *aḥsana* to the three components of religion named in the ḥadīth of Gabriel, recorded by Muslim (8) and al-Bukhārī (50): the angel asks the Prophet ﷺ about *islām* (the five pillars — testimony, prayer, alms, fasting, pilgrimage), then about *īmān* (belief in God, His angels, His books, His messengers, the Last Day, and divine decree), and then about *iḥsān*. The Prophet ﷺ answered:

> *"Iḥsān is that you worship God as though you see Him; and if you do not see Him, then He sees you."* (Muslim 8; al-Bukhārī 50)

The verse's *man aḥsana ʿamalā* is the verbal form of *iḥsān*. The reward promised is not for deeds merely performed but for deeds *made excellent* — performed in the awareness of being seen. This is the connection to verse 28's *"desiring His Face"*: the excellence of the deed lies in its orientation.

**"We Do Not Waste": The Guarantee Against Injustice**

The precise thing promised is that nothing will be *lost*. The Qur'an is insistent on this at the level of the individual act:

> *"So whoever does an atom's weight of good shall see it, and whoever does an atom's weight of evil shall see it."* (Qur'an 99:7–8)

The guarantee answers a real and recurring human experience: the good deed that goes unrecognised, the sacrifice nobody noticed, the years of patient labour that produced no visible result. The verse's claim is that the accounting is perfect and that no entry is missing. This is why the Qur'an can ask believers to act without expectation of human recognition — the recognition they need is guaranteed elsewhere.

There is also a social dimension. The verse follows immediately upon the command not to overlook the poor believers in favour of the notables (v. 28). The people whose deeds are most likely to go unnoticed in this world — those with no platform, no wealth, no audience — are precisely the people the verse reassures. The chapter is telling the unnamed worshipper of verse 28 that his morning and evening invocation is recorded.

**The Verse Between the Two Trials**

The commentators note the verse's placement: it stands between the trial of wealth in the garden narrative and the trial of the world's transience in the water parable. It is the chapter's hinge, and its function is to state what survives both.

The structure is instructive. Verses 32–43 will show wealth destroyed; verses 45–46 will show the world itself as chaff. Between them the verse states the counterweight: *"We neglect not the reward of one who is virtuous in deed."* The reader is given the assurance before the demonstration, so that the demonstration does not read as despair.

The commentators have drawn the pastoral point that the order is deliberate. A person who hears only that the world passes may conclude that nothing matters; a person who hears first that no good act is wasted, and then that the world passes, arrives at the correct conclusion — that the world's passing is precisely why the good act matters.

The verse is thus the chapter's answer to nihilism, placed before the material that would otherwise invite it.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:31

> **For such as these, theirs shall be the Gardens of Eden with rivers running below. Therein they shall be adorned with bracelets of gold, and shall wear green garments of fine silk and rich brocade, reclining upon couches. Blessed indeed is the reward, and how beautiful a resting place!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Jannāt ʿAdn: The Gardens of Perpetual Residence**

The reward named for the people of verse 30 is *jannāt ʿadn* — Gardens of Eden, or more precisely Gardens of *Perpetual Residence*. The noun *ʿadn* is from *ʿ-d-n*, to settle, to dwell permanently — the same root that produces themaʿādin, mines, i.e. places where a thing is found settled in the earth. The Qur'an uses the phrase at 9:72, 13:23, 16:31, 19:61, 20:76, 35:33, 38:50, 40:8, and 98:8, and the commentators treat it as naming the highest region of the Garden.

The word answers verse 3 directly: the believers will remain *forever* — *mākithīna fīhi abadan*. The Garden's defining feature, before any of its contents, is permanence. This is what makes it the *mirfaq* (v. 16) and the *murtafaq* (v. 31) that does not fail.

**"Rivers Running Below"**

*Tajrī min tahtihā al-anhār* — the phrase occurs in more than two dozen Qur'anic descriptions of the Garden, beginning at 2:25. The commentators note that in a desert civilisation, running water is the single most legible image of abundance: not stored water, not a well that can run dry, but water that *runs*. The preposition *min tahtihā* — "from beneath it" — has been read as indicating rivers flowing beneath the palaces and through the gardens, so that the inhabitant is never far from water.

For the first hearers in Arabia, the image was not decorative. It was the difference between the oasis and the desert, and the Qur'an knew exactly what it was promising.

**Gold Bracelets, Green Silk, and Brocade**

*Yuḥallawna fīhā min asāwira min dhahabin wa yalbasūna thiyāban khuḍran min sundusin wa istabraq* — "they will be adorned therein with bracelets of gold, and will wear green garments of fine silk and brocade." The commentators note the parallel descriptions at 22:23, 35:33, 76:21 (where the bracelets are of silver, or of silver and gold), and 44:53.

*Sundus* is fine, thin silk; *istabraq* is thick brocade, a loanword from Persian that the commentators identify as heavy woven fabric. The pairing — the fine and the heavy — indicates garments for every occasion, and al-Qurṭubī notes that the two terms between them cover the whole range of luxurious cloth.

Green is specified, and al-Qurṭubī records the reason: it is the colour most pleasing to the eye. At 55:76 the inhabitants of the Garden *"recline upon green cushions and splendid carpets."* Al-Rāzī offers an allegorical reading: the green silken garments represent what the believers earned by their deeds, while the bracelets represent an additional bounty from God beyond their earning — a distinction the commentators value because it preserves the primacy of grace.

A ḥadīth recorded by al-Qurṭubī connects the extent of one's ornaments in the Garden to the reach of one's ablutions in this world — the water of *wuḍūʾ* determining the reach of the bracelet. Whether or not one accepts the specific report, the principle it expresses is standard: the Hereafter's adornment corresponds to this world's purification.

**Reclining Upon Couches**

*Muttakiʾīna fīhā ʿalā al-arāʾik* — the verb *ittakaʾa* means to recline at ease, to lean back in comfort; *arāʾik* are couches, and the commentators — Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī — describe them as curtained or canopied and bejewelled. The same image appears at 15:47, 36:56, 37:44, 52:20, 56:15, 76:13, 83:23, 83:35, 88:13.

The commentators make the sociological point explicitly: gold jewellery, fine silk, and raised couches signified immense luxury in Arabian desert life, where rough wool and sitting on the ground were the norm. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī read the images as indicating that the inhabitants of the Garden will be *like kings* — ornamented and enthroned.

There is an irony the commentators draw out, and it is the sharpest point of the verse. Gold and silk are *forbidden to Muslim men* in this world, according to well-established ḥadīth — al-Bukhārī (5835) and Muslim (2067) record the Prophet ﷺ taking silk in his right hand and gold in his left and saying: *"These two are forbidden to the males of my community and permitted to their females."* The pleasures of the Garden thus include precisely what was withheld in this world. The prohibition is not a deprivation but a deferral — and the deferral is itself a discipline, teaching the believer that the good things he forgoes are not lost but stored.

**"Blessed Indeed Is the Reward, and How Beautiful a Resting Place"**

*niʿma al-ajru wa ḥasuna murtafaqā* — the same two nouns as verse 2's *ajran ḥasanan*, and the same *murtafaq* as verse 29's description of Hell. The chapter's two destinations are described with identical vocabulary, and the difference is entirely one of quality.

The commentators note that *niʿma* is an exclamatory form of praise — "how excellent!" — and that the verse ends where verse 29 ended, with the resting place. The structural parallelism is unmistakable: v. 29, *"an evil drink indeed, and how evil a resting place"*; v. 31, *"blessed indeed is the reward, and how beautiful a resting place."* The reader is being shown two rooms and asked to choose.

**The Literal and the Symbolic**

Al-Qurṭubī records that many commentators take these sensual descriptions as symbolic rather than literal, and the Sufi tradition — al-Qushayrī and those al-Qurṭubī transmits — reads the luxuries of the Garden as symbols of the spiritual joys of witnessing God in His Majesty and Beauty, and of the intimacy and union the righteous will experience.

The mainstream position holds both readings together: the descriptions are true as descriptions, and they also point beyond themselves. The Qur'an consistently describes the Hereafter in the vocabulary of this world because that is the only vocabulary available — and it repeatedly warns that the reality exceeds the description, as in the ḥadīth qudsī recorded by al-Bukhārī (4779) and Muslim (2824):

> *"I have prepared for My righteous servants what no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and no human heart has conceived."*

The verse's function is not to give a specification but to make the reward *desirable* — to ensure that the reader wants it, in the concrete way that human beings want things. Desire is the engine of moral effort, and the Qur'an does not disdain to engage it.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:32

> **Set forth for them the parable of two men: For one of them We made two gardens of grapevines, and surrounded them with date palms, and placed crops between them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter's Second Trial**

*Wa-ḍrib lahum mathalan rajulayn* — "and set forth for them a parable of two men." The chapter moves from its first narrative — faith under persecution — to its second: prosperity without gratitude. Al-Ṭabarī and the commentators connect the parable directly to verse 28: the prominent Makkans who refused to sit with the poor believers are being shown what their wealth is worth.

The verb *ḍaraba al-mathal* — to strike/set forth a parable — is the Qur'an's standard formula for introducing an illustrative narrative, and verse 54 will state the method: *"We have employed every kind of parable for mankind in this Qur'an."* The commentators note the logic: abstract warning is resisted; narrative is absorbed. A Makkan notable might argue with a verse about ingratitude, but he cannot argue with a story about a man with two gardens — because he will recognise himself.

**Two Gardens, Not One**

*jaʿalnā li-aḥadihimā jannatayni min aʿnāb* — "We made for one of them two gardens of grapevines." The dual is significant. A single garden is a possession; two gardens are an *estate*. The commentators note the deliberate excess: the man is not merely comfortable, he is redundantly provisioned, with surplus capacity.

The choice of crops is equally deliberate. Vines (*aʿnāb*) and date palms (*nakhīl*) are the two signature cultivations of the Near East, and the Qur'an pairs them repeatedly as emblems of prosperity and divine beneficence: 6:99, 6:141, 23:19, 36:34. Ibn Kathīr and the commentators observe that the Makkans had themselves specified these very crops as the condition of their belief, at 17:90–91:

> *"And they say: We will not believe in thee until thou makest a spring gush forth for us from the earth, or until thou hast a garden of date palms and grapevines, and makest rivers to gush forth in its midst abundantly."* (Qur'an 17:90–91)

The parable therefore answers a challenge the Quraysh had actually issued. They asked for gardens of palms and vines as proof of prophethood; the chapter gives them a story about a man who had exactly that, and lost it. The thing they demanded as evidence of God's favour turns out to be the thing the chapter identifies as a test.

**"We Made": The Ownership Question Settled in the First Verb**

The verse's grammar makes a theological claim before the story begins: *jaʿalnā* — "We made." The gardens are God's making, given to the man. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī note the significance: everything the man will subsequently boast about is introduced as a divine gift. His error, when it comes, will be to speak of it as an achievement.

This is the structural irony of the whole parable, planted in its first line. The reader is told at the outset who made the gardens; the owner will spend the next ten verses forgetting.

**The Transmitted Identification of the Two Men**

Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī report that the two men were brothers — either two Israelites, or two brothers of the Makhzūm clan in Makkah — one a believer and one a disbeliever. The disbelieving brother invested his entire inheritance in worldly goods; the believing brother gave his away in charity. Some commentators suggest these are the same two men debating resurrection at 37:51–60:

> *"And one of them said: I had a companion who said, 'Art thou among those who affirm the truth — that when we are dead and become dust and bones, we shall be requited?'"* (Qur'an 37:51–53)

The Qur'an itself names neither man, and the commentators present the identifications as reports rather than as facts. What the parable requires is not their identity but their *contrast*: one has everything and no faith; the other has nothing and faith.

**"And Placed Crops Between Them"**

*Wa jaʿalnā baynahumā zarʿā* — "and We placed between them a cultivated field." The commentators explain the arrangement: the two vine-gardens were bordered with palm trees and filled between with grain or other crops. Al-Ṭabarī reads the layout as indicating intensive, well-planned cultivation — the estate was not merely large but efficiently designed.

The detail serves the parable's realism. This is not a mythical paradise but a working agricultural property, the kind any Arabian listener could picture: vines on trellises, palms around the perimeter, field crops in between, water running through. Verse 33 will add the stream. The chapter builds the estate carefully, because the reader must feel its value before he can feel its loss.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:33

> **Both gardens brought forth their produce and failed not in the least, and We made a stream gush forth in their midst.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Failed Not in the Least": The Gardens Did No Wrong**

*Kilatā al-jannatayni ātat ukulahā wa lam taẓlim minhu shayʾan* — "each of the two gardens gave its fruit, and did not wrong him of it in any way." The phrase *lam taẓlim minhu shayʾan* is literally "did not wrong him in it at all," and al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī explain it as meaning the gardens did not fail him — they produced fully, without shortfall, in every season.

The commentators have noted the deliberate use of *ẓulm* here, and the contrast it sets up. The gardens did no wrong. Verse 35 will say of their owner: *wa dakhala jannatahu wa huwa ẓālimun li-nafsihi* — "he entered his garden, wronging himself." The only injustice in the parable is committed by the man against himself, and the verse plants the word two verses early so the reader catches the exchange.

There is a further implication. The gardens' perfect performance removes every excuse. A man whose property underperforms may blame the soil, the weather, the season. This man's estate never failed him once. His ingratitude is therefore not the understandable resentment of a disappointed owner but the arrogance of an untroubled one.

**"We Made a Stream Gush Forth in Their Midst"**

*Wa fajjarnā khilālahumā naharā* — the verb *fajjara* is intensive: to cause to gush, to burst forth. The stream runs *khilālahumā* — between the two gardens, through their midst. Water is the decisive element: in Arabia, land without water is worthless, and land with running water is an estate.

The verse is also the direct answer to the Quraysh's challenge at 17:91 — *"until thou makest rivers to gush forth in its midst."* They asked for exactly this. The parable supplies it, and then shows what happens to the man who has it.

**Prosperity as Divine Action, Not Human Achievement**

Both clauses of the verse have God as their subject: the gardens *gave* (a divinely enabled capacity) and *We made* the stream gush. The commentators — al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī — stress that the parable's whole economic reality is presented as grace. Nothing in verses 32–33 is attributed to the owner's labour, planning, or acumen, though of course such things were involved.

This selective narration is the parable's method. The Qur'an is not denying that the man worked; it is refusing to let his work occupy the foreground, because the foreground is where gratitude or ingratitude happens. The person who sees his property as the fruit of his own effort will boast; the person who sees it as a gift will thank. The verses arrange the facts so that gratitude is the only reasonable response, and then watch the man choose otherwise.

**The Verse in the Chapter's Architecture**

Verses 32–33 correspond exactly to verses 7–8. Verse 7: God adorned the earth as a test. Verse 8: God will make it a barren plain. Verse 32: God made two gardens. Verse 33: they produced perfectly, with a stream running through. And verses 40–42 will bring the barren plain — *ṣaʿīdan zalaqā*, a flattened, slippery plain — and the water sinking beyond reach.

The parable is the general principle of verses 7–8 enacted in a single life. The reader who has understood verses 7–8 can predict the parable's ending; the parable exists so that he *feels* it.

**Why Prosperity Is Harder Than Persecution**

The commentators have long observed the structural point: the chapter places the trial of wealth *after* the trial of persecution, and treats it at greater length. Persecution is visibly dangerous; the believer knows he is in a test. Prosperity is invisibly dangerous; the prosperous man does not know he is in a test at all, because everything is going well.

This is the parable's central insight and the reason it follows verse 28. The Makkans who refused to sit with the poor were not wicked in their own estimation — they were successful, respected, generous by the standards of their society. The parable tells them what success looks like from God's side, and it is not flattering.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:34

> **And he had [abundant] fruit and said unto his companion as he conversed with him, "I am greater than you in wealth and stronger in men."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Thamar: Fruit, and Also Profit**

*Wa kāna lahu thamar* — "and he had fruit." Al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Zamakhsharī note the word's double range: *thamar* means the fruit of trees, and it also means *profit*, *yield*, *accumulated wealth* — including, they specify, gold and silver. The commentators read both senses as intended: the man has agricultural produce and liquid capital.

The word's etymology is worth noting, because it carries the parable's theme. *Thamar* is from *th-m-r*, and a *thamarah* is the fruit at the end of a process — the outcome, the return. Wealth is *thamar* because it is what accumulates at the end of labour. The man's error, which verse 35 will state, is to treat the accumulated end as self-sustaining, as though the process that produced it need never run again.

**"I Am Greater Than You in Wealth"**

*Fa-qāla li-ṣāḥibihi wa huwa yuḥāwiruhū anā aktharu minka mālan* — the boast is delivered in conversation, and the Qur'an specifies the setting: *yuḥāwiruhū*, "while conversing with him." The verb *ḥāwara* means to converse back and forth, to dialogue — the same root as *ḥiwār*, discussion. The commentators note that the boast is not a soliloquy; it is addressed to a specific person, the poor believer, in a face-to-face exchange.

This detail is the parable's most socially precise touch, and it links the story directly to verse 28. The Makkans did not merely hold their wealth privately; they used it to establish rank over the Prophet's ﷺ poorer followers. The rich man in the parable does the same thing. He does not say "I am wealthy"; he says *"I am greater than you"* — the statement is comparative, and its function is to place the other man below him.

**"And Stronger in Men"**

*Wa aʿazzu nafarā* — "and stronger/more honourable in number of men." Al-Zamakhsharī explains *nafar* as children, especially sons, and also aides, retainers, and servants. In a tribal society, the number of men who will fight for you is the measure of your security; sons are both heirs and defenders.

The Qur'an pairs wealth and children as the two markers of worldly status repeatedly, and verse 46 of this chapter will state the pairing as a doctrine:

> *"Wealth and children are the adornment of the life of this world."* (Qur'an 18:46)

But the Qur'an also denies that these markers indicate divine favour — 9:55 and 9:85 warn that God's provision of wealth and children to the hypocrites is not a blessing but a gradual increase in their punishment; 23:55–56 makes the point explicitly:

> *"Do they think that what We supply them with of wealth and children — We are hastening unto them good things? Nay, but they perceive not."* (Qur'an 23:55–56)

And 34:35 records the exact boast in the mouths of other disbelievers: *"We are greater in wealth and children, and we shall not be punished."*

The verse is therefore not describing an unusual man. It is describing the standard human assumption that success is a verdict, and it is placing that assumption in the mouth of a character the chapter is about to ruin.

**The Psychology of the Comparative Boast**

There is a specific mechanism at work here that the parable isolates with unusual clarity. Wealth on its own does not usually produce this kind of speech. Wealth compared with someone else's does. The rich man is not addressing a crowd about his estate; he is addressing *one poorer man* and measuring himself against him.

The commentators have noted that the poor man's presence is what the wealth needs in order to become status. Remove the comparison and the gardens are simply gardens. This is why verse 28's demand — that the poor be removed from the gathering — was so serious: it was a demand for the conditions under which wealth can be experienced as superiority.

The parable's answer is delivered in verses 39–41, and it comes from the same poor man. The comparison the rich man initiated is completed by someone else, and the terms reverse.

**Why the Companion Is Called Ṣāḥib**

The word *ṣāḥib* — companion, associate — is used of the poor man throughout (vv. 34, 37). The commentators note the warmth of the term. He is not a stranger or a beggar; he is a companion, and in the transmitted reports a brother. The boast is therefore delivered inside a relationship of affection and familiarity, which makes it worse rather than better: this is not a man posturing before rivals but a man belittling someone close to him.

The Qur'an's choice to make the exchange a dialogue between intimates is deliberate. Most of the arrogance that damages communities is not the arrogance of enemies but of people who are supposed to be on the same side.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:35

> **And he entered his garden, wronging himself, and said, "I think not that this will ever perish.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Wronging Himself": The Verse's Own Diagnosis**

*Wa dakhala jannatahu wa huwa ẓālimun li-nafsihi* — "and he entered his garden while wronging himself." The phrase *ẓālim li-nafsih* is one of the Qur'an's standard descriptions of the sinner, appearing at 3:117, 10:44, 35:32 and elsewhere, and it makes a specific theological claim: sin is not primarily an offence against a rule but an injury to the sinner.

Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī specify the injury here: the man wrongs himself through pride and through his refusal to acknowledge that his provision comes from God. The commentators connect this back to verse 33's *"and they did not wrong him in any way"* — the gardens were faithful to him; he was unfaithful to himself.

The logic deserves articulation because it is counterintuitive. We ordinarily think of injustice as having a victim *other* than the agent. The Qur'an insists that the primary victim of wrongdoing is the one who commits it, because the wrongdoer damages the only thing he permanently owns — his own soul's relation to its Lord. The man in the garden was not harming the poor companion by boasting; the companion's condition was unchanged. He was degrading himself.

**"I Think Not That This Will Ever Perish"**

*qāla mā aẓunnu an tabīda hādhihi abadan* — the verb *bāda* means to perish utterly, to be annihilated; *abadan* is the same "forever" of verse 3 and verse 20. The man's claim is therefore an absolute: this estate will never, ever cease to exist.

Al-Zamakhsharī names the moral error precisely: *ṭūl al-amal* — "long hope," excessive hope in worldly things. The term describes the condition of a person who lives as though his circumstances were permanent, and who therefore never reckons with their ending. The Qur'an identifies the same illusion at 104:2–3:

> *"Who amasses wealth and counts it over, thinking that his wealth will make him immortal."* (Qur'an 104:2–3)

The commentators contrast the claim with the Qur'an's own statement of universal impermanence at 28:88 — *"everything perishes save His Face"* — and 55:26 — *"all that is upon it passes away."* The man has asserted of a garden what is true only of God.

**The Mechanism of the Illusion**

The parable shows how the illusion forms, and its account is psychologically exact. The man has two gardens. They have never failed him (v. 33). They have a stream running through them that he did not dig. Nothing in his experience has ever corrected the assumption that the arrangement is stable. Induction from unbroken good fortune produces the conviction that the fortune is necessary.

This is precisely the cognitive error the chapter warned against at verses 7–8, and the reason those verses precede the parable. The reader who has been told that the earth's adornment will be made a barren plain watches the garden-owner form the opposite conviction from the same evidence. Both men see the same world; one has been told what it is.

The practical application is direct and applies to every reader in a position of comfort. Stability is not permanence. A career, a marriage, a body, a country, a currency — each can appear, from inside, to be a permanent structure, and each is a temporary arrangement. The discipline of remembering this is not pessimism but accuracy, and the Qur'an supplies it before the loss rather than after.

**"And He Entered His Garden"**

The verse's narrative detail is worth pausing on: he *entered* it. The commentators do not labour the point, but the movement matters. The boast of verse 34 was made in conversation; this one is made inside the property, alone with the evidence. The man walks among his vines and palms, with the stream running beside him, and the sight confirms his conclusion.

The Qur'an frequently places its characters in the setting where their error becomes visible. Pharaoh's boast comes at the height of his power; Qārūn's comes as he goes out among his people in his finery (28:79); this man's comes inside his estate. The setting is not scenery; it is the occasion of the sin.

**The Unspoken Premise**

Everything in the verse rests on an assumption the man does not state: that the garden is *his*. The possessive is in the verse — *jannatahu*, "his garden" — and the pronoun is doing the theological damage. Verse 32 said *"We made for one of them two gardens"*; the man says *"my garden."* The transfer from divine gift to personal possession has been completed silently, and the boast about its permanence follows automatically.

This is why the believer's response in verse 39 will not be an argument about permanence. It will be a formula of attribution: *"mā shāʾ Allāh, lā quwwata illā bi-llāh"* — "what God wills; there is no strength except in God." The correction of the boast is not a better prediction but a corrected grammar.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:36

> **Nor do I think that the Hour is imminent. And if I am brought back to my Lord, I shall surely find something better than this in the Hereafter."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Positions Held at Once**

*Wa mā aẓunnu al-sāʿata qāʾimah* — "and I do not think the Hour will be established." Al-Ṭabarī reads this, together with the conditional that follows, as indicating the man's overall doubt about the Hereafter or his own resurrection.

The verse then records something remarkable: *wa laʾin rudidtu ilā rabbī la-ajidanna khayran minhā munqalabā* — "and if I am returned to my Lord, I shall surely find better than it as a place of return." The man is simultaneously sceptical about the resurrection and confident about his prospects in it.

The commentators — al-Rāzī in particular — explain the psychology. The two positions are not contradictory in the way they first appear; they are two expressions of the same underlying conviction, which is that *his standing is deserved*. Whether or not there is a Hereafter, he reasons, a person of my merit will do well. If there is nothing, I have lost nothing. If there is something, I shall find better than this.

**The Assumption of Merit Transcending Death**

Al-Rāzī's analysis is the sharpest in the commentary tradition: the man believes his wealth and good fortune belong to him *on the basis of his personal merit*, and since he assumes that merit survives death, he expects wealth and children in the Hereafter as well.

The Qur'an records the identical claim elsewhere and answers it:

> *"Hast thou considered him who disbelieves in Our signs and says, 'I shall surely be given wealth and children'? Has he looked into the Unseen, or taken a covenant from the Merciful? Nay! We shall record what he says, and We shall prolong his punishment."* (Qur'an 19:77–79)

The answer is epistemological: the claim requires access to the Unseen or a divine covenant, and the man has neither. His confidence is not knowledge; it is his worldly self-assessment projected forward.

**Why the Projection Is Invalid**

The Qur'an's account of the Hereafter rests on a criterion the man has never consulted. The Scale weighs *deeds*, not assets — and specifically deeds performed in faith:

> *"And the weighing on that Day will be the truth. So those whose scales are heavy — they are the successful. And those whose scales are light — they are the ones who have lost their souls, because they wronged Our signs."* (Qur'an 7:8–9)

> *"And We shall set up the just scales for the Day of Resurrection, and no soul shall be wronged in anything. Though it be the weight of a grain of mustard seed, We shall bring it. And sufficient are We as reckoners."* (Qur'an 21:47)

The man's error is a category mistake. He is applying the metric of this world — accumulated advantage — to a realm measured by an entirely different standard. The parable will make the point concretely: the poor man, who has nothing by the worldly metric, receives in verse 40 the promise of something better.

**"Better Than This": The Comparison That Reverses**

*Munqalab* is the place to which one returns, the destination. The man expects a *better* destination than his gardens. The believer will use the same comparative in verse 40 — *"it may be that my Lord will give me better than your garden"* — and the reversal is the parable's structural hinge.

The commentators (al-Jalālayn, al-Rāzī) note that the believer's "better" is either in this world or in the Hereafter, and usually both: 4:77, 7:169, 12:57, 16:30 all state that the Hereafter is better for the reverent. The man's use of the comparative is not wrong in itself; it is wrong in its *basis*. Both men expect something better. One expects it because of what he has; the other because of who gives it.

**The Scepticism of the Comfortable**

There is a sociological observation embedded in the verse that the commentators have drawn. Doubt about the Hereafter is not distributed randomly; it correlates with comfort. The person whose life is going well has less motivation to believe that the arrangement will be reviewed. The person whose life is going badly has more.

This does not make disbelief a mere function of interest — the Qur'an takes the intellectual content of unbelief seriously and argues with it. But it does mean that the *resistance* to belief is often motivated rather than reasoned, and the parable exhibits the motive in its purest form. The man is not troubled by the evidence for resurrection; he is undisturbed by the possibility, and undisturbed people do not investigate.

Verse 36 is thus a diagnosis as much as a report. It describes a state of mind recognisable in every prosperous society, including our own: a vague agnosticism accompanied by an unexamined confidence that whatever comes next will go well for people like us.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:37

> **His companion said unto him as he conversed with him, "Do you disbelieve in the One Who created you from dust, then from a drop, then fashioned you as a man?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reply Begins With the Man, Not the Garden**

*Qāla lahu ṣāḥibuhu wa huwa yuḥāwiruhū a-kafarta bi-lladhī khalaqaka min turābin thumma min nuṭfatin thumma sawwāka rajulā* — the poor companion's answer does not address the estate at all. It addresses the man's *origin*. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the rhetorical strategy: the argument against pride is not that the property might be lost but that the owner was made from nothing.

The commentators note that the question *a-kafarta* — "do you disbelieve?" — identifies the boast as unbelief rather than mere bad manners. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī read the connection as causal: the man's confidence in his permanence *is* disbelief, because it assigns to a creature an attribute that belongs only to God.

**From Dust**

*min turāb* — the Qur'an states human origin from dust at 3:59, 30:20, 35:11, 40:67, and elsewhere. The commentators read it as establishing human lowliness in relation to God: the substance from which the man was made is the substance under his feet, trodden by his own servants, blown about by the wind in his own garden.

The Qur'an's use of this argument is consistent and it is directed at pride specifically. At 22:5 and 35:11 the stages of human origin are given as an argument for the resurrection; here they are given as an argument against arrogance. The same facts serve both purposes, because both errors rest on the same forgetting.

**Then From a Drop**

*thumma min nuṭfah* — the *nuṭfah* is the drop of fluid, the seminal emission. The Qur'an returns to this image constantly: 16:4, 23:13–14, 36:77, 75:37, 76:2, 80:19. At 75:37 the argument is explicit:

> *"Was he not a drop of fluid emitted, then a clot, then He created and fashioned?"* (Qur'an 75:37–38)

And at 16:4: *"He created man from a drop of fluid, and behold, he is an open disputant."* The juxtaposition in that verse is the whole of the Qur'an's moral psychology in a single line: from a drop, to a disputant. The distance between the origin and the posture is the measure of the forgetting.

**"Then He Fashioned You as a Man"**

*thumma sawwāka rajulā* — al-Zamakhsharī explains *sawwāka* as God's having shaped him into a mature individual; al-Rāzī adds that the shaping includes the endowment of intelligence and *taklīf* — moral and legal responsibility. The verse's three stages are therefore not merely biological but normative: dust (the substance), a drop (the beginning of life), a man (a responsible agent).

The final stage is the one that undoes the boast. The man was *made into a person accountable for his choices* — which means that his wealth is not merely a possession but a trust, and that his boast is not merely an opinion but an act for which he will answer. Al-Rāzī's insistence on *taklīf* here is precisely to the point: the argument is not "you are small" but "you are answerable."

**Why This Argument Rather Than Another**

The companion could have argued about the impermanence of property, the injustice of pride, or the reality of the Hereafter. He chose the argument from origin, and the choice is instructive. Arguments about the future can be debated; the argument from origin cannot, because the man's own body is the evidence. He is made of dust; he began as a drop; he is now a man. No premise needs to be granted.

This is the Qur'an's characteristic method, and it recurs throughout: when the claim to be answered is pride, the answer is a description of the claimant. The man who says "I am greater than you in wealth" is answered with an account of what he is apart from wealth — and the account reduces him to the same dust as the man he is addressing.

There is a social equality built into the argument that the parable needs. In a society organised by lineage and property, the statement that every human being originates identically is a level claim of enormous force. The rich man and the poor man are, in the verse's terms, the same creature at different stations of a temporary arrangement.

**The Companion's Standing**

It is worth noting who delivers this argument. He is the man with nothing — the one who was asked to leave the gathering in verse 28's occasion, the one being told in verse 34 that he is lesser in wealth and men. And he is the one who knows the truth and can state it.

The chapter's ordering is deliberate. The poor man has already been described in verse 28 as one who *"calls upon his Lord morning and evening, desiring His Face"* — and now he demonstrates what that constancy has produced: not resentment, but clarity. He is not arguing from envy; he has nothing to gain by the garden's destruction, and verse 40 will show that he does not desire it. He is arguing from knowledge of God.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:38

> **But He is God, my Lord, and I ascribe none as partner unto my Lord."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Companion's Confession in Three Clauses**

*Lākinna huwa Allāhu rabbī wa lā ushriku bi-rabbī aḥadā* — "But as for me: He is God, my Lord, and I do not associate anyone with my Lord." The verse moves from argument to testimony. Having established the man's origin (v. 37), the companion states his own position, and the position is not a counter-claim about wealth but a statement of faith.

The structure is worth examining. *Huwa Allāh* — "He is God": the affirmation of divinity. *Rabbī* — "my Lord": the affirmation of a personal relationship of lordship and sustenance. *Wa lā ushriku bi-rabbī aḥadā* — "and I associate none with my Lord": the negation that completes the testimony. This is the *shahādah* in compressed form.

**"My Lord": The Possessive That Matters**

The commentators note the shift from *Allāh* to *rabbī* — from the Name of divinity in the absolute to "my Lord" in the first person. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī read the possessive as the companion's acknowledgment that all poverty and wealth come from God, with the practical consequence — al-Rāzī's point — that he is grateful in plenty and patient in adversity.

The contrast with verse 35 is exact and total. The rich man said *"his garden"* (*jannatahu*) — claiming ownership of the gift. The poor man says *"my Lord"* (*rabbī*) — claiming ownership by nothing, and belonging entirely. The two possessives are the parable's moral centre. One man owns a garden and has no Lord; the other has a Lord and owns nothing.

**"I Associate None With My Lord": The Shirk That Is Not Idolatry**

The companion's denial of *shirk* is striking in context. Neither man is worshipping a statue. What the companion is denying is the subtler form of association that the rich man has just committed: treating a creature — one's own wealth, one's own merit, one's own permanence — as though it occupied the place of God.

The Islamic tradition classifies this as *shirk khafī*, hidden shirk, and the ḥadīth literature is explicit about its prevalence. Al-Tirmidhī records from Maḥmūd ibn Labīd:

> *"The thing I fear most for you is the lesser shirk." They said, "What is the lesser shirk, O Messenger of God?" He said, "Ostentation (riyāʾ)."* (al-Tirmidhī; graded ḥasan)

And Ibn Mājah (4202) records from Abū Saʿīd that the Prophet ﷺ said: *"Shirk in this community is more hidden than the creeping of an ant."* When asked how to guard against it, he taught: *"O God, I take refuge in Thee from associating anything with Thee knowingly, and I ask Thy forgiveness for what I do unknowingly."*

The companion's statement is therefore not a claim of ritual purity but a statement of orientation: whatever God gives me, I do not make it a rival to Him. This is the exact opposite of the rich man's posture, and the parable sets the two confessions side by side so the reader can hear the difference.

**Gratitude as the Antidote to Boasting**

Al-Rāzī's gloss supplies the practical content of the confession: the man who knows that wealth and poverty both come from God is *grateful in plenty and patient in adversity*. This is the disposition the chapter is prescribing, and it is available at every level of material circumstance.

The mechanism is clear. Boasting requires the belief that one's advantages are one's own achievement; despair requires the belief that one's disadvantages are one's own failure. Both are errors about attribution, and both are cured by the same correction. The companion has neither boast nor complaint because he has attributed everything correctly — which is why he can speak calmly to a man who has just insulted him.

**Why the Confession Comes Before the Warning**

The order of the companion's speech is instructive: verse 37 argues, verse 38 confesses, verse 39 prescribes the formula, verses 40–41 warn. The confession comes *before* the warning, and the reason matters. A warning delivered by an enemy is received as hostility; a warning delivered by someone who has first stated his own faith is received as counsel.

The companion does not begin by predicting the garden's destruction. He begins by saying who his Lord is. Only after establishing that he speaks from conviction rather than resentment does he tell the other man what to say on entering his garden (v. 39), and only then what may happen to it (vv. 40–41).

This is a model of religious counsel that the chapter clearly intends to be generalisable. The person who wishes to warn another about the danger of his attachments should first make clear that his own life is ordered differently — not better in wealth, but differently in orientation. Otherwise the warning will be read, accurately, as the complaint of the have-not.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:39

> **When you entered your garden, why did you not say, '[This is] as God wills. There is no strength save in God.' If you see that I am less than you in wealth and children,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Correction Is a Sentence, Not an Argument**

*Wa law lā idh dakhalta jannataka qulta mā shāʾa Allāh lā quwwata illā bi-llāh* — "If only, when you entered your garden, you had said, 'What God wills! There is no strength except in God.'" The companion does not continue to argue. He prescribes a formula.

The commentators note the shift: verses 37–38 reasoned from creation and confessed faith; verse 39 gives the man something to *say*. The practical wisdom here is considerable. A person in the grip of pride is rarely argued out of it; but he can be given a sentence that, if he says it, reorganises his understanding from the inside. The formula is a spiritual instrument, not a proposition to be evaluated.

**Mā Shāʾa Allāh**

The phrase — "what God wills," "as God wills" — is, as Ibn Kathīr notes, the source of the Muslim custom of uttering it whenever one is pleased by good fortune. The Qur'an grounds it at 7:188:

> *"Say: I have no power over benefit or harm to myself, save as God wills."* (Qur'an 7:188; see also 10:49)

The phrase performs a single operation: it attributes the thing being admired to God's will rather than to the admirer's achievement. Said on entering one's garden, it converts a moment of possession into a moment of acknowledgment. The garden is the same; the owner's relation to it is entirely different.

This is why the companion specifies the *moment*: "when you entered your garden." The formula is not a general belief to be held but a sentence to be said at the point of maximum danger — the point at which the beauty of the thing is most likely to produce the boast. The Qur'an's spiritual prescriptions are consistently located at the moment of temptation rather than in the abstract.

**Lā Quwwata Illā bi-llāh**

"There is no strength except in God." Al-Rāzī explains it as the acknowledgment that no individual can accomplish anything without God's help. The fuller formula current in Muslim speech is *lā ḥawla wa lā quwwata illā bi-llāh* — "there is no turning and no strength except in God" — and Ibn Kathīr records the Prophetic report, transmitted in al-Bukhārī (6409) and Muslim (2704) from Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī, that the Prophet ﷺ called it:

> *"A treasure from the treasures of the Garden."* (al-Bukhārī 6409; Muslim 2704)

Al-Bukhārī (7386) and Muslim (2706) record another formulation: *"Be mindful of God and He will be mindful of you... Know that if the whole community gathered to benefit you, they could not benefit you except with what God has written for you."*

The commentators note that the phrase is uttered both in gratitude for good and in the face of difficulty — the two situations in which a person is most likely to overestimate his own agency. In the first he attributes success to himself; in the second he despairs as though the matter were entirely in human hands. The phrase corrects both.

**The Two Phrases as a Complete Theology of Possession**

Taken together the two clauses cover the whole field. *Mā shāʾa Allāh* addresses the *existence* of the thing: it is here because God willed it. *Lā quwwata illā bi-llāh* addresses the *maintenance* of the thing: it continues to function because God empowers it. The first answers "where did this come from?"; the second answers "what keeps it going?"

The rich man's boast in verse 35 — "I think not that this will ever perish" — is precisely a failure on the second question. He believed the garden's continuation was guaranteed by its own existence. The formula denies this: there is no strength, no operative power, except in God. A garden that is not being held in being by God every moment is already gone.

**"If You See That I Am Less Than You in Wealth and Children"**

*Fa-in tara-ni anā aqalla minka mālan wa waladā* — the companion now takes up the comparison the other man began, and the Qur'an's phrasing is careful: *in tarā-nī* — "if you see me." He does not dispute the fact; he grants it for the sake of argument. He is indeed less in wealth and children.

The concession is rhetorically decisive. The companion refuses to argue about the comparison, because the comparison is not where the truth lies. He accepts the premise entirely and then shows that it does not entail the conclusion. This is the most effective form of disagreement: grant everything, and demonstrate that it does not follow.

There is also a humility in the concession that the commentators have valued. The poor man does not protest his dignity, does not demand respect, does not moralise about the insult. He says, in effect: yes, you have more; watch what happens next.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:40

> **it may be that my Lord will give me something better than your garden, and unleash against it a reckoning from the sky, so that it becomes a flattened plain.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"It May Be": The Language of Hope, Not of Malice**

*fa-ʿasā rabbī an yuʾtiyani khayran min jannatika* — "it may be that my Lord will give me better than your garden." The particle *ʿasā* expresses hope or expectation, not certainty and not desire for the other's ruin. Al-Jalālayn and al-Rāzī note that the believer's hope is for something better *for himself*, whether in this world or the Hereafter; the parallel verses are 4:77, 7:169, 12:57, 16:30.

The distinction matters enormously for reading the passage. The poor man is not cursing his brother. He is stating two possibilities, and the first concerns himself: God may give *me* something better. The second — the calamity — follows as a warning, not as a wish. The commentators have drawn the ethical point: religious counsel should begin with what God may do for the counsellor, not with what God may do to the counselled.

There is a further irony the verse contains. The rich man said in verse 36, *"if I am brought back to my Lord, I shall surely find something better than this."* The poor man says, *"it may be that my Lord will give me something better than your garden."* The same comparative — *khayran min* — is used by both, and the chapter awards it to the one who did not claim it as his due.

**"A Reckoning From the Sky"**

*wa yursila ʿalayhā ḥusbānan mina al-samāʾ* — *ḥusbān* is glossed variously. Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, and al-Ṭabarī read it as a punishment from above. Al-Bayḍāwī, al-Rāzī, and al-Zamakhsharī specify thunderbolts — *ṣawāʾiq* — and read the whole as a torrential storm that washes away the trees and crops. The word's root *ḥ-s-b* carries the sense of reckoning, calculation, and also of a shower of stones or arrows; the commentators note that both senses are apt, since the calamity is simultaneously a physical destruction and a settling of accounts.

The thunderbolt appears elsewhere in the Qur'an as an agent of divine punishment and a demonstration of power: 4:153, 13:13, 14:13, 14:17, 51:44. And 2:264–266 gives the parallel parable:

> *"As one of you would not wish to have a garden of date palms and vines, with rivers flowing beneath it, wherein he has all fruits — and old age overtakes him, and he has weak offspring — and a fiery whirlwind strikes it and it burns. Thus God makes clear the signs to you, that you may reflect."* (Qur'an 2:266)

And 68:17–32 tells the story of the owners of the garden who resolved to harvest it at dawn without giving the poor their due, and found it stripped bare by night. The Qur'an has a small genre of garden parables, and this is its fullest treatment.

**"So That It Becomes a Flattened Plain"**

*fa-tuṣbiḥa ṣaʿīdan zalaqā* — *ṣaʿīd* is the bare surface of the earth, the same word as verse 8's *ṣaʿīdan juruzā*; *zalaq* denotes ground so smooth and slippery that the foot cannot hold. The commentators explain it as bare, level earth from which all vegetation has been removed, leaving a surface too slick to walk upon.

The image is the exact inverse of verse 32: two gardens of vines, surrounded by palms, with crops between them, and a stream running through. What remains is a flat, wet, empty plane. The chapter has now completed the movement it announced at verses 7–8 — the adornment made a barren plain — and has done so in the compass of eight verses.

**The Structural Symmetry of the Parable**

The correspondence is precise and worth setting out, because it shows how carefully the parable is constructed. Verse 32: two gardens of vines. Verse 40: a flattened plain. Verse 33: a stream gushing between them. Verse 41: water sinking beyond reach. Verse 33: they failed not in the least. Verse 42: his fruit encompassed by ruin. Verse 34: "I am stronger in men." Verse 43: "he had no party to help him."

Every element of the estate has a corresponding element in its destruction, and every boast has a corresponding reversal. The parable is not a cautionary tale with a moral attached; it is a set of balances, each settled.

**The Speech as Duʿāʾ Rather Than as Curse**

The commentators note that the companion's words are cast in the optative — *ʿasā* — and not as an imprecation. He does not call down destruction on the garden; he expresses the hope that God will give him better and send a reckoning.

The distinction matters and the commentators have drawn it. A curse directs harm at a person; a duʿāʾ of this kind refers the matter to God and leaves the outcome with Him. The companion does not claim to know what the garden owner deserves, and he does not ask for it directly. He states two possibilities — a better gift, or a reckoning — and lets God choose between them.

This is consistent with his earlier speech at verses 37–38, where he affirmed his own faith without asserting superiority. The Qur'an presents him throughout as a man who does not take judgment into his own hands, and verse 40 is the sharpest instance: even when wishing for the ruin of an arrogance that insulted him, he phrases it as hope rather than demand.

The instruction the tradition draws is about the register of the believer's speech toward those who wrong him.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:41

> **Or its water may sink deep, so that you cannot seek after it."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Possibility: The Water Goes**

*Aw yuṣbiḥa māʾuhā ghawran fa-lan tastaṭīʿa lahu ṭalabā* — "or its water may become sunken, and you will not be able to seek it." The word *ghawr* is from *gh-w-r*, to sink, to become deep and unreachable — the same root as *ghār*, the deep hollow, and *ghāʾir*, sunken. The commentators read it as the water table dropping beyond the reach of wells and channels.

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī note the specificity of the addition: *"you will not be able to seek it."* The loss is not merely of the water but of the *capacity to retrieve it*. A garden destroyed by storm can be replanted; a garden whose water has gone is unrecoverable, because the water is not a possession but a condition. Without it nothing can be regrown.

**"You Will Not Be Able": The Removal of Agency**

The phrasing addresses the man's *capability*, not merely his property. *Fa-lan tastaṭīʿa lahu ṭalabā* — "you will never be able to seek it." The *lan* particle negates the future emphatically.
