The man who boasted in verse 34 of being "stronger in men" — of having retainers and sons to do his bidding — is told that his capacity to act will itself be removed.

This is the parable's final reversal, and it is more complete than the loss of the garden. Property can be rebuilt; agency cannot. The verse predicts a state in which the man has hands and money and servants, and nothing that can be done.

**The Two Threats Compared**

The companion offered two possibilities: a calamity from the sky (v. 40) and the withdrawal of the water (v. 41). The first is violent and sudden; the second is quiet and irreversible. The commentators note that both are described as occurring *by morning* — *fa-tuṣbiḥa*, *aw yuṣbiḥa* — so that either would be discovered at daybreak, after a single night.

The pairing covers the full range of how estates fail: catastrophically, by fire and flood; or gradually, by the quiet loss of the condition on which everything rested. The rich man had considered neither. His boast in verse 35 was that the garden would never perish *abadan* — forever — and verse 41 answers with the specific mechanism by which it will perish overnight.

**The Verse as a Statement About Dependency**

The commentators have drawn a general principle from the verse that extends well beyond agriculture. Every estate, in every economy, rests on inputs its owner does not produce. The farmer does not make rain. The merchant does not make the roads, the currency, or the peace that lets his goods travel. The professional does not make his health, his education's infrastructure, or the demand for his skill. In each case the visible asset is the tip of a structure whose foundations are held by someone else.

The Qur'an states the principle directly at 56:63–64:

> *"Have you considered the seed you sow? Is it you who make it grow, or are We the Grower? If We willed, We would make it chaff, and you would not cease to wonder."* (Qur'an 56:63–65)

And at 67:30, which reads as a direct commentary on this verse:

> *"Say: Have you considered — if your water were to sink deep into the earth, who then would bring you flowing water?"* (Qur'an 67:30)

The question is unanswerable, and its unanswerability is the verse's content. The man with two gardens never asked it, and verse 41 is the asking.

**The Practical Discipline the Verse Prescribes**

The tradition has drawn a concrete practice from this material. The believer who sees his property, his family, or his work is instructed to say what verse 39 prescribed — *mā shāʾa Allāh, lā quwwata illā bi-llāh* — precisely at the moment of admiration, because that moment is when the illusion of self-sufficiency forms. The formula is not a superstition appended to pleasure; it is the correction of an inference the mind makes automatically.

The inference is: *this is here, it has been here, therefore it will be here.* The inference is invalid, and verse 41 supplies the counter-instance. The water did not promise to stay. Nothing in the garden's three hundred seasons of perfect yield (v. 33) indicated that the next season would differ. The verse is training the reader to hold his possessions with the grip of a borrower rather than an owner — which is not a lesser enjoyment but a safer one.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:42

> **And his fruit was encompassed. So he began to wring his hands on account of what he had spent on it while it lay in waste upon its trellises, saying, "Would that I had not ascribed anyone as partner unto my Lord!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"His Fruit Was Encompassed": The Prediction Fulfilled in Four Words**

*Wa uḥīṭa bi-thamarihi* — "and his fruit was encompassed." Ibn Kathīr and al-Jalālayn read it as ruin coming upon the whole: either he lost all his wealth, or the garden was destroyed unexpectedly, exactly as the believing companion warned. The verb *uḥīṭa bi-* — "it was surrounded, closed in upon" — is the same verb used at verse 29 of the Fire whose canopies *enclose* its inhabitants (*aḥāṭa bihim surādiquhā*).

The commentators have noted the deliberate echo. The rich man is now *enclosed* by his loss in the way the wrongdoers will be enclosed by the Fire. The image of total surrounding — no exit, no remainder — is applied first to a garden and then to Hell, and the chapter intends the reader to carry the association.

Note what is not described. The Qur'an does not narrate the storm, or the fire, or the sinking of the water. It reports the outcome in a single passive clause and moves immediately to the man's reaction. The parable's interest was never the mechanism of destruction; it was what the destruction does to the man.

**"He Began to Wring His Hands"**

*fa-aṣbaḥa yuqallibu kaffayhi ʿalā mā anfaqa fīhā* — "and he became one who turns his hands over, on account of what he had spent on it." Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr identify the gesture as the classic expression of remorse and regret. The verb *yuqallibu* is intensive and frequentative — not a single clasp but a repeated turning of the palms, the involuntary motion of a person who cannot undo what has happened.

The prepositional phrase tells us what he is grieving over: *ʿalā mā anfaqa fīhā* — "on account of what he had expended upon it." Not on the beauty of the garden, not on the loss of pleasure, not on the humiliation. On the *expenditure*. The commentators have observed the precision: his regret is financial. He is calculating what he put in and what has come out.

This is the parable's final diagnosis of the man, and it is damning in a quiet way. Even his grief is denominated in cost. A man who had loved the garden would grieve its beauty; a man who had loved God would grieve his ingratitude. This man grieves his outlay — which tells the reader what the garden had always been to him.

**"While It Lay in Waste Upon Its Trellises"**

*Wa hiya khāwiyatun ʿalā ʿurūshihā* — "and it [the garden] was fallen in upon its trellises." *Khāwiyah* denotes a structure collapsed and hollow, fallen in on itself; *ʿurūsh* are the trellises or arbours on which vines are trained. The commentators describe the scene: the vines still hanging on the frames, but the frames collapsed, the whole structure caved in.

The image is deliberately specific and deliberately poignant. It is not a scorched field but a *ruined garden* — the form still visible, the substance gone. The trellises stand because the man built them; the vines lie because God withdrew what held them. The scene displays, in physical form, the exact distinction the man had refused to acknowledge: his contribution and God's contribution, and which of them was load-bearing.

**"Would That I Had Not Ascribed Anyone as Partner Unto My Lord!"**

*Wa yaqūlu yā laytanī lam ushrik bi-rabbī aḥadā* — the exclamation *yā laytanī* is the Arabic formula of anguished wish: "oh, would that I..." The commentators note the content of the wish with care. He does not say "would that I had not lost my garden." He does not say "would that the storm had not come." He says: *would that I had not committed shirk.*

This is the parable's turning point and its most important line. The man has, at the moment of total loss, correctly identified what the actual disaster was — and it was not the destruction of the garden. The garden was decoration (v. 7); its loss is a misfortune. The *shirk* — the assignment to himself of what belongs to God — was the catastrophe, and he names it.

Al-Zamakhsharī and Ibn Kathīr note the connection to verse 38, where the believing companion said *"I do not associate anyone with my Lord."* The two men used the same phrase. One said it as a confession while he had nothing; the other says it as a regret when he has lost everything. The chapter's argument is that the first was in time and the second was not.

**Why the Repentance Comes Too Late**

The verse does not say the man became a believer, and the commentators differ on his final state. What the verse shows is *recognition* without *recovery*. He sees the truth clearly and can do nothing with it — which is the Qur'an's standard description of the state of the disbeliever at the moment of death:

> *"And repentance is not for those who go on doing evil deeds, until when death comes to one of them, he says, 'Now I repent'; nor for those who die while they are disbelievers."* (Qur'an 4:18)

The practical instruction is the sharpest in the chapter. Insight that arrives after the loss has no salvific value. The man's understanding was available to him in verse 39, when his companion handed him the formula to say on entering the garden; he did not say it. Verse 42 shows him saying something very like it, when the garden is already on the ground.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:43

> **And he had no party to help him apart from God; nor could he help himself.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Boast of Verse 34, Answered**

*Wa lam takun lahu fiʾatun yanṣurūnahu min dūni Allāh* — "and he had no band to help him apart from God." The word *fiʾah* denotes a party, a band, a group of supporters. The verse answers verse 34 directly: the man who boasted *"I am stronger in men"* — stronger in sons, retainers, and servants — finds himself with no party at all.

The commentators note the completeness of the reversal. He had *nafar* — men, numbers. He had no *fiʾah* — no party willing or able to act. The distinction is instructive: the people around a wealthy man in prosperity are numerous; the people who can help him in ruin are none. Retainers are attached to the estate, not to the man, and when the estate goes they go with it.

Ibn Kathīr connects the verse to 28:81, the end of the story of Qārūn:

> *"So We caused the earth to swallow him and his dwelling. And he had no party to help him apart from God, nor was he of those who defend themselves."* (Qur'an 28:81)

The phrasing is nearly identical, and the Qur'an's repetition of it marks it as a formula: this is what happens to wealth that has been made into a rival of God.

**"Nor Could He Help Himself"**

*Wa mā kāna muntaṣirā* — "and he was not one who could defend himself." The active participle *muntaṣir* denotes one who obtains victory or aid for himself. The verse closes both avenues: no external help, and no internal capacity.

This is the fulfilment of verse 41's prediction — *"you will not be able to seek it."* The man stands in the ruin of his estate with his intelligence, his experience, and his money entirely intact, and there is nothing that can be done. The commentators have noted the theological point: the withdrawal of divine support leaves the agent with all his faculties and none of his power.

**The Alternative Punctuation and What It Changes**

Ibn Kathīr records that verses 43–44 can be punctuated differently, so that *"there"* (*hunālika*) attaches to the preceding sentence rather than the following one: *"And he had no party to help him apart from God, nor could he help himself — there. Protection belongs to God..."* On this reading, *hunālika* describes the place and moment of the man's helplessness, in the ruined garden.

The alternative does not alter the sense materially, but it sharpens the scene: the helplessness is located *there*, at the trellises, in the specific place where the boast was made. The commentators preserve both readings, and the ambiguity is itself characteristic of the Qur'anic text, whose unvoweled consonantal skeleton permits more than one legitimate pointing.

**"Apart From God": The Qualification That Reads as Irony**

*Min dūni Allāh* — "apart from God." The phrase is the Qur'an's standard formula for false reliance, and its appearance here is bitterly apt. The man had, in effect, lived as though he had a party apart from God — his sons, his servants, his estate, his own foresight. Verse 43 reports that the party was never real.

The Qur'an states the principle repeatedly: 2:107, 2:120, 4:123, 4:173, 6:70 — there is no protector other than God. The parable's function is not to state the principle but to exhibit a man discovering it.

**The Social Anatomy of a Vanished Party**

The commentators have observed that the verse describes a real and recurring social phenomenon rather than a mere narrative reversal. A wealthy man's entourage is attached to the wealth. The servants, the clients, the flatterers, the relatives who visit, the partners who propose ventures — each is oriented toward the estate rather than toward the man, and each recalculates the moment the estate fails. The disappearance is not treachery in the ordinary sense; it is the ordinary operation of a relationship whose basis was never the person.

This is why the Qur'an's account of friendship is so severe about its criteria. At 43:67 it states:

> *"Friends on that Day will be enemies to one another, save the reverent."* (Qur'an 43:67)

The exception is the friendships formed on *taqwā* — because those were never formed on advantage and therefore have nothing to dissolve when advantage goes. The garden owner had *nafar*, men; he had no *fiʾah*, party. The distinction the verse draws is between the two.

**The Two Helplessnesses**

Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī note that the verse denies help from two directions, and the double denial is complete. *Wa lam takun lahu fiʾatun yanṣurūnahu min dūni Allāh* — no external party. *Wa mā kāna muntaṣirā* — no internal capacity. A person in ruin may still be rescued by others; or, if abandoned, may still rescue himself. The verse removes both.

The commentators connect this to the Qur'an's account of what remains when God's support is withdrawn. At 17:75 the Qur'an says of the Prophet ﷺ: *"Then you would find no helper against Us"* — and the implication throughout is that human capability is itself a sustained gift. The man standing in his collapsed garden has his intelligence, his experience, and his money, and none of them operates, because the thing that made them effective was never his.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:44

> **There, protection belongs to God, the Real. He is best in reward and best in requital.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Hunālika: The Place Where the Truth Becomes Visible**

*Hunālika al-walāyatu li-llāhi al-ḥaqq* — "There, protection belongs to God, the Real." The adverb *hunālika* — "there, at that place and time" — locates the statement. Al-Bayḍāwī and Ibn Kathīr note that on one reading it belongs with verse 43; on the standard reading it opens verse 44 and means: *in that situation*, at the moment of total ruin, it becomes evident that protection is God's alone.

The commentators draw the general principle: certain truths are only apprehended at the point where all alternatives have failed. A person surrounded by options can argue about whether God is necessary; a person standing in a collapsed garden cannot. The verse is not saying that protection *begins* there — it was always God's — but that its exclusivity *becomes manifest* there.

**Walāyah and Wilāyah: The Two Readings of a Single Word**

Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī record the grammatical dispute. The word is written identically in the unvoweled text and can be vocalised either *walāyah* or *wilāyah*.

*Walāyah* denotes closeness, nearness, friendship, attachment, and protection — the state of being a *walī*, one who is near and who protects. On this reading: "there, friendship and protection belong to God."

*Wilāyah* denotes authority — specifically authority derived from relationship to a higher authority. In political usage it is the authority of a governor appointed by the caliph; in Sufi discourse it names the spiritual authority of the saints, derived from their nearness to God, and gives its name to the *awliyāʾ* and to the doctrine of *walāyah* discussed at verses 9 and 12. On this reading: "there, authority belongs to God."

The commentators note that the two meanings overlap in ordinary usage, and the Qur'anic text's refusal to disambiguate allows both. The combined sense is complete: at the moment of ruin, both the protection and the authority belong to God alone.

**Al-Ḥaqq: The Real**

*Al-ḥaqq* is one of the divine Names, and al-Bayḍāwī records the alternative reading in which it functions as an adjective describing the protection: "true protection belongs to God." Both readings are sound, and the Name is among the ninety-nine, favoured especially in philosophical and mystical writing.

The Name is precisely apt here. *Ḥaqq* denotes what is real, true, fixed, and deserving — the opposite of *bāṭil*, which is void, false, and insubstantial. The rich man's estate was *bāṭil* in the Qur'an's sense: it looked substantial and was not. The verse names God as *the Real* at the exact point where the unreal has collapsed, and the naming is the parable's conclusion.

The Qur'an uses the Name elsewhere in the same function:

> *"That is because God is the Real, and that what they call upon apart from Him is the false, and that God is the Exalted, the Great."* (Qur'an 22:62)

**"Best in Reward and Best in Requital"**

*Huwa khayrun thawāban wa khayrun ʿuqubā* — *thawāb* is reward, recompense for good; *ʿuqubā* is the issue or consequence, here punitive. The commentators note the symmetry: God is best in both. The verse ends the parable not with the ruin but with the character of the One who is left standing after it.

The point is theologically necessary. A parable ending with the destruction of a garden could be read as a lesson in fatalism — everything perishes, so nothing matters. The verse forecloses this: the One who remains is *best in reward*, which means that what was done for Him is not lost (v. 30: *"We neglect not the reward of one who is virtuous in deed"*). And He is *best in requital*, which means the boast of verse 35 has an accounting.

**The Parable's Conclusion, Stated**

Read as a unit, verses 42–44 deliver the parable's argument in three movements: the loss (v. 42), the discovery of helplessness (v. 43), and the identification of what is real (v. 44). The rich man's error was not that he loved his garden — the Qur'an does not condemn enjoyment of property — but that he treated it as real in the sense reserved for God. Verse 44 gives the Name that settles the matter.

**Why Protection Is Located in a Place**

The commentators note that the verse locates protection in *hunālika* — "there, in that place, at that time" — rather than in a person or a moment. The word is spatial and temporal at once, and the commentators have read it as identifying a specific juncture: the moment when the garden is gone and there is nothing left to appeal to except God.

The placement is the point. Protection is not generally available on the person's own terms; it is available at the point where his own resources are exhausted. The garden owner reached that point in verse 42, and the verse describes what he found there.

The commentators have drawn the general lesson that most people discover God's protection precisely where their own arrangements fail, and that the failure is therefore not an obstacle to faith but the condition under which it becomes possible. The man who never runs out of options never needs protection, and never discovers it.

This is the parable's structural function in the chapter: it is the story of a man arriving at *hunālika*.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:45

> **Set forth for them a parable of the life of this world: [It is] like water We send down from the sky. Then it mixes with the vegetation of the earth. Then it becomes chaff, scattered by the winds. And God is capable of all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Parable of the Parable**

*Wa-ḍrib lahum mathala al-ḥayāti al-dunyā* — "and set forth for them a parable of the life of this world." After thirteen verses of narrative, the chapter compresses its whole teaching into four images. The commentators — al-Zamakhsharī, Ibn Kathīr — note that this verse recapitulates the moral of vv. 32–44 in universal form: what was true of one man's garden is true of the entire world.

**Water From the Sky**

*Kamāʾin anzalnāhu mina al-samāʾ* — "like water We send down from the sky." The commentators note the theological load carried by the first image: the life of this world is *dependent on divine beneficence*, and the dependence is total. Nothing in the sequence begins from within the world; the water comes from outside it.

This is the Qur'an's standard argument for providence, and it recurs at 39:21 and 57:20, both of which the commentators cite as parallels:

> *"Hast thou not seen that God sends down water from the sky, and leads it through springs in the earth, then brings forth thereby crops of varying colours? Then they wither and thou seest them turning yellow; then He makes them chaff. Truly in that is a reminder for those who possess hearts."* (Qur'an 39:21)

> *"Know that the life of this world is but play and diversion, adornment and boasting among you, and rivalry in increase of wealth and children — like the similitude of rain whose vegetation pleases the disbelievers; then it withers and thou seest it turning yellow; then it becomes chaff."* (Qur'an 57:20)

**It Mixes With the Vegetation of the Earth**

*Fa-khtalaṭa bihi nabātu al-arḍ* — "and the vegetation of the earth mingles with it." The verb *ikhtalaṭa* denotes intermingling, interpenetration. The commentators explain the image: the water enters the soil and the plants take it up, so that water and vegetation become a single process that cannot be separated.

The image is more subtle than it appears. It describes the moment of maximum flourishing — when the garden is at its greenest, when the water and the growth are indistinguishable, when everything is working. This is the state the rich man mistook for permanence. The verse locates the peak of beauty and immediately moves past it.

**Then It Becomes Chaff, Scattered by the Winds**

*Fa-aṣbaḥa hashīman tadhrūhu al-riyāḥ* — *hashīm* is dry, crumbled plant matter — chaff, stubble, the residue after the substance has gone; *tadhrūhu* means the winds scatter and disperse it. The commentators note the two-stage ending: first the plant dries and crumbles in place, then the wind removes even the residue.

The sequence is complete: water descends, growth flourishes, growth crumbles, residue is blown away. There is no stage at which the process can be arrested, and no stage that lasts. The verse's grammar reinforces this — each stage is introduced by *fa-*, the particle of immediate sequence, so that the flourishing and the scattering are presented as continuous rather than as separated by a long interval.

The chapter has now used the same image three times at different scales: v. 8 (the whole earth made a barren plain), v. 40 (the garden made a flattened plain), v. 45 (all vegetation made chaff). The parable of the two men is not a special case; it is the general law shown at one address.

**"And God Is Capable of All Things"**

*Wa kāna Allāhu ʿalā kulli shayʾin muqtadirā* — the verse closes by returning agency to God. The commentators note the placement: after describing a sequence that appears entirely natural — rain, growth, decay, wind — the verse asserts divine capability over all of it. The natural process is not autonomous; it is the mode of God's action.

The word *muqtadir* is intensive: not merely *qādir*, able, but *muqtadir*, fully and effectively powerful. The Qur'an uses it of God's power over the specific things human beings find hardest to accept — the resurrection, the replacement of one people by another (18:45 and 7:188 among others).

The closing assertion answers the question the parable raises. If the world is water and chaff, what is real? Verse 44 answered: God, *al-Ḥaqq*, the Real. Verse 45 adds: and His capability extends over every stage of the process, including the scattering.

**Ḥaṭīman and the Three Stages**

The commentators note that the verse describes a three-stage dissolution, and that each stage is a distinct word rather than a restatement. The vegetation mixes with the earth (*fākhtalaṭa bihi nabātu al-arḍ*); it becomes *ḥaṭīmā* — dry, broken stubble; the winds scatter it.

The word *ḥaṭīm* is specific. It is not merely dry but crushed and broken, the residue of a plant that has been threshed. The commentators note that the sequence moves from integration to fragility to dispersal, and that no stage is recoverable. Once the plant is stubble it cannot be replanted; once scattered it cannot be gathered.

The image is drawn from agriculture because agriculture is the clearest available case of something that grows, matures, and is then destroyed without malice. The farmer does not hate his crop; the season simply ends. The commentators have read the parable as making exactly that point about the world — its passing is not a punishment but a property.

The verse closes with *"and God is capable of all things,"* which the commentators read as the answer to any inference of loss: the One who scatters can restore, and the parable of dissolution is followed in verses 107–108 by its counterpart.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:46

> **Wealth and children are the adornment of the life of this world, but that which endures—righteous deeds—are better in reward with thy Lord, and better [as a source of] hope.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter's Thesis in One Verse**

*Al-mālu wa'l-banūna zīnatu al-ḥayāti al-dunyā* — "wealth and children are the adornment of the life of this world." The word *zīnah* returns from verse 7, closing the frame the chapter opened. Verse 7 said God made the earth's contents an adornment as a test; verse 46 names the two chief adornments specifically and states what is better.

The commentators note that wealth and children are singled out because they are the two sources of status and security in human societies — wealth for the first, children for the second. In a tribal Arabian context the pairing was exhaustive: property and lineage were the whole of a man's standing. The rich man of the parable boasted of exactly these two in verse 34: *"I am greater than you in wealth and stronger in men."*

The Qur'an's treatment of the pair is consistent and double-edged. They are genuine blessings and genuine adornments — the verse does not disparage them. But they are not indications of divine favour (9:55, 9:85, 23:55–56), they cannot protect against punishment (3:10, 3:116, 9:69, 26:88), and like every other adornment they pass away (vv. 7–8). At 63:9 the Qur'an issues its sharpest warning:

> *"O you who believe! Let not your wealth or your children divert you from the remembrance of God. And whoever does that — they are the losers."* (Qur'an 63:9)

**"But That Which Endures — Righteous Deeds"**

*Wa al-bāqiyātu al-ṣāliḥāt* — "the enduring righteous deeds." The commentators identify these as the good deeds that persist in their reward, and the tradition has gathered specific lists. The most famous is the report in Muslim (2695) and al-Tirmidhī:

> *"Two phrases light on the tongue, heavy in the Scale, beloved to the Merciful: 'Glory be to God and His praise; glory be to God, the Tremendous.'"* (al-Bukhārī 6682; Muslim 2694)

And the tradition transmitted from the Companions identifying *al-bāqiyāt al-ṣāliḥāt* as *subḥāna Allāh, wa'l-ḥamdu lillāh, wa lā ilāha illā Allāh, wa Allāhu akbar* — the four phrases of glorification. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī report this reading alongside the general one.

The parallel verse is 19:76, which uses nearly identical language:

> *"And God increases in guidance those who are guided. And the enduring righteous deeds are better with thy Lord in reward, and better in recourse."* (Qur'an 19:76)

**"Better in Reward, and Better as a Hope"**

*Khayrun ʿinda rabbika thawāban wa khayrun amalā* — the double comparative is significant, and the commentators explain both terms. Better *in reward*: the return is greater. Better *as a hope* (or *in expectation*, *amal*): the thing one can actually rely upon.

The second comparative is the more searching. Human beings do not merely desire reward; they *hope* — they orient their lives around expectations. The verse asks which of the two is a sounder basis for hope. Wealth as a basis for hope is what the rich man of the parable had, and verse 42 showed what happened to it. Righteous deeds as a basis for hope are guaranteed by verse 30: *"We neglect not the reward of one who is virtuous in deed."*

There is a practical dimension here that the chapter has been building toward. Hope is not a mood; it is an investment strategy. Whatever a person places his hope in determines where he puts his attention, his time, and his resources. The verse is asking the reader to audit his own hopes — to notice which of the two categories his actual expectations belong to.

**The Verse as the Parable's Verdict**

Read after verses 32–44, verse 46 is the judgment on the two men. The rich man had the adornment in full measure and was ruined. The poor companion had none of it and was vindicated — promised in verse 40 something better than the garden. The chapter has now demonstrated its thesis narratively and states it doctrinally.

The ordering of the chapter's teaching is worth noting. Verse 7: adornment as test. Verses 32–44: the test failed by one man and passed by another. Verse 45: the adornment as water and chaff. Verse 46: the adornment named, and the enduring thing named against it. The reader arrives at the doctrine only after living through the story — which is why the doctrine sticks.

**Why This Comes Before the Eschatological Section**

Verse 46 closes the chapter's second major unit, and verses 47–53 open the third: the Day of Judgment, the mountains moved, the Book set down, the scene of the guilty reading their record. The connection is causal. If the adornment is what perishes and the enduring deeds are what remain, then the Day on which the deeds are reckoned is the only occasion that matters — and the chapter proceeds immediately to describe it.

**Al-Bāqiyāt al-Ṣāliḥāt: What the Tradition Identifies**

The commentators record several identifications of *"that which endures — righteous deeds."* The most widely transmitted is the report from the Prophet ﷺ recorded by Aḥmad and others that they are *subḥāna Allāh, wa al-ḥamduli Allāh, wa lā ilāha illa Allāh, wa Allāhu akbar, wa lā ḥawla wa lā quwwata illā bi Allāh* — the phrases of remembrance.

Other commentators read the phrase more broadly as every act performed for God's sake. The two readings are not in competition: the phrases of remembrance are the paradigm case of a deed that costs nothing, is available to everyone, and endures, and they are cited because they exemplify the category.

The commentators have drawn the practical point that the enduring deeds are not necessarily the impressive ones. The person who cannot give wealth can say *subḥāna Allāh*, and the verse classifies that with the deeds that outlast everything else.

**The Verse's Two Weights**

The commentators note the verse's final clause: *"better with thy Lord in reward, and better in hope."* Two superlatives are used, and they are different — reward is what is given, hope is what is relied upon while waiting.

The distinction addresses two distinct needs. The person who asks what he will receive is answered by *thawāb*; the person who asks what he can hold onto in the meantime is answered by *amal*. The commentators have observed that the second is often the harder question, since the interval between the deed and the reward is where faith is tested.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:47

> **On the Day We will set the mountains in motion, and you will see the earth an open plain. And We will gather them, and leave not one of them behind.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Removal of the Earth's Fixed Points**

*Wa yawma nusayyiru al-jibāla wa tarā al-arḍa bārizah* — "and on the Day We cause the mountains to move, and you will see the earth exposed." The mountains are the Qur'an's standing image of stability. At 16:19, 21:31, and 31:10 they are described as placed upon the earth *"lest it shake with you"*; at 78:7 they are called *awtād*, pegs or stakes, in the sense of the stakes that hold a tent. They are the fixed points of the terrestrial order, the landmarks by which travellers navigate and the symbol of permanence in every language of the region.

The Day begins with their removal. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī read *nusayyir* as their being taken from the earth; al-Rāzī reads it as their being rendered non-existent; al-Qurṭubī reads it with 27:88 as their being lifted and passing through the air like clouds. The Qur'an's descriptions elsewhere are equally varied and equally total: scattered (20:105; 77:10), pulverised to powder (56:5), ground up (69:14), like heaps of shifting sand (73:14), like carded wool (70:9; 101:5).

The theological point is not geological but symbolic, and the commentators make it: the removal of the mountains is the removal of everything that appeared immovable. The reader who has just been shown a garden turned to a flattened plain (vv. 40–42) and all vegetation turned to chaff (v. 45) now sees the same operation performed on the earth's own frame. The chapter is systematic: first the decoration, then the substrate.

**"You Will See the Earth an Open Plain"**

*Wa tarā al-arḍa bārizah* — *bārizah* comes from a root meaning to emerge, to come into clear view. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and al-Ṭabarī explain that with the mountains, the trees, and all human constructions removed, the earth becomes an unobstructed plain in which nothing hides anything. Al-Rāzī adds a further reading: the emergence refers to the dead coming out of their graves for the gathering.

The image is one of total exposure, and it is the eschatological counterpart to the cave. The sleepers were hidden in a mountain, sealed behind a wall, unknown for three centuries — and God saw them (v. 26: *"how well He sees"*). On this Day there will be no mountain, no cave, no wall. Verse 47 removes the possibility of concealment that the whole first narrative turned upon.

**"We Will Gather Them, and Leave Not One Behind"**

*Wa ḥasharnāhum fa-lam nughādir minhum aḥadā* — the verb *ḥashara* denotes gathering, mustering, assembling for a purpose; *nughādir* — from *gh-d-r*, to leave behind, to abandon — is negated absolutely: not one of them will be left.

The commentators note the significance of the double negation: not only will all be gathered, but *no exception* will be made. This answers a specific human anxiety and a specific human hope. The anxiety: that one might be overlooked in the vastness of the resurrection — that the unnamed, the forgotten, the unburied, the lost at sea will somehow be missed. The hope: that one might slip through. Verse 47 forecloses both.

Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr connect the verse to the Qur'an's repeated assurance of the completeness of the gathering:

> *"That is a gathering on an appointed Day, and We were never unaware of it."*

And 21:104: *"On the Day We roll up the heaven as a scroll is rolled up, as We began the first creation, so We shall repeat it."*

**Why the Verse Follows the Parable**

The placement is deliberate and its logic is clear. Verse 46 has just stated that wealth and children are the adornment and that enduring deeds are better. Verse 47 opens the scene where the comparison is settled. The man who boasted of being "stronger in men" (v. 34) will be gathered with everyone else, and his *nafar* will not be with him. The poor companion, whom he belittled, will be gathered with him, and the distinction between them will be reversed.

The commentators have noted that the Qur'an's eschatological scenes consistently function as the resolution of its social teachings. The parables raise a question about the distribution of goods; the Day answers it.

**The Mountains in Motion**

The commentators note that the verse's first image is the mountains being set in motion — *nusayyiru al-jibāl*. The mountains are the Qur'an's standard symbol of permanence and immovability, and the verse begins the Day's description by moving them.

The Qur'an states the same elsewhere:

> *"And the mountains will be set in motion, and will become a mirage."* (Qur'an 78:20)

The commentators have drawn the argument: if the fixed points of the earth move, nothing that rests on them remains. The verse's logic is that the removal of the earth's stability is the condition of its becoming *"an open plain"* — *qāʿan ṣafaṣafā*, a level expanse with no elevation and no hollow.

**Nothing Left Behind**

The commentators note the completeness of the summons: *"We will gather them, and leave not one of them behind."* No exception is admitted, and the commentators have read the phrase as excluding every hope of being overlooked.

The verse answers a specific human expectation — that in a vast assembly one might go unnoticed. It states the opposite: the gathering is total, and the person who counted on the crowd to hide him will find the crowd assembled for exactly that purpose.

**The End of Geography**

The commentators note that the verse removes the features by which people orient themselves: elevation, shelter, hiding places. What remains is an open plain on which everyone is visible to everyone else, and the verse's phrase *"as We created you the first time"* (v. 48) will describe the corresponding removal of possessions.

The two verses together strip the person of place and of property, and the commentators have read this as the necessary prelude to judgment: nothing external remains by which anyone can be identified except what he brought.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:48

> **They shall be arrayed before thy Lord in ranks. "Indeed, you have come unto Us as We created you the first time. Nay, but you claimed that We would never appoint a tryst for you."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Arrayed Before Thy Lord in Ranks"**

*Wa ʿuriḍū ʿalā rabbika ṣaffā* — "and they will be presented before your Lord in a row/rank." The verb *ʿaraḍa* means to present, to parade, to bring before for inspection — the same verb used of troops presented to a commander. Al-Qurṭubī describes the ranks as similar to those formed for prayer; al-Rāzī compares them to the ranks around the Kaʿbah. Al-Qurṭubī adds that each rank is occupied by a particular religious community.

The word *ṣaff* can be read as singular — a single line, so that all are visible to one another (al-Rāzī) — or as a collective. Either reading produces the same effect: an ordered assembly in which no one is hidden and no one is out of place. The chaos of history is resolved into formation.

There is a resonance with the Qur'an's description of the angels at 37:165 and 78:38 — *"the Day when the Spirit and the angels stand in ranks"* — and with the phrase *"arrayed in ranks"* used of the birds at 38:19. The image is of a cosmic order in which human beings finally take their assigned place.

**"You Have Come Unto Us as We Created You the First Time"**

*La-qad jiʾtumūnā kamā khalaqnākum awwala marrah* — the commentators read the statement in two complementary ways, both recorded by al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī.

**First**, as an argument for the resurrection. The Makkan disbelievers accepted God as Creator and denied His power to raise the dead. The verse answers: you have come to Us as We made you at first — the One who originated you has, evidently, the power to return you. The Qur'an makes the argument explicitly at 17:51:

> *"Then they will say: Who will bring us back? Say: He Who originated you the first time."* (Qur'an 17:51; see also 36:79, 41:21)

**Second**, as a statement of condition. They come as they were created — alone, bare, without worldly support. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī cite 6:94:

> *"[God will say:] Now you have come unto Us alone, just as We created you the first time, and you have left behind that which We bestowed upon you."* (Qur'an 6:94; see also 19:80)

Al-Rāzī draws the social consequence, and it is the verse's sharpest point: this is an answer to the prominent Makkans who considered themselves superior to the poor believers. On that Day all such distinctions are absent. The man who said *"I am greater than you in wealth and stronger in men"* (v. 34) arrives with neither.

The ḥadīth literature supplies the physical detail. Al-Bukhārī (3349) and Muslim (2860) record from ʿĀʾishah:

> *"You will be gathered barefoot, naked, and uncircumcised." I said, "O Messenger of God, men and women, all looking at one another?" He said, "The matter will be too grave for them to look at one another."* (al-Bukhārī 3349; Muslim 2860)

**"Nay, But You Claimed That We Would Never Appoint a Tryst for You"**

*Bal zaʿamtum allan najʿala lakum mawʿidā* — the verb *zaʿama* denotes a claim or assertion, often with the implication that it is unfounded; *mawʿid* is an appointed time, a tryst. Al-Zamakhsharī explains it as the time of Resurrection and Gathering of which the prophets warned.

The verse identifies the specific error being answered: not atheism but the denial of the appointment. The Makkan position was that this life is all there is — *"there is nothing but our worldly life; we die and we live, and nothing destroys us except time"* (45:24). The verse's rebuttal is delivered not as an argument but as an observation: here you are, at the appointment.

**The Appointment You Denied**

The commentators note that the verse's rebuke is not primarily about the resurrection as a fact but about a specific claim: *"you claimed that We would never appoint a tryst for you."* The word *mawʿid* — a tryst, an appointed time and place — is the same word used at verse 59 of the destroyed towns and at 20:59 of Pharaoh.

The commentators have observed that the disbelievers' error was not ignorance of God but a denial of appointment. They did not say God does not exist; they said He has not fixed a time. The verse answers by presenting them at the time.

This connects the verse to verse 55, where the disbelievers wait for *"the punishment to come upon them face to face"* before believing. The chapter treats the refusal to accept an appointed future as the root of the refusal to act in the present.

**What Ranks Exclude**

The commentators note that the formation in *ṣaff* excludes two things: concealment and precedence. In a rank no one stands behind anyone else, and no one is hidden by the person in front.

The commentators have drawn the social consequence, which al-Rāzī states sharply: the distinctions by which people organised themselves in the world — wealth, lineage, followers — have no place in a formation where everyone occupies the same relation to the One before whom they stand. The man who said *"I am greater than you in wealth and stronger in men"* (v. 34) is in the same rank as the poor believer he addressed.

**The Reversal Stated**

The commentators note that the verse is the chapter's explicit reversal of its central conflict. The Quraysh had rejected the message partly because its earliest adherents were poor, and the chapter has answered by narrating a poor believer superior to a rich one (vv. 32–43).

Verse 48 completes the answer by placing the reversal in the Hereafter, where it cannot be argued with. The commentators have read the verse as directed at the reader's own categories: whatever he uses to rank people will be absent from the ranks.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:49

> **And the Book will be set down. Then thou wilt see the guilty fearful of what is in it. And they will say, "Oh, woe unto us! What a book this is! It leaves out nothing, small or great, save that it has taken account thereof." And they find present [therein] whatsoever they did. And thy Lord wrongs no one.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Book of Deeds, Not the Book of Revelation**

*Wa wuḍiʿa al-kitāb* — "and the Book will be set down." The commentators are unanimous that this is not the Qur'an or scripture generally but the record of each person's deeds. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī cite the parallel descriptions: the record as comprehensive witness (10:61, 34:3–4, 36:12, 45:29–30); written by noble guardians, understood as angels (82:10–12); fastened as an omen upon every person's neck (17:13); given into the right hand for a favourable reckoning or the left for punishment (69:18–25, 84:7–11; see also 83:7–9, 18–20).

The Qur'an's most vivid statement of the matter is at 17:13–14:

> *"And every human being's deed have We fastened to his neck, and We shall bring forth for him on the Day of Resurrection a book which he will find wide open. 'Read your book! Sufficient is your own soul against you this Day as a reckoner.'"* (Qur'an 17:13–14)

The phrase *"sufficient is your own soul as a reckoner"* is the key to this verse's psychology. The accusation is not external. The person reads his own record and finds it accurate.

**"Fearful of What Is In It"**

*Fa-tarā al-mujrimīna mushfiqīna mimmā fīhi* — *mushfiq* denotes one who is apprehensive, fearful with a dread that anticipates what is coming. Al-Rāzī identifies two objects of the fear: punishment from God, and disgrace before other people — because the book openly reveals evil deeds that were hidden during earthly life.

The second fear deserves attention, because it identifies what the guilty person actually dreads. Sin committed in secret carries two burdens: the guilt, and the management of concealment. The concealment is often the heavier of the two, since it must be maintained continuously and against everyone. Verse 49 removes the possibility of concealment, and with it the entire apparatus of reputation the person built.

This is why the Qur'an so often describes the Day in terms of exposure — the earth brought bare (v. 47), the secrets made manifest (86:9), the hearts' contents brought out (100:10). The Day is not primarily a courtroom; it is the end of privacy.

**"It Leaves Out Nothing, Small or Great"**

*Lā yughādiru ṣaghīratan wa lā kabīratan illā aḥṣāhā* — and here the commentators note a detail that ties the verse to verse 47. The verb *yughādiru* — "leaves out" — is the same verb used at verse 47's *"We will leave not one of them behind"* (*fa-lam nughādir minhum aḥadā*). The chapter uses one word for the completeness of the gathering and the completeness of the record: nobody will be left behind, and nothing will be left out.

The phrase *ṣaghīratan wa lā kabīrah* — "small or great" — establishes the comprehensiveness. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the order (small before great) is deliberate: the person is more likely to imagine that small things are overlooked. The verse removes that hope first.

The Qur'an states the same at 99:7–8: *"So whoever does an atom's weight of good shall see it, and whoever does an atom's weight of evil shall see it."*

**"And They Find Present Whatsoever They Did"**

*Wa wajadū mā ʿamilū ḥāḍirā* — "and they found what they had done, present." The word *ḥāḍir* — present, at hand — is significant. The deeds are not merely recorded; they are *there*. The commentators explain that the record makes the deeds themselves present to the person, so that the reading is not the inspection of a document but the encounter with one's own acts.

This is the Qur'an's answer to the ordinary experience of moral forgetfulness. Human beings lose track of what they have done — not only the details but the cumulative shape of their conduct. The verse promises that nothing will be lost, and that the person will confront the whole of it at once.

**"And Thy Lord Wrongs No One"**

*Wa lā yaẓlimu rabbuka aḥadā* — the verse closes with the assurance that runs through the Qur'an's eschatology. Ibn Kathīr cites the principle: punishment on that Day is only for what one's own hands sent forward (3:182, 8:51, 22:10) — the deeds one freely chose. The Qur'an states it at 50:29: *"The Word will not be changed with Me, and I am not unjust to the servants."*

Ibn Kathīr also records the ḥadīth that God will place no one in either the Garden or the Fire until all accounts between human beings have been settled — the rights people hold against one another rendered, even between those bound for the Garden and those bound for the Fire. The justice of the Day includes interpersonal justice, not only the individual's account with God.

The closing assurance is necessary precisely because the verse has just described a record that omits nothing. A comprehensive accounting could be read as harshness; the verse forestalls the reading. Nothing is omitted *and* nothing is added; the person is judged on exactly what he did, and on all of it.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:50

> **When We said unto the angels, "Prostrate before Adam," they prostrated, save Iblīs. He was of the jinn and he deviated from the command of his Lord. Will you then take him and his progeny as protectors apart from Me, though they are an enemy unto you? How evil an exchange for the wrongdoers!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Why the Story of Iblīs Appears Here**

The verse interrupts the eschatological sequence to recall the primordial scene. Al-Rāzī explains the connection: the story is a further admonition to the wealthy and proud Makkans, reminding them that Iblīs's refusal to prostrate was based on the same pride and false sense of superiority that animates them. The Qur'an records Iblīs's reason at 7:12:

> *"He said: I am better than him. Thou createdst me of fire, and him Thou createdst of clay."* (Qur'an 7:12; see also 38:76)

The parallel with verse 34 — *"I am greater than you in wealth and stronger in men"* — is exact. Both speakers ground a claim of superiority in the accident of their origin, and both are ruined by it. The chapter places the two statements within twenty verses of each other, and the reader is meant to hear the echo.

The fuller accounts of the creation and fall are at 2:30–39, 7:11–25, 15:28–43, 17:61–65, 20:115–24, and 38:71–85.

**"He Was of the Jinn"**

*kāna mina al-jinni fa-fasaqa ʿan amri rabbihi* — the identification raises the classical question the commentators debate at length. Al-Zamakhsharī reports the argument that the verse itself explains Iblīs's refusal: had he been an angel, he would have prostrated, since angels are protected from disobedience —

> *"They disobey not God in what He commands them, and do what they are commanded."* (Qur'an 66:6; see also 21:27)

— and it was precisely because he was of the jinn, a species endowed with choice, that he refused. Others, al-Zamakhsharī records, held that he was indeed an angel — perhaps the chief of them — and that his disobedience transformed him into a satan, and so into a jinn.

The mainstream position, articulated by Ibn ʿAbbās and adopted by al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr, is the first: Iblīs was never an angel. He was of the jinn, raised to the company of the angels for his worship, and the command was addressed to the assembly of which he was a part. The verse's grammar supports this: *"they prostrated, save Iblīs"* — an exception that identifies him as outside the class, not a member who failed.

*Fasaqa* — "he deviated, transgressed, went out" — is from *f-s-q*, to depart from a path. Al-Ṭabarī notes the preposition *ʿan* — "away from" — indicating exit rather than mere shortfall.

**"Will You Then Take Him and His Progeny as Protectors?"**

*Afa-tattakhidhūnahu wa dhurrīyatahu awliyāʾa min dūnī* — the interrogative is one of astonished rebuke. The word *awliyāʾ* is the plural of *walī*, the same root as verse 44's *walāyah* — protection, alliance, guardianship. The verse asks whether human beings will take as their protectors the very being who is their enemy.

Al-Zamakhsharī explains "taking them as protectors" as obeying them instead of God. The Qur'an states the same at 7:30:

> *"Some He has rightly guided, and some are deserving of error. Truly they took the satans as protectors apart from God, and deem themselves rightly guided."* (Qur'an 7:30; see also 4:119)

The final clause — *"and deem themselves rightly guided"* — is the crucial addition. The person who follows Satan does not experience himself as following Satan; he experiences himself as following his own judgment, his own interest, his own insight. The Qur'an's account of temptation is not that evil presents itself as evil but that it presents itself as reasonable.

**"They Are an Enemy Unto You"**

*Wa hum lakum ʿaduw* — the declaration of enmity is stated as a fact, not a warning. The Qur'an calls Satan *ʿaduwwun mubīn*, a manifest enemy, at 2:208, 6:142, 7:22, 12:5, 17:53, 28:15, and elsewhere. The account of his intention is given in his own words at 7:16–17:

> *"He said: Because Thou hast led me astray, I shall surely sit in wait for them on Thy straight path. Then I shall come upon them from before them and from behind them, and from their right and from their left, and Thou wilt not find most of them grateful."* (Qur'an 7:16–17)

The verse's rhetorical force lies in the conjunction of the two facts: they are your enemies, and you are taking them as protectors. Al-Rāzī calls the result *biʾsa al-badal* — an evil exchange — the exchange of God's true protection (vv. 42–44) for the illusory protection of Satan (14:22).

**The Exchange Named**

Verse 44 ended with *"there, protection belongs to God, the Real."* Verse 50 asks why anyone would exchange that for the protection of the enemy. The chapter's argument is now complete on this point: the only real protection is God's, and every alternative is not merely weaker but hostile.

The practical application is the one the Qur'an presses throughout: the choice between God and Satan is not presented to human beings as a choice between two options on a ballot. It is presented as a series of small decisions — about wealth, about rank, about whom to sit with, about what to say on entering one's garden — each of which is in fact an alignment. The man who boasted in verse 34 did not decide to follow Iblīs; he decided to be proud. The verse identifies what that decision was.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:51

> **I did not make them witnesses to the creation of the heavens and the earth, nor to their own creation. And I take not those who lead astray as a support.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument From Absence at the Creation**

*Mā ashadtu khulqa al-samāwāti wa'l-arḍi wa lā khalqa anfusihim* — "I did not make them witnesses of the creation of the heavens and the earth, nor of their own creation." Al-Zamakhsharī states the logic: since the satans were not partners with God in creation, it is illogical to treat them as partners in worship. Al-Rāzī adds the second clause's force: they were not even witnesses of *their own* creation, which emphasises that they are themselves mere creatures.

The argument form is the Qur'an's standard one against *shirk*, and it recurs at 10:28 and 13:16:

> *"Say: Is there any of your partners who originates creation, then brings it back again? Say: God originates creation, then brings it back. How then are you perverted?"* (Qur'an 10:34)

> *"The parable of those who take protectors apart from God is that of the spider that makes a house. Truly the frailest of houses is the spider's house, if they but knew."* (Qur'an 29:41)

The reasoning is worth setting out explicitly, because it is one of the Qur'an's most economical arguments. Worship is the acknowledgment of ultimate dependence. Ultimate dependence can only be owed to the source of existence. A being who was not present at the creation of the heavens and the earth — and who was not present at his own creation — is not the source of existence, and is therefore not a possible object of ultimate dependence. The argument does not require any premise the idolater denies; it requires only that he notice what he already believes about the being he worships.

**"Nor of Their Own Creation"**

The addition is rhetorically devastating. It is one thing to be absent from a creation one did not make; it is another to be absent from one's own. The verse removes the last possible ground of the claim — that the being might be self-existent or self-originating. He was made, and he was not there when it happened.

Al-Rāzī draws the conclusion: whatever status the satans have, it is entirely derivative. They are creatures, and creatures cannot be lords.

**"I Take Not Those Who Lead Astray as a Support"**

*Wa mā kuntu muttakhidha al-muḍillīna ʿaḍudā* — *ʿaḍud* is the upper arm, and by extension a helper, an assistant, one who strengthens. The verse states that God does not take those who lead others astray as assistants.

The commentators read the statement in two directions. First, as a factual declaration: the satans have no share in God's governance; they are not deputies, not intermediaries, not agents. Second, as a moral principle: God's aid does not extend to those whose function is misguidance. The person who seeks help through the satans is seeking help from beings who have no standing with the One who gives help.

There is an implicit answer here to a common religious error — the attempt to placate or enlist hostile spiritual powers. Many religious systems include practices designed to propitiate malevolent beings, on the reasoning that such beings can do harm and should therefore be appeased. The verse denies the premise: they have no power that is not permitted, and they are not God's assistants. The Qur'an's instruction is therefore not to placate them but to seek refuge from them:

> *"Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of mankind, the King of mankind, the God of mankind, from the evil of the whisperer who withdraws."* (Qur'an 114:1–4)

**The Verse's Function in the Chapter**

Verses 50–51 form a digression that is not a digression. The chapter has described the Day of gathering (v. 47), the presentation in ranks (v. 48), and the Book set down (v. 49). Before proceeding to the fate of the guilty (vv. 52–53), it asks the reader to consider *whose advice he has been following*. The record in verse 49 will show the deeds; verses 50–51 identify the source of the counsel that produced them.

The connection to the parable is direct. The rich man did not worship an idol; he followed his own assessment of his wealth and his permanence. The verse asks whether that assessment was his own or whether it was whispered — and reminds him that the whisperer was not present at the creation of anything, including himself.

**Why the Verse Denies Their Witness**

The commentators note that the verse says God did not take Iblīs and his progeny as *witnesses* to the creation — *shuhadāʾ* — and have asked what the denial accomplishes, since no one claimed they were.

Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī explain that the denial is aimed at the implication of the Quraysh's practice. If a being is worshipped, it is being treated as one who knows and approves — as a witness to the arrangement. The verse denies the premise: these beings were not present, do not know, and were not consulted, so their worship rests on nothing.

The Qur'an makes the same argument at 35:40 and 46:4–6, challenging the idolaters to produce evidence for their partners' share in creation. The argument is not that the partners are evil but that they are absent — which is a more devastating objection, since evil at least implies existence and power.

**Mutakhidhī al-Muḍillīna ʿAḍudā**

The commentators note the verse's final phrase: *"I take not those who lead astray as a support"* — *ʿaḍud*, literally the upper arm, hence a helper or ally.

The commentators have drawn the point that God's refusal to take the misleaders as support is a statement about the character of His help. The person who is aided by one who leads him astray is not helped but used. The verse assures the reader that divine support does not come in that form, which is why the believer's reliance is properly placed on God alone rather than on any patron however powerful.

The phrase also answers the Quraysh's own claim to be the guardians of the sanctuary. Their patronage is not God's, and the verse distinguishes the two.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:52

> **On the Day when He says, "Call those whom you claimed as My partners," they will call upon them, but they will not respond to them, and We will place a gulf between them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command That Exposes the Claim**

*Wa yawma yaqūlu nādū shurakāʾiya alladhīna zaʿamtum* — "and on the Day He will say: call those whom you claimed were My partners." The commentators — al-Rāzī in particular — note that this is one of several verses in which God challenges the polytheists to invoke their "partners" (6:22, 16:27, 28:62), and that the challenge is not a genuine invitation but a *rebuke*: the invocation will be made, and it will fail, publicly.

The verb *zaʿama* returns from verse 48 — the same word for an unfounded claim. The Qur'an is consistent in identifying the error as a claim without warrant, connecting back to verse 15's demand for a *sulṭān bayyin*.

**"They Will Call Upon Them, But They Will Not Respond"**

*Fa-daʿawhum fa-lam yastajībū lahum* — the commentators cite the parallel accounts of the partners' abandonment: 6:94, 10:28, 16:86, 28:63. The Qur'an's description at 16:86 is particularly pointed:

> *"And when those who were associated see their partners, they will say, 'Our Lord, these are our partners whom we called upon apart from Thee.' But they will cast to them the word: 'Truly you are liars.'"* (Qur'an 16:86)

The partners do not merely fail to help; they *deny the relationship*. This is the eschatological form of the social fact the Qur'an repeatedly observes: alliances built on false premises dissolve when the premises are tested.

There is a resonance with the parable that the chapter intends. The rich man boasted of being *"stronger in men"* (v. 34) and found that *"he had no party to help him"* (v. 43). The verse generalises the reversal: every *fiʾah*, every party, every claimed protector that is not God will behave the same way on that Day.

**"We Will Place a Gulf Between Them"**

*Wa jaʿalnā baynahum mawbiqā* — the noun *mawbiq* is from *w-b-q*, and the commentators offer three readings. Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and al-Ṭabarī read it as a place of destruction, or a valley of Hell into which the polytheists will be cast. Al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Zamakhsharī read it as the *enmity* that will manifest between the idolaters and their claimed partners. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī add a third: the vast intermediate expanse — the *barzakh* — between those who held the angels to be God's partners and the angels themselves, the former in the depths of Hell and the latter in the highest celestial realm.

The third reading is significant for the chapter's structure. *Barzakh* — the barrier, the intermediate realm — is the concept the source commentary identifies as the sūrah's hidden architecture: the sleepers in the *barzakh* between life and death, Moses meeting Khiḍr at the junction of the two seas, Dhu'l-Qarnayn sealing the pass between two mountains. Here the same concept appears in its eschatological form: an unbridgeable separation placed between the worshipper and the object of his worship.

The commentators note the irony in the enmity reading. In this world the idolater feels closeness to his deity; on that Day the closeness is revealed as enmity, and the enmity as permanent.

**The Verse's Argument**

Read as a whole, verse 52 makes a claim about the nature of false worship that is worth isolating. The person who worships something other than God is not merely making a theological error; he is entering a relationship that will fail him at the moment he needs it most, and that will turn against him when it fails.

The Qur'an's imagery for this is the spider's house at 29:41 — the frailest of houses, which looks like a structure and is not. The verse here gives the same teaching eschatologically: the invocation is made, and nothing answers.

**The Barzakh as Separation**

The commentators note that the verse places a *barzakh* — a barrier, an intervening space — between the callers and the called. The word is the same used at 23:100 of the interval between death and resurrection, and at 25:53 of the barrier between the two seas.

The commentators have drawn the meaning: the *barzakh* is not a wall but a distance that cannot be crossed. The idols will not refuse to answer; they will be unable to reach. The verse describes a failure of connection rather than a refusal.

The Qur'an states the same at 6:94 — *"now you have come unto Us alone"* — and at 26:100–101, where the intercessors are absent. The commentators have read the verse as removing the last hope of the polytheist: not that his gods will reject him, but that they will simply not be reachable.

The instruction is about the nature of reliance. A support that cannot be reached when needed was never a support, and the verse asks the reader to examine what he relies upon by the single test of whether it can reach him at the moment of need.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:53

> **The guilty will see the Fire, and know they shall fall into it, but they will find no means of escape therefrom.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Will See the Fire"**

*Wa raʾā al-mujrimūna al-nāra* — the verse moves from the failed invocation to the sight of the destination. The commentators note the sequence: they called (v. 52), nothing answered, and now they see. The seeing is the moment of confirmation.

**Ẓannū: Knowing or Supposing**

*Fa-ẓannū annahum muwāqiʿūhā* — "and they thought/knew that they would fall into it." Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī note that *ẓann* can mean either conjecture or firm conviction, and they offer two readings.

On the first reading, *ẓannū* means they became *certain* of their fate — the certainty produced by seeing. On al-Rāzī's second reading, the Fire is still distant, but they *think* it is near because of the intensity of its raging and roaring, which they can hear, and they fear that they will fall into it at that moment. The Qur'an describes the Fire's sound at 25:12:

> *"When it sees them from a distant place, they hear its raging and roaring."* (Qur'an 25:12)

Both readings are preserved by the commentators, and both make the same point: there is no uncertainty left. The anticipatory dread is as complete as the certainty.

**"They Will Find No Means of Escape"**

*Wa lam yajidū ʿanhā maṣrifā* — *maṣrif* is from *ṣ-r-f*, to turn away, to divert; it denotes a turning, a deflection, a way out. Al-Rāzī explains that they have no means of changing course, because they are being driven forward by the angels; see 37:1–3, 50:21. The Qur'an describes the driving at 19:86 — *"And We shall drive the guilty unto Hell as a thirsty herd"* — and at 39:71 — *"those who disbelieved will be driven unto Hell in throngs"*; see also 41:19.

The image is one of directed motion without agency. The guilty are not wandering toward the Fire and failing to find an exit; they are being conveyed, and the verse denies them the capacity to deflect.

**The Verse in the Chapter's Moral Argument**

The commentators connect verse 53 back to the parable. The rich man said in verse 36, *"nor do I think that the Hour is imminent"* — *wa mā aẓunnu al-sāʿata qāʾimah*. Verse 53 uses the same verb *ẓanna* of the guilty at the moment the Hour has arrived. The man who did not think the Hour was coming now knows, with a knowledge that cannot be unlearned, that he is falling into the Fire.

The chapter's eschatological section (vv. 47–53) thus completes its narrative sections. Every boast has been answered: the garden's permanence by its destruction, the boast of men by the absence of a party, the doubt about the Hour by the arrival of the appointment, and the confidence in a better Hereafter by the sight of the Fire.

**Anticipation as a Portion of the Punishment**

The commentators have drawn a distinction the verse implies. Punishment in the Qur'an's eschatology is not only the infliction of harm but the *knowledge* of the harm to come. The guilty see the Fire before they enter it; they hear it before they reach it (25:12); they are certain of their fall while it is still ahead of them. The interval between seeing and arriving is itself a portion of the reckoning.

This is a psychologically exact account, and the commentators have noted its parallel in this world. The person awaiting a verdict, a diagnosis, or a sentence often reports that the waiting exceeded the event. The Qur'an's eschatology takes this ordinary experience and makes it structural: the Day is arranged so that nothing is a surprise, and the certainty of what is coming is part of what is coming.

**The Removal of Alternatives**

*Wa lam yajidū ʿanhā maṣrifā* — the verse's final clause denies the *maṣrif*, the turning away. Al-Rāzī explains that they are driven forward by the angels and cannot deflect. The commentators note that this is the exact inverse of the worldly situation the disbeliever relied upon. In this world he could turn: toward wealth, toward allies, toward argument, toward mockery. Verse 53 states that the capacity to turn is itself a feature of this world and does not survive into the next.

The chapter's structure makes the point by contrast. The youths of the cave turned — they withdrew from their people and took refuge (v. 16). The poor companion turned — he refused the frame his brother offered (v. 38). Dhu'l-Qarnayn turned — three times, following a means. The guilty on that Day will find no *maṣrif*, and the verse's force depends on the reader having watched turning be possible throughout.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:54

> **And indeed We have employed every kind of parable for mankind in this Quran. And man is the most contentious of beings.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Every Kind of Parable"**

*Wa la-qad ṣarrafnā fī hādhā al-qurʾāni li'l-nāsi min kulli mathal* — "and We have certainly set forth in this Qur'an, for mankind, every kind of parable." The verb *ṣarrafa* is intensive: to turn about, to vary, to present in diverse forms. Al-Ṭabarī and the commentators cite the parallel statements at 17:89, 30:58, and 39:27:

> *"And We have certainly set forth for mankind in this Qur'an every kind of parable, that they may take heed."* (Qur'an 39:27)

The claim is not that the Qur'an contains every conceivable illustration but that it has varied its modes of presentation exhaustively — narrative, parable, argument, law, warning, promise, dialogue, natural observation. The chapter has just demonstrated the claim: a story of sleepers, a parable of two gardens, a parable of water and chaff, an eschatological scene, a recall of the primordial scene.

The stated purpose at 39:27 is *"that they may take heed"* — and at 30:58, *"that they may understand."* The variety of the parables exists because human beings differ in what reaches them. One person is moved by a story, another by an argument, another by a threat. The Qur'an's method is to address all of them.

**"And Man Is the Most Contentious of Beings"**

*Wa kāna al-insānu akthara shayʾin jadalā* — *jadal* is disputation, contention, argumentativeness. Al-Ṭabarī reads it as human beings vainly opposing and arguing against the prophets; al-Bayḍāwī adds that the contention is based on false ideas. The Qur'an states the same at 16:4:

> *"He created man from a drop of fluid, and behold, he is an open disputant."* (Qur'an 16:4)

The juxtaposition is one of the Qur'an's characteristic reversals, and it is deliberate: from a drop of fluid, to the most contentious of creatures. The same contrast appeared at verse 37, where the companion reminded the rich man that he was created *"from dust, then from a drop, then fashioned as a man."* The origin is humble; the posture is disputatious; the distance between them is the measure of the ingratitude.

**Why the Two Clauses Belong Together**

The verse's structure is a diagnosis and an explanation. Clause one: God has provided every kind of illustration. Clause two: human beings argue anyway. The conjunction *wa* — "and" — carries the implication: the failure to be persuaded is not due to a shortage of evidence.

This is the Qur'an's account of unbelief, and it is important that it is not primarily an intellectual account. The person who rejects the message is not usually someone who has weighed the arguments and found them wanting; he is someone engaged in *jadal* — contention, which is a posture rather than an inquiry. The verse identifies the activity by name, and naming it is part of the correction: the person who recognises that he is arguing rather than seeking has taken the first step toward the second.

Verse 56 will develop the point: *"We send the messengers only as bearers of glad tidings and warners; and those who disbelieve argue with falsehood in order to refute the truth thereby."* The chapter is precise about the difference between argument and contention, and it locates itself on one side of the line.

**The Verse as a Transition**

Verse 54 closes the eschatological section and opens the final movement of the chapter's middle portion — the discussion of what prevents belief (v. 55), the function of the messengers (v. 56), the description of the one who turns away from God's signs (v. 57), and the destroyed towns (v. 59). Having shown the reader the Day and its record, the chapter turns to the question of why the record will be what it is: not because the evidence was insufficient, but because the response was contentious.

**Al-Insān Aktharu Shayʾin Jadalā**

The commentators note the verse's closing clause — *"and man is the most contentious of beings"* — and have discussed whether *al-insān* is generic or specific. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī record both readings: the generic (human beings are given to disputation) and the specific (the disbeliever, or al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith, is meant).

The word *jadal* is not neutral. It denotes argument conducted to prevail rather than to discover — the same root as *jadal* in the sense of wrangling. The Qur'an uses it pejoratively at 22:8 and 40:4.

The commentators have connected the clause to the preceding one: *"We have employed every kind of parable for mankind."* The two halves form a diagnosis. Every form of explanation has been provided, and the obstacle is not a shortage of evidence but a disposition to dispute.

The instruction the tradition draws is that the believer should recognise when an exchange has become *jadal* rather than inquiry, and that continuing it is not a service to the truth. The verse is a warning about the disputant's own state as much as about the argument.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:55

> **And naught prevents men from believing when guidance comes unto them, and from seeking forgiveness of their Lord, save that [they await] the wont of those of old to come upon them, or the punishment to come upon them face-to-face.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question the Verse Asks and Answers**

*Wa mā manaʿa al-nāsa an yuʾminū idh jāʾahum al-hudā wa yastaghfirū rabbahum illā an taʾtiyahum sunnatu al-awwalīna aw yaʾtiyahumu al-ʿadhābu qubulā* — the verse opens with *mā manaʿa*, "what prevented," a construction that implies an expectation: given the guidance that has come, belief would be the natural response. The verse then identifies the single thing that blocks it.

Al-Zamakhsharī explains the blockage: people wait to see whether the same worldly destruction that befell earlier peoples will be visited upon them as well. The Qur'an makes the same point at 16:33, 43:86, and 47:18.

**Sunnat al-Awwalīn: The Wont of Those of Old**

*Sunna* in Qur'anic usage denotes a settled way, an established practice, a law of dealing. *Sunnat al-awwalīn* is God's customary dealing with earlier peoples who rejected their messengers — the destruction that came upon Noah's people, ʿĀd, Thamūd, the people of Lot, and Pharaoh. Al-Zamakhsharī reads the verse as describing the disbelievers' demand: they will not believe until they see the sign that destroyed the earlier nations.

The Qur'an states the immutability of this *sunna* at 35:43:

> *"So do they await aught but the wont of those of old? Thou shalt find no alteration in the wont of God, and thou shalt find no change in the wont of God."* (Qur'an 35:43)

There is a deep irony here that the commentators note. The disbelievers are demanding a miracle as a condition of belief, and the miracle they are demanding is the one that destroyed the people who demanded it before them. They are asking for the punishment as a sign.

**"Or the Punishment to Come Upon Them Face to Face"**

*Aw yaʾtiyahumu al-ʿadhābu qubulā* — *qubulan* is read in three ways. Al-Zamakhsharī reads it as the punishment of the Hereafter. Al-Ṭabarī reads it as "suddenly" — the manner in which divine punishment is said to come at 6:44, 7:95, and 26:202. Al-Zamakhsharī also records the alternative vocalisation *qabalan*, meaning "in the future," or "openly, face to face."

The commentators note the Qur'an's repeated statement that punishment comes without warning:

> *"Then, when they forgot that whereof they had been reminded, We opened unto them the gates of every thing, until, when they rejoiced in that which they were given, We seized them suddenly, and behold, they were in despair."* (Qur'an 6:44)

**The Diagnosis: Why Guidance Does Not Produce Belief**

The verse's claim is worth isolating because it is counterintuitive. One would expect the obstacle to belief to be a shortage of evidence, or an intellectual difficulty. The verse identifies something else: a *posture* — the demand that belief be preceded by a demonstration of catastrophic power.

This is not an epistemological position; it is a negotiation. The person who says "I will believe when X happens" has not stated a condition of knowledge but a term of surrender, and the condition is chosen so that it will not be met. The Qur'an identifies the same demand repeatedly from the Makkans: 17:90–93 (make a spring gush forth, have a garden of palms and vines, make the sky fall in pieces), 6:158 (they will not believe until they see the angels or their Lord).

The practical force is considerable. The verse is asking the reader to notice his own conditions. What would it take for you to believe? The answer is often not a piece of evidence but an event — and events of the kind demanded are not evidences but judgments.

**The Verse's Connection to the Chapter's Occasion**

The connection to the Quraysh's challenge is direct. They demanded three answers as proof of prophethood. The chapter supplied two of them. Verse 55 explains why the supply will not produce the result they promised: the demand was never really about answers.

**Why the Demand for Signs Is Refused**

The commentators note that the verse reports a conditional demand — the disbelievers will not believe *"until"* specific conditions are met — and that the Qur'an declines to meet it.

The commentators explain the refusal on the basis of the Qur'an's own principle, stated at 6:109–111:

> *"And even if We sent down unto them the angels, and the dead spoke unto them, and We gathered together all things before them face to face, they would not believe unless God willed."* (Qur'an 6:111)

The point is that the demand is not a genuine request for evidence but a device for postponement. A person who will accept a sign only on his own terms has already decided, and meeting the terms would not change the decision. The commentators have observed that the Qur'an distinguishes between the seeker who asks in order to believe and the objector who asks in order to delay, and answers only the first.

The verse names the demand's content — the punishment of the ancients, or punishment face to face — and verses 58–59 answer it by describing the forbearance that the demanders mistake for indifference.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:56
