> **And We send not the messengers, save as bearers of glad tidings and as warners. And those who disbelieve dispute falsely in order to refute the truth thereby. They take My signs and that whereof they were warned in mockery.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Limit of the Prophetic Office**

*Wa mā nursilu al-mursalīna illā mubashshirīna wa mundhirīn* — "and We send the messengers only as bearers of glad tidings and as warners." The commentators note that *mursalīn* here covers all bearers of God's message, including both *rusul* (messengers) and *anbiyāʾ* (prophets). The dual function is the fixed formula of the Qur'an: 2:213, 4:165, 6:48, and of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ specifically at 2:119, 7:188, 11:2, 17:105, 25:56, 33:45, 34:28 — and at 18:2, the chapter's own opening statement of purpose.

The verse's function here is to *limit*. The messengers are not sent as miracle-workers on demand, not as political deliverers, not as guarantors of outcomes. They bring two things: news of good for those who believe, and warning for those who do not. Everything beyond that is outside the office.

This is the answer to the demand of verse 55. The disbelievers asked for a sign; the verse explains that signs of the kind they want are not what messengers are for. The messenger's job is *balāgh* — delivery — and delivery has been made.

**"They Dispute Falsely in Order to Refute the Truth"**

*Wa yujādilu alladhīna kafarū bi'l-bāṭili li-yudḥiḍū bihi al-ḥaqq* — the verb *jādala* returns from verse 54's *jadal*; *yudḥiḍ* means to make slip, to cause to stumble, to knock down. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Bayḍāwī read the clause as identifying the *purpose* of the disputation: not to discover the truth but to trip it up.

The verse draws a distinction the Qur'an maintains carefully throughout — between argument and contention. The Qur'an itself argues; it invites its opponents to bring their proof (2:111); it reasons from creation, from history, from the nature of God. What it condemns is *mujādalah bi'l-bāṭil* — disputation whose instrument is falsehood and whose aim is refutation rather than discovery.

The distinction is available to the reader as a diagnostic. An inquiry asks "what is the case?"; a contention asks "how can this be defeated?" The two look identical from outside and are entirely different activities. The verse names the second and identifies its practitioners.

**"They Take My Signs and That Whereof They Were Warned in Mockery"**

*Wa-ittakhadhū āyātī wa mā ūʿidū huzuwā* — the commentators note that mockery is the worst kind of denial. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī explain: the person who doubts may be brought to certainty; the person who argues may be answered; the person who mocks has closed the door from the inside. Al-Rāzī adds that the mockery manifests profound ignorance and hardness of heart.

The Qur'an speaks of those who take God's signs or religion itself in mockery at 2:231, 5:57, 45:9, and 45:35:

> *"And when it is said, 'Fear God,' pride carries him off to sin. Hell will suffice him, and how evil a resting place!"* (Qur'an 2:206)

The psychological mechanism is worth naming. Mockery is a defence against being addressed. To take a warning seriously is to accept that one may be in danger, and that acceptance is uncomfortable. Ridicule avoids the discomfort by refusing the frame — but the refusal is not an answer, and the danger is unaffected by it.

**The Verse's Function**

Verse 56 completes the diagnosis begun in verse 54. Human beings are contentious (v. 54); their contention is not about evidence but about defeating the truth (v. 56); and when the truth cannot be defeated, they mock it (v. 56). The chapter has now named the three stages by which a person loses the guidance that has been offered to him — and verse 57 will describe the resulting condition.

**The Messenger as Bearer, Not Author**

The commentators note the verse's careful distribution of roles: the messengers are sent *"save as bearers of glad tidings and as warners,"* and the disputation is attributed to those who disbelieve.

The commentators have drawn the point that the verse limits the Prophet's ﷺ responsibility. He transmits; he does not manufacture belief, and the failure of others to believe is not a failure of his office. The Qur'an states the same at 5:99 — *"the Messenger's duty is only to convey"* — and at 16:82.

The instruction is relevant beyond prophethood. The person who conveys a truth is responsible for the conveying, not for the reception, and the verse releases him from an obligation he cannot discharge. The commentators have read this as the answer to the grief the chapter addresses at verse 6.

The verse also names the disputation's method — *jidāl* conducted *"in order to refute the truth thereby"* — which identifies the purpose before the content. The commentators have noted that knowing an argument's purpose is often more informative than examining its content.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:57

> **And who does greater wrong than one who has been reminded of the signs of his Lord, then turns away from them and forgets that which his hands have sent forth? Surely We have placed coverings over their hearts, such that they understand it not, and in their ears a deafness. Even if thou callest them to guidance, they will never be rightly guided.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Three Stages Named in One Sentence**

*Wa man aẓlamu mimman dhukkira bi-āyāti rabbihi fa-aʿraḍa ʿanhā wa nasiya mā qaddamat yadāh* — the verse describes a sequence: reminded, then turning away, then forgetting what his hands sent forward. The rhetorical question *wa man aẓlamu mimman* — "who is more unjust than" — recurs at 32:22 (in identical form), 6:21, 7:37, 10:17, 29:68, 39:32, and at 18:15 within this chapter.

The three stages are causally connected and the commentators have drawn the connection. The reminder comes (v. 56's *mā ūʿidū*); the person turns away from it; and the turning produces forgetting — specifically forgetting his own prior deeds, the accumulated weight of what he has done.

The forgetting is the crucial stage. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī note that the verse does not describe a person who has never been warned but one who *was* warned and then forgot his own accountability. The moral memory — the awareness that one's acts are recorded and will be reckoned — is what the turning away destroys. Verse 49 described the Book that omits nothing; this verse describes the person who has lost the sense that such a Book exists.

**"We Have Placed Coverings Over Their Hearts"**

*Innā jaʿalnā ʿalā qulūbihim akinnatan an yafqahūhu wa fī ādhānihim waqrā* — *akinnah* is the plural of *kinān*, a covering, a sheath; *waqr* is a heaviness in the ear, a deafness. The identical statement appears at 6:25, and a similar one at 2:7:

> *"God has set a seal upon their hearts and upon their hearing, and over their eyes is a covering; and theirs shall be a tremendous punishment."* (Qur'an 2:7)

The commentators' treatment of these verses is careful and it matters for reading this one correctly. The divine act of covering is consistently read as *consequent upon* the human turning, not antecedent to it. The verse's own sequence demonstrates this: first the reminder, then the turning away, then the covering. God does not cover a heart that has not turned.

Al-Ṭabarī's formulation and the standard Sunni position is that God's placing of the coverings is a *judicial* act — the ratification of a choice already made, the withdrawal of a capacity the person has refused to use. The parallel at 61:5 is explicit:

> *"And when they turned away, God turned their hearts away. And God guides not the transgressing people."* (Qur'an 61:5)

**"Even If Thou Callest Them to Guidance"**

*Wa in tadʿuhum ilā al-hudā fa-lan yahtadū idhan abadā* — the verse addresses the Prophet ﷺ directly, and it returns to the theme of verse 6. There he was told not to destroy himself with grief over their disbelief; here he is told that his calling will not reach them. The two verses together state the limit of the prophetic office and the limit of the prophet's responsibility.

The word *abadan* — "forever" — is the chapter's term of permanence, used at verse 3 of the believers' reward, at verse 20 of the apostate's loss, and at verse 35 of the rich man's boast about his garden. Here it is applied to the condition of the covered heart. The commentators note that the verse is describing the fixed state produced by sustained refusal, not foreclosing repentance for any individual who turns.

**The Practical Warning**

The verse is not primarily about other people. Its force for the reader is diagnostic: the covering of the heart is described as the *result* of turning away from reminders, and every reader receives reminders. The mechanism the verse describes — reminder, aversion, forgetfulness — operates in every human life, and it operates gradually.

The Islamic tradition's practice of regular remembrance (*dhikr*), of the Friday gathering, of the annual fast, exists precisely as a counter-measure. The person who hears the same reminders repeatedly is not being told something new; he is being kept from the forgetting that verse 57 identifies as the final stage. This is one reason the chapter is recited weekly: the repetition is the medicine.

**The Turning Away Named First**

The commentators note the verse's order: the person is reminded of his Lord's signs, *then* he turns away from them, *then* the covering and the deafness follow. The sequence is causal and the commentators have drawn its consequence.

The covering is not the cause of the turning away but its result. This matters for the verse's theology, which the commentators are careful to preserve: God's action in placing the covering follows a human action, and the human action is the person's own. The Qur'an states the same principle at 61:5:

> *"And when they turned away, God turned their hearts away."* (Qur'an 61:5)

The commentators have read the two verses together as establishing that spiritual insensibility is acquired rather than assigned. No one begins covered; the covering is where consistent turning away arrives.

**Waqran: The Weight in the Ears**

The commentators note the word *waqr* — a heaviness, a stopping-up — used of the ears. The image is of an obstruction rather than a defect: the ear is present and the sound is present, and something is between them.

The commentators have drawn the practical instruction that the *waqr* is not perceptible from inside. A person with a stopped ear does not experience deafness as a symptom; he experiences the world as quiet. The verse exists to give the reader an external description of a condition he cannot detect in himself, which is why the tradition's countermeasure is repeated exposure to the reminders rather than introspection.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:58

> **And thy Lord is Forgiving, Possessed of Mercy. Were He to take them to task for that which they have earned, He would have hastened the punishment for them. Nay, but theirs is a tryst, beyond which they shall find no refuge.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Names Before the Warning**

*Wa rabbuka al-ghafūru dhū al-raḥmah* — "and your Lord is the Forgiving, the Possessor of Mercy." The verse begins with the Names, and the commentators note that the warning that follows is thereby framed. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī read the sequence as explanatory: the reason God does not punish immediately is that He is Forgiving and Merciful.

*Ghafūr* is the intensive form of the One who covers and forgives; *dhū al-raḥmah* — "possessor of mercy" — is a construction that denotes mercy as an abiding attribute rather than an occasional act. The Qur'an's standard pairing of these qualities is at 15:49–50:

> *"Inform My servants that I am the Forgiving, the Merciful, and that My punishment is the painful punishment."* (Qur'an 15:49–50)

**"Were He to Take Them to Task for What They Have Earned"**

*Law yuʾākhidhuhum bi-mā kasabū la-ʿajjala lahumu al-ʿadhāb* — the conditional states what would follow if the Names were withdrawn. The parallel verses at 16:61 and 35:45 make the scale explicit:

> *"And were God to take people to task for what they have earned, He would not leave upon it a single creature. But He grants them respite until an appointed term; and when their term comes — truly God is Ever-Seeing of His servants."* (Qur'an 35:45)

The verse therefore makes a claim about the actual state of the world: the fact that punishment is not immediate is not evidence that it is not deserved. Every reader is living in the respite.

**"Theirs Is a Tryst"**

*Bal lahum mawʿidun lan yajidū min dūnihi mawʾilā* — *mawʿid* returns from verse 48, the appointed time the disbelievers denied would ever be set. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī read it as the appointed time of their death or destruction; al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī also note the identification by some commentators of the Makkans' specific tryst with the Battle of Badr (2 AH / 624 CE), at which several prominent opponents of the Prophet ﷺ were killed. Al-Rāzī reads it alternatively as the Day of Resurrection.

*Mawʾil* is a place or means of refuge — the same concept as verse 27's *multaḥad* and verse 43's absence of a party. The verse states: when the tryst arrives, there will be nowhere to go.

**A Warning Specific to Makkah**

Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī note that the verse functions as a warning to the Makkan disbelievers specifically, whom God did not punish immediately despite their extreme enmity toward the Prophet ﷺ. The respite is not approval; it is the space for repentance.

The practical instruction is the one the Qur'an presses repeatedly: the delay of consequence is not the absence of consequence. Human beings systematically infer from the absence of immediate punishment that no punishment is coming, and the Qur'an identifies this inference as one of the characteristic errors of the disbelieving heart:

> *"And let not those who disbelieve think that Our respite to them is good for them. We grant them respite only that they may increase in sin; and theirs shall be a humiliating punishment."* (Qur'an 3:178)

The verse's mercy is real and its warning is real, and the two are not in tension: the respite *is* the mercy, and the tryst is what makes the respite matter.

**The Respite as the Actual Mercy**

The commentators have made a point about the verse that is easily missed. The respite is not a suspension of justice pending its eventual execution; it is itself one of the two Names the verse opens with. *Al-Ghafūr* and *dhū al-raḥmah* are not adjectives qualifying a delay; they are the reason for the delay, and the delay is what makes forgiveness possible. A punishment executed immediately would leave nothing to forgive.

Al-Rāzī states the logic: God leaves them until their appointed term in order to give them time to repent and mend. The interval between the verse's warning and the verse's tryst is therefore not empty; it is the space in which the entire moral life of the addressee occurs. Every day of the respite is a day in which the direction can still be changed.

**Why Delay Reads as Absence**

The commentators address the inference the delay invites. Human beings reason from timing: an offence followed by nothing appears to have no consequence, and the appearance accumulates into a conviction. The Qur'an identifies this reasoning as a specific error and names it:

> *"And they say: What is the matter with this Messenger — he eats food and walks in the markets? Why is no angel sent down to him, to be a warner with him? Or a treasure thrown down to him, or a garden from which he eats? And the wrongdoers say: You follow nothing but a man bewitched."* (Qur'an 25:7–8)

And at 46:24, of the people of ʿĀd when they saw the punishment approaching: *"This is a cloud bringing us rain! Nay, it is what you sought to hasten — a wind wherein is a painful punishment."* They had mistaken the beginning of the punishment for a mercy because it did not look like what they expected.

The practical instruction is the discipline of not reading timing as verdict. The verse asks the reader to hold two things simultaneously: that God is Forgiving and Merciful, and that a tryst has been set which cannot be missed. Neither statement cancels the other, and the person who keeps only one of them has misread the verse.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:59

> **And those towns, We destroyed them for the wrong they did, and We set a tryst for their destruction.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Destroyed Towns as Standing Evidence**

*Wa tilka al-qurā ahlaknāhum lammā ẓalamū* — "and those towns — We destroyed them when they wronged." The commentators note that the Qur'an refers repeatedly to the destruction of whole towns for disbelief and wrongdoing, and that all such references occur in Makkan sūrahs — 6:131, 7:96, 11:100–102, 28:59, 46:26–27 — because they were intended as warnings for the Quraysh.

The towns are the standard Qur'anic catalogue: the people of Noah, ʿĀd (the people of Hūd), Thamūd (the people of Ṣāliḥ), the people of Lot, Midian (the people of Shuʿayb), and Pharaoh's Egypt. The Qur'an's method with them is consistent: it does not narrate them exhaustively but returns to them as a class, so that the pattern rather than the individual story becomes the lesson.

**"For the Wrong They Did"**

*Lammā ẓalamū* — the cause of destruction is specified as *ẓulm*, wrongdoing. The commentators note the significance: the Qur'an never attributes destruction to divine caprice or to unbelief considered merely as an intellectual state. It attributes it to wrongdoing — which includes the injustice of *shirk*, the oppression of the weak, the fraud in the market (Midian's specific crime), and the persecution of the prophets.

The verse thereby answers a question the reader may raise about the destroyed towns: why were they destroyed? The answer is not "because they disagreed with their prophet" but "because they wronged." The Qur'an is careful to keep its account of divine punishment moral rather than arbitrary.

**"We Set a Tryst for Their Destruction"**

*Wa jaʿalnā li-mahlikihim mawʿidā* — *mahlik* is the time or place of destruction. The commentators explain that God appointed a specific time, known only to Him, for the punishment of each town, and that the time could neither be advanced nor delayed. The Qur'an states the principle at 34:30:

> *"Say: Yours shall be the tryst of a Day that you cannot delay by a single hour, nor advance."* (Qur'an 34:30)

The point is one of precision. The destruction of the towns was not a general threat that might or might not materialise; it was a scheduled event, and it occurred at its appointed hour. This is the evidence the verse offers the Quraysh: the *sunna* of verse 55 is not a pattern that has exceptions.

**Why This Verse Concludes the Section**

Verse 59 closes the chapter's third movement — the discussion of unbelief, contention, mockery, and the covered heart (vv. 54–59) — and it does so with historical evidence. The argument is not that punishment *might* come; it is that it *has* come, repeatedly, to identifiable peoples, at appointed times.

The verse's placement immediately before the story of Moses and Khiḍr (vv. 60–82) is significant. The chapter moves from the destruction of towns to a journey in search of knowledge — from the *sunna* of punishment to the *sunna* of guidance. Both are God's dealing with human beings, and the chapter will show them operating in the same world.

**The Catalogue and Its Function**

The commentators note that the Qur'an's references to destroyed towns cluster in the Makkan sūrahs, and the reason is contextual: they were addressed to a community that had not yet been punished and could still choose. The towns named across the Qur'an — Noah's people, ʿĀd, Thamūd, the people of Lot, Midian, Pharaoh's Egypt — function not as history lessons but as precedents, and the Qur'an says so:

> *"That is from the tidings of the towns which We relate unto thee. Of them are some that stand, and some that are mown down. And We wronged them not, but they wronged themselves; and their gods whom they called upon apart from God availed them not at all when the command of thy Lord came. They increased them in nothing but ruin."* (Qur'an 11:100–101)

The phrase *"of them are some that stand, and some that are mown down"* is significant: the ruins were visible to the Qur'an's first hearers. The Quraysh travelled past the carved tombs of Thamūd at Madāʾin Ṣāliḥ on their trade route to Syria. The warning was not abstract; it was a landmark on their road.

**The Appointment as an Argument Against Chance**

The commentators press the phrase *wa jaʿalnā li-mahlikihim mawʿidā*. The destructions were not disasters — plagues, famines, earthquakes read as misfortune — but *appointments*, fixed in advance and executed on schedule. Al-Ṭabarī notes that this is what distinguishes the Qur'anic account from a merely naturalistic one: an event that occurs at a pre-announced time, for a stated reason, to a people who were warned, is not an accident.

The verse's force for its original audience was therefore not "destruction happens" — everyone knows that — but "destruction is *scheduled*, and the schedule is not published to you." The Quraysh could not know whether their own tryst was a year away or a decade. The uncertainty is not a weakness of the warning but its mechanism: a deadline known in advance invites procrastination; a deadline concealed requires continuous readiness.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:60

> **And when Moses said unto his servant, "I shall continue on till I reach the junction of the two seas, even if I journey for a long time."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter's Third Trial: Knowledge**

The chapter now opens its second great narrative. Where the cave story tested faith and the garden parable tested wealth, this one tests *knowledge* — and the person tested is not an ordinary believer but Moses, one of the five greatest messengers, the one to whom God spoke directly.

The commentators note a striking feature: this is the only account of Moses in the Qur'an with no parallel in the Biblical text. It is, in that sense, entirely a Qur'anic contribution — which suggests that its purpose is not historical but pedagogical.

**The Occasion: A Question About Who Knows Most**

Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Zamakhsharī record the ḥadīth that supplies the story's occasion. Al-Bukhārī (122, 3401, 4725, 4727) and Muslim (2380) transmit from Ubayy ibn Kaʿb, from the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"Moses stood up addressing the Children of Israel, and he was asked, 'Who is the most knowledgeable of people?' He said, 'I.' God was displeased with him, because he had not referred the knowledge back to God. So God revealed to him: 'I have a servant at the junction of the two seas who is more knowledgeable than you.' Moses said, 'O Lord, how may I reach him?' He said, 'Take a fish in a basket, and where you lose it, there he is.'"* (al-Bukhārī 122, 4725; Muslim 2380)

The commentators are careful about the rebuke. Al-Zamakhsharī and others note that Moses's answer "I" was true within the scope of his knowledge — he did not know of Khiḍr — and that the displeasure concerned the failure to qualify the statement by referring knowledge to its source. The lesson is not that Moses lied but that the superlative "most knowledgeable" is God's alone.

This is a severe lesson delivered to the greatest of prophets, and the chapter's placement of it is deliberate. If Moses required correction on the subject of knowledge, no scholar, teacher, or preacher is exempt.

**"His Servant": Joshua**

*Li-fatāhu* — "to his young man/servant." The commentators — al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī — identify him as Joshua (Yūshaʿ ibn Nūn), the son of Nun. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that *fatā* means a youth or young man, and records the Prophetic instruction to refer to one's servant as *fatā* or *fatāt* rather than "my slave" — a point of etiquette the tradition preserved.

The word is the same as verse 13's *fityah*, the youths of the cave. The commentators connect them: Joshua is in the position of disciple, following and serving and learning; and the relationship will reverse when Moses meets Khiḍr.

**The Structural Reversal the Chapter Sets Up**

Al-Zamakhsharī notes the reversal explicitly: in the first part of the story Moses is the master and Joshua the disciple, and it is Moses who sets the goal and parameters of the journey. When Moses meets Khiḍr, Moses becomes the disciple and Khiḍr the master — and Khiḍr himself is described only as *"a servant from among Our servants"* (v. 65).

The hierarchy the chapter establishes is not one of rank but of the specific knowledge in question. Moses remains the greater prophet; Khiḍr knows something Moses does not. The chapter is precise about this and will be precise about it again at verse 65.

**"Even If I Journey for a Long Time"**

*Aw amḍiya ḥuqubā* — *ḥuqub* is a long, indefinite period; the commentators gloss it as years. The determination expressed is significant: Moses is not making a day trip. He has committed to a search of indefinite duration.

The commentators have drawn the instruction: the pursuit of knowledge justifies indefinite effort and indefinite travel. This is the basis of the Islamic tradition of *riḥlah fī ṭalab al-ʿilm* — travelling in search of knowledge — which produced the great ḥadīth collections, whose compilers crossed the entire Islamic world to verify single reports. Al-Bukhārī, Muslim, al-Tirmidhī, and the rest travelled for decades. The precedent they took was this verse: a prophet of God, already given the Torah, setting out on foot to learn from someone else.

**Majmaʿ al-Baḥrayn: The Junction of the Two Seas**

The destination is *majmaʿ al-baḥrayn* — the meeting-place of the two seas. The commentators offer identifications — the meeting of the Persian Gulf and the Mediterranean, or a location at Tangiers (al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī) — while noting that the suggestions are vague or geographically difficult, and that the location is ultimately immaterial to the story.

What matters is the concept. The Qur'an describes the two seas and their barrier at 25:53, 27:61, and 55:19–20:

> *"He let loose the two seas, meeting together. Between them is a barrier they do not transgress."* (Qur'an 55:19–20)

The *barzakh* — the barrier that is also a meeting-place — is the concept the source commentary identifies as the chapter's hidden architecture. The sleepers are in a *barzakh* between life and death; Dhu'l-Qarnayn seals a *barzakh* between two mountains; and Moses meets Khiḍr at a *barzakh* between two seas.

**The Allegorical Reading**

Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī report the reading that the two seas are Moses and Khiḍr themselves — two seas of knowledge. The freshwater river, linear and directional, symbolises rational thought and the knowledge of the Divine Law that leads to salvation; the salt sea, vast and unfathomable, symbolises the direct knowledge of God that cannot be penetrated by linear reasoning. On this reading the barrier between the seas is the boundary between the exoteric and the esoteric, which the two can meet at but not cross into each other.

Al-Qushayrī adds a further reading: the two seas are the world of spirit and the world of body, and the human soul is the junction where they meet. The commentators preserve these readings without insisting on them, and the literal sense stands alongside: a place where two bodies of water meet, and where a prophet is told to look for a teacher.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:61

> **Then when they reached the junction of the two, they forgot their fish, and it made its way to the sea, burrowing away.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sign They Did Not Recognise**

*Fa-lammā balaghā majmaʿa baynihimā nasiyā ḥūtahumā fa-ttakhadha sabīlahu fī al-baḥri sarabā* — "and when they reached the meeting-place between the two, they forgot their fish, and it took its way into the sea, making a path." The commentators note the irony that opens the story's central movement: they arrived at the destination of their journey and did not know it.

Al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Zamakhsharī report that they settled to sleep by a rock near a spring known in the transmitted accounts as the "Spring of Life" (*ʿAyn al-Ḥayāh*), not knowing that this was the junction they sought.

**The Fish Revived**

The transmitted details, gathered by al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabrisī, al-Rāzī, and al-Zamakhsharī, are specific. The fish was salted and preserved, and they had already eaten part of it. At the spring, water from it reached the fish — either because one of them splashed it while making ablution for prayer, or because the stream's water flowed near the basket — and the salted, partially eaten fish sprang back to life, overturned the basket, and made for the sea.

Al-Qushayrī draws the significance: the revival of the fish at that place is an indication of Khiḍr's presence, insofar as Khiḍr represents life that transcends the limits of time — both in his "greening" effect upon his surroundings (see v. 65) and in his apparent agelessness.

The miracle is quietly placed. The Qur'an does not announce it; it reports the fish's escape as though narrating a travel mishap, and the reader is left to notice what happened. This is characteristic of the story's method: the extraordinary is embedded in the ordinary, and the reader who is not paying attention will miss it, exactly as the travellers did.

**"Burrowing Away"**

*Fa-ttakhadha sabīlahu fī al-baḥri sarabā* — *sarab* is a tunnel, a burrow, a passage. Al-Jalālayn and al-Qurṭubī report the remarkable detail: although one would expect a tunnel to the sea to fill with water flowing inward, the sea water was miraculously held back from the passage, allowing the fish to travel through it.

The image is one of the Qur'an's most vivid: a fish cutting a dry tunnel through the sea. The ḥadīth in al-Bukhārī (4725) describes the water standing up like an arch or a canopy on either side of the fish's path, and reports that when Moses and Joshua returned, they found the trace of the passage.

**Why the Forgetting Matters**

The verse says *"they forgot their fish"* — both of them. Verse 63 will reveal that Joshua witnessed the miracle and then forgot to report it, and that the forgetting was itself divinely arranged: *"and none made me forget to mention it except Satan."*

The commentators have drawn a lesson from the arrangement. The sign was given, and the means of noticing it was removed, so that the travellers would pass on — and so that their hunger (v. 62) would bring them back. The story's structure is a lesson in providence: the destination is reached, missed, and returned to, and the missing is part of the arriving.

The practical instruction is one the spiritual tradition has drawn consistently: the place you are looking for is often the place you have already passed through, and the ordinary needs that make you turn back are not interruptions of the search but instruments of it.

**The Spring of Life and Its Significance**

The transmitted reports gathered by al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Ṭabrisī name the spring *ʿAyn al-Ḥayāh* — the Spring of Life — and the commentators connect it to the water of Paradise mentioned in the ḥadīth literature and to the Qur'anic description of the Garden's rivers. Al-Qushayrī draws the significance for the story: the revival of a salted, partially eaten fish at that place is an indication of Khiḍr's presence, because Khiḍr represents life that transcends the limits of time.

The detail is more than decorative. The chapter's three narratives are all concerned with the overcoming of time: sleepers preserved across three centuries, a servant whose life extends beyond ordinary spans, a barrier that holds until the Hour. The fish reviving at the spring is the chapter's smallest instance of the same theme — a dead thing restored to life at the exact place where the seeker is looking for a teacher whose defining characteristic is life.

**The Instruction About Missing What One Seeks**

The commentators have drawn a lesson that the spiritual tradition has valued extensively. The travellers reached their destination and did not know it. They slept at the spring, the sign occurred within arm's reach, and they walked on. The sign was not withheld; the perception of it was.

This is a common structure in the pursuit of anything difficult. The person seeking a teacher, a vocation, an answer, or a reconciliation frequently passes through the place where it was available and does not recognise it, because the recognition requires a form of attention he has not yet developed. The verse does not treat the missing as a catastrophe. It treats it as a stage: the sign occurs, is missed, produces a need (hunger, v. 62), and the need brings the seeker back.

The commentators have noted the mercy in the arrangement. Had the fish not escaped — had the sign not occurred — there would have been nothing to return to. The missing presupposes the finding, and the return presupposes both.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:62

> **Then when they had passed beyond, he said to his servant, "Bring us our meal. We have certainly met with weariness on this journey of ours."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hunger That Turns Them Back**

*Fa-lammā jāwazā qāla li-fatāhu ātinā ghadāʾanā laqad laqīnā min safarinā hādhā naṣabā* — "and when they had passed beyond, he said to his young man, 'Bring us our meal; we have certainly met with weariness from this journey of ours.'" Al-Ṭabrisī and al-Zamakhsharī note the suggestion that God induced Moses's hunger in order to remind him of the fish and so bring him back to the junction.

The mechanism is worth noting because it is characteristic of the story's whole method. The turn back is not produced by a revelation, a vision, or an angel. It is produced by *hunger* — the most ordinary of bodily signals. Al-Qushayrī and the commentators have observed that providence frequently works through needs rather than through announcements.

**"We Have Met With Weariness"**

*Naṣab* is fatigue, toil, exhaustion. The commentators note the significance: Moses does not feel the weariness until *after* he has passed the junction. Al-Qushayrī's reading, preserved in the tradition, draws the allegory: while at the junction, Moses was sustained like a child in the womb, needing nothing; his passing beyond symbolises the soul's birth into worldly existence and its separation from its source of nourishment, and the weariness is the weariness of that separation.

The reading is homiletic rather than strictly exegetical, but it identifies something real in the narrative's structure. The place of meeting is a place of rest; leaving it produces the fatigue that makes return necessary.

**Why the Verse Is Necessary to the Story**

Structurally, verse 62 does the work of getting the travellers back. Without the hunger, they would have continued and the story would not happen. The Qur'an could have accomplished this with a direct revelation — "return to the place where you lost the fish" — and it does not. Instead it uses a meal.

The commentators have noted the lesson. The means by which God directs His servants are frequently invisible to them at the time. Moses experienced a hunger; the reader is told that the hunger was a navigation. This is the same principle the story will teach explicitly at verses 79–82, when Khiḍr explains the hidden reasons behind three apparently indefensible acts. The chapter is training the reader to suspect that what looks like an ordinary event may be an instrument.

**The Servant's Position**

The verse also advances the relationship between Moses and Joshua. Moses addresses him as *fatā* — the same term as verse 60 — and asks him to bring the meal. The relationship remains that of master and disciple, and Joshua's failure to report the fish (v. 63) will be delivered in this subordinate position. The commentators note the delicacy of the exchange that follows: Joshua must tell the greatest prophet of his age that he has known something for some time and failed to mention it.

The chapter is preparing the ground for its central lesson — that knowledge is distributed in ways that do not correspond to rank — by first showing a young disciple holding a piece of information his master lacks.

**The Ordinary as Instrument**

The commentators have made much of the fact that the turn-back is caused by hunger. Al-Ṭabrisī records the suggestion that God induced the hunger specifically to remind Moses of the fish; al-Zamakhsharī reads the hunger as arising naturally after passing beyond the place of rest. Either way, the mechanism is a bodily need rather than a revelation.

The instruction is one the Islamic tradition has drawn repeatedly: providence usually operates through needs rather than through announcements. The person waiting for a sign to change direction frequently receives one in the form of an appetite, a fatigue, a dissatisfaction, a restlessness he cannot name. The verse legitimises attending to these as information rather than dismissing them as distraction.

This is not a claim that every urge is guidance. It is the narrower claim that the direction of a life is often set by ordinary conditions — hunger, weariness, the need for a meal — and that a person who despises the ordinary will miss the direction it carries.

**Why the Verse Reports the Weariness**

*Laqad laqīnā min safarinā hādhā naṣabā* — "we have met with weariness from this journey of ours." The commentators note that Moses states the weariness to his servant, in the plural, as a shared experience. There is no complaint in it and no suggestion of turning back; it is an observation offered as a reason to eat.

The detail humanises the greatest of the five messengers at the moment before his instruction. He is tired. He wants food. He does not know he has already been to the place he is looking for. The chapter's account of the pursuit of knowledge begins with a prophet who is hungry, tired, and mistaken about his own location — and the reader is thereby told what kind of person this story is for.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:63

> **He said, "Didst thou see? When we took refuge at the rock, indeed I forgot the fish—and naught made me neglect to mention it, save Satan—and it made its way to the sea in a wondrous manner!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Didst Thou See?" — Not a Question**

*Qāla a-raʾayta idh awaynā ilā al-ṣakhrati fa-innī nasītu al-ḥūt* — al-Ṭabrisī notes that the opening phrase is not genuinely interrogative. Joshua knew that Moses had not witnessed the fish's escape; the phrase is his way of introducing the marvel. The Arabic *a-raʾayta* functions here as an attention-opening formula: "consider this," "have you noticed."

There is a delicacy in the exchange the commentators note. Joshua must inform the greatest prophet of his age that he has been carrying a piece of information for some time and failed to mention it. The formula softens the disclosure.

**"Indeed I Forgot the Fish"**

The commentators — al-Jalālayn, al-Rāzī — record that Joshua, like Moses, knew they were seeking the junction of the two seas, and that according to some reports he even knew the loss of the fish was to be the sign of arrival. Yet he fell asleep after witnessing the escape and forgot the matter upon waking.

The forgetting is thus not negligence but sleep — and verse 63 attributes it to Satan, while verse 63's own phrasing leaves room for the reading that the forgetting was within the divine arrangement. Al-Qurṭubī reports that Joshua offered the excuse that Satan had caused him to neglect mentioning it.

There is an important theological point embedded in this detail. Joshua is not rebuked. The Qur'an records his oversight, records his explanation, and moves on. The commentators have drawn the general ruling, which the ḥadīth literature states explicitly. Ibn Mājah (2043) and al-Bayhaqī record:

> *"Truly God has absolved my community of mistakes, forgetfulness, and what they are compelled to do."* (Ibn Mājah 2043; al-Ṭabarānī; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The verse enacts the principle: forgetting is reported, not punished.

**"It Made Its Way to the Sea in a Wondrous Manner"**

*Wa-ttakhadha sabīlahu fī al-baḥri ʿajabā* — the word *ʿajab* returns from verse 9's *"a marvel among Our signs."* The chapter uses the same noun for the sleepers and for the fish, and the echo is significant: both are signs, and both are signs that the observers initially failed to register.

Joshua's marvel at the fish is now explicit, and it is the moment of recognition. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī note that Moses, on hearing the account, either immediately realised or had been apprised beforehand that the place where the fish was lost was where he would meet the servant more knowledgeable than himself.

**The Structure of the Recognition**

The chapter has arranged the recognition in three stages: the sign occurs (v. 61), the sign is missed (v. 62), and the sign is reported (v. 63). Each stage is necessary. Had the travellers noticed the fish immediately, they would have recognised the junction at once and the story would have proceeded without the return journey — and without the lesson that the return journey carries.

Al-Qushayrī's allegorical reading, preserved in the tradition, sees Moses's retracing of his steps as the spiritual journey back to God — reversing the "fall" into worldly existence, a return to the *fiṭrah*, the primordial nature in which the human being was created (30:30). The reading captures something structural: the journey to knowledge here requires a return, and the return is not a failure but the path.

**The Attribution to Satan and Its Limits**

The commentators discuss Joshua's statement that Satan made him neglect to mention the fish. Al-Qurṭubī reports the reading that the attribution is an excuse offered for the oversight; others read it as an accurate description of how forgetfulness operates. The ḥadīth literature supports both: the Prophet ﷺ is reported to have said of a lapse in prayer, *"That is one of the works of Satan"* (al-Bukhārī 1232), while the absolution for forgetfulness is stated absolutely.

The commentators resolve the tension in the standard way: the attribution to Satan describes the *occasion*, while the absolution covers the *accountability*. A person may be genuinely unaccountable for a lapse and the lapse may still have been the work of an adversary. Neither fact cancels the other, and the verse preserves both by reporting Joshua's explanation without endorsing or rejecting it.

**The Marvel as the Content of the Report**

*Wa-ttakhadha sabīlahu fī al-baḥri ʿajabā* — the commentators note that Joshua's report is not merely informational. He is *marvelling* — the word *ʿajab* carries the astonishment as well as the description. The verse thereby does two things at once: it transmits the fact and transmits the appropriate response to it.

This is characteristic of the Qur'an's narrative method. The reader is not only told what happened but shown how a witness reacted, and the reaction is part of the instruction. The chapter opened by asking whether the sleepers were *"a marvel among Our signs"* (v. 9) and concluded that the ordinary order is a greater marvel. Here the marvel is a fish, and the witness's astonishment is the correct proportion: astonished at the event, and not thereby blinded to the greater wonder of the order it interrupted.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:64

> **He said, "That is what we were seeking!" So they turned back, retracing their steps.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"That Is What We Were Seeking"**

*Qāla dhālika mā kunnā nabghi* — "that is what we were seeking." The verb *baghā* means to seek, to search for. The commentators note the immediacy: Moses does not deliberate, does not consult, does not verify. On hearing the account of the fish he identifies the place at once.

The phrase *dhālika* — "that" — refers to the event, not to a location. What they were seeking was not a coordinate but an occurrence: the place where the fish would be lost. The verse thereby makes a point about the nature of signs. A sign is not a place but an event that happens at a place, and the person looking for a place may walk through the sign without recognising it.

**"So They Turned Back, Retracing Their Steps"**

*Fa-rtaddā ʿalā āthārihimā qaṣaṣā* — *irtadda* means to turn back, to return; *ʿalā āthārihimā* means "upon their tracks"; *qaṣaṣā* is from *q-ṣ-ṣ*, to follow a track, to trace footsteps — the same root as the *qaṣaṣ* (narratives) of verse 13. The commentators describe the action precisely: they walked back along the exact path they had come, following their own footprints.

The detail is not incidental. The return is a *retracing*, not a general reversal. The commentators have noted the implication: the place one is seeking is often on the road one has already travelled, and finding it requires going back over the same ground with different eyes.

**The Verse's Lesson About Effort**

There is a practical instruction here that the Islamic tradition has drawn. Moses had committed to travelling *"even for a long time"* (v. 60). He travelled, he arrived, he passed on, he grew hungry, and he walked back. The whole circuit was necessary, and none of it was wasted.

The verse corrects a common assumption about seeking — that progress is linear and that backtracking is failure. In the pursuit of knowledge, and particularly in the pursuit of the knowledge that requires a teacher, the path frequently doubles back. The person who refuses to retrace will pass his destination and never know it.

**The Meeting Is Now Imminent**

Verse 64 closes the journey and verse 65 opens the encounter. The chapter has spent five verses on the approach — the commitment, the sign, the missing of the sign, the hunger, the return — for a meeting that will occupy seventeen verses. The proportion is deliberate: the chapter is more interested in how Moses came to his teacher than in the marvels the teacher performs.

**Retracing as Method**

The commentators have drawn a lesson from *fa-rtaddā ʿalā āthārihimā qaṣaṣā* that extends beyond the narrative. The verb *qaṣṣa* — to follow a track, to trace footsteps — is precise: they did not simply reverse direction but followed their own footprints back. The commentators note the implication that the return required attention to the ground they had already covered.

The Islamic tradition's practice of *murājaʿah* — review, revision, going back over material already studied — rests on this instinct. The ḥadīth at al-Bukhārī (5033) records the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"Keep reciting the Qur'an constantly, for by Him in Whose Hand is the soul of Muhammad, it escapes faster than a camel from its tether."* (al-Bukhārī 5033; Muslim 791)

The instruction is to return, repeatedly, over the same ground. The verse gives the precedent in narrative form: the destination of a search is frequently on the road already travelled, and reaching it requires the willingness to walk it again with different eyes.

**The Economy of the Approach**

The commentators have noted the proportion of the narrative. Five verses on the approach (vv. 60–64), seventeen on the encounter and its explanation (vv. 65–82). The approach includes a commitment, a destination, a sign, a missed sign, a hunger, and a return. The encounter includes a greeting, a request, a warning, a promise, three acts, three objections, a parting, and three explanations.

The proportion is instructive. The chapter spends more time on how Moses *came to* his teacher than on what the teacher did — and the marvels, extraordinary as they are, are not the point of the story. The point is the humility of the journey and the patience required to receive what it leads to. The reader who skips the approach to reach the marvels has skipped the lesson.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:65

> **There they found a servant from among Our servants whom We had granted a mercy from Us and whom We had taught knowledge from Our Presence.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"A Servant From Among Our Servants"**

*Fa-wajadā ʿabdan min ʿibādinā* — the man is not named in the Qur'an. The commentators — Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, and all the classical exegetes — identify him as Khiḍr (or al-Khaḍir, "the Green One") on the basis of ḥadīth. Al-Bukhārī (3402) records the Prophet ﷺ explaining the name:

> *"He was called al-Khaḍir because he sat upon a white dry ground — and behold, it stirred green beneath him."* (al-Bukhārī 3402)

Al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and al-Ṭabrisī report the same explanation: wherever he prayed or stood, the ground became green — renewed with life. Islamic art reflects the tradition: in Persian miniatures Khiḍr is invariably depicted in green.

The Qur'an's refusal to name him is significant. The chapter identifies him only by two things: he is a *servant*, and he has been given mercy and knowledge. Both the name and the biography are supplied by the tradition, not by the text — and the text's economy keeps the reader's attention on what he *knows* rather than on who he *is*.

**Is Khiḍr a Prophet?**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī record the dispute. The majority hold that he is a prophet; a minority hold that he is a righteous servant or an angel. The commentators marshal several arguments for prophethood:

- He received direct, unmediated knowledge from God, which is the mark of prophethood (al-Rāzī).
- Moses followed him and sought knowledge from him, and nothing can be superior to a prophet except another prophet (al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī).
- At verse 82 he states that his acts were not done on his own command — indicating they were done on God's command, which is another mark of prophethood, as at 21:27: *"They precede Him not in speech, and they act according to His command."*
- The inner meanings of his acts (vv. 78–82) can only be known through divine revelation, which is given to prophets alone (al-Qurṭubī).

The mainstream position accepts the first and third arguments as decisive: an act that outwardly violates the revealed law cannot be licit unless performed under direct divine command, and direct divine command is prophecy.

**"A Mercy From Us"**

*ātaynāhu raḥmatan min ʿindinā* — al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and al-Zamakhsharī understand the mercy as prophethood, or as a spiritual perfection that allows him to remain unencumbered by base matter, bestowing perpetual life. Al-Qushayrī reads it as the latter.

The commentators note the echo with verse 10: the youths of the cave prayed *"grant us mercy from Thy Presence"* (*min ladunka raḥmah*); Khiḍr is described as having been granted *"a mercy from Us"* (*min ʿindinā*). The chapter's two accounts of divinely given knowledge use the same vocabulary, and the connection is deliberate: what the youths asked for in prayer, Khiḍr possesses as a gift.

**"Knowledge From Our Presence"**

*Wa ʿallamnāhu min ladunnā ʿilmā* — the phrase *min ladunnā* is the Qur'an's term for what comes directly from God rather than through ordinary channels, and it is the source of the technical term *ʿilm ladunī*, "knowledge from the Presence." Al-Ghazzālī and other Sufis use the phrase for the knowledge acquired through divine unveiling (*mukāshafah*) rather than through acquisition; al-Rāzī records this usage. The Baghdādī master Junayd (d. 298) described Khiḍr's knowledge as *"the unveiling of lights from the recesses of the Unseen."*

Al-Jalālayn, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Zamakhsharī gloss the knowledge as knowledge of the Unseen — the inner realities of things, revealed directly, without a human teacher, through divine inspiration rather than human effort.

**Two Seas of Knowledge**

The tradition preserves Khiḍr's own statement of the relationship, recorded by Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabrisī: God had given Khiḍr knowledge He had not given Moses, and had given Moses knowledge He had not given Khiḍr. Hence the commentators' reading of the two seas as the two prophets.

Al-Sulamī, in his commentary on verse 66, states the balance precisely: Moses was more knowledgeable than Khiḍr in what Moses had taken from God; Khiḍr was more knowledgeable in other ways. And Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabrisī preserve the report that Khiḍr, watching a sparrow by the water, said to Moses:

> *"My knowledge and your knowledge, compared with the knowledge of God, is nothing but what this sparrow takes in its beak from the sea."*

The saying is the chapter's answer to the occasion of the story. Moses was rebuked for saying "I am the most knowledgeable"; the correction was not that he was less than he thought in the absolute, but that the superlative belongs to God. The sparrow image makes the point at a scale that no prophet can dispute.

**The Question of Khiḍr's Life**

Al-Zamakhsharī records reports placing Khiḍr in the time of the legendary Persian king Faraydūn, at the beginning of Dhu'l-Qarnayn's era, and in the time of Moses. The tradition has widely held that Khiḍr remains alive, and he occupies a significant place in Sufi practice as a figure who guides and initiates seekers — Ibn ʿArabī (d. 1240) claimed to have received instruction from him.

The commentators differ on the question, and it is not settled by the Qur'an. What the text establishes is the encounter with Moses; what the tradition adds is the biography. The distinction matters, and the classical scholars were careful to observe it.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:66

> **Moses said unto him, "Shall I follow thee, that thou mightest teach me some of that which thou hast been taught of sound judgment?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Request, and Its Manners**

*Qāla lahu Mūsā hal attabiʿuka ʿalā an tuʿallimani mimmā ʿullimta rushdā* — al-Rāzī draws out what the request reveals: Moses's genuine quest for inner knowledge for its own sake, his absence of pride, and his willingness to humble himself before one more knowledgeable despite his own status as a prophet.

The phrasing is worth examining word by word, because the etiquette is the lesson. *Hal attabiʿuka* — "may I follow you?" — a question, not a statement; Moses asks permission. *ʿAlā an tuʿallimanī* — "on condition that you teach me" — the purpose is stated openly. *Mimmā ʿullimta* — "some of what you have been taught" — the preposition *min* is partitive: not all, but some. Moses does not ask for the whole of Khiḍr's knowledge; he asks for a portion. *Rushdā* — sound judgment, right guidance — the same word the youths used in their prayer at verse 10.

Al-Rāzī connects the story to the chapter's occasion: the Quraysh were told they could test Muhammad's prophethood by his knowledge of the sleepers. The account of Moses and Khiḍr makes clear that even the greatest prophets do not possess all knowledge — for, as 12:76 states, *"above every possessor of knowledge is One more knowing."*

**The Rebuke Embedded in the Scene**

Al-Rāzī also notes the contrast with the Makkan idolaters. Their arrogance and rejection of revelation stand against Moses's humility and sincerity on meeting Khiḍr. The chapter sets the two postures side by side: a prophet who travels for weeks to learn, and notables who refuse to sit with the poor.

The instruction generalises. The measure of a person's relation to knowledge is not how much he has but whether he is willing to be taught. Moses had the Torah, direct speech with God, and the leadership of a nation; he asked to follow a man he had never met.

**The Islamic Tradition of Seeking Knowledge**

The verse generated an entire discipline. The ḥadīth literature is full of the virtue of travelling for knowledge, and the practice of *riḥlah fī ṭalab al-ʿilm* — the journey in search of knowledge — became one of the defining institutions of Islamic civilisation. Al-Bukhārī travelled across Khurasan, Iraq, the Hijaz, and Egypt over sixteen years. Al-Tirmidhī, Abū Dāwūd, al-Nasāʾī, and Ibn Mājah did the same. The great chains of transmission (*isnād*) exist because scholars crossed continents to hear a single ḥadīth from the one remaining person who had it.

Muslim (2699) records the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"Whoever travels a road in search of knowledge, God will make easy for him a road to the Garden."* (Muslim 2699)

The precedent for all of it is this verse: a prophet of God, already given revelation, saying to an unknown servant, *"may I follow you, that you may teach me?"*

**"Shall I Follow Thee": The Position of the Disciple**

The verb *ittabaʿa* — to follow — is the verb of discipleship. Moses does not ask to sit with Khiḍr, or to converse with him, or to exchange views. He asks to *follow*, which means to go where the other goes and to accept the direction of the journey from him.

The commentators — al-Qushayrī, al-Rāzī — note that this establishes the terms of the whole encounter, and that Moses's subsequent difficulty (vv. 71–78) is precisely a difficulty with following. He has agreed to a relationship whose requirement he is not yet able to meet, and the story is the account of his discovering that.

**Rushdā and the Kind of Knowledge Sought**

The commentators note that Moses does not ask for knowledge in general but for *rushd* — sound judgment, right direction, the capacity to find the way. The word is the same used of the youths at verse 10 (*"and grant us rushdā in our affair"*) and of Ibrāhīm at 21:51.

The commentators have drawn the significance of the choice. Moses is not asking for information; he is asking for the discernment that directs action. The distinction is the verse's lesson, and it explains why the subsequent episodes are about *judging* actions rather than about learning facts. Moses's difficulty throughout is not ignorance of what Khiḍr did but inability to judge it correctly.

The commentators have also noted that Moses asks for *"some of what you have been taught"* — the partitive *min* — which is the etiquette of the student: he does not ask for the whole, and he does not assume that his teacher's knowledge is his to command.

The tradition has drawn from the verse the principle that the seeker specifies his need and states it modestly, and that a request stated this way is more likely to be granted than one that asks for everything.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:67

> **He said, "Truly thou wilt not be able to bear patiently with me.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Statement of Fact, Not a Refusal**

*Qāla innaka lan tastaṭīʿa maʿiya ṣabrā* — "indeed, you will not be able to bear patiently with me." The construction *lan tastaṭīʿa* is emphatic: *lan* negates the future absolutely. Al-Ṭabrisī and Ibn Kathīr note that this is not a protest or a rebuff but a statement of truth and a foreshadowing of the parting at verse 78.

The commentators are careful to observe what Khiḍr is *not* doing. He is not refusing Moses. He is warning him. The distinction matters for the ethics of the scene: a teacher who knows a student is not ready for a particular teaching does the student a service by saying so in advance.

**Why Moses Cannot Bear It**

Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabrisī give the reason, and it is the theological core of the story. Moses has been sent as a prophet of the Divine Law. Khiḍr has been entrusted with acts that outwardly appear contrary to that Law but possess an inner significance. Moses, as the bearer of the Law, cannot witness apparent violations of it and remain silent — not because he lacks self-control but because his *office* requires him to object.

The commentators draw the consequence: the two have distinct and even apparently contradictory vocations, and therefore Moses cannot be Khiḍr's companion for long. Al-Ṭabrisī's formulation is precise: like the waters of the two seas that briefly commingle at the *barzakh* between them, Moses and Khiḍr can travel together for a short time, but each must ultimately go his own way so that one prophet's mission is not compromised by the other's.

**The Necessity of the Parting**

This is the story's most important theological claim, and it is worth stating plainly. The chapter is not teaching that esoteric knowledge supersedes the Law. It is teaching that the two operate in different registers and that a person in one register cannot function in the other's. Moses is not demoted; he is *out of place*. And Khiḍr's vocation, which the commentators describe as exclusively hidden and secret in contrast to the prophets' public proclamation, cannot be exercised under the scrutiny of a law-giver.

Ibn Taymiyyah addresses the passage at length in his responses to those who used it to justify antinomianism, and his argument is the mainstream one: whatever Khiḍr did was done under direct divine command specific to him, and it does not generalise into a licence for anyone else. The Law remains binding on everyone, including those who claim special knowledge. The story describes a unique divine arrangement, not a category of religious practice.

**"Patience" as the Condition of Learning**

The word *ṣabr* is the hinge of the entire encounter. It appears at verse 67, again at verse 68, in Moses's promise at verse 69, in Khiḍr's rebuke at verse 72, and in the final parting at verse 78. The chapter is teaching that patience is not merely a virtue adjacent to learning but its *precondition*: the person who cannot withhold judgment until he understands cannot be taught anything that requires understanding.

This is why Khiḍr's warning is not discourtesy. He is identifying the exact faculty whose absence will end the apprenticeship — and naming it before the first test, so that Moses knows what is at stake.

**Patience as the Faculty Being Tested**

The commentators have observed that *ṣabr* is the hinge of the entire episode, and its recurrence — verses 67, 68, 69, 72, 75, 78, 82 — marks it as the subject rather than the setting. The chapter is not primarily a story about three strange acts; it is a story about whether a person can withhold judgment long enough to be taught.

The Islamic tradition treats *ṣabr* as one of the two halves of religion, the other being *shukr*, gratitude. Ibn al-Qayyim's *ʿUddat al-ṣābirīn* devotes an entire work to the classification of patience: patience in obedience, patience against disobedience, and patience under decree. Moses's difficulty in this story is the third kind — patience under a decree whose wisdom is not disclosed — and the commentators have noted that it is the hardest, because the other two at least have a stated reason.

**What the Warning Does for the Reader**

The commentators note that Khiḍr's warning is delivered *before* the first act, and that its function is not only to inform Moses but to prepare the reader. The person who hears "you will not be able to bear this" and then watches the bearer fail has learned something he could not have learned from the failure alone: that the difficulty was foreseen, named, and accepted anyway.

This is why the story does not diminish Moses. He was warned, he undertook the condition anyway (v. 69), and he failed — and the failure is reported in revelation, which means God willed it to be reported. The commentators have drawn the conclusion that no subsequent student need be ashamed of finding the same thing hard, and that the story exists partly to make the difficulty discussable.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:68

> **And how canst thou bear patiently that which thou dost not encompass in awareness?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reason Given**

*Wa kayfa taṣbiru ʿalā mā lam tuḥiṭ bihi khubrā* — "and how can you be patient with what you have no acquaintance with?" The noun *khubr* denotes intimate, tested knowledge — knowledge gained through acquaintance with a thing, as against knowledge gained through report or inference. The verb *aḥāṭa bi-* means to encompass, to comprehend fully.

Khiḍr's point is epistemological: patience with a thing requires some grasp of it, and Moses has no grasp of what he is about to see. Al-Rāzī and al-Qushayrī connect the phrase to the Qur'anic principle at 2:255:

> *"And they encompass nothing of His knowledge, save what He wills."* (Qur'an 2:255)

What any person encompasses of divine knowledge is a matter of God's will and giving. Moses has been taught what is just and righteous with regard to the outward and apparent reality of things; he has not been given the knowledge of the Unseen granted to Khiḍr (al-Qushayrī).

**The General Principle of Patience and Understanding**

The verse states something true of all learning, and it is worth isolating. A person cannot be patient with a procedure he does not understand. Watch someone perform a task whose purpose is hidden from you, and every step will look like an error. The medical student watching surgery for the first time cannot bear it; the surgeon performing it is calm. The difference is not temperament but *khubr*.

Khiḍr is therefore not describing a personal limitation of Moses but a structural feature of the situation. Any observer without the relevant knowledge would react as Moses will react. The verse is not a criticism; it is a diagnosis.

**The Practical Consequence for Discipleship**

Al-Qushayrī and al-Rāzī draw the implication for the student–teacher relation, which verse 70 will formalise. Since the student cannot evaluate what he does not yet understand, the only workable arrangement is that the teacher determines when explanation is given. The student's task is to follow and to wait; the teacher's task is to disclose at the right time.

This is the logic of every genuine apprenticeship. The apprentice who demands the reason for every instruction before complying will never learn the craft, because the reasons become intelligible only after the practice has been internalised. The verse identifies the difficulty honestly — and Moses's response in verse 69 will show how hard it is to accept.

**The Sufi Reading**

Al-Qushayrī adds a further dimension: Moses, unlike Khiḍr, is bound by time and the limits of the material body, which veiled him from the unseen realities granted to Khiḍr, who in his agelessness appears free of the body's encumbrance. The reading is homiletic rather than exegetical, and the mainstream commentators do not press it. What it captures is the sense that the two men operate in different conditions — which is what verse 67 has already said in plain terms.

**Khubr and the Kinds of Knowledge**

The commentators distinguish several terms for knowledge in the Qur'an and the tradition, and the verse's use of *khubr* is specific. *ʿIlm* is knowledge generally; *maʿrifah* is knowledge of a particular thing through acquaintance; *khubr* is knowledge gained through tested, intimate experience — the knowledge of one who has handled the matter. Al-Rāzī notes that the verse denies Moses *iḥāṭah*, encompassment, of this kind of knowledge.

The distinction matters for reading the story correctly. Moses is not being told that he is ignorant in general. He has the Torah, direct speech with God, and the leadership of a nation. He is being told that he lacks *this* knowledge — the tested acquaintance with the inner dimension of events — and that without it he cannot be patient.

**The Principle for All Learning**

The commentators have drawn a general principle that applies far beyond the story. Patience with a procedure requires some grasp of its purpose; without the grasp, every step looks like an error. The medical student cannot bear to watch surgery; the surgeon is calm. The apprentice cannot bear the apparently wasteful steps of the craft; the master performs them without thought. The difference is not temperament but *khubr*.

This is why genuine education requires a period of uncomprehending compliance, and why the demand to be convinced before complying is self-defeating. The person who will only follow what he already understands can never learn anything that would change his understanding. The verse states the condition honestly, and verse 70 will supply the mechanism by which it can nonetheless be met.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:69

> **He said, "Thou wilt find me patient, if God wills, and I shall not disobey thee in any matter."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Promise, and Its Qualification**

*Qāla satajidunī in shāʾa Allāhu ṣābirā wa lā aʿṣī laka amrā* — "you will find me, if God wills, patient, and I will not disobey you in any matter." Al-Qushayrī reads the confidence as evidence of the strength of Moses's preparedness to receive this knowledge and the sincerity of his seeking.

The commentators note the qualification immediately: *in shāʾa Allāh*. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabrisī connect it explicitly to verses 23–24, where the Prophet ﷺ was instructed never to promise anything about the future without acknowledging God's will.

The connection is one of the chapter's most instructive structural links. The Prophet ﷺ omitted *in shāʾa Allāh* and fifteen days of silence followed. Moses includes it — and the qualification proves accurate in a way he did not anticipate: he did not remain patient, and the "if God wills" was what made his promise honest rather than false.

There is a lesson here about the function of the phrase. *In shāʾa Allāh* is not a hedge that weakens the promise; it is the accurate description of a promise. Moses fully intended to be patient. He was not. The qualification did not express doubt about his intention but truth about his capacity.

**"I Shall Not Disobey Thee in Any Matter"**

*Wa lā aʿṣī laka amrā* — the commitment is comprehensive: not merely to accompany but to obey. The commentators note that Moses accepts the terms of discipleship in full, including the condition Khiḍr is about to state.

The verse thereby raises the stakes of everything that follows. Moses has not merely agreed to travel with Khiḍr; he has undertaken obedience. His three objections (vv. 71, 74, 77) are therefore not merely expressions of surprise but breaches of a promise — which is why Khiḍr's responses at verses 72, 75, and 78 are framed as reminders of the agreement rather than as answers to the objections.

**Why Moses's Failure Is Instructive Rather Than Damning**

The commentators are unanimous that the story does not diminish Moses. He is a prophet, and his objections are the objections of a law-giver witnessing what appears to be injustice: the sabotaging of a poor man's boat, the killing of a boy, the refusal of payment for honest work. Every one of these reactions is *correct* within the register Moses operates in.

The lesson is not that Moses was wrong to object. It is that the register he operates in cannot accommodate what Khiḍr is doing, and that a person who undertakes to learn in such a situation must either accept the terms or withdraw. The chapter is teaching about the limits of knowledge, not about the failures of prophets.

There is a further point the commentators draw. Moses's failure is *reported in revelation*, which means God willed it to be reported. The instruction required a prophet to fail visibly so that no subsequent student would be ashamed of finding the same thing difficult.

**The Qualification That Proved True**

The commentators have noted the precision of Moses's promise. He says *"you will find me patient, if God wills"* — and the qualification turned out to be the accurate part of the sentence. He fully intended to be patient; he was not; and the *in shāʾa Allāh* was what made his statement honest rather than false.

This is the practical lesson of verses 23–24 delivered in narrative form. The Prophet ﷺ omitted the phrase and fifteen days of silence followed. Moses included it and his failure was covered by it. The commentators have drawn the ruling that the phrase is not a weakening of intention but an accurate description of capacity: the speaker's will is his own, and its execution is not.

Al-Qurṭubī records the juristic discussion of the phrase's effect on oaths: a promise qualified by *in shāʾa Allāh* does not bind as an oath, because the speaker has explicitly made its fulfilment conditional on a will other than his own. The verse supplies the scriptural ground, and the story supplies the illustration.

**Why the Failure Is Reported**

The commentators address a question the verse raises: if Moses knew he might fail, why did he undertake the condition? The answer the tradition gives is that the undertaking is itself the sincere act. The seeker renews his commitment after failure because the alternative is to stop, and the renewal is not naivety but persistence.

The chapter's treatment of this is one of its most humane features. Moses fails, apologises (v. 73), and is not expelled. He fails again, undertakes the condition a third time with explicit terms (v. 76), and only then does the apprenticeship end — and even then the full explanation is given. The story does not treat a broken undertaking as disqualifying. It treats it as part of what learning costs.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:70

> **He said, "If thou wouldst follow me, then question me not about anything, till I make mention of it to thee."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Condition Stated**

*Qāla fa-ini ttabaʿtanī fa-lā tasʾalnī ʿan shayʾin ḥattā uḥditha laka minhu dhikrā* — "if you follow me, do not question me about anything until I make mention of it to you." The commentators identify this as the formal condition of the apprenticeship.

The grammar is emphatic: the prohibitive *lā* with the *nūn* of emphasis (*tasʾalannī* in some readings), and the indefinite *shayʾ* — "anything at all." The condition admits no exceptions.

**The Etiquette of the Student**

Al-Qushayrī observes that the request establishes the proper etiquette of a student toward a teacher and a disciple toward a master. Having placed his trust in Khiḍr as guide, it is for Moses only to follow; it is not for him to seek out truths until the teacher determines that the time has come. Al-Rāzī adds the complementary point: the words also express the teacher's responsibility to take account of the student's needs and capacities in deciding when to disclose.

The arrangement is the standard one in every traditional discipline, and it exists for a reason the story demonstrates. Premature explanation destroys the lesson. A student told in advance why the ship will be holed will not experience the discipline of withholding judgment, and the withholding is the thing being taught.

**Why the Condition Is Necessary**

Verse 68 established that Moses cannot be patient with what he does not encompass. Verse 70 provides the mechanism by which he might nonetheless learn: not by understanding in advance, but by deferring the question. The condition converts an impossible requirement (understand before you object) into a possible one (wait until the explanation is offered).

This is the structure of faith in its most general form, and the chapter knows it. The believer is not required to comprehend divine wisdom before obeying it; he is required to obey and to trust that the explanation exists. Verse 82 will supply the explanation for all three acts, and it will be sufficient — but it comes at the end, not the beginning.

**The Condition and the Law**

Ibn Kathīr and the commentators address an obvious difficulty: Moses is a prophet of the Law, and the Law requires him to prevent wrongdoing. How can he agree not to object?

The resolution the commentators offer is that the agreement covers *questioning* — asking for the reason — and that the promise is a personal undertaking within a specific pedagogical relationship, not a suspension of the Law. Khiḍr's acts are licit because they are performed under direct divine command, which Moses does not yet know. The story's structure requires the gap: if Moses knew, there would be nothing to learn.

Ibn Taymiyyah's insistence on this point is the mainstream position: nothing in the story licenses any person to act outside the revealed law on the grounds of possessing hidden knowledge. Khiḍr acted under a specific divine instruction that no one else has received.

**The Teacher's Responsibility in the Condition**

Al-Rāzī notes that the condition, while addressed to the student, also defines the teacher's obligation. By undertaking to explain at a time of his own choosing (*ḥattā uḥditha laka minhu dhikrā*), Khiḍr accepts responsibility for the timing of the disclosure. The student's task is to wait; the teacher's task is not to withhold indefinitely but to speak when the student can bear it.

The commentators have drawn the ruling for religious instruction. A teacher who withholds explanation as a display of authority has violated the arrangement; a teacher who explains prematurely has destroyed the lesson. The balance is the craft of teaching, and the story exhibits both sides of it — the withholding through three acts, and the full disclosure at verse 78.

**Why Moses Could Accept the Condition**

The commentators note that Moses accepts a condition that is, on its face, extraordinary: he is a prophet bearing a revealed law, and he undertakes not to object to what he sees. Al-Qushayrī reads the acceptance as evidence of the strength of his preparedness and the sincerity of his seeking.

The acceptance is possible because of what the condition does *not* require. Moses is not asked to approve the acts, to abandon his judgment, or to concede that they are right. He is asked not to *question* them — to withhold the demand for an account until an account is offered. The commentators have distinguished this carefully from the suspension of moral judgment, which no prophet could undertake and which the story does not describe. The Law remains the Law; the question is deferred, not withdrawn.

The distinction is the one Ibn Taymiyyah pressed against those who read the story as licensing antinomianism: deferring a question within a pedagogical relationship is not the same as declaring the law inapplicable, and the story never suggests the second.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:71

> **So they went on till, when they had embarked upon a ship, he made a hole therein. He said, "Didst thou make a hole in it in order to drown its people? Thou hast done a monstrous thing!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses's Objection**

*Qāla a-kharaqtahā li-tughriqa ahlahā laqad jiʾta shayʾan imrā* — "did you make a hole in it to drown its people? You have done a grave thing!" The word *imr* denotes something enormous, monstrous, calamitous — the same register as verse 5's *kaburat kalimatan*, "a monstrous word."

The commentators are clear that Moses's objection is entirely reasonable within the law he bears. Sabotaging a vessel carrying passengers is attempted destruction of life, and the Law forbids it absolutely. Ibn Kathīr notes that Moses's reaction is that of a law-giver witnessing an apparent violation of the law he was sent to establish.

**The Structure of the Test**

The chapter has arranged the three acts in ascending order of difficulty. The first — damaging property — is the least grave. The second — killing a boy — is the gravest. The third — working without payment for people who refused hospitality — is the mildest, and the commentators note that it is the one that finally ends the apprenticeship.

Al-Qushayrī's observation is the sharpest in the commentary tradition: Moses's first two protests concerned the welfare of *others* — the people of the ship, the boy. His third protest arose from his own hunger and desire. The comment is not a criticism of Moses but an observation about the sequence: the last objection is the one that reveals the objection is no longer about principle.

**Why the Objection Is Not a Failure of Faith**
