The commentators are unanimous that the story does not indict Moses. He has been given the Law, and the Law says: do not destroy the property of the poor, do not kill the innocent, do not let the unjust go unpaid. Every one of his objections applies the Law correctly. The story's point is not that the Law is wrong but that a register exists in which the Law's outward application does not exhaust the matter — and that access to that register is given to whom God wills, not to whoever demands it.

Ibn Taymiyyah's insistence on the point is the mainstream position: the story does not create a category of religious action outside the Law. Khiḍr acted under specific divine instruction. Nobody else has that instruction.

**The Allegorical Reading**

Al-Qushayrī and al-Kāshānī offer the reading that the ship symbolises the body, which carries the individual across the sea of material existence and enables worship; Khiḍr's breaking of it represents the way the body's hold upon the soul is loosened through spiritual exercise and asceticism. The reading is homiletic and the commentators do not insist on it. What it captures is the sense that the first lesson concerns the relation to one's own instruments.

**The Ship's Owners and the Structure of the Act**

The commentators note a detail that sharpens the difficulty. Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabrisī, and al-Zamakhsharī report that the ship's owner recognised Khiḍr as a prophet and carried the two travellers free of charge. The people about to be harmed are people who have just done them a kindness — which removes any reading of the act as retribution and leaves it as what it appears to be: an unprovoked damage to a benefactor's property.

This is why Moses's objection is not merely legal but moral. He is not applying a technical rule; he is reacting to an apparent betrayal of hospitality, which in Arabian culture was among the most sacred of obligations. The Prophet ﷺ said, as recorded by al-Bukhārī (6018) and Muslim (48): *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him honour his guest."* The act violates the obligation from the other side.

**The Lesser Harm Before It Is Explained**

The commentators note that the principle of choosing the lesser of two harms (al-Rāzī's reading at v. 79) is invisible to the observer at the moment of the act. Moses sees one harm — the hole — and no second harm to compare it against, because the second harm (the king's seizure) is future and unknown to him. His judgment is therefore correct on the evidence available and incorrect on the evidence available to Khiḍr.

This is the structural point of the episode, and it generalises. A person evaluating an action from outside typically sees only the harm it does, because the harm it prevents has not yet been prevented and is therefore invisible. Every preventive act looks like an unprovoked injury until the prevented injury is identified. The verse places the reader in Moses's position deliberately, so that he experiences the difficulty rather than being told about it.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:72

> **He said, "Did I not say unto thee that thou wouldst not be able to bear patiently with me?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reminder, Not the Reproof**

*Qāla a-lam aqul innaka lan tastaṭīʿa maʿiya ṣabrā* — the phrasing is identical to verse 67 with the addition of the interrogative prefix and the reference to the prior statement. Khiḍr does not explain, does not defend, does not answer the objection. He points back to his own prediction.

Al-Qushayrī reads the reminder as a warning that one's resolve must be stronger to follow such a path. The commentators note what Khiḍr does *not* do: he does not offer the explanation. The explanation is withheld until verse 79, and the withholding is the pedagogy.

**Why the Explanation Is Withheld**

The reason is the one verse 70 established: the teacher determines when the disclosure comes. A premature explanation would end the lesson. Moses is being asked to do something genuinely difficult — to witness acts that violate everything he stands for and to say nothing — and the difficulty is the training.

There is a further reason the commentators identify. Had Khiḍr explained the ship immediately, Moses would have learned the reason for one act and would have had no reason to withhold judgment on the next. The withholding forces the development of a disposition, not the accumulation of information.

**The Repetition as Structure**

The verse is the first of two identical reminders (see v. 75). The repetition is structural: the chapter marks each failure with the same sentence, so that the reader experiences the accumulating weight of the promise being broken. By the third time, Khiḍr does not remind — he parts (v. 78).

The escalation is the lesson. A person who has been told twice that he cannot bear something, and who has twice found that he cannot, has learned something that no amount of warning could teach.

**The Withheld Explanation as Pedagogy**

The commentators have drawn the lesson that a teacher who explains on demand teaches information, while a teacher who withholds explanation teaches a disposition. Khiḍr is teaching the second. The explanations all arrive together at verses 79–82, after the apprenticeship has ended, and their timing is not arbitrary: they could not have been received earlier.

Al-Qushayrī states the point directly: understanding follows endurance rather than preceding it. The person who requires the reason before the experience will never have the experience, and the reason will remain inaccessible to him. The withholding is therefore not stinginess but the only available method.

**The Verbatim Repetition at Verse 75**

The commentators note that verse 72 is repeated verbatim at verse 75, and that the repetition is the pedagogy's rhythm. Each failure is marked with the same sentence, so that the reader experiences the accumulating weight of a promise being broken. By the third failure, Khiḍr does not remind — he parts (v. 78).

The escalation teaches what warning cannot. A person told once that he cannot bear something may attribute the failure to circumstance. Told twice, with two identical failures, he has learned something about himself that no amount of advance notice could have supplied. The chapter's method throughout is to let the reader *experience* a truth rather than be informed of it, and the repetition is the mechanism.

There is a further function the commentators note. The reminder is impersonal — Khiḍr does not say "you have failed again" but "did I not tell you?" The rebuke is directed at the accuracy of the prior prediction rather than at the failure itself, which preserves the relationship through two breaches and makes the third ending a matter of terms rather than of blame.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:73

> **He said, "Take me not to task for having forgotten, nor make me suffer much hardship on account of what I have done."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Take Me Not to Task for Having Forgotten"**

*Qāla lā tuʾākhidhnī bi-mā nasītu* — the commentators note the echo with 2:286, the closing supplication of Sūrah al-Baqarah:

> *"Our Lord, take us not to task if we forget or err."* (Qur'an 2:286)

The same verb *tuʾākhidh* — to take to task, to hold accountable — and the same plea. The connection is one of the Qur'an's quiet structural links, and it identifies the register Moses is speaking in: the register of the servant asking forbearance for a lapse.

**Was It Forgetting?**

The commentators differ on whether Moses genuinely forgot his promise. Some read it as genuine forgetfulness in the heat of the moment; others read *nasītu* as a courteous way of saying "overlooked." Al-Qurṭubī preserves both.

The distinction matters less than the posture. Whether or not the lapse was literal forgetting, Moses does not defend himself, does not argue that his objection was justified, does not appeal to the gravity of what he witnessed. He asks to be excused. The commentators have valued this as a model of how a student should respond when he has broken a term of the relationship.

**"Nor Make Me Suffer Hardship in My Affair"**

*Wa lā turhiqnī min amrī ʿusrā* — *arhaqa* means to burden, to press upon, to impose difficulty; *ʿusr* is hardship, difficulty. The plea is that Khiḍr not make the apprenticeship harder than it already is.

Al-Qushayrī reads the plea as an acknowledgment that the path is already demanding enough without the addition of rebuke. The commentators note the humility: Moses does not ask to be released from the condition; he asks that its enforcement not be made harsh.

**The Verse's Function**

Verse 73 completes the first cycle: act, objection, reminder, apology. The chapter will repeat the cycle at verses 74–76 with a higher stake, and then break it at verses 77–78. The rhythm is deliberate, and it teaches by accumulation.

**The Posture of the Apology**

The commentators have valued the verse as a model of how a student should respond to his own breach. Moses does not defend the objection — he does not argue that the ship was being sabotaged, that the passengers were endangered, that any reasonable person would have spoken. He asks to be excused.

Al-Qurṭubī notes the humility: the plea is not for the condition to be relaxed but for the *enforcement* of it not to be made harsh (*wa lā turhiqnī min amrī ʿusrā*). Moses does not ask to be released from the apprenticeship; he asks that its continuation not be made unbearable. The commentators have seen in this the disposition that makes further learning possible: the person who argues about his failure ends the relationship, while the person who acknowledges it and asks to continue keeps it open.

**"Forgetfulness" and the Absolution for It**

The commentators connect the plea to the Qur'anic principle stated at 2:286 — *"Our Lord, take us not to task if we forget or err"* — and to the ḥadīth recorded by Ibn Mājah (2043):

> *"Truly God has absolved my community of mistakes, forgetfulness, and what they are compelled to do."* (Ibn Mājah 2043; al-Ṭabarānī; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The verse enacts the principle in a narrative: the lapse is reported, explained, and not punished. Joshua's oversight at verse 63 was handled the same way. The chapter's account of failure is consistently non-catastrophic — which is one of its most practically useful features, since the fear of irreversible failure is one of the principal reasons people abandon difficult undertakings.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:74

> **So they went on till they met a young boy, and he slew him. He said, "Didst thou slay a pure soul who had slain no other soul? Thou hast certainly done a terrible thing!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Act, and the Escalation**

*Fa-nṭalaqā ḥattā idhā laqiyā ghulāman fa-qatalah* — the verse reports the killing without elaboration. Ibn Kathīr reports from the transmitted accounts that Khiḍr struck the boy's head with a stone; al-Bukhārī's version (4726) describes other means. The Qur'an's economy is characteristic: the manner is irrelevant; the act is not.

**"A Pure Soul Who Had Slain No Other Soul"**

*Qāla a-qatalta nafsan zakiyyatan bi-ghayri nafsin laqad jiʾta shayʾan nukrā* — *zakiyyah* denotes pure, innocent, unstained; the phrase *bi-ghayri nafsin* — "without [the shedding of] a soul" — is the Qur'an's legal formula for unjustified killing. Ibn Kathīr notes that Moses's objection invokes the Law directly, as at 5:32:

> *"We prescribed for the Children of Israel that whosoever slays a soul — unless it be for another soul or for working corruption upon the earth — it is as though he slew mankind all together."* (Qur'an 5:32)

The word *nukr* — a terrible, abhorrent, unrecognisable thing — is stronger than verse 71's *imr*. Ibn Kathīr notes that Moses considered this act worse than the first, and the language marks the escalation.

**The Difficulty the Verse Creates**

This is the hardest moment in the story, and the commentators address it directly. The killing of an innocent child is, within the revealed Law, the gravest of crimes. Khiḍr's act cannot be justified by any principle available to Moses, and the verse does not pretend otherwise.

The resolution comes at verses 80–81, and it is worth stating in advance so that the reader understands what the chapter is claiming: God knew what the boy would become, and His decree concerning him was executed through Khiḍr under specific instruction. The commentators — al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī — are emphatic that this is a unique divine act, not a generalisable ruling, and that no human being may act on it.

Ibn Kathīr records the discussion of whether the boy had reached puberty, and the dominant view that he had not — which is why he is described as *zakiyyah*. The tradition reports that his parents were believers and that God knew he would grow up to lead them into disbelief and oppression (vv. 80–81).

**The Allegorical Reading**

Al-Qushayrī and al-Kāshānī read the slain boy as the soul that inclines toward evil — *al-nafs al-ammārah bi'l-sūʾ* — which is "slain" when one overcomes anger and the negative passions. The reading is homiletic; the commentators preserve it without insisting.

**The Escalation and Its Purpose**

The commentators note the ordering of the three acts: property, life, labour. The middle act is the gravest, and it is placed where the apprentice has already been reminded once. The reader experiences the escalation with Moses: the first objection was answered with a reminder, and the second follows anyway.

The purpose of the escalation is to establish that the difficulty is not proportionate to the objective gravity of the act. Moses objected to the ship (a repairable harm) and to the killing (an irreversible one) with the same inability to withhold. The commentators have drawn the conclusion that what is being tested is not Moses's moral discernment — which is correct throughout — but his capacity to defer, which fails at every level equally.

The word *nukr* — a terrible, abhorrent thing — is stronger than verse 71's *imr*, and the escalation in language marks the escalation in the act. The chapter does not soften the second act or explain it away at this point; it lets the reader feel its full weight, and reserves the explanation for verse 80.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:75

> **He said, "Did I not say unto thee that thou wouldst not be able to bear patiently with me?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Sentence, a Second Time**

The verse repeats verse 72 verbatim: *qāla a-lam aqul laka innaka lan tastaṭīʿa maʿiya ṣabrā*. The addition of *laka* — "to you" — in some readings sharpens the address.

The commentators note that the repetition is the pedagogy. Khiḍr again does not explain, does not defend, does not answer the objection about the boy. He points to the prediction. The reader is being shown what it is like to be told twice that you cannot do a thing, and to find twice that you cannot.

**The Absence of Explanation as the Point**

A teacher who explains on demand teaches information. A teacher who withholds explanation teaches a disposition. Khiḍr is teaching the second, and the chapter's structure makes this unmistakable: the explanations all arrive together at verses 79–82, after the apprenticeship has ended.

The lesson generalises far beyond the story. There are things that can only be understood after they have been endured, and the demand for explanation in advance is precisely what prevents the endurance. The person who cannot act until he understands will never understand the things that require action.

**Moses's Position**

The commentators are careful to preserve Moses's dignity throughout. He is not being punished or humiliated; he is being shown the limit of his register. And the limit is not a deficiency — it is the shape of his office. The bearer of the Law cannot be indifferent to apparent violations of the Law, and a prophet who could be indifferent would be unfit for his mission.

The verse's function is therefore diagnostic rather than punitive. It tells the reader what kind of knowledge Khiḍr has and what kind Moses has, and it makes clear that neither can be converted into the other by effort.

**The Identical Sentence and Its Effect**

The verse repeats verse 72 word for word. The commentators note that the verbatim repetition is not redundancy but method: the same reminder, after a graver act, tells the reader that the standard has not shifted. Khiḍr does not say "this one was worse" or "I understand your reaction." He says the same thing, which means the condition is the same.

The effect on the reader is considerable. By the second identical reminder, the reader has begun to anticipate the third failure, and the anticipation is itself instructive: it teaches that a person's inability is *his* inability, predictable across circumstances, not an accident of the particular case. Moses is not unlucky in what he encounters; he is consistent in how he responds.

**The Absence of Defense**

The commentators note what Khiḍr does not do at this point. He does not explain the killing. He does not hint that there is a reason. He does not soften the reminder with reassurance. The withholding is total, and it is total precisely because the explanation would end the lesson.

Al-Qushayrī's observation is the key: the disciple's resolve must be stronger to follow such a path. The strength required is not intellectual but volitional — the capacity to keep a promise against the pressure of what one sees. The chapter is teaching that this capacity is a distinct faculty from moral insight, that Moses possesses the second in full and the first insufficiently, and that the two are not the same thing.

This is a distinction the tradition has maintained carefully. A person of excellent judgment may lack the capacity to defer it; a person of limited judgment may possess great capacity to follow. Neither is superior in the abstract, and the story is about the specific situation in which the second is required.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:76

> **He said, "If I question thee concerning aught after this, then keep my company no more. Thou hast attained sufficient excuse from me."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Undertaking**

*Qāla in saʾaltuka ʿan shayʾin baʿdahā fa-lā tuṣāḥibnī qad balaghta min ladunnī ʿudhrā* — "if I question you about anything after this, do not keep me as a companion; you have attained sufficient excuse from me."

The commentators note that Moses's statement, like Khiḍr's warning, anticipates the parting at verse 78. Moses himself foresees the outcome and states its terms. There is no deception in the scene: both parties know the apprenticeship is likely to end, and both continue.

**"Thou Hast Attained Sufficient Excuse From Me"**

*Qad balaghta min ladunnī ʿudhrā* — the phrase is idiomatically rich. *ʿUdhr* is an excuse, a justification; *balaghta* means you have reached, attained. The sense: you have now been given enough grounds to end this, and if it ends you will have no complaint against me and I none against you.

Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr read it as Moses's acknowledgment that he has already given Khiḍr sufficient justification to leave, having twice violated the condition. The statement is an acceptance of responsibility without self-defence — the same posture as verse 73, and the commentators have valued it as a model.

**Why the Undertaking Is Made Knowing It Will Fail**

There is something instructive in Moses making a promise he has already broken twice. The commentators have drawn the lesson: the sincere seeker renews his commitment even after failure, because the alternative is to stop. The undertaking is not naive; it is the only way forward.

The chapter's treatment of failure is one of its most humane features. Moses fails, apologises, and tries again. He is not expelled for the second failure; the third ends the apprenticeship, and even then the explanation is given. The story does not treat a broken undertaking as a disqualification. It treats it as part of the process.

**The Stakes of the Third Test**

The verse raises the stakes precisely by naming them. If Moses objects again, the relationship ends. The reader now knows that the next act will be decisive, and the chapter has arranged that the next act will be the mildest of the three — which is its own instruction. The thing that finally breaks the apprenticeship is not the gravest act but the one that touches the apprentice's own need.

**Moses Sets His Own Terms**

The commentators note that the undertaking in this verse is *Moses's*, not Khiḍr's. He is not being given another chance with the same conditions; he is defining the consequence himself — *fa-lā tuṣāḥibnī*, "do not keep me as a companion." The commentators have seen in this a model of accountability: the person who has breached a term twice should specify, in advance, what the third breach will cost.

The practice has an analogue in the Islamic tradition's treatment of oaths and undertakings. Al-Qurṭubī discusses at 5:89 the expiation for a broken oath and the seriousness with which the tradition treats spoken commitments; the Qur'an says at 17:34, *"and fulfil the covenant — truly the covenant shall be questioned."* Moses's self-imposed term is the narrative form of that seriousness.

**Why the Undertaking Is Made Anyway**

The commentators address the obvious question: why promise a third time what one has failed twice? Al-Qushayrī's answer is that the sincere seeker renews his commitment because the alternative is to abandon the search, and the renewal is not naivety but persistence.

There is a further point the verse makes. Moses's statement includes a forecast: he expects to fail. He does not say "I will not ask again"; he says "if I ask you again, do not keep me." The conditional form acknowledges the likelihood of breach while undertaking the condition nonetheless. This is a more honest posture than a confident promise, and the commentators have valued it as such: the person who knows his weakness and undertakes anyway, with terms, is in a better position than the one who promises confidently and is surprised.

The chapter's treatment of the failure is consistent with this. The third breach ends the apprenticeship — as Moses himself specified — but the explanation is still given in full. The terms are honoured by both parties, and the relationship ends cleanly rather than in recrimination.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:77

> **So they went on till they came upon the people of a town and sought food from them. But they refused to show them any hospitality. Then they found therein a wall that was about to fall down; so he set it up straight. He said, "Hadst thou willed, thou couldst have taken a wage for it."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Town That Refused Hospitality**

*Fa-nṭalaqā ḥattā idhā atayā ahla qaryatinistaṭʿamā ahlahā fa-abaw an yuḍayyifūhumā* — "so they set out, until when they came to the people of a town, they asked its people for food, and they refused to host them." Al-Rāzī reports speculations identifying the town as Antioch or as Ayla (Eilat); the Qur'an does not name it.

The commentators note the significance of the refusal. Hospitality (*ḍiyāfah*) was among the most sacred obligations in Arabian culture, and its refusal to travellers in need was a serious moral failure. Al-Bukhārī (6018) and Muslim (48) record the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him honour his guest."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 48)

The town's refusal establishes the context for what follows: Khiḍr is about to do an unremunerated kindness for people who have just refused them a meal.

**The Wall About to Fall**

*Fa-wajadā fīhā jidāran yurīdu an yanqaḍḍa fa-aqāmah* — "and they found in it a wall that wanted to collapse, and he set it up." The commentators note the Qur'an's attribution of *wanting* to the wall — *yurīdu an yanqaḍḍa* — a vivid personification that describes the wall's state of imminent collapse. The verb *aqāmah* means he raised it, set it upright, restored it.

Al-Bukhārī (4726) records that Khiḍr set his hands against it and it stood straight — a detail the tradition preserves and the Qur'an omits.

**Moses's Third Objection**

*Qāla law shiʾta la-ttakhadhta ʿalayhi ajrā* — "if you had wished, you could have taken a wage for it." The commentators — al-Qurṭubī, Ibn Kathīr — note that although this is not phrased as a question, it contains an implicit one: why did you not ask for payment, when we are hungry and these people refused us?

Al-Qushayrī's observation is the sharpest: Moses's earlier protests concerned the welfare of others; this one arose from his own hunger and desire. The comment identifies the mechanism by which the last objection differs from the first two. It is not less justified in principle — a worker deserves his wage — but it is motivated differently.

**The Moral Lesson the Commentators Draw**

The Sufi commentators read a lesson into Moses's protest and Khiḍr's reaction: doing a good deed and then seeking worldly reward for it nullifies its spiritual merit. Spiritual virtue comes from God and should be sought for its own sake, not for worldly return (al-Qushayrī).

The principle is standard in Islamic ethics and it is grounded in the concept of *ikhlāṣ* — sincerity, purity of intention. The ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī (1) and Muslim (1902) from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb is the foundation:

> *"Actions are but by intentions, and every person shall have only what he intended."* (al-Bukhārī 1; Muslim 1902)

The commentators connect verse 77 to the chapter's own statement of the criterion at verse 28 — those who *"call upon their Lord morning and evening, desiring His Face."* The desire for God's Face and the desire for a wage are the two poles, and the chapter has been moving between them since the garden parable.

**The Allegorical Reading**

Al-Qushayrī and al-Kāshānī complete the three-part allegory: if the broken ship is the body subdued through asceticism, and the slain boy is the lower soul overcome, then the repaired wall is *al-nafs al-muṭmaʾinnah* — the soul at peace (89:27) — which can be restored through the perfecting of character once the passions have been subdued. The reading is homiletic and the commentators do not insist on it.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:78

> **He said, "This is the parting between thee and me. I shall inform thee of the meaning of that which thou couldst not bear patiently:**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"This Is the Parting Between Thee and Me"**

*Qāla hādhā firāqu baynī wa baynik* — the statement is final and unargued. Al-Qushayrī notes the reversal: Moses did not have the patience to refrain from questioning, and now Khiḍr does not have the patience to keep company. The condition Moses set in verse 76 has been met, and it is met on his own terms.

The commentators — al-Qushayrī, Ibn Kathīr — draw the structural point. The junction between the two seas of knowledge, like the junction between the two physical seas, can only be a temporary meeting. Despite the brief intermingling, the two must carry out their vocations separately — like the two seas, whose distinct natures as salt and fresh must remain separated even where they mingle at the *barzakh*.

Al-Qushayrī adds a further observation: Moses wished to remain and continue learning; Khiḍr preferred to leave, preferring solitude with God to the company of created beings. The comment identifies a difference in vocation that the chapter has been describing throughout — Khiḍr's is hidden and solitary, Moses's is public and communal.

**"I Shall Inform Thee of the Taʾwīl"**

*Sa-unabbiʾuka bi-taʾwīli mā lam tastaṭiʿ ʿalayhi ṣabrā* — the word *taʾwīl* is decisive for the story's place in Islamic thought. Al-Rāzī explains it as the meaning or hidden wisdom of the acts. The term appears at 3:7 in a similar sense, and in later Islamic usage it comes to denote the inner or esoteric meaning of Qur'anic verses and ḥadīth — a usage the commentators connect to 3:7's statement that the *taʾwīl* of certain verses is known only to God.

The commentators draw the implication: if *taʾwīl* here signifies inner meaning, then Khiḍr's use of the term suggests that there is a hidden dimension to events and to revelation, known only to God and to those given knowledge from His Presence (v. 65).

This is the theological ground of the entire Islamic tradition of *tafsīr* and *taʾwīl*, and it is also the ground of the controversies around it. Ibn Taymiyyah and the scholars who followed him insisted that legitimate *taʾwīl* is bounded by the language and the transmitted understanding, and that unbounded esoteric reading is the method of those who use the Qur'an to say what they already believe. The chapter itself sets the limit: Khiḍr's *taʾwīl* is not his own construction; it is what God informed him of (v. 82: *"I did not do it upon my own command"*).

**The Timing of the Explanation**

The commentators note that the explanation comes *at the parting*, not before. Al-Qushayrī reads this as indicating that Moses's soul had been purified and strengthened sufficiently to receive the meaning by the end of the encounter — that the difficulty he could not bear patiently was itself what prepared him to understand.

The lesson is one of the chapter's deepest. The explanation was not withheld because Moses was unworthy of it; it was withheld because it could not be received before the experience. The order is not arbitrary. Understanding follows endurance, not the reverse.

**What the Parting Does Not Mean**

The commentators are careful about what the scene does not establish. It does not establish that Khiḍr is superior to Moses — the tradition preserves the sparrow saying precisely to prevent that reading. It does not establish that esoteric knowledge supersedes the Law. It does not establish that any person may act on hidden knowledge contrary to the revealed law.

What it establishes is narrower and more useful: that God distributes knowledge in ways that do not correspond to rank, that the person who knows most in one register may know least in another, and that humility before a teacher is required even of a prophet.

**The Verse as the Chapter's Method**

The commentators note that this verse is the chapter's own statement of how it intends to be read. Khiḍr says he will explain what Moses could not bear patiently, and the explanation follows in verses 79–82 — three acts, each with a reason that was not visible at the time.

The commentators have drawn the parallel with the chapter as a whole. The reader encounters four narratives — the sleepers, the garden, Moses and Khiḍr, Dhu'l-Qarnayn — and in each case the surface event is not the point. The sleepers' story is about God's power over time; the garden's is about the source of what a person has; Khiḍr's acts are about the limits of human judgment; Dhu'l-Qarnayn's is about power rightly used.

In each case the meaning is supplied after the event, and in each case the meaning is not what the observer expected. The verse states the pattern explicitly: there is a *taʾwīl* — an interpretation, an eventual meaning — behind what appears, and it will be given, but not at the moment of appearance.

The instruction is about the reader's own patience. The person who demands the meaning of an event at the moment it occurs will misjudge it, as Moses did three times. The chapter asks for the patience it narrates.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:79

> **As for the ship, it belonged to indigent people who worked the sea. I desired to damage it, for just beyond them was a king who was seizing every ship by force.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Explanation**

*Ammā al-safīnatu fa-kānat li-masākīna yaʿmalūna fī al-baḥri fa-aradtu an aʿībahā wa kāna warāʾahum malikun yaʾkhudhu kulla safīnatin ghaṣbā* — the explanation is economical and complete. Three facts: the owners were poor working people; a king behind them was confiscating every sound vessel by force; damaging the ship made it undesirable and preserved it.

The word *masākīn* — plural of *miskīn*, the indigent, those with nothing — is significant. The ship was not a merchant's capital but the livelihood of poor labourers. Losing it would have been ruin; having it damaged was a repairable inconvenience.

**"I Desired to Damage It"**

*Fa-aradtu an aʿībahā* — the verb *arāda* expresses intention, and *aʿāba* means to render defective. The commentators note the precision of the wording: Khiḍr does not say "I damaged it" but "I desired to damage it" — the desire being the instrument through which the divine purpose was executed.

**The Moral Principle: The Lesser of Two Harms**

Al-Rāzī draws the general ruling, and it is one of the most widely applied principles in Islamic jurisprudence: when forced to choose between two harms, one chooses the lesser. Damaging the ship inflicted a lesser harm on its owners than the permanent seizure that would otherwise have occurred.

The principle — *akhaff al-ḍararayn*, the lighter of the two harms — became a foundational maxim of the *qawāʿid fiqhiyyah*, the legal maxims collected by scholars such as al-ʿIzz ibn ʿAbd al-Salām (d. 1262) in *Qawāʿid al-aḥkām fī maṣāliḥ al-anām* and al-Suyūṭī in *al-Ashbāh wa'l-naẓāʾir*. Its applications run across the tradition: the permissibility of eating carrion under starvation, of paying a bribe to avert a greater injustice, of accepting a lesser contractual loss to avoid total forfeiture.

The verse supplies the scriptural ground for all of it, and the commentators have drawn the practical instruction: a harm deliberately incurred to prevent a greater harm is not a wrong but a mercy. The person who looks only at the harm will condemn the act; the person who sees both harms will recognise the kindness.

**The Parallel With Moses's Own Infancy**

The commentators draw a connection that gives the passage its deepest resonance. Khiḍr's damaging of the ship to save it parallels the casting of the infant Moses into the river — an act that appeared likely to result in his death and was in fact the means of his deliverance from Pharaoh's order to kill every male Israelite child. The Qur'an records the command at 28:7:

> *"And We inspired the mother of Moses: 'Suckle him, and when thou fearest for him, cast him into the river and fear not, nor grieve. We shall return him to thee and make him one of the messengers.'"* (Qur'an 28:7)

Moses himself was the beneficiary of exactly the kind of act he objected to. His mother did what looked like killing to prevent killing; Khiḍr did what looked like destruction to prevent seizure. The chapter's arrangement — Moses objecting to an act structurally identical to the one that saved his life — is one of its most instructive ironies, and the commentators do not miss it.

**The Allegorical Reading**

Al-Kāshānī reads the damaged ship as the body "broken" through spiritual exercise and asceticism, so that it may not be "seized" by the lower soul inclined to evil. The reading is homiletic; the commentators preserve it without insisting.

**The King Who Seized Ships and the Reading of Tyranny**

The commentators note that the king is described as seizing every ship *ghaṣbā* — by force, usurpation. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr read this as an unjust ruler, and the detail establishes the moral context of Khiḍr's act: the damage was inflicted to protect the poor from a tyrant's confiscation.

The commentators have drawn a jurisprudential point from the arrangement. When a ruler acts unjustly, the subject may take steps to place his property beyond the ruler's reach, even at the cost of damaging it. Al-ʿIzz ibn ʿAbd al-Salām's *Qawāʿid al-aḥkām* and al-Suyūṭī's *al-Ashbāh wa'l-naẓāʾir* both discuss the principle of choosing the lesser harm, and this verse is among its scriptural grounds. The application extends to concealing wealth from an unjust levy, accepting a lesser contractual loss to avoid total forfeiture, and paying a bribe to avert a greater injustice.

**Why the Owners Are Called Masākīn**

The commentators note that the Qur'an specifies the owners as *masākīn* — the indigent — rather than as merchants or shipowners. The specification matters: the ship was not capital in a portfolio but the livelihood of poor working people, and its loss would have been ruin rather than inconvenience.

This detail completes the contrast the chapter has been building since verse 28. The Makkans asked that the poor be removed from the gathering. Khiḍr damaged a poor man's boat to save it. The chapter's valuation of the poor is consistent throughout, and the reader who has followed it from the sleepers onward will recognise the pattern: the people this story protects are the people the world overlooks.

The verse is also the chapter's answer to the accusation that religion is indifferent to material harm. Khiḍr's act caused real damage to real property, and the verse explains that the damage was the lesser of two harms — not that it did not matter. The chapter takes material loss seriously enough to require its explanation.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:80

> **And as for the young boy, his parents were believers and we feared that he would make them suffer much through rebellion and disbelief.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Explanation**

*Wa ammā al-ghulāmu fa-kāna abawāhu muʾminayni fa-khashinā an yurhiqahumā ṭughyānan wa kufrā* — "and as for the boy, his parents were believers, and we feared that he would oppress them with rebellion and disbelief." The verb *yurhiqa* — from the same root as verse 73's *turhiqnī* — means to burden, to press upon, to overwhelm.

The commentators note the shift in pronoun: *"we feared"* — the plural. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr read this as the plural of majesty, or as indicating that the knowledge was given to Khiḍr by God. The fear is not uncertainty; it is the divine knowledge that the boy would grow into rebellion and disbelief and would drag his believing parents with him.

**The Hardest Verse in the Story**

The commentators address the difficulty directly, and their treatment deserves attention. Ibn Kathīr quotes the ḥadīth:

> *"God does not decree anything for a believer save that it is better for him."*

The reasoning runs as follows. The boy was young, and his parents had seen nothing but goodness in him; to them his death could only appear a tragedy. They could not know what God had spared them. The act was, from the divine side, an act of mercy on the parents — and the commentators discuss whether it was also mercy on the boy, on the reasoning that dying before committing the sins he was destined for spared him their consequence.

**Why No One May Act on This Verse**

The commentators are emphatic, and the emphasis is necessary. Nothing in this verse licenses any human being to kill another on the grounds of foreseeing their future corruption. Khiḍr acted on knowledge given to him directly by God (v. 82: *"I did not do it upon my own command"*). No one else has that knowledge, and the Law — which is the standing instruction for all human beings — forbids the act absolutely.

Ibn Taymiyyah's insistence on this point is the mainstream position, and it addresses a real danger: throughout Islamic history, individuals and movements have claimed special knowledge as grounds for setting aside the Law. The chapter's own structure forecloses the claim, because it makes Khiḍr state explicitly that he acted under command rather than on his own initiative.

**"We Feared": The Pronoun and Its Reading**

The commentators discuss the plural in *fa-khashinā* — "we feared." Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr read it as the plural of majesty; others read it as indicating that the knowledge was conveyed to Khiḍr by God, or that Khiḍr spoke of himself and the divine command together. The commentators preserve the readings without settling the question, and the sense is unaffected: the knowledge of the boy's future was given, not inferred.

The verb *khashiya* — to fear with reverence and knowledge — is significant. It is not the fear of uncertainty but the fear that follows from knowing. Al-Rāzī notes that the Qur'an uses *khashyah* of a fear grounded in awareness, as at 35:28: *"Only those of His servants who have knowledge fear God."* Khiḍr's "fear" is the appropriate response to a knowledge he has been given.

**The Two Wills and the Limits of the Story**

The commentators identify the story as a demonstration of the distinction between God's prescriptive will — what He commands, disclosed in the Law — and His decretive will, what He brings about, which transcends human comprehension. Moses's objection rested on the first; Khiḍr's act was executed under the second. The two are not contradictory, but they operate at different levels, and access to one does not confer competence in the other.

Al-Rāzī states the jurisprudential corollary, citing the Prophetic principle: *"We judge according to the outward, and God takes charge of the inward."* The judges of the Law rule on appearances, and this is not a defect but the correct operation of the office. Khiḍr's judgments rested on the true purposes of events, which is a different faculty granted to him specifically.

The mainstream insistence on the point is necessary because the story has repeatedly been used to justify setting aside the Law on grounds of special insight. Ibn Taymiyyah's response is the standard one: Khiḍr stated at verse 82 that he did not act on his own command, and no one else possesses the command he acted under.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:81

> **So we desired that their Lord give them in exchange one who is better than him in purity, and nearer to mercy.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Their Lord"**

The phrase *rabbuhumā* — "their Lord" — is significant. The commentators note that the exchange is attributed to the parents' Lord, not to Khiḍr. Khiḍr is the instrument; the One who gives the substitute is the One who sustained the parents' faith. The wording preserves the theological point that nothing in the story is Khiḍr's independent doing.

**The Structure of Consolation**

The verse answers the human experience of loss in a form the tradition has used extensively. Ibn Kathīr's citation of the ḥadīth — *"God does not decree anything for a believer save that it is better for him"* — is the standard consolation offered in bereavement, and its scriptural ground is this verse.

The commentators are careful not to make the verse a theodicy of easy comfort. The parents' grief was real, and the verse does not say they were spared grief; it says they were spared something worse. The distinction matters: the promise is not that the believer will not suffer but that what he suffers is not the worst available outcome.

The practical instruction is the one the chapter has been teaching since verse 7. The adornment of this world — including children, whom verse 46 names specifically — is not the substance of the believer's good. The parents lost a son and received a better one, and both the loss and the replacement were within the mercy.

**What the Verse Does Not Claim**

The commentators are careful, and the care is important. The verse does not say the parents were spared grief. They grieved; the boy died; the loss was real and felt. What the verse says is that they were spared something worse — the corruption of their faith by a son who would have oppressed them into disbelief.

The distinction protects the verse from becoming a cheap comfort. The believer's promise is not immunity from suffering but the assurance that what he suffers is not the worst available outcome, and that the One who permits it is the One who described Himself at verse 58 as *"Forgiving, Possessed of Mercy."* The chapter has been building toward this assurance since verse 7, and the verse delivers it in the hardest case the story contains.

**"Nearer in Mercy"**

*Aqraba ruḥmā* — the commentators gloss *ruḥm* as mercy and as kinship-tenderness, the compassion that binds relatives. The substitute child will be closer in the specific quality the parents most needed: not merely obedient but *tender*. The commentators have noted the precision — the parents did not lose a son and receive wealth; they lost a son and received a better son.

The chapter's teaching about children is thus complete. Verse 46 named wealth and children as the adornment of this world. Verse 34 recorded the boast about them. Verse 81 shows them given, taken, and given again, with the exchange governed entirely by God and the parents' faith preserved throughout. The adornment is not condemned; it is relocated, as verse 46 said it must be.

**The Parents' Faith as the Object of Protection**

The commentators note a detail that determines the reading of the whole episode: the verse identifies the object of the divine concern as the parents' *faith*, not their comfort. God's purpose in the exchange is that the substitute be *"better in purity, and nearer to mercy"* — that is, a child who will not corrupt them.

This is consistent with the chapter's valuation throughout. The youths of the cave were preserved in their faith (v. 13: *"We increased them in guidance"*); the poor companion's faith made him superior to the rich (vv. 37–38); the believers' deeds are what carry weight (v. 105). In each case faith is the thing being protected, and material circumstance is secondary. The commentators have drawn the instruction that the believer's greatest asset is his faith, and that divine providence is ordered toward its preservation rather than toward his comfort.

**The Verse and the Doctrine of the Better Exchange**

The commentators connect the verse to the Qur'an's repeated promise of substitution:

> *"And whoever reveres God, He will appoint for him a way out, and provide for him whence he does not reckon."* (Qur'an 65:2–3)

The verse supplies the narrative instance: a specific loss, followed by a specific and better replacement, in a case where the loss appeared unqualified. The instruction is not that every loss is replaced in kind — the verse does not claim that. It is that the One who permits the loss is the One who provides the substitute, and that the believer's hope is properly placed in the Provider rather than in the preservation of any particular good.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:82

> **And as for the wall, it belonged to two orphan boys in the city, and beneath it was a treasure belonging to them. Their father was righteous, and thy Lord desired that they should reach their maturity and extract their treasure, as a mercy from thy Lord. And I did not do this upon my own command. This is the meaning of that which thou couldst not bear patiently."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Explanation**

*Wa ammā al-jidāru fa-kāna li-ghulāmayni yatīmayni fī al-madīnati wa kāna taḥtahu kanzun lahumā wa kāna abūhumā ṣāliḥā* — "and as for the wall, it belonged to two orphan boys in the city, and beneath it was a treasure belonging to them, and their father was righteous."

The explanation completes the three acts, and the commentators note the pattern: in each case the apparent harm or apparent folly conceals a benefit. The ship was damaged to be saved; the boy was killed to spare his parents; the wall was repaired without payment to preserve orphans' inheritance for them.

**"Their Father Was Righteous"**

The clause *wa kāna abūhumā ṣāliḥā* is one of the most theologically significant in the story. The commentators — Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī — draw the conclusion that God preserved the orphans' treasure *for the sake of their father's righteousness*. The parents were dead; the father's piety continued to operate on their behalf.

The principle is standard in Islamic teaching and it is grounded in this verse. The Qur'an states it at 2:133 and elsewhere; the ḥadīth literature develops it in the doctrine of the *ṣadaqah jāriyah*, the ongoing charity. Muslim (1631) records:

> *"When a person dies, his deeds cease except three: a continuing charity, a knowledge from which others benefit, or a righteous child who prays for him."* (Muslim 1631)

The verse demonstrates the principle in action. A righteous man's piety reached beyond his death to protect his children's inheritance, and the mechanism was a stranger repairing a wall in a town that refused him hospitality.

The practical instruction is one of the most encouraging in the chapter: the good a person does is not bounded by his lifetime. The reader who wonders whether his conduct matters beyond himself is given a case in which it demonstrably did.

**"A Treasure Beneath It"**

The commentators — Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī — raise the question of what the treasure was. Some suggest it may have been knowledge rather than wealth — a thing hidden or "buried" for the boys to discover when they were capable of understanding it. Al-Rāzī connects this to the earlier mention of *taʾwīl*: the hidden meaning of Khiḍr's acts, like the buried treasure, was obscured from Moses's outward vision.

The reading is not the plain sense, and the commentators do not insist on it. But the resonance is deliberate: the chapter is full of buried things — a treasure under a wall, a meaning under an act, a fish under a basket, sleepers under a mountain.

**"I Did Not Do This Upon My Own Command"**

*Wa mā faʿaltuhu ʿan amrī* — the statement is the theological seal of the entire story, and the commentators treat it as decisive. Khiḍr disclaims personal initiative in all three acts. Everything he did was under divine instruction, and the instruction was specific to him.

This is the sentence that prevents the story from becoming a licence. Ibn Taymiyyah and the mainstream commentators insist on it: the esoteric knowledge Khiḍr possessed did not make him independent of God's command; it made him *more* precisely subject to it. The person who claims hidden knowledge as grounds for autonomous action is doing the opposite of what Khiḍr did.

**Al-Rāzī's Account of the Trajectory of Knowledge**

Al-Rāzī draws a conclusion from the story that has shaped the Islamic understanding of religious learning. He observes that after Moses had perfected his knowledge of the *sharīʿah* — the Divine Law as applied to outward phenomena — God sent him to Khiḍr to learn on a more inward level. The proper trajectory, for al-Rāzī, is from mastery of the Law, which orders and judges according to appearances, to knowledge of the inward, which apprehends the true realities of things (*ḥaqāʾiq al-umūr*).

The sequence is not reversible. The person who has not mastered the Law has no standing to claim inward knowledge, and the mainstream tradition has consistently used this story to make exactly that point against those who would invert the order.

**"This Is the Taʾwīl of That Which Thou Couldst Not Bear Patiently"**

The story ends where it began — with the patience Moses could not sustain. The commentators note that the explanation is given in full, without reservation, at the moment of parting. Khiḍr does not withhold the meaning because Moses failed; he withholds it until Moses has completed the experience.

The chapter's account of knowledge is thus complete: it is given by God (v. 65), it is not coextensive with rank (vv. 60, 65), it requires patience to receive (vv. 67–68), and its inner dimension is disclosed in God's time rather than the student's (vv. 78, 82).

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:83

> **And they question thee about Dhu'l-Qarnayn. Say, "I shall recite unto you a remembrance of him."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third of the Three Questions**

*Wa yasʾalūnaka ʿan dhī al-qarnayn qul sa-atlū ʿalaykum minhu dhikrā* — the chapter now turns to the second of the Quraysh's three questions. The source of the questioning is the account Ibn Isḥāq records and the commentators transmit: the Jewish scholars of Yathrib told the delegation to ask about a man who travelled until he reached the east and the west of the earth. Al-Ṭabarī also reports that some of the People of the Book came to the Prophet ﷺ directly with the question.

The response is framed as *dhikr* — a remembrance, a mention, something to be recalled. Al-Ṭabarī notes the modesty of the framing: the Prophet ﷺ does not claim to give a complete account but a *remembrance*, a portion of what is worth remembering.

**The Name: Dhu'l-Qarnayn, "He of the Two Horns"**

The commentators gather a remarkable range of explanations, and their honesty about the uncertainty is instructive. Al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Suyūṭī, and al-Zamakhsharī report:

- He had what appeared to be two horns on his head; according to one account he was struck on the right horn and killed, then resurrected, then struck on the left and died again.
- The title refers to his rule over both Rūm (the western lands) and Persia — the two "horns" of the known world.
- It refers to his journeys, which circled both horns of the earth, the east and the west.
- He had a dream in which he drew near the sun and seized its eastern and western horns, after which the people called him by the name.
- The two sides of his head were of copper, or he wore a headdress or helmet with two horns.
- It was a title indicating bravery and fearlessness.
- It means he lived for two centuries (*qarnayn*) — though al-Suyūṭī notes this is inconsistent with the historical Alexander, who died young.

**Who Was He?**

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Suyūṭī report a ḥadīth identifying him as a youth from Rūm who built Alexandria — that is, Alexander son of Philip, Alexander the Great. Al-Rāzī records this as the dominant identification in the classical commentaries. Al-Ṭabrisī reports that some modern Islamic scholars have identified him with Cyrus the Great. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī record other positions: a righteous servant granted sovereignty, knowledge, and wisdom; a prophet; an angel.

The commentators also preserve reports that an angel ascended with him through the sky until he could see the whole earth and the sea that surrounds it; that he was one who loved God and whom God loved; and that the clouds, and even light and darkness, were placed at his service — a guiding light illuminating his path by night, darkness protecting him from behind.

The tradition's uncertainty about his identity is not a defect but a feature. The Qur'an names no king, no date, and no dynasty, and the commentators have accordingly differed for fourteen centuries without the difference affecting the lesson. The chapter is not teaching history; it is teaching what power looks like when it is held rightly.

**"Say: I Shall Recite Unto You"**

The command to *recite* rather than to *report* is characteristic. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the Prophet ﷺ is instructed to deliver what has been revealed, in the manner of revelation, and not to reconstruct an account from other sources. The phrase *sa-atlū* — "I shall recite" — is the verb of Qur'anic recitation, the same as verse 27's *"recite that which has been revealed unto thee."*

The framing is a quiet assertion of the revelation's independence. The Quraysh asked a question drawn from the traditions of the People of the Book; the answer comes as recitation, in the Prophet's ﷺ own Book, and it is not dependent on their sources for its authority.

**Why the Chapter Does Not Answer the Question**

The commentators have discussed at length why the Qur'an does not settle Dhu'l-Qarnayn's identity. Al-Ṭabarī records multiple identifications and does not adjudicate; al-Rāzī lists the candidates and observes that the Qur'an gives none of them; Ibn Kathīr transmits the reports and declines to endorse any.

The commentators have drawn the lesson from the silence. The chapter's interest is in the man's conduct, not his name, and supplying the name would have invited the reader to treat the narrative as history rather than as instruction. By naming only a title, the Qur'an directs attention to the qualities — the means given, the justice exercised, the barrier built — rather than to identification.

The same principle governs the sleepers' narrative. Verse 22 says: *"Say: My Lord knows best their number"* — the Qur'an declines to settle the number, and the commentators have read the refusal as part of the teaching.

The instruction is that the Qur'an supplies what is needed for guidance and withholds what is not, and that the reader's curiosity about the withheld matters is not a religious obligation. The chapter answers the question it was asked in the terms that serve the reader, which is not always the terms in which it was posed.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:84

> **Truly We established him in the land, and gave him the means to all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Established Him in the Land"**

*Innā makkannā lahu fī al-arḍ* — the verb *makkana* means to establish firmly, to give power and place, to enable. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Suyūṭī explain that God made the land smooth and easy for him, so that he could journey through it and bring it under his control.

The commentators note that the establishment is attributed entirely to God. Dhu'l-Qarnayn's power is not his own achievement; it is a divine grant. This is the same framing the chapter gave the gardens of verse 32 — *"We made for one of them two gardens"* — and the parallel is deliberate. Both men received power from God. The chapter is about to show what each did with it.

**"And Gave Him the Means to All Things"**

*Wa ātaynāhu min kulli shayʾin sababā* — *sabab* is a means, a cause, a rope by which one reaches a thing; the plural *asbāb* is the standard Qur'anic term for the means by which ends are attained. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Suyūṭī gloss it as knowledge of all things; al-Rāzī, al-Suyūṭī, and al-Zamakhsharī add the power and the tools with which to acquire what he desired.

Al-Rāzī's formulation is precise: since God gave him the means to all things, whenever he desired something he would follow the appropriate means to attain it. The verse thereby introduces the concept that governs the whole narrative — *sabab*, means — which will appear three more times (vv. 85, 89, 92).

**The Theology of Means**

The verse establishes a principle the chapter will demonstrate: Dhu'l-Qarnayn's achievements are not miracles in the sense of suspensions of natural order. They are accomplished *through means* — routes, tools, labour, engineering. God gave him the means; he used them.

This is significant for the chapter's account of power. The youths of the cave were preserved by a miracle. Khiḍr acted under direct divine command. Dhu'l-Qarnayn built a wall out of iron and copper, with a workforce, using a construction technique the Qur'an describes in detail (vv. 96–97). The chapter is showing that righteous power operates through ordinary causality, and that the divine grant is the availability of the means rather than their suspension.

The practical instruction is one the Islamic tradition has drawn consistently: reliance on God (*tawakkul*) does not dispense with the use of means. The ḥadīth recorded by al-Tirmidhī (2517) is the standard statement:

> *"Tie your camel, and then place your trust in God."* (al-Tirmidhī 2517)

Dhu'l-Qarnayn is the chapter's embodiment of the principle. He was given the means to all things, and he followed the means.

**"The Means to All Things" and the Question of Scope**

The commentators discuss the scope of *min kulli shayʾin sababā* — "the means to all things." Al-Ṭabarī and al-Suyūṭī read it as knowledge of things generally; al-Rāzī, al-Suyūṭī, and al-Zamakhsharī read it as the power and tools to acquire what he desired. The commentators do not read it as omniscience or omnipotence — Dhu'l-Qarnayn is a creature, and verse 91 will immediately state that God encompasses all that is with him.

The phrase means, practically, that whatever he undertook, the means were available. The commentators have noted the significance for the story's account of power: Dhu'l-Qarnayn's achievements are never blocked by a shortage of resources. The wall is built because the iron, the copper, the bellows, and the labour are all there. The chapter is describing a sovereignty without material constraint — and then showing what such a sovereignty does when it meets people who need a wall.

**Sabab as a Qur'anic Concept**

The word *sabab* is the Qur'an's standard term for a means, a cause, a way of reaching something. It appears at 20:69 and 40:36–37 of Pharaoh's claim to build a tower to reach the *asbāb* of the heavens, and at 22:15 of the one who thinks God will not help the Prophet ﷺ: *"let him stretch a rope (sabab) to the ceiling and then cut it."* The word carries the sense of a thing one takes hold of in order to reach what is otherwise out of reach.

The chapter's use of it is consistent and deliberate. Dhu'l-Qarnayn is given the means (v. 84) and then *follows* them three times (vv. 85, 89, 92). The means are given; the following is his. The commentators have drawn the principle that the Islamic tradition states as *tawakkul* combined with *asbāb*: reliance on God does not dispense with the means, and the means do not operate independently of God.

The ḥadīth at al-Tirmidhī (2517) is the tradition's standard formulation: *"Tie your camel, and then place your trust in God."* The verse gives the same teaching at the scale of a world sovereignty.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:85

> **So he followed a means,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Words, Repeated Three Times**

*Fa-atbaʿa sababā* — "so he followed a means." The verse is the shortest in the chapter and it functions as a refrain, repeated at verse 89 and, in variant form, at verse 92. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr gloss it as his journeying along a route between east and west, following landmarks and stopping points.

The repetition is structural. It marks the three legs of the journey — west, east, and the pass between the two mountains — and each time it reminds the reader of verse 84: the means were given, and he followed them.

**Why the Verse Is Given Its Own Space**

The Qur'an could have compressed the journey into the following verse, as it does at verses 86, 90, and 93. Instead it gives the following of the means a verse of its own, three times. The commentators have noted the effect: the journey is not a transition between destinations but the substance of the achievement.

The instruction is about process. The person who wants the western horizon, the eastern horizon, and the mountain pass does not acquire them by wishing; he acquires them by following the means, one leg at a time, three times over. The refrain is the chapter's statement that power, like knowledge, is exercised rather than possessed.

**The Refrain and the Doctrine of Process**

The commentators note that the Qur'an gives the following of the means a verse of its own, three times, rather than compressing the journey into the following verses. The effect is to make the *process* visible. Dhu'l-Qarnayn does not arrive; he travels, and the travelling is narrated.

The instruction is about the nature of real attainment. The person given the means does not thereby possess the ends. He must follow the means, one leg at a time, and the following is the work. The chapter states this in three words and then repeats it, which is the Qur'an's way of marking something as central.

**The Resonance With the Language of Following**

The commentators note that the chapter uses the language of following for both the seeker of knowledge and the wielder of power. Moses asks at verse 66, *"may I follow you?"* Khiḍr conditions the apprenticeship at verse 70, *"if you follow me."* Dhu'l-Qarnayn *"follows a means"* three times.

The resonance is not accidental in effect. The chapter is teaching that both knowledge and power are exercised through following — a teacher, a route, a method — and that neither is acquired by possession. The person who will not follow a teacher will not learn; the person who will not follow a route will not arrive. Both figures in the chapter submit to the requirement, and both are shown at work.

**Why the Journey Is Narrated at All**

The commentators have asked why the Qur'an narrates the legs of the journey rather than simply stating that Dhu'l-Qarnayn reached the west, the east, and the pass. The answer the structure supplies is that the journey is the evidence of the character. A reader told that a king travelled to the ends of the earth learns his range; a reader shown him following a means, three times, learns his method.

The chapter is more interested in method than in range, and the refrain is how it says so. The same preference governs the wall: verse 96 describes the construction in technical detail rather than announcing the result. In both cases the Qur'an shows the work rather than the wonder.

**Why the Qur'an Gives Three Words Their Own Verse**

The commentators have asked why the following of the means is given a verse of its own, three times, rather than being compressed into the verses that describe the destinations. The answer the structure supplies is that the Qur'an wants the *method* visible, and a method becomes visible through repetition.

A single mention would be a detail of the narrative. Three mentions, in identical words, establish a pattern — and the pattern is the chapter's claim about how power operates. Dhu'l-Qarnayn is not carried to his destinations; he travels to them, and the travelling is narrated because it is the substance of his achievement. The commentators have drawn the instruction that attainment is a sequence of legs rather than a single arrival.

**The Refrain and the Chapter's Other Journeys**

The commentators note that the chapter contains three journeys: the youths' flight to the cave, Moses's search for the junction of the two seas, and Dhu'l-Qarnayn's three legs. Each is undertaken for a specific end, and each is narrated in terms of the movement rather than the arrival.

The youths travelled to preserve their faith and found three centuries of preservation. Moses travelled to find a teacher and had to retrace his steps to reach him (v. 64). Dhu'l-Qarnayn travelled three times and built a wall that will hold until the Hour. In each case the journey is longer and less direct than the destination requires, and in each case the length is part of the lesson.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:86

> **till when he reached the place of the setting sun, he found it setting in a murky spring, and there he found a people. We said, "O Dhu'l-Qarnayn! Thou mayest punish, or thou mayest treat them well."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"He Found It Setting in a Murky Spring"**

*ḥattā idhā balagha maghriba al-shamsi wajadahā taghrubu fī ʿaynin ḥamiʾah* — *ḥamiʾah* denotes murky, muddy, warm with sediment; some readings have *ḥāmiyah*, meaning hot. The commentators — Ibn Kathīr, al-Jalālayn, al-Suyūṭī — are unanimous that the statement is not to be taken as an astronomical claim but as a description of appearance: to the human eye at the westernmost point of known land, the sun appears to set into the distant ocean, which appears dark from a distance.

Al-Rāzī explains the reading: the westernmost point of Dhu'l-Qarnayn's known world was bordered by the ocean, and as the sun set it appeared to descend into the water. The Qur'an's phrase *wajadahā* — "he found it" — is precisely the language of perception rather than of assertion, and the commentators press the point: the verse reports what he found, not what is.

This is an important principle of Qur'anic interpretation, and the classical exegetes were careful about it. The Qur'an frequently describes phenomena as they appear to human observers — the sun rising and setting, the sky as a canopy, the mountains as pegs — and the commentators consistently distinguish between the description of appearance and the assertion of physical fact. Al-Rāzī, in particular, developed this into a systematic hermeneutic.

**"And There He Found a People"**

*Wa wajada ʿindahā qawman* — the commentators report the legend that this people resided in a city of twelve thousand gates (Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī). The context indicates they were in a state of disbelief or idolatry (al-Rāzī, al-Suyūṭī).

**The Choice Given**

*Qulnā yā dhā al-qarnayni immā an tuʿadhdhiba wa immā an tattakhidha fīhim ḥusnā* — "We said: O Dhu'l-Qarnayn, either you punish, or you take a way of kindness with them." The commentators explain the two options: to slay them for their persistence in idolatry (al-Rāzī, al-Suyūṭī, al-Zamakhsharī), or to call them to Islam and seek their guidance (al-Zamakhsharī), or to be generous and forgiving (al-Rāzī).

The structure of the choice is theologically significant, and the commentators have drawn its implications. God does not command the punishment; He authorises a *choice*. Dhu'l-Qarnayn, as the holder of power, is given discretion — and verse 87 will show how he exercises it.

**God's Direct Speech to Him**

The commentators note that God's addressing Dhu'l-Qarnayn directly suggests he received a form of revelation (*waḥy*), which supports the position that he was a prophet (al-Rāzī, al-Suyūṭī). Others understand the communication as inspiration (*ilhām*) rather than prophetic revelation (al-Suyūṭī).

The dispute matters less than its implication: Dhu'l-Qarnayn's sovereignty is not autonomous. He is addressed, instructed, and given a mandate. This is the chapter's central claim about power — that legitimate sovereignty operates under divine instruction and is answerable to it.

**The Fourth Trial of the Chapter**

The chapter's four trials are now all in place: faith under persecution (the sleepers), wealth without gratitude (the garden owner), knowledge without humility (Moses), and power without accountability (Dhu'l-Qarnayn — or rather, power *with* accountability, since he passes the test). The verse sets up the fourth, and verses 87–88 will show the answer.

**The Hermeneutic Principle the Verse Establishes**

The commentators' unanimity on the perceptual reading of *"he found it setting in a murky spring"* makes this verse one of the clearest instances of a principle that runs throughout Qur'anic interpretation: the Qur'an frequently describes phenomena as they appear to human observers, and the description of appearance is not an assertion of physical mechanism.

Al-Rāzī developed this into a systematic hermeneutic, and the classical exegetes applied it consistently — to the sun's rising and setting, to the sky as a canopy, to the mountains as pegs. The principle is not a concession to modern science but an observation about language: a text addressed to all human generations describes the world in the terms in which all of them encounter it.

The verb *wajadahā* — "he found it" — is decisive for the reading. The verse reports Dhu'l-Qarnayn's perception, not a cosmological fact, and the commentators press the point. A reader who mistakes the description for an assertion will find a difficulty where the text offers none.

**The Choice as an Exercise of Delegated Authority**

The commentators note the structure of the divine address: *"either you punish, or you take a way of kindness."* God does not command one option; He authorises a choice and leaves its exercise to the sovereign. Al-Zamakhsharī reads the kindness as calling them to Islam and seeking their guidance; al-Rāzī reads it as generosity and forgiveness.

The structure is theologically significant. Legitimate sovereignty, in the chapter's account, operates within a delegated discretion — bounded above by the divine instruction and below by the requirement of justice. The sovereign is neither a mere executor of commands nor an autonomous legislator. He is a decision-maker within a mandate, and verses 87–88 will show how he discharges it.

The commentators have drawn the implication for governance: the ruler's discretion is real, and so is his accountability for how he uses it. The verse does not tell Dhu'l-Qarnayn what to do; it tells him what he may do, and the reader is left to evaluate the choice he makes.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:87

> **He said, "As for the one who has done wrong, we shall punish him. Then he shall be brought back to his Lord, whereupon He will punish him with a terrible punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exercise of Discretion**

*Qāla ammā man ẓalama fa-sawfa nuʿadhdhibuhu thumma yuraddu ilā rabbihi fa-yuʿadhdhibuhu ʿadhāban nukrā* — Dhu'l-Qarnayn's answer to the divine choice is not a choice of one option over the other but a *specification*: punishment for the wrongdoer, and the verse that follows will give kindness to the believer.

Al-Suyūṭī and al-Rāzī identify the wrongdoer as one who ascribed partners to God and persisted in disbelief after being called to monotheism. The punishment *"we shall inflict"* is his being slain in this life (al-Rāzī, al-Suyūṭī); the punishment *"his Lord will inflict"* is the punishment of the Hereafter (al-Suyūṭī).

**The Two Punishments Distinguished**

The verse names two punishments and separates them by an entire realm. The first is temporal and administered by the sovereign; the second is eternal and administered by God. Al-Suyūṭī notes the significance: the worldly punishment does not exhaust the matter, and the verse does not suggest that execution settles the account.

This is a distinctive feature of the Qur'anic account of justice, and it distinguishes it from systems in which the state's penalty is the final word. The person punished in this life is then *"brought back to his Lord"* — *yuraddu*, returned — and the real reckoning begins. The commentators have drawn the implication for the exercise of power: the ruler's punishment is provisional and limited, and the ruler who imagines that his judgment is ultimate has misunderstood his office.

**The Speech as a Public Declaration**

The commentators note that Dhu'l-Qarnayn's answer is a *statement of policy*, announced to the people he has just encountered. He is not merely deciding what to do; he is telling them what will happen. This is characteristic of legitimate sovereignty as the chapter presents it: the terms are declared in advance, so that those addressed can act on them.

The structure — declaration of terms, then the opportunity to choose — is the same structure as the prophetic mission stated at verse 2 and verse 56: warning and glad tidings. Dhu'l-Qarnayn is exercising a function analogous to the prophetic one, and the commentators who hold him to be a prophet take this as evidence.

**The Declaration as Public Policy**

The commentators note that Dhu'l-Qarnayn's statement is addressed to the people he has just encountered — it is a proclamation, not a private decision. He tells them what will happen to the wrongdoer among them, and he tells them before they have chosen.

This is the structure of the prophetic mission as the chapter has described it: warning and glad tidings, delivered in advance so that the hearer can act. Verse 2 stated the Book's purpose in these terms; verse 56 stated the messengers' function in the same terms. Dhu'l-Qarnayn's declaration uses the same structure, and the commentators who hold him to be a prophet take this as evidence.

**"A Terrible Punishment" and the Chapter's Use of Nukr**

The word *nukr* returns from verse 74, where Moses called the killing of the boy a terrible thing. The commentators note the echo and its function: the chapter uses the same adjective for an act that horrified a prophet and for the divine punishment that awaits persistent unbelief. The two are not equated; verse 82 resolved the first by disclosing its hidden mercy, while this verse affirms the second without qualification.

The contrast is instructive. An act that *appears* terrible may conceal a mercy; a punishment that is *declared* terrible conceals nothing. The chapter is careful to distinguish what looks severe from what is severe, and to leave no ambiguity about which is which.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:88

> **But as for the one who believes and works righteousness, he shall have a reward, that which is most beautiful, and we shall speak unto him that which is easy from our command."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Branch of the Policy**

*Wa ammā man āmana wa ʿamila ṣāliḥan fa-lahu jazāʾan al-ḥusnā wa sa-naqūlu lahu min amrinā yusrā* — the believer who works righteousness receives *al-ḥusnā*, "the most beautiful." Al-Rāzī notes the two possible readings: the reward itself is the most beautiful thing; or the reward is for the most beautiful deed — which al-Zamakhsharī identifies as the testimony of God's Oneness, and al-Rāzī as believing and working righteousness.

The formula *alladhī āmana wa ʿamila ṣāliḥan* is the chapter's recurring pair — 2:25, 3:57, 4:57, 5:69, 7:42, 19:60, 28:80, 34:37, and verses 2, 30, 107 of this chapter. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī note that both faith and deeds are required, and the pairing recurs in dozens of places.

**The Echo of Verse 2**

*Al-ḥusnā* recalls verse 2's *ajran ḥasanan* — "a beautiful reward." The chapter's opening statement of the Book's purpose and a pagan king's declaration of policy use the same vocabulary, and the connection is the chapter's claim: the righteous sovereign and the revealed Book are aligned in what they promise.

**"That Which Is Easy": The Principle of Yusr**

The commentators read Dhu'l-Qarnayn's undertaking — *"we shall speak unto him that which is easy from our command"* — as a commitment to impose only what is bearable. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī specify zakāh and kharāj to a degree and manner the people can meet; Ibn Kathīr reads it as kindness generally.

The concept of *yusr* is one of the Qur'an's central principles for legislation, and the commentators connect it to 2:185:

> *"God desires ease for you, and desires not hardship for you."* (Qur'an 2:185)

And to 94:5–6: *"So truly with hardship comes ease; truly with hardship comes ease."* The Prophetic instruction recorded by al-Bukhārī (69) and Muslim (1734) states the same for those in authority:

> *"Make things easy and do not make them difficult; give glad tidings and do not repel people."* (al-Bukhārī 69; Muslim 1734)

The commentators have drawn the ruling for governance: obligation must be proportionate to capacity, and the ruler who exceeds what his subjects can bear has departed from the model the chapter sets. This is not leniency as a preference but as a principle of justice — a demand beyond capacity is not a demand but an injury.

**The Chapter's Answer to the Fourth Trial**

The commentators note the symmetry between verses 87 and 88 and the completeness of the answer. The sovereign does not choose punishment *or* kindness; he distributes both by desert, and undertakes ease for the righteous. This is the exercise of power the chapter endorses.

The contrast with the garden owner is exact and the commentators do not miss it. Both men were given by God. One said *"I am greater than you in wealth and stronger in men"* (v. 34); the other says *"what my Lord has established me in is better"* (v. 95). One treated his possession as grounds for superiority; the other treats his as grounds for service. The chapter has now shown the trial of power passed, and the reader can see what passing looks like.

**The Sovereign's Speech as an Act of Governance**

The commentators note that Dhu'l-Qarnayn's undertaking concerns how he will *speak* to the believer — *"we shall speak unto him that which is easy from our command."* The verse locates the quality of governance partly in the manner of address, not only in the substance of the demand.

This is a distinctive emphasis, and the commentators have drawn its implications.
