A ruler may impose reasonable obligations in a manner that makes them burdensome — through contempt, through arbitrariness, through the refusal to explain. The verse requires that the *speech* itself be easy, which is a constraint on the ruler's conduct rather than on his policy.

The distinction between the demand and its delivery is one the tradition takes seriously. The Prophetic instruction cited above — to make things easy and not to repel people — addresses manner as much as measure, and the verse supplies the Qur'anic basis for reading it that way: what is spoken *"from our command"* is to be spoken in the easy register, not merely kept within the limit of capacity.

The commentators have drawn the further point that speech is the instrument by which authority is ordinarily exercised. A sovereign who cannot command gently has not moderated his rule; he has merely softened its presentation, and the verse's wording — *"that which is easy"* — applies to the content of what is said, not only to its tone.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:89

> **Then he followed a means,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain, the Second Time**

*Thumma atbaʿa sababā* — the verse repeats verse 85 exactly, with *thumma* (then) in place of *fa-* (so). Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr gloss it as before: he journeyed along a route.

The commentators note that the refrain's repetition marks the structure of the narrative — three legs, each introduced by the same formula. The reader is being given the rhythm of the journey, and the rhythm is the point: the man who has been given the means to all things does not teleport between destinations. He travels.

**The Turn From West to East**

The verse marks the transition from the western extremity to the eastern. Al-Ṭabarī and the commentators describe the scope: having dealt with the people at the setting of the sun, he now turns toward its rising. The Qur'an's geography is the geography of the known inhabited world, and the chapter's claim is that Dhu'l-Qarnayn's sovereignty reached both of its ends.

The commentators have noted the theological significance of the pairing. The sun's setting and rising are the two fixed points by which human beings orient themselves, and the Qur'an uses them elsewhere as markers of the whole: *"Lord of the easts and the wests"* (70:40), *"unto God belong the east and the west"* (2:115). Dhu'l-Qarnayn's journey from one to the other is the chapter's way of saying that his dominion was comprehensive — and verse 91 will immediately subordinate it: *"We encompassed in awareness all that was with him."*

**Why the Chapter Repeats the Formula**

The repetition serves a function the commentators identify. By stating three times that Dhu'l-Qarnayn *followed a means*, the chapter insists that his achievements were accomplished through process rather than by fiat. The wall he will build at verse 96 is made of iron and copper, assembled by labourers, heated with bellows — and the journey to reach it was made in stages.

The instruction is about the nature of real power. The person given the means does not thereby possess the ends; he must follow the means to reach them, and the following is the work.

**The Refrain's Cumulative Effect**

The commentators have observed that by the second repetition the refrain has become a signature. The reader now expects the pattern: he followed a means, and he reached a place. The expectation is part of the instruction, because it establishes that the pattern is not accidental but characteristic.

This is how the chapter makes its point about power. A single instance of using means might be a concession to circumstance; three instances establish a method. Dhu'l-Qarnayn is not occasionally practical; he is systematically so. And the wall of verses 96–97 will be built the same way — by requisition, by organisation, by bellows, by poured copper.

The practical instruction is available to any reader. The means are given; the following is the work. The person who waits for the destination to arrive without the legs of the journey will wait indefinitely, and the chapter says so three times in three words.

**The Refrain as a Statement of Character**

The commentators note that by the second repetition the refrain has become a signature, and the signature describes a disposition rather than a route. Dhu'l-Qarnayn is a man who follows means. The chapter states this three times in three words, which is its way of making the point unmistakable.

The disposition is the chapter's answer to the trial of power. Verse 84 said God gave him the means to all things; the refrain shows what he does with the gift. He does not command the destination into being, does not wait for it to be delivered, and does not treat the gift as a substitute for the journey. He follows.

**Why the Journey Is Narrated Rather Than Summarised**

The commentators have asked why the Qur'an narrates the legs rather than simply stating the destinations. The answer the structure supplies is that the journey is the evidence. A reader told that a king reached the ends of the earth learns his range; a reader shown him following a means, twice, learns his method — and it is the method, not the range, that the chapter is teaching.

The same preference governs the wall. Verse 96 describes the construction in technical detail rather than announcing the result, and verse 97 reports the outcome in a single clause. Throughout the Dhu'l-Qarnayn narrative the Qur'an shows the work and abbreviates the wonder, which is the opposite of how legend ordinarily proceeds.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:90

> **till he reached the place of the rising sun. He found it rising over a people for whom We had not made any shelter from it.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Eastern Extremity**

*Ḥattā idhā balagha maṭliʿa al-shamsi wajadahā taṭluʿu ʿalā qawmin lam najʿal lahum min dūnihā sitrā* — "until he reached the place of the sun's rising, and found it rising upon a people for whom We had made no shelter from it." As at verse 86, the commentators read the description as perceptual rather than astronomical: at the easternmost point of the known land, the sun appears to rise directly over the inhabitants.

**A People Without Shelter**

Al-Ṭabarī reports that this people lived in a land with neither mountains nor trees for natural shade, nor buildings or other structures for shelter. Al-Jalālayn and al-Zamakhsharī suggest the land could not sustain buildings, or that the people had no knowledge of construction. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī report that when the sun rose they took refuge in the water or burrowed underground, emerging when it passed its highest point and began to set.

The description is one of the Qur'an's most economical portraits of a people at the margin of what its first audience could imagine, and the commentators have noted its function: Dhu'l-Qarnayn's dominion extends not only to the wealthy and powerful but to those with nothing — people with no shade, no buildings, no technology.

**The People Without Shelter and the Range of Sovereignty**

Al-Ṭabarī reports that this people lived in a land with neither mountains nor trees for shade nor buildings for shelter, and that they took refuge in the water or burrowed underground when the sun rose. The commentators' descriptions are consistent: a people at the margin of what the Qur'an's first audience could imagine, without technology, without construction, without shade.

The detail is theologically purposeful. The chapter has brought its sovereign to three peoples, and none of them is powerful: idolaters at the western extremity, a people without shelter at the eastern extremity, and a people who can barely communicate at the pass. Dhu'l-Qarnayn's dominion is measured not by the grandeur of its subjects but by its reach toward the weakest of them.

**The Contrast With Verse 28**

The commentators draw the contrast, and it is the chapter's sharpest social statement. The Makkans asked that the poor be removed from the gathering so they would not have to sit with them. Dhu'l-Qarnayn, with the means to all things, travels to the ends of the earth to attend to people who have no shelter from the sun — and, at the pass, refuses payment for building them a wall.

The two postures are the chapter's poles, and the chapter does not leave the reader to choose between them. Verse 28 commanded the Prophet ﷺ to keep company with the poor; verse 90 shows a sovereign doing the same at the scale of an empire. The instruction is the same at both scales.

**"For Whom We Had Made No Shelter"**

The commentators note that the clause attributes the absence of shelter to God — *lam najʿal lahum*. This is not a statement of neglect but of the diversity of human circumstance: God has placed people in every condition, and the sovereign's task is to attend to each.

The verse thereby rebukes the inference the garden owner made at verse 34 — that material condition indicates standing. The people without shelter are not thereby deficient; they are differently placed, and their placement imposes an obligation on the one who has been given the means. The chapter's account of providence is not that God gives equally but that He gives variously and holds each recipient to the use of what he received.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:91

> **Thus it was. And We encompassed in Our awareness all that was with him.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Thus It Was"**

*Kadhālika* — "so it was," "thus." The commentators read the phrase as a summarising conclusion: matters stood as described. The word returns from verse 17 (*"that is among the signs of God"*) and verse 19 (*"and in this way We raised them"*), where it functions as a marker of divine arrangement.

**"We Encompassed in Awareness All That Was With Him"**

*Wa qad aḥaṭnā bi-mā ladayhi khubrā* — the commentators offer two readings. Al-Ṭabarī reads *mā ladayhi* — "what was with him, what lay before him" — as referring to the strange people Dhu'l-Qarnayn encountered: God had full knowledge of them. Ibn Kathīr, al-Suyūṭī, and al-Zamakhsharī read it as "that which was with him" in the sense of his possessions: God knew his armies, his tools, and his means.

The verb *aḥāṭa bi-* — to encompass — is the same verb used at verse 68 (*"that which thou dost not encompass in awareness"*) and at verse 91's noun *khubr*, the intimate tested knowledge Khiḍr said Moses lacked. The chapter uses the same vocabulary for divine knowledge and for the human knowledge Moses did not have, and the contrast is the point: what Moses could not encompass, God encompasses — including the contents of a sovereign's treasury and the condition of a people without shelter.

**The Verse as a Check on Achievement**

The commentators note the placement: immediately after describing a dominion reaching both ends of the inhabited world, the verse states that God encompasses everything its holder has. The reader is not permitted to mistake the scope of the power for its independence.

This is the same operation the chapter performed at verse 26 after the cave narrative (*"unto Him belongs the Unseen of the heavens and the earth"*) and will perform at verse 109 (*"the sea would be exhausted before the Words of my Lord were exhausted"*). The chapter consistently closes its accounts of extraordinary achievement with a statement of divine encompassment, and the repetition is the discipline it teaches.

The practical instruction applies to every form of attainment. The person who has travelled far, acquired much, and built greatly is told what the king is told: all that is with you is encompassed by a knowledge greater than yours. The statement is not a diminishment of the achievement; it is the frame that keeps the achievement from becoming an idol — which is precisely what happened to the man with two gardens.

**The Verse and the Ethics of Achievement**

The commentators have drawn a practical instruction from the verse's placement that applies well beyond sovereignty. The statement *"We encompassed in awareness all that was with him"* is not a threat; it is a description, and its function is to keep achievement in proportion.

The person who has built, acquired, or travelled extensively faces a specific danger: the identification of himself with his accumulation. The Qur'an names the danger at 96:6–7:

> *"Nay, truly man transgresses, because he considers himself self-sufficient."* (Qur'an 96:6–7)

The word *istaghnā* — to consider oneself sufficient — is precisely the error the verse forecloses. Dhu'l-Qarnayn had the means to all things and was told that all he had was encompassed. The instruction is available at every scale: the reader is not asked to diminish his achievements but to hold them within a frame larger than himself, which is what makes it possible to lose them without being destroyed. The garden owner had no such frame, and verse 42 showed the consequence.

**The End of the Refrains**

The commentators note that this verse closes the three-part structure introduced at verses 84–85, 88–89, and 92. Each part opened with a means followed and closed with a destination reached, and verse 91 supplies the final summary: *"thus it was."*

The commentators have observed that the summary is deliberately contentless. It does not describe the eastern people's condition in detail or record any exchange with them. It states that the matter was as described, and that God's awareness encompassed everything Dhu'l-Qarnayn had.

The effect is to close the narrative on the note of divine knowledge rather than on the note of the traveller's achievement. The three legs have displayed a sovereign's range; the final verse places that range inside a wider awareness. The commentators have read this as the chapter's consistent method: every display of power is concluded by a statement of God's knowledge or God's promise.

The pattern holds for the barrier that follows. Verse 96 will describe the construction, and verse 98 will place it inside the Promise — the same closing move, applied to the greatest work of the three.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:92

> **Then he followed a means,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain, the Third Time**

*Thumma atbaʿa sababā* — the identical verse, now introducing the third and final leg of the journey. The commentators gloss it as before: a route, a path (see vv. 85–86).

The repetition's function is now complete. Three legs — west, east, and the pass — each introduced by the same three words. The chapter has established a rhythm, and the reader arrives at verse 93 expecting the pattern: he followed a means, and he reached a place.

**Why the Refrain Matters Structurally**

The commentators have noted that the refrain does something more than mark transitions. It establishes *method*. Dhu'l-Qarnayn does not arrive at his destinations by wish or by command; he follows means. The wall of verses 96–97 will be built the same way — by iron, by bellows, by copper, by organised labour.

The chapter is teaching a consistent doctrine of achievement: what is given by God is realised through means, and the person who has been given the means must still follow them. The refrain is the doctrine in three words, and its repetition is the emphasis.

**The Third Direction and the Return to the Middle**

The commentators note the structure of the three legs. Verses 85 and 89 took Dhu'l-Qarnayn to the two extremities — west and east. Verse 92 takes him to a third place, which verse 93 identifies as *between* two mountain barriers. After reaching the limits of the inhabited world in both directions, he returns to an intermediate point.

The commentators connect this to the concept the chapter has been working with throughout: the *barzakh*. The sleepers were in a *barzakh* between life and death. Moses met Khiḍr at a *barzakh* between two seas. Dhu'l-Qarnayn's great work is performed at a *barzakh* between two mountains, and it will itself become a *barzakh* in the strictest sense — a barrier that holds until the Hour.

The chapter's three narratives are thus organised around the same concept, and the source of the story's unity is not its plots but its recurring structure: a meeting-place between two opposed realms, at which something decisive becomes possible.

**The Refrain's Final Function**

The commentators have observed that by the third repetition the refrain has become a formula the reader recognises, and its recognition is itself part of the instruction. The person who has followed means twice will follow them a third time; the method is established, and the third leg is where it produces its greatest result.

There is a practical observation here about the relation between effort and outcome. The first two legs produced encounters — with idolaters, with a people without shelter. The third produced the wall. The chapter does not suggest that the third leg was more important; it suggests that the capacity built by the first two was what made the third possible. The person who wants the wall must first travel the two roads that lead to it.

**The Third Leg and the Accumulation of Method**

The commentators have observed that the refrain's third repetition does more than mark a transition; it establishes that the method is characteristic rather than occasional. A person who uses means once may be adapting to circumstance; a person who uses them three times has a way of working.

This matters for the wall. Verse 96 will describe a construction project requiring requisitioned iron, organised labour, worked bellows, and poured copper — and the reader arrives at it already knowing that this is how Dhu'l-Qarnayn operates. The wall is not an exception to his method but its largest application. The commentators have drawn the instruction that the capacity for great work is built by smaller journeys: the roads had to be travelled before the pass could be reached.

**Between the Two Extremes**

The commentators note the geography of the three legs: west, east, and the space *between* two mountains. The chapter's concern with intermediate places — the *barzakh* — reaches its practical culmination here. The sleepers' intermediate state was passive; Moses's meeting at the junction was brief; Dhu'l-Qarnayn's is the site of a permanent work. The intermediate realm is thus first a place of preservation, then a place of encounter, then a place of construction — three ascending stages of one idea.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:93

> **till he reached the place between the two mountain barriers. He found beyond them a people who could scarcely comprehend speech.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Significance of the People's Condition**

The description is not incidental. The chapter has now brought its sovereign to three peoples: idolaters at the western extremity, a people without shelter at the eastern extremity, and a people who can barely communicate at the mountain pass. None of them is powerful, wealthy, or articulate. The chapter's account of righteous power is built entirely out of its dealings with the weak.

The contrast with verse 28 is now complete. The Makkans refused to sit with the poor. Dhu'l-Qarnayn travels to the ends of the earth to build a wall for people who cannot explain themselves clearly — and refuses payment for it.

**The Sadd and Its Identification**

The commentators report the dominant identification of the two mountain barriers as the mountains of Armenia and Azerbaijan, which formed a barrier against the lands and peoples to the north (al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī). Al-Suyūṭī records the alternative that they were near where the Mediterranean meets the Atlantic. The tradition's uncertainty about the location has persisted for fourteen centuries and has not affected the lesson — a fact worth noting in itself, since the Qur'an's refusal to specify has kept the story's meaning independent of its geography.

The word *sadd* denotes a barrier, a dam, a mound raised to block a passage — distinct from the *radm* Dhu'l-Qarnayn will undertake at verse 95, which the commentators describe as larger and stronger. The people ask for a *sadd*; he undertakes a *radm*.

**A People Who Could Scarcely Comprehend Speech**

Al-Suyūṭī reads the phrase as meaning they spoke a language only they could understand; al-Rāzī reads it as their understanding others only with great difficulty or through gestures. The commentators address the obvious difficulty — how they communicated the request of verse 94 — and offer two solutions: God gave Dhu'l-Qarnayn a special ability to understand their language, as He gave Solomon the language of the birds (27:16); or there was a translator.

The description is theologically purposeful. The chapter's final great act of power is performed for people who cannot explain themselves clearly — the least articulate, least organised, least able to advocate for themselves of all the peoples Dhu'l-Qarnayn encounters. The commentators have drawn the implication: the measure of a sovereign is not how he treats those who can reward him but how he treats those who cannot even make their case.

**The Setting for the Final Trial**

The commentators note that verse 93 sets the scene for the chapter's last great act. The reader has seen faith preserved (the cave), wealth ruined (the garden), knowledge humbled (Khiḍr), and power exercised with discretion (vv. 86–88). The final scene is power exercised with *generosity* — and the chapter places it at a barrier, which is the concept the whole sūrah has been circling since the sleepers' cave.

**Why the Greatest Work Is Done for the Least Articulate**

The commentators have drawn a lesson from the verse's arrangement that is easy to miss. Dhu'l-Qarnayn's three encounters are ordered by the capacity of the people to make their case: the western people are addressed and answer; the eastern people are observed and provided for; the people of the pass can barely speak and must be understood through gesture or a translator.

The final and greatest work — the wall — is performed for the last group. The commentators have seen in this a statement about the measure of power: it is not shown in dealings with those who can advocate for themselves but with those who cannot. A sovereign's justice is tested by the weakest party before him, because that party has nothing to offer in return. The instruction generalises to every person in a position to help, and the natural inclination toward the eloquent is exactly what the verse reverses.

**The Barrier as the Chapter's Only Enduring Construction**

The commentators note that the wall is the chapter's only act of construction that endures beyond the narrative. The garden was destroyed, the ship was damaged, the boy was killed, and the sleepers died. The wall stands — until the Promise comes.

The chapter is thus careful to place the wall's endurance under the same condition as everything else, which is why verse 98 immediately announces its end. Nothing in the sūrah escapes the rule of verses 7–8; the wall is the last and largest instance of it, and the reader who has followed the chapter knows before verse 98 what will be said.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:94

> **They said, "O Dhu'l-Qarnayn! Truly Gog and Magog are workers of corruption in the land. Shall we assign thee a tribute, that thou mightest set a barrier between them and us?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Yaʾjūj and Maʾjūj: The Names and Their Traditions**

*Yā dhā al-qarnayni inna yaʾjūja wa maʾjūja mufsidūna fī al-arḍ* — Gog and Magog. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī note the variant forms *Yājūj*/*Ājūj* and *Mājūj* in Arabic literature. Ibn Kathīr, al-Suyūṭī, and al-Zamakhsharī report that they are the offspring of Noah's son Japheth, and note the parallel at Genesis 10:2, where Magog is listed among Japheth's sons.

The commentators record further speculation: that they were ancestors or distant relatives of the Turks (Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Suyūṭī, al-Zamakhsharī) or of the Daylamites (al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī) — speculations the source material observes seem to rest on the fearsome reputation these peoples had as warriors for medieval Muslims. Al-Rāzī offers etymologies: *Yaʾjūj* from *taʾajjuj*, to ignite a fire, and *Maʾjūj* from *mawj*, the waves of the sea — both destructive forces.

The Qur'an mentions them again at 21:96–97:

> *"Until, when Gog and Magog are let loose and they rush down from every hill, and the true promise draws nigh."* (Qur'an 21:96–97)

The commentators note the biblical parallels: Ezekiel 38:2, where the prophet is told to prophesy against Gog of the land of Magog; and Revelation 20:8, where Gog and Magog are the nations Satan rouses in the end times.

**"Workers of Corruption in the Land"**

*Mufsidūna fī al-arḍ* — the term *fasād fī al-arḍ*, corruption upon the earth, is a standard Qur'anic category denoting both spiritual corruption and violent, wicked behaviour (2:11–12, 5:33). Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī report that Gog and Magog were said to devour people and to consume all the food and resources of the land; al-Suyūṭī and al-Zamakhsharī report that the people had suffered much torment and slaughter from them.

The commentators record a ḥadīth describing them as huge in size (al-Suyūṭī), while al-Rāzī reports speculative accounts describing them as very small or very large, with the claws and teeth of beasts, each male begetting a thousand offspring. The tradition's diversity here is considerable, and the sober commentators have not pressed the physical details.

**The Verse as the Setup**

Verse 94 sets up the chapter's clearest statement about the ethics of power, which verse 95 will deliver. The people have identified their need, identified the one who can meet it, and offered the ordinary consideration. What follows is the chapter's answer to the question of what a righteous ruler does when offered payment for a work of mercy.

**The People's Reasoning and Its Soundness**

The commentators note that the offer is entirely reasonable. A people threatened by a destructive neighbour, approached by a sovereign with visible means, offer the ordinary consideration for the ordinary service. There is nothing disreputable in the proposal, and the chapter does not present it as such.

This is important for reading verse 95 correctly. Dhu'l-Qarnayn's refusal is not a rebuke of the people; it is a statement about himself. He does not say "your offer is insulting" or "you should have known better." He says *"what my Lord has established me in is better"* — a statement about his own resources, not about their presumption.

The commentators have drawn the etiquette from the exchange: the person who declines a payment should locate the reason in his own sufficiency rather than in the other's inadequacy. The chapter models this in a single sentence.

**Fasād fī al-Arḍ as a Qur'anic Category**

The commentators note that *mufsidūna fī al-arḍ* — workers of corruption in the land — is a technical Qur'anic term. At 5:33 the phrase appears in the legal prescription concerning those who wage war against God and His messenger and *strive for corruption in the land*; at 2:11–12 it describes those who say *"we are reformers"* while being the actual corrupters; at 2:205 it describes the one who *"strives for corruption in the land, destroying crops and cattle."*

The term covers both spiritual corruption and violent, destructive behaviour, and the commentators apply both here: al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī report that Gog and Magog devoured people and consumed the land's resources; al-Suyūṭī and al-Zamakhsharī report that the people had suffered much torment and slaughter.

The verse's identification of the threat in these terms is significant for the chapter's argument. The wall is not built against a rival power or a competing faith but against *fasād* — corruption, destruction, the consuming of what others have made. The chapter's final act of power is a defence of productive life against predation, and the commentators have consistently read it that way.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:95

> **He said, "That wherewith my Lord has established me is better; so aid me with strength. I shall set a rampart between you and them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refusal**

*Qāla mā makkanī fīhi rabbī khayr* — "what my Lord has established me in is better." The verse is the chapter's answer to the fourth trial, and it is delivered in six words. Al-Rāzī and al-Suyūṭī explain the sense: God had given Dhu'l-Qarnayn such wealth and expanse of land that he had no need of what the people could offer; Ibn Kathīr adds that he would provide the service simply for the sake of righteousness and doing good.

The verb *makkana* returns from verse 84 — *"We established him in the land."* Dhu'l-Qarnayn's refusal is grounded in the acknowledgment of the grant: what God established me in is better than what you can pay me. The chapter has now given its clearest statement of the disposition that passes the trial of power.

**The Parallel With Solomon**

Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr note the parallel with Solomon's response to the Queen of Sheba's gifts at 27:36:

> *"So when he came to Solomon, he said: Do you offer me wealth? What God has given me is better than what He has given you. Nay, but you rejoice in your gift."* (Qur'an 27:36)

The commentators place Dhu'l-Qarnayn and Solomon in the same category — the righteous sovereign, given power by God, who refuses payment for exercising it. The Qur'an mentions Solomon's rule extensively (27:15–44, 38:30–40) and the commentators have consistently compared the two.

The comparison is instructive in what it reveals about the Qur'an's account of power. Both men had absolute authority. Both were offered wealth by those they dealt with. Both declined, on the same ground: what God has given me is better. The chapter is establishing a pattern, not describing an exception.

**"I Shall Set a Rampart Between You and Them"**

*Ajʿal baynakum wa baynahum radmā* — *radm* is a barrier larger and stronger than the *sadd* the people asked for. Al-Jalālayn and al-Suyūṭī note the escalation: they asked for a *sadd*; he undertook a *radm*. The commentators have drawn the instruction: the righteous sovereign does not do the minimum requested but what the need requires.

The verse completes the chapter's fourth trial. The man with the means to all things, offered payment by the weak, refuses the payment, enlists their labour, and undertakes more than they asked for. The contrast with the garden owner — who had two gardens and could not stop boasting — is the chapter's whole moral argument in miniature.

**The Refusal and Its Ground**

The commentators note that Dhu'l-Qarnayn's refusal is not an ascetic rejection of wealth but a statement of sufficiency. Al-Rāzī and al-Suyūṭī explain: God had given him such wealth and expanse that he had no need of what they could offer. The refusal is therefore not a moral judgment on payment in general — the Qur'an permits wages, contracts, and compensation throughout — but a declaration that in this case he does not need it.

This distinction matters for the tradition's use of the verse. It is not cited to argue that public service must be unpaid; it is cited to establish that the righteous ruler does not *require* payment from those he protects, and that where he is already provided for, taking from the weak is unnecessary. The commentators have drawn the ruling that a ruler with sufficient resources should not tax the vulnerable for works that benefit them.

**Why He Accepted Their Labour**

The commentators have drawn a point from the fact that Dhu'l-Qarnayn refused their money and accepted their strength. The distinction is not incidental. A wall built by hired contractors is the ruler's monument; a wall built by the threatened community under the ruler's direction is their shared defence, and they will maintain it.

Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, and al-Suyūṭī explain that he asked for able-bodied men, tools, and materials. The commentators have seen in this a model of public works: the ruler provides the design, the materials, and the direction; the people provide the labour; the result belongs to both. The chapter's account of the project includes the people at every stage — verse 96 records them bringing iron and blowing the bellows.

There is a further point the commentators note. By accepting their labour, Dhu'l-Qarnayn gave them a share in their own protection. The people who build a wall will defend it; the people who pay for one may not. The refusal of payment was therefore not only generous but strategically sound, and the chapter's account of righteous power includes its practical wisdom.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:96

> **Bring me pieces of iron." Then, when he had leveled the two cliffs, he said, "Blow!" till when he had made it fire, he said, "Bring me molten copper to pour over it."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Construction Described in Detail**

*Ātūnī zubara al-ḥadīd ḥattā idhā sāwā bayna al-ṣadafayni qāla-nfukhū ḥattā idhā jaʿalahu nāran qāla ātūnī ufrigh ʿalayhi qiṭrā* — the verse is the most technically detailed passage in the Qur'an, and the commentators have described the process step by step. Al-Jalālayn and al-Suyūṭī explain:

- *Zubar al-ḥadīd* — pieces or ingots of iron — were brought and used to fill the gap between the two cliffs, levelling the opening (*sāwā bayna al-ṣadafayn*; *ṣadaf* denotes the side of a mountain or cliff).
- *Infukhū* — "blow!" — the order to work the bellows upon the heap of iron until it was intensely hot or flaming.
- *Ufrigh ʿalayhi qiṭrā* — "pour molten copper over it" — *qiṭr* is molten copper, or alternatively molten brass or iron; the pouring filled the interstices between the iron pieces and formed a solid mass.

The result, al-Suyūṭī notes, was a wall that could neither be scaled from above nor breached from below.

**Why the Qur'an Gives the Method**

The detail is remarkable and the commentators have drawn its significance. This is a miracle-adjacent narrative — a sovereign given the means to all things, addressing a people beyond the range of ordinary communication, building a barrier that will hold until the Hour. The Qur'an could have described the barrier as raised by divine power in a moment.

Instead it describes a *construction project*: materials requisitioned, a workforce organised, bellows worked, metal heated, copper poured. The commentators have seen in this a statement about how God's purposes are accomplished in the world — through means, through labour, through technique. Verse 84 said God gave him the means to all things; verse 96 shows what the means look like when they are used.

The practical instruction is one the Islamic tradition has drawn consistently. Reliance on God does not dispense with engineering. The ḥadīth at al-Tirmidhī (2517) — *"tie your camel, and then place your trust in God"* — states the principle; this verse exhibits it at the scale of a civilisation-defining project.

**The Command "Blow!"**

*Qāla-nfukhū* — a single imperative, and the commentators note its force. The construction is not performed by Dhu'l-Qarnayn alone; it requires the coordinated breath of a workforce upon the bellows. The verse records the moment at which the sovereign's design becomes the people's physical effort.

There is a resonance with the Qur'an's use of breath and spirit — *rūḥ*, *nafakh* — elsewhere, and the commentators have not pressed it. What the verse establishes is the collaboration: the ruler directs, the people blow, the copper is poured, and the barrier stands.

**The Wall in Islamic Eschatology**

The commentators connect the barrier to 21:96–97 and to the ḥadīth literature on the end times. Al-Bukhārī (3346) and Muslim (2880) record from Zaynab bint Jaḥsh that the Prophet ﷺ woke from sleep saying:

> *"There is no god but God! Woe to the Arabs from an evil that has drawn near. Today a breach like this has been opened in the wall of Gog and Magog"* — and he made a circle with his thumb and forefinger. (al-Bukhārī 3346; Muslim 2880)

The tradition has consistently read the barrier as real and as gradually failing, and the commentators have differed on its location and its current state. What the Qur'an states is verse 98: when the Promise comes, God will make it level with the ground.

**The Bellows and the Organisation of Labour**

The commentators note that the single imperative *infukhū* — "blow!" — implies a coordinated workforce operating bellows on a heap of iron large enough to fill a mountain gap. Al-Jalālayn and al-Suyūṭī describe the sequence: iron pieces brought, the gap levelled, fire worked upon the heap until it was intensely hot, molten copper poured over it to fill the interstices.

The detail is remarkable for what it implies about scale and organisation. Filling a mountain pass with iron, heating the mass to incandescence, and pouring copper over it is not the work of one man or of a day. It requires requisition, transport, a labour force, fuel, bellows in quantity, and coordination — and the verse records all of it in a single sentence. The commentators have drawn the conclusion that the Qur'an is describing an actual engineering project rather than a supernatural event, and that this is deliberate.

**Why the Qur'an Records a Construction Technique**

The commentators have asked why the Qur'an includes technical detail of this kind, and the answer the structure supplies is that the technique is the point. Verse 84 said God gave Dhu'l-Qarnayn the means to all things; verse 96 shows what the means look like when used. The gift is the availability of iron, copper, fire, and labour; the work is the assembling of them.

The instruction is the one the tradition states as *tawakkul* combined with *asbāb* — reliance on God together with the use of means. The ḥadīth at Jāmiʿ al-Tirmidhī is the standard formulation:

> *"Tie your camel, and then place your trust in God."* (Jāmiʿ al-Tirmidhī 2517)

The verse exhibits the principle at the scale of a civilisation-defining project, and it does so by describing the project rather than by stating the principle.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:97

> **Thus they were not able to surmount it, nor could they pierce it.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Barrier Tested**

*Fa-mā staṭāʿū an yaẓharūhu wa-mā staṭāʿū lahu naqbā* — al-Suyūṭī explains the two verbs: they could not scale it from above (*yaẓharūhu*, from *ẓ-h-r*, to appear above, to climb over) and could not pierce it from below (*naqb*, a hole bored through).

The verse is a report of the outcome, and the commentators note its two-fold structure. A barrier can fail in two ways: by being climbed or by being breached. The verse denies both, which is to say the construction was complete in the only two dimensions that matter.

**The Two Modes of Failure and the Completeness of the Work**

The commentators note that a barrier can fail in exactly two ways: by being climbed or by being breached. The verse denies both, and the double denial is a statement about the quality of the construction rather than about the weakness of the attackers. Al-Suyūṭī's gloss is precise: they could not scale it from above nor pierce it from below.

The commentators have drawn the general principle: a thing properly built does not require continuous defence, because its quality is its defence. The wall stands because of how it was made, not because anyone is guarding it. This is the chapter's statement about the durability of good work, and it stands in deliberate contrast to verse 35 — the garden owner's claim that his garden *"will never perish"* — which was a claim about permanence made without reference to how the thing was made or who sustained it.

**Staṭāʿa and the Chapter's Vocabulary of Limits**

The verb *istaṭāʿa* returns from verses 67, 68, and 78, where it described Moses's inability to bear patiently with Khiḍr. The chapter uses the same word for the limit of a prophet's patience and the limit of Gog and Magog's strength, and the recurrence is thematically significant: the chapter is concerned throughout with limits — the limit of what Moses could bear, the limit of what the garden could last, the limit of the sleepers' knowledge of time, the limit of the sea as ink (v. 109).

Verse 97 states a limit that holds, and verse 98 will state when it stops holding. The pairing is the chapter's eschatology in miniature: every barrier holds until the Promise comes, and then God makes it level with the ground. The reader is told both facts, in that order, so that neither is held without the other.

**The Sufficiency of Good Work**

The commentators have drawn a practical instruction from the verse's economy. The Qur'an does not narrate an attack, a siege, or a battle. It reports the outcome in a single clause: *they were not able*. The barrier's efficacy is stated as a fact rather than dramatized, which is characteristic of the chapter's method — it states outcomes rather than staging scenes.

The instruction is about the relation between quality and effort. The work was done thoroughly at verse 96 — iron levelled, bellows worked, copper poured — and verse 97 is the result. The chapter does not describe ongoing maintenance, and the commentators have not supplied any. What holds is what was made well.

**The Verse and the Question of Maintenance**

The commentators note that the Qur'an describes no ongoing maintenance of the wall — no garrison, no repairs, no watch. The barrier holds because of how it was built. Al-Suyūṭī's gloss is that Gog and Magog could neither scale it nor pierce it, and the verse states this as a settled condition rather than a defended one.

The commentators have drawn the general principle: durable protection is built into the thing rather than added to it. A structure that requires constant defence has been built to the wrong standard; a structure built correctly defends itself. The instruction extends beyond engineering to every form of protection — of a community, a reputation, a conviction, a family — and the chapter's point is that the quality of the original work determines the endurance of the result.

The tradition has nonetheless recorded the report, preserved by al-Suyūṭī, that Khiḍr and Elisha meet every night at the barrier to prevent Gog and Magog from getting out. The commentators preserve the report without making it part of the Qur'anic claim, which is the verse's own: they were not able.

**Why the Attack Is Not Narrated**

The commentators note that the Qur'an reports the outcome without staging the attempt. There is no siege, no assault, no dramatic confrontation — only the statement of inability. This is characteristic of the chapter's method throughout: it states outcomes rather than performing scenes. The effect is to make the wall's efficacy a matter of fact rather than of narrative tension. The reader is not invited to wonder whether it will hold; he is told that it does, and then told at verse 98 when it will stop.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:98

> **He said, "This is a mercy from my Lord. And when the Promise of my Lord comes, He will crumble it to dust. And the Promise of my Lord is true."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"This Is a Mercy From My Lord"**

*Qāla hādhā raḥmatun min rabbī* — the chapter's most instructive sentence about power. Having built the greatest engineering work in the Qur'an, at his own expense, for a people who cannot even speak clearly, Dhu'l-Qarnayn attributes it to God's mercy.

The commentators — al-Rāzī and others — note the two readings: the barrier itself is a mercy, or the *ability* to construct it is the mercy. Both readings make the same point: the achievement is not his.

The contrast with the garden owner is now complete and it is the chapter's central moral comparison. The man with two gardens said *"I think not that this will ever perish."* The man with a barrier of iron and copper says *"this is a mercy from my Lord, and when the Promise comes He will make it level with the ground."* Both had something great. One attributed it to himself and asserted its permanence; the other attributed it to God and announced its end. The chapter awards the second man the trial.

**"When the Promise of My Lord Comes"**

*Fa-idhā jāʾa waʿdu rabbī jaʿalahu dakkāʾ* — *dakkāʾ* denotes levelled, flattened, reduced to the ground. Al-Suyūṭī explains the timing: with the onset of the events of the Hour — after Jesus has killed the Antichrist, according to some — as the Resurrection draws near.

The commentators connect the crumbling of the barrier to the Qur'an's descriptions of the mountains at the Hour: 20:105, 56:5, 69:14, 70:9, 73:14, 77:10, 78:20, 81:3, 101:5. The barrier joins the mountains, the gardens, and the vegetation in the chapter's catalogue of things that will be levelled.

The phrase *waʿdu rabbī* — "the Promise of my Lord" — returns from verse 21, where the discovery of the sleepers was so that people *"might know that God's Promise is true."* The Qur'an repeats the affirmation at 4:122, 10:4, 10:55, 28:13, 30:60, 31:33, 35:5, 40:77.

**"And the Promise of My Lord Is True"**

*Wa kāna waʿdu rabbī ḥaqqā* — the statement closes the Dhu'l-Qarnayn narrative and returns the chapter to the Name it used at verse 44: *al-Ḥaqq*, the Real. What is true is God's Promise; what is real is God. The barrier, the gardens, the mountains, and the adornment of the earth are all *not* that — and the chapter has spent ninety-eight verses demonstrating it.

**The Statement as a Model**

The commentators have drawn a practical instruction from the verse that is worth stating. A person who accomplishes something great faces a choice about how to describe it. The natural description is achievement: I built this. The verse gives the alternative: this is a mercy from my Lord, and it will not last.

The alternative is not false modesty. Dhu'l-Qarnayn does not deny that he built the wall; verse 96 shows him directing the construction in detail. What he denies is that the wall is *his* in the sense that matters — self-originated, self-sustaining, permanent. The distinction is the whole of the chapter's teaching about wealth, power, and knowledge, and it is delivered in the mouth of the one man in the Qur'an who had the strongest grounds for boasting.

**The Attribution and the Announcement of the End**

The commentators note that Dhu'l-Qarnayn's statement contains both halves of the chapter's teaching about achievement: the attribution (*"this is a mercy from my Lord"*) and the announcement of the end (*"when the Promise of my Lord comes, He will crumble it to dust"*). Neither half is separable from the other, and the commentators have observed that the second is what makes the first credible.

A person who says "this is a mercy from God" while believing the thing will last forever has not actually attributed it; he has decorated his ownership with pious language. Dhu'l-Qarnayn's attribution is verified by his willingness to state the barrier's end. The commentators have drawn the test: the sincerity of an attribution is measured by whether the speaker can also say when the thing will be gone.

**The Wall in the Tradition's Eschatology**

The commentators connect the verse to 21:96–97 — *"until, when Gog and Magog are let loose and they rush down from every hill, and the true promise draws nigh"* — and to the ḥadīth at al-Bukhārī (3346) and Muslim (2880) from Zaynab bint Jaḥsh, in which the Prophet ﷺ woke saying:

> *"There is no god but God! Woe to the Arabs from an evil that has drawn near. Today a breach like this has been opened in the wall of Gog and Magog"* — making a circle with his thumb and forefinger. (al-Bukhārī 3346; Muslim 2880)

The tradition has consistently read the barrier as real and as gradually failing, and the commentators have differed on its location and current state. Al-Suyūṭī records the report that Khiḍr and Elisha meet every night at the barrier to prevent Gog and Magog from getting out. What the Qur'an states is the verse's own claim: when the Promise comes, God will make it level with the ground — and the Promise is true.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:99

> **And We shall leave them, on that Day, to surge against one another like waves. And the trumpet shall be blown, and We shall gather them together.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Shall Leave Them to Surge Against One Another"**

*Wa taraknā baʿḍahum yawmaʾidhin yamūju fī baʿḍ* — the verb *māja* means to surge, to roll like waves, to move in confused multitude. Ibn Kathīr identifies the day as the Last Day, before the Resurrection but after the slaying of the Antichrist.

The commentators differ on who surges against whom. Ibn Kathīr reads it as Gog and Magog and human beings, whose lives and property they are overtaking. Al-Jalālayn and Ibn Kathīr also report the reading that it is human beings and jinn coming against one another. Al-Jalālayn adds the report that these forces will not be able to enter Makkah, Madinah, or Jerusalem.

The image is of total social dissolution — the order that held human communities together removed, and the resulting motion described as the motion of water. The chapter has used water throughout: the stream in the garden (v. 33), the water that sinks (v. 41), the water that becomes chaff (v. 45), the sea that would be exhausted as ink (v. 109). Here water is the image of chaos rather than of life.

**"And We Shall Gather Them Together"**

*Fa-jamaʿnāhum jamʿā* — the verb *ḥashara* of verse 47 returns in the form *jamaʿa*. The commentators note that the gathering of verse 47 — *"We will leave not one of them behind"* — is here restated at the end of the eschatological sequence.

The chapter's structure closes on itself. Verse 47 announced the gathering; verses 48–53 described the presentation, the Book, the Fire; verses 54–98 gave the final narratives; verses 99–108 return to the Day. The reader arrives at the end of the chapter having been shown the gathering from two directions.

**The Image of Surging**

The commentators note the verb *māja* — to surge, to roll like waves, to move in confused multitude — and its aptness. Water in motion has no order, no direction, and no internal structure; it moves by pressure and mass. To describe human beings surging against one another as waves is to describe the complete dissolution of the social order that gives human collective life its shape.

The chapter has used water throughout as an image of life: the stream in the garden (v. 33), the water sent down from the sky (v. 45), the sea that would be exhausted as ink (v. 109). Here water becomes the image of chaos, and the reversal is the verse's content. The element that made the garden flourish is the same element whose motion describes the end of order.

**The Trumpet and the Tradition's Account**

Ibn Kathīr identifies the trumpet as blown by Isrāfīl or another archangel, and the Qur'an refers to it at 20:102, 23:101, 27:87, 36:51, 39:68, 50:20, 69:13, 74:8, and 78:18. At 39:68 the two blows are described:

> *"And the trumpet shall be blown, and all who are in the heavens and all who are on the earth shall swoon, save whom God wills. Then it shall be blown a second time, and behold, they are standing, looking on."* (Qur'an 39:68)

The commentators note the sequence in this verse: the surging, then the trumpet, then the gathering. The dissolution precedes the summons, and the summons precedes the assembly — which is the order of verse 47 restated at the end of the chapter's eschatological sequence.

**The Gathering Restated**

*Fa-jamaʿnāhum jamʿā* — the commentators note that the gathering announced at verse 47 (*"We will leave not one of them behind"*) is restated here, at the close of the Dhu'l-Qarnayn narrative and immediately before the presentation of Hell. The chapter's structure closes on itself: the reader arrives at the end having been shown the gathering from two directions, and the completeness of it — nobody left behind, nothing left out (v. 49) — is stated twice.

The repetition is not redundancy. It is the chapter's way of ensuring that the reader does not reach the end of the narratives without carrying the eschatology with him. The wall crumbles, the peoples surge, the trumpet sounds, and everyone is gathered — including the reader.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:100

> **And We shall present Hell, on that Day, as an array before the disbelievers,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Before the Disbelievers"**

*Li'l-kāfirīn* — the presentation is specified as being for the disbelievers. The commentators note that the verse thereby distinguishes the two groups before the destination is named: verse 100 presents Hell to one group, verse 107 presents the Garden as a welcome to the other. The chapter's final movement is a diptych, and the two panels use the same verb of presentation.

**Presentation as a Portion of the Punishment**

The commentators note that the verse describes a *display* — the verb *ʿaraḍa* is the verb of presenting troops or goods for inspection, used at verse 48 of the people arrayed before their Lord. Hell is not merely a destination the disbelievers arrive at; it is shown to them, in advance of arrival, so that they see it and hear it.

Al-Jalālayn explains the purpose: so that they can see it and hear its raging and groaning before they enter. The Qur'an describes the Fire's perception of them at 25:12 — *"when it sees them from a distant place, they hear its raging and roaring"* — and at 67:7–8 the Fire is described as almost bursting with rage, its keepers asking each company whether no warner came to them.

The commentators have drawn the psychological point: the anticipation is itself a portion of the reckoning. The Day is arranged so that nothing is a surprise, and the certainty of what is coming is part of what is coming.

**The Demand Granted**

The commentators connect the verse to the demand recorded at verse 55 — that the disbelievers wait for the punishment to come upon them face to face — and to the mockery at verse 56. The thing they asked to see is displayed, and the display is not a vindication but the beginning of the consequence.

The chapter's argument about mockery is now complete. The person who mocks a warning is not making an intellectual claim; he is declining to be addressed. Verse 100 ends the declination by addressing him anyway, in the only register left. The commentators have noted the justice of the structure: the disbeliever chose the visible over the reported, and the Day gives him the visible.

**The Diptych Completed by Verse 107**

The commentators note that verse 100 and verse 107 form a pair using the same verb of presentation and the same noun of hospitality. Hell is presented to the disbelievers; the Gardens of Firdaws are a *nuzul* for the believers. The chapter's final eschatological image is a diptych, and the two panels are drawn with the same instruments.

The symmetry is the chapter's last argument. Both groups arrive; both are received; what is set before each is described in the same vocabulary. The difference is not in the manner of the reception but in what is served.

**The Verse's Argument Against Postponement**

The commentators connect the presentation to the chapter's repeated warnings about delay. The disbeliever's posture throughout has been one of postponement — waiting for the wont of the ancients (v. 55), doubting that the Hour is imminent (v. 36), demanding a sign before believing. Verse 100 ends the postponement by making the deferred thing visible.

The Qur'an states the mechanism at 57:14:

> *"They will call out to them: Were we not with you? They will say: Yes, but you put yourselves to trial, and waited, and doubted, and vain desires deluded you, until the command of God came; and the Deluder deluded you concerning God."* (Qur'an 57:14)

The four elements — self-testing, waiting, doubt, and delusion — are the stages of postponement, and the verse names the delusion as being *concerning God*: the belief that His forbearance means His indifference. Verse 58 stated the truth about the forbearance (*"thy Lord is Forgiving, Possessed of Mercy"*); verse 100 states what happens when the tryst arrives.

The practical instruction is the discipline of not treating delay as evidence. The reader who reasons that nothing has happened, therefore nothing will happen, is performing exactly the inference the verse is answering.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:101

> **those whose eyes were veiled from the remembrance of Me, and could not hear.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Could Not Hear"**

*Wa kānū lā yastaṭīʿūna samʿā* — the verb *istaṭāʿa* returns from verses 67, 68, 78, and 97. The chapter's word for incapacity is applied here to the disbelievers' hearing. The commentators note the irony: the capacity they could not exercise for guidance is the same capacity whose limits the chapter has traced elsewhere.

The verse's force for the reader is diagnostic. The covering is not described as an arbitrary imposition but as the settled state of those who turned away. Every reader receives reminders; the verse describes what happens to the faculty when the reminders are consistently refused.

**The Two Faculties and Their Covering**

The commentators note that the verse names the two organs by which guidance is received — sight and hearing — and describes both as disabled. The Qur'an consistently pairs them as the instruments of perception whose misuse is the root of unbelief:

> *"Say: He is the One Who originated you, and made for you hearing, and sight, and hearts. Little thanks you give!"* (Qur'an 67:23)

> *"And We had certainly created for Hell many of the jinn and mankind. They have hearts wherewith they understand not, eyes wherewith they see not, and ears wherewith they hear not. They are like cattle — nay, they are further astray. They are the heedless."* (Qur'an 7:179)

The verse's description is therefore not of a physical defect but of a functional one: the faculties are present and do not perform their function, because they have been covered.

**"From the Remembrance of Me"**

The commentators note that the covering is specified — *ʿan dhikrī*, from My remembrance. The verse does not describe general blindness but a directed one: they could see the world; they could not see what would have reminded them of God. This is the condition verse 57 described as the outcome of turning away from the signs.

The chapter has now traced the full arc: the signs are given (v. 56), the person turns away (v. 57), the covering is placed (v. 57), and the covered state is what he brings into the Day (v. 101). The covering is not an arbitrary imposition but the settled condition of consistent refusal, and the verse describes it from the inside — as the disbeliever's own experience of the world.

**The Diagnostic Force for the Reader**

The commentators have drawn the practical instruction. The covering is described as the *result* of turning away, and every reader receives reminders. The mechanism the verse describes operates gradually and is not perceptible from inside, which is precisely why the verse exists: to give the reader an external description of a condition he cannot detect in himself.

The tradition's countermeasure is regular remembrance — *dhikr*, the Friday gathering, the annual fast, the repeated recitation. The person who hears the same reminders repeatedly is not learning something new; he is being kept from the covering. This is one of the reasons the chapter is recited weekly, and the practice is the verse's practical answer.

**Why Sight and Hearing Rather Than the Heart**

The commentators note that verse 57 located the covering on the *heart* and the deafness in the *ears*, while this verse places the covering over the *eyes*. The variation is not careless. The Qur'an's account of spiritual perception distributes it across the faculties — heart, sight, hearing — and describes the failure at each.

At 22:46 the Qur'an states the point explicitly:

> *"Have they not travelled in the land, that they might have hearts wherewith to understand, or ears wherewith to hear? Truly it is not the eyes that are blind, but the hearts that are in the breasts."* (Qur'an 22:46)

The verse clarifies that the blindness is not optical, and verse 101's description of veiled eyes should be read in that light. The faculties named are the ones by which a person takes in the world; the covering is on the taking-in, not on the organ.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:102

> **Do the disbelievers reckon that they can take My servants as protectors apart from Me? Verily We have prepared Hell as a welcome for the wrongdoers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Answers Itself**

*Afa-ḥasiba alladhīna kafarū an yattakhidhū ʿibādī min dūnī awliyāʾ* — "do the disbelievers reckon that they can take My servants as protectors apart from Me?" The interrogative is one of incredulity. The commentators note the reference of *ʿibādī* — "My servants" — which includes the angels, Jesus, and the righteous beings whom the polytheists invoked.

The verse returns to the concept of *walāyah* from verse 44 and *awliyāʾ* from verse 50. The chapter has now asked the question three times: will you take Satan and his progeny as protectors (v. 50)? Do you reckon you can take My servants as protectors (v. 102)? And it has answered both with the same fact: protection belongs to God, the Real (v. 44).

Al-Qurṭubī cites the Qur'an's standard statement of the matter:

> *"The parable of those who take protectors apart from God is that of the spider that makes a house. Truly the frailest of houses is the spider's house, if they but knew."* (Qur'an 29:41)

And the repeated warnings against seeking protectors other than God: 7:3, 13:16, 42:9, 2:257, 42:46.

**"Hell as a Welcome"**

*Innā aʿtadnā jahannama li'l-kāfirīna nuzulā* — *nuzul* is what is prepared for a guest, the provision set out on arrival, the hospitality offered to one who comes. Al-Qurṭubī and the commentators note the irony: the word denotes an offering of hospitality, and it is applied to Hell.

The Qur'an uses the same ironic register elsewhere — the disbelievers "given glad tidings" of painful punishment (31:7, 45:8), the Fire described as their "refuge" (24:57), Hell as a "welcome" at 56:52–56 and 56:92–94. The commentators note the counterpart at verse 107, where the Garden is described as a *nuzul* for the believers.

The rhetorical effect is considerable. Hospitality is the most basic social obligation, and in Arabian culture the most sacred. To describe Hell as the hospitality prepared for its guests is to say that the disbelievers will receive exactly what is due to them, in the form they would recognise — and that the recognition will not soften it.

**The Verse's Function**

Verse 102 closes the chapter's argument about protection. The reader has been shown: the youths with no protector but God, preserved (vv. 9–26); the garden owner with no party to help him (v. 43); protection belonging to God the Real (v. 44); Satan and his progeny as enemies taken as protectors (v. 50); the claimed partners failing to respond (v. 52); and now the final question. The chapter's answer is consistent throughout, and it is the answer the last verse will require the reader to act on.

**"My Servants": The Reference and Its Force**

The commentators note that *ʿibādī* — "My servants" — refers to the beings whom the polytheists invoked: the angels, Jesus, and the righteous dead. The verse's force lies in the possessive: they are *God's* servants, and taking them as protectors apart from God is a category error, because a servant cannot protect against his Master.

The commentators cite the Qur'an's own statements on the point:

> *"Say: Call upon those whom you claim apart from God. They possess not the weight of an atom in the heavens or the earth, nor have they any share in either, nor is there for Him any supporter among them."* (Qur'an 34:22)

> *"And those whom they call upon apart from God create nothing, but are themselves created. They are dead, not living; and they know not when they shall be raised."* (Qur'an 16:20–21)

The verse's question is therefore rhetorical in the strictest sense: the answer is contained in the terms of the question, and the person who asks it honestly has already answered it.

**The Chapter's Three Questions About Protection**

The commentators note that the chapter asks the question about protection three times and answers it identically each time. Verse 44: *"there, protection belongs to God, the Real."* Verse 50: *"will you then take him and his progeny as protectors apart from Me, though they are an enemy unto you?"* Verse 102: *"do the disbelievers reckon that they can take My servants as protectors apart from Me?"*

The repetition is the chapter's doctrinal spine. The reader has been shown the garden owner with no party to help him (v. 43), the sleepers with no protector but God (v. 26), the claimed partners failing to respond (v. 52), and the disbelievers' helplessness before the Fire (v. 53). Each narrative is an exhibit, and verse 102 is the closing argument.
## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:103

> **Say, "Shall I inform you who are the greatest losers in respect to their deeds?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Introduces the Chapter's Verdict**

*Qul hal unabbiʾukum bi'l-akhsarīna aʿmālā* — "Say: shall I inform you of those who are the greatest losers in respect of their deeds?" The interrogative is an invitation, and the commentators note its rhetorical function: the reader is asked to consider a question he already has an answer to, and the answer the verse will give is not the one he expects.

The word *akhsar* is the elative of *khusr* — loss, ruin, the state of one whose transaction has failed. The Qur'an uses the vocabulary of commerce for the religious life consistently: 2:16 (*"their trade has not profited them"*), 35:29 (*"who hope for a trade that will not fail"*), 61:10 (*"a trade that will deliver you from a painful punishment"*). The religious life is a transaction, and this verse identifies those who lose most in it.

**The Vocabulary of Loss**

The commentators note that the verse uses the language of commerce, which the Qur'an employs consistently for the religious life. *Khusr* is the loss of a trader whose transaction has failed; *akhsar* is the one who loses most. The Qur'an states the same framing at 2:16 (*"their trade has not profited them"*), 35:29 (*"who hope for a trade that will not fail"*), 61:10 (*"shall I guide you to a trade that will deliver you from a painful punishment?"*), and 103:2 (*"truly man is in loss"*).

The framing is deliberate and the commentators have drawn its implications. A transaction has inputs, a process, and a return. The verse is not describing people who made no investment; it is describing people whose investment was large and whose return was nil. The loss is proportionate to the outlay, which is what makes it the *greatest* loss.

**"In Respect to Their Deeds"**

The commentators note the specification, and it is decisive. *Bi'l-akhsarīna aʿmālā* — the greatest losers *in respect of their deeds*. The verse is not describing the idle or the passive but the active: people whose efforts were considerable and whose loss is measured against them.

This is the chapter's sharpest observation about the human condition, and verse 104 will explain it. The worst outcome in the Qur'an's accounting is not failure to act but action in the wrong direction, sustained over a lifetime, and believed to be virtue. The person who does nothing has not invested and cannot lose much; the person who has invested everything in the wrong direction has.

**Why the Question Comes Here**

The commentators note the placement. The verse follows the diptych of verses 100–102 — Hell presented to the disbelievers, Hell as a welcome. Having shown the destination, the chapter asks who ends up there, and the answer is not "the wicked" in the ordinary sense. It is people who thought they were doing well.

The question is put through the Prophet ﷺ — *qul*, "say" — which places it in the register of proclamation. The chapter's final section is not a narrative or a parable but a direct address, and it opens by inviting the hearer to identify the greatest losers. The reader who answers "other people" has not yet understood the question, and verses 104–105 exist to correct him.

**The Invitation Rather Than the Accusation**

The commentators note that the verse is framed as an offer — *hal unabbiʾukum*, "shall I inform you?" — rather than as a denunciation. The reader is invited to learn something he does not know, and the framing implies that he does not already know it.

This is significant for the verse's audience. The person who already knows who the greatest losers are will answer the question with a list of other people. The verse's offer presupposes that the answer will surprise him, and verses 104–105 confirm the presupposition: the greatest losers are those who reckon themselves virtuous. The commentators have drawn the homiletic point that the verse is addressed most urgently to the religiously confident; the disbeliever who knows he disbelieves is not the primary addressee, but the believer certain of his own standing is. This is why the tradition has read the verse alongside the ḥadīth on the "minor shirk" — ostentation — as a warning directed inward.

**Why the Question Precedes the Conclusion**

The commentators note the placement: the verse follows the diptych of verses 100–102 and precedes the chapter's final two verses. Having shown the two destinations, the chapter asks who goes to which, and the answer determines everything.

The structure is deliberate. A reader who has followed the narratives might conclude that the destinations are settled by categories he can identify from outside — believers here, disbelievers there. The verse interrupts that conclusion by asking him to identify the greatest losers, and the answer turns out to describe a *condition* rather than a label. The reader who recognises the condition in himself has understood the chapter; the one who does not has read it without benefit.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:104

> **Those whose efforts go astray in the life of this world, while they reckon that they are virtuous in their works."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Definition of the Greatest Loss**

*Alladhīna ḍalla saʿyuhum fī al-ḥayāti al-dunyā wa hum yaḥsabūna annahum yuḥsinūna ṣunʿā* — "those whose striving has gone astray in the life of this world, while they reckon that they are doing well in their work."

Two clauses, and the second is what makes the loss total. Al-Zamakhsharī and Ibn Kathīr note the verb *ḍalla* — to go astray, to be lost, to perish — applied not to the persons but to their *striving*. The effort is not merely unsuccessful; it is *lost*, the way a thing is lost that cannot be recovered.

**"While They Reckon That They Are Doing Well"**

*Wa hum yaḥsabūna annahum yuḥsinūna ṣunʿā* — the commentators note that *yuḥsinūna ṣunʿā* means they consider themselves skilled, excellent, virtuous in their workmanship. The Qur'an states the same at 6:108 and elsewhere: the deeds of the disbelievers and wrongdoers seem fair to them.

This is the verse's central insight and it is psychologically precise. The greatest losers are not people who know they are failing. They are people who believe they are succeeding — whose self-assessment is positive, whose peers confirm it, whose efforts are visible and considerable. The loss consists precisely in the fact that they cannot detect it.

The commentators have drawn the practical warning, and it is one of the most uncomfortable in the chapter. The ordinary human test of whether one is on the right path is whether things seem to be going well. This verse declares that test invalid. The person whose striving has gone astray experiences it as going well; the discrepancy between felt success and actual loss is what makes the loss "greatest."

**Ghurūr: The Tradition's Treatment of Delusion**

The commentators connect the verse to the concept of *ghurūr* — delusion, self-deception — which the Islamic tradition has treated extensively. Al-Ghazzālī's *Iḥyāʾ ʿUlūm al-Dīn* devotes a section to the categories of the deluded: the scholar deluded by his knowledge, the worshipper by his worship, the wealthy man by his generosity, the Sufi by his spiritual states. In each case the delusion consists in taking a partial good for a sufficient one.

The tradition's diagnostic is uncomfortable and the verse supplies its scriptural ground. The deluded person is not stupid or wicked; he is engaged, diligent, and sincere within a frame that does not contain what matters. The verse describes exactly this: *striving* that goes astray, not idleness. The effort is real; the direction is not.

**The Connection to the Chapter's Other Figures**

The commentators note that the verse describes in general terms the condition the chapter has exhibited in particulars. The garden owner reckoned himself virtuous in his works — he had built a flourishing estate and said so (vv. 34–36). The Makkans of verse 28 reckoned themselves people of standing entitled to choose their company. The disbelievers of verse 56 mocked the warnings while considering themselves sound.

Every one of them passed their own test. The chapter's argument is that the test was invalid, and verse 105 explains why: the deeds lacked the one thing that gives deeds weight, and the absence was invisible to the people performing them.

**Why the Verse Speaks of Striving Rather Than of Neglect**

The commentators note that the verse's subject is *saʿy* — striving, effort, endeavour — and that the loss is defined in relation to it. The greatest losers are not the negligent; they are the diligent. The Qur'an's harshest eschatological verdict falls on effort expended in the wrong direction.

The reason is arithmetic, and the commentators have drawn it. A person who does nothing has invested nothing and loses little. A person who has invested a lifetime of labour, sacrifice, and sincerity into a framework that does not contain what matters loses everything he invested. The loss is proportionate to the outlay, which is why the verse calls it the greatest. This is why the verse is more unsettling than a simple condemnation of the wicked: the wicked are easily identified and easily avoided, but the diligent and sincere person whose striving has gone astray is not, and he may be the reader.

**The Criterion the Verse Implies**

The commentators note that the verse does not state the criterion by which striving is judged sound or astray, and that verse 105 supplies it: belief in the signs and in the meeting. The criterion is not the intensity of the effort, its social usefulness, or the sincerity of the agent, but its orientation toward God and the Hereafter.

The instruction is available as a diagnostic. The reader can assess his own striving by asking not whether it is intense or admirable but whether it is ordered toward the meeting — which is the question verse 110 will put to him directly: *"whosoever hopes for the meeting with his Lord, let him perform righteous deeds."* The chapter ends by giving the test it introduced here.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:105

> **They are those who disbelieve in the signs of their Lord, and in the meeting with Him. Thus their deeds have come to naught, and on the Day of Resurrection We shall assign them no weight.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Identification**

*Ulāʾika alladhīna kafarū bi-āyāti rabbihim wa liqāʾihi fa-ḥabiṭat aʿmāluhum* — "those are the ones who disbelieved in the signs of their Lord and in the meeting with Him; so their deeds have come to naught." The commentators identify the two objects of disbelief: the signs (*āyāt*) — the revelations and the phenomena that point to God — and the meeting (*liqāʾ*) — the encounter with God on the Day of Judgment.

The pairing is significant. Disbelief in the signs is an error about the present; disbelief in the meeting is an error about the future. Together they remove both the evidence and the accountability, and the result is the condition verse 104 described: striving that goes nowhere, experienced as success.

**"We Shall Assign Them No Weight"**

*Fa-lā nuqīmu lahum yawma al-qiyāmati waznā* — the verb *aqāma al-wazn* means to establish a weight, to give weight to. The commentators connect it to the Qur'an's imagery of the Scale at 7:8–9 and 21:47. The statement is not that the deeds will be weighed and found light; it is that no weight will be established for them at all.

There is a well-known ḥadīth that the commentators cite in connection with this verse, recorded by al-Bukhārī (4729) and Muslim (2788) from Abū Hurayrah:

> *"There will come a great, fat man on the Day of Resurrection, and he will not weigh with God as much as the wing of a gnat. Recite, if you wish: 'And We shall assign them no weight on the Day of Resurrection.'"* (al-Bukhārī 4729; Muslim 2788)

The ḥadīth makes the point physically: bulk and weight in this world are not weight in that one, and the verse's phrase is literal in the reckoning even where it is metaphorical in the marketplace.

**The Chapter's Answer to the Garden Owner**

The verse is the doctrinal statement of what the parable narrated. The garden owner had wealth, children, a flourishing estate, and a confident self-assessment. Verse 105 states what all of it amounts to in the reckoning, and the answer is: no weight. The chapter has now closed the circle it opened at verses 7–8, and the reader can see that the parable was not a story about one man but a demonstration of the verse.

**The Two Objects of Disbelief**

The commentators note that the verse identifies two objects: the signs (*āyāt*) and the meeting (*liqāʾ*). The first is an error about the present — the rejection of the evidence available now. The second is an error about the future — the rejection of the accountability to come. Together they remove both the ground of belief and the consequence of unbelief, and the result is the condition verse 104 described.

The commentators note that the Qur'an pairs these two repeatedly, as at 7:147:

> *"As for those who deny Our signs and the meeting of the Hereafter, their deeds have come to naught. Are they requited for anything save what they used to do?"* (Qur'an 7:147)

The pairing is not accidental. Belief in the signs without belief in the meeting produces a person who assents but does not act; belief in the meeting without the signs produces a person who acts without knowing toward what. The two together are the minimum structure of a religious life, and their joint absence is what the verse identifies.

**"No Weight" and the Imagery of the Scale**

The commentators connect *fa-lā nuqīmu lahum yawma al-qiyāmati waznā* to the Qur'an's imagery of the Scale at 7:8–9 and 21:47:

> *"And We shall set up the just scales for the Day of Resurrection, and no soul shall be wronged in anything. Though it be the weight of a grain of mustard seed, We shall bring it. And sufficient are We as reckoners."* (Qur'an 21:47)

The verse's statement is stronger than a light weighing: no weight is *established* for them at all. The commentators cite the ḥadīth at al-Bukhārī (4729) and Muslim (2788) from Abū Hurayrah, in which the Prophet ﷺ said of a great, fat man that he would not weigh with God as much as the wing of a gnat, and then recited this verse.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The commentators are careful about the distinction, and it matters. The verse does not say the disbeliever's acts were without worldly effect — a well that was dug gave water; a wall that was built stood; a life that was saved was saved.
