It says the acts have no weight *in the reckoning*, because they were not offered to God.

The distinction between worldly efficacy and eternal value is the verse's whole point, and it is the distinction verse 104's people failed to make. The chapter has now closed the circle it opened at verses 7–8: the adornment is real and passes away; the deeds endure and are weighted; and the person who invested in the first rather than the second is the greatest loser.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:106

> **That is their recompense—Hell—for having disbelieved and for having taken My signs and My messengers in mockery.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Recompense Named**

*Dhālika jazāʾuhum jahannamu bi-mā kafarū* — "that is their recompense: Hell, because they disbelieved." The commentators note the structure: the punishment is stated, and then its two causes are given.

The first cause is *kufr* — disbelief. The second is *ittikhādh al-āyāt wa'l-rusul huzuwā* — taking the signs and the messengers in mockery. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr connect the second to verse 56, where the chapter stated that the disbelievers *"take My signs and that whereof they were warned in mockery."*

**Mockery as a Named Cause**

The commentators note that the verse could have stated disbelief alone, and that its addition of mockery is deliberate. Al-Rāzī's reasoning at verse 56 applies: the person who doubts may be brought to certainty; the person who argues may be answered; the person who mocks has closed the door from inside and, in closing it, insulted the one who knocked.

The Qur'an's treatment of mockery is severe and consistent. At 9:65–66 it records the case of men who mocked and were told that by their mockery they had disbelieved after believing:

> *"And if thou askest them, they will surely say, 'We were only talking idly and playing.' Say: Was it God and His signs and His messenger that you were mocking? Make no excuse. You have disbelieved after your belief.'"* (Qur'an 9:65–66)

The commentators have drawn the ruling that ridicule of the sacred is not a peripheral offence but a constitutive one — not an expression of unbelief but an instance of it.

**The Chapter's Consistent Concern With Speech**

The commentators note the pattern the verse completes. Verse 5 condemned the *"monstrous word"* issuing from the mouths of those who claim God has a child. Verse 15 condemned fabricating a lie against God. Verse 35 recorded the boast. Verse 56 named mockery. Verse 106 makes mockery a named cause of the Fire.

The chapter is teaching that speech is not a secondary register of the religious life. What a person says about God, His signs, and His messengers is not commentary upon his belief; it is part of it, and it is accounted for. The commentators have drawn the practical instruction: the tongue is a site of religious obligation, and the casual remark about sacred things carries the weight the verse assigns to it.

**The Recompense Stated Plainly**

The commentators note the verse's directness. After the extended narratives, the parables, and the eschatological scenes, the chapter states the outcome in a single declarative sentence: *that is their recompense — Hell — because they disbelieved and took My signs and My messengers in mockery.*

The plainness is the point. The chapter has given the reader a hundred verses of story and argument, and the verse reduces it to a statement of fact with two reasons attached. The reader who has followed the chapter knows what each reason means, because he has watched both operate — in the garden owner, in the Makkans, in the man who asked to have the poor removed.

**The Two Causes and Their Relation**

The commentators note that the verse gives two causes for the recompense — disbelief, and taking the signs and messengers in mockery — and that the second is presented as an aggravation rather than an alternative. Mockery does not replace disbelief; it completes it.

The relation is causal in a specific sense. A person who merely disbelieves may remain open to evidence; a person who mocks has made the rejection part of his self-presentation, and the social cost of reversing it becomes high. The commentators have observed that mockery is often performed for an audience, which binds the mocker to his position in a way that private doubt does not.

This is why the Qur'an treats it as gravely as it does. The person who mocks has not only rejected the message but recruited others into the rejection, and the recruitment makes his own return harder. The verse names both causes because both are operative: the disbelief is the state, and the mockery is the mechanism by which the state becomes fixed.

**The Settlement of the Chapter's Account of Speech**

The commentators note that the chapter has tracked speech throughout: the monstrous word (v. 5), the fabricated lie (v. 15), the boast (v. 34), the confession (v. 38), the formula of attribution (v. 39), the mockery (v. 56), and now the accounting. The verse is the settlement of that account, and the instruction the tradition has drawn is that the tongue is a site of religious obligation with real consequences. The casual remark about sacred things is not weightless, and the verse assigns it a weight that most speakers do not anticipate.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:107

> **Those who believe and perform righteous deeds, theirs shall be the Gardens of Paradise as a welcome.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Panel of the Diptych**

*Inna alladhīna āmanū wa ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāti kānat lahum jannātu al-firdawsi nuzulā* — the verse answers verse 102 with the same noun: *nuzul*, the welcome, the hospitality prepared for a guest. Al-Qurṭubī and the commentators note the exact parallelism: Hell is a *nuzul* for the disbelievers; the Gardens of *Firdaws* are a *nuzul* for the believers.

The formula *alladhīna āmanū wa ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt* returns for the last time — the chapter has used it at verses 2, 30, 88, and now 107. Its repetition across the sūrah is the refrain of its soteriology: belief and righteous deeds, together, in that order.

**Jannāt al-Firdaws**

*Firdaws* is the highest region of the Garden, and the commentators cite the ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī (2790) and Muslim (2831):

> *"When you ask God, ask for al-Firdaws. It is the middle of the Garden and the highest of the Garden, and above it is the Throne of the Merciful, and from it the rivers of the Garden flow."* (al-Bukhārī 2790)

The Qur'an uses the term also at 23:11, at the close of the description of the believers' qualities: *"they are the inheritors who inherit al-Firdaws, abiding therein forever."*

The commentators — al-Qurṭubī among them — note that the verse names the highest Garden as the *nuzul*, the initial hospitality. The standard reading is that this is a statement of generosity: what is prepared for the believer's arrival is not a way-station but the summit.

**Nuzul as Reception**

The commentators note that *nuzul* denotes what is set out for a guest before the meal — the first thing offered on arrival. The Garden is therefore described not merely as a destination but as a reception: the believer arrives and finds something prepared for him, as a guest finds a table.

The commentators have drawn the theological point. The Garden is not earned in the sense of being purchased; it is *prepared*. The Qur'an states this at 3:133: *"And hasten unto forgiveness from your Lord, and a Garden whose breadth is the heavens and the earth, prepared (uʿiddat) for the reverent."* The verb of preparation is the same family as verse 102's *"We have prepared (aʿtadnā) Hell"* — both destinations are prepared in advance, and the arrival finds what has been made ready.

**The Vindication of the Chapter's Poor**

The commentators note that the verse vindicates everyone the chapter has defended: the seven youths who left home with nothing, the poor companion who had no garden, the believers of verse 28 whom the notables wanted removed, the two orphan boys whose treasure was preserved. Each is described by the formula *alladhīna āmanū wa ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt*, and each receives the *nuzul*.

The chapter's social argument and its eschatology are the same argument. The people this world rates lowest are the people verse 107 rates highest, and the chapter has spent a hundred verses making the reader see it. The reader who has followed the cave, the garden, Khiḍr, and Dhu'l-Qarnayn arrives at the verse knowing who its subjects are — because he has met them.

**The Formula's Final Appearance and Its Work**

The commentators note that *alladhīna āmanū wa ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt* appears here for the last time in the chapter, having occurred at verses 2, 30, and 88. Its repetition across the sūrah is the refrain of its soteriology, and its final placement — immediately after the description of Hell as a welcome — is deliberate.

The chapter has used the formula in three registers: as the recipients of the Book's glad tidings (v. 2), as those whose reward will not be neglected (v. 30), and as those to whom a righteous sovereign speaks easily (v. 88). Each register adds a dimension: the promise, the assurance, and the social treatment. Verse 107 supplies the fourth and final dimension — the destination. The commentators have drawn the instruction that the formula is not a description of a class but a specification of a path: the reader is not asked whether he belongs to it but whether he is doing the two things it names.

**The Garden as Prepared Rather Than Earned**

The commentators note that *nuzul* — the provision prepared for a guest — implies preparation by another. The Garden is not a wage extracted from a contract but a reception arranged in advance. The Qur'an states the same at 3:133: *"a Garden whose breadth is the heavens and the earth, prepared for the reverent."*

The same verbal family appears at verse 102 of the Fire, and in both cases the destination is made ready before anyone arrives at it. The commentators have drawn the theological point that the believer's reward is not proportionate to his deeds in any arithmetic sense, since the deeds of a finite life cannot purchase an eternal Garden; the deeds are the condition of the reception, not its price. This is why the chapter pairs faith and deeds rather than deeds alone, and why verse 105 says the deeds of the disbeliever have no weight while verse 30 says the deeds of the virtuous will not be neglected. The scale is real, and so is the generosity that exceeds it.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:108

> **Abiding therein, they seek not any change therefrom.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Seek Not Any Change Therefrom"**

*Lā yabghūna ʿanhā ḥiwalā* — *ḥiwal* is a turning away, a shifting, a transfer from one place or state to another; *yabghūna* means they seek or desire. The commentators explain the phrase: they do not desire to be moved from it, or to exchange it for anything else.

The commentators have drawn the significance, and it is the verse's deepest point. The perfection of the Garden is not only that its pleasures are great but that the *desire for change* is absent. In this world, no satisfaction is complete: the person who has what he wanted begins to want something else, and the restlessness is not a defect of the object but a condition of the world. The verse describes a state in which the restlessness itself has ended.

This is the answer to the garden owner's condition. He had two gardens, a stream, abundant fruit, and children — and he was not at rest. He boasted, he feared their loss, he claimed permanence for them. His dissatisfaction was not caused by a shortage but by the nature of what he had. Verse 108 describes the opposite condition: possession without anxiety, enjoyment without the itch for more.

**The Chapter's Last Description of the Reward**

The verse closes the chapter's eschatological section and prepares the final two verses. Having described the two destinations, the chapter turns to the inexhaustibility of God's Words (v. 109) and the Prophet's ﷺ humanity (v. 110). The reader is left with the picture of a resting place where nothing is sought — and then told, immediately, that the Words of his Lord could not be written down by the sea.

**Abiding Forever**

*Khālidīna fīhā* — the commentators note that the verse answers verse 3's *mākithīna fīhi abadan* and verse 31's *jannāt ʿadn*. The chapter's promise of permanence, stated at the outset, is confirmed at the close, and the confirmation is attached to the absence of the desire to leave.

The commentators have drawn the practical instruction. The believer's hope is not for a greater quantity of good but for a state in which the restlessness ends — which is what the Qur'an means by *salām*, peace, and what the Garden's greeting expresses: *"Their greeting therein will be, 'Peace'"* (10:10). The reader who has watched the garden owner's restlessness across ten verses knows what is being promised, and why it is worth more than the garden.

**The Verse as the Chapter's Definition of Success**

The commentators note that the chapter opened by promising the believers *"a beautiful reward"* (v. 2) and closes its eschatology by defining what makes a reward beautiful: it does not end, and the desire for it to be otherwise does not arise. The two criteria are inseparable. A good that ends cannot satisfy, because the anticipation of its ending is present in the enjoyment of it; a good that satisfies cannot be lost, because losing it would require wanting something else.

The Qur'an states the same at 89:27–30, in the address the tradition calls the culmination of the spiritual life:

> *"O soul at peace! Return unto thy Lord, well-pleased, well-pleasing. Enter among My servants, and enter My Garden."* (Qur'an 89:27–30)

The commentators connect *al-nafs al-muṭmaʾinnah* — the soul at peace — to this verse's *lā yabghūna ʿanhā ḥiwalā*. Peace is not the presence of pleasure but the absence of the itch, and the verse is the chapter's only direct description of it.

**Ḥiwalā and the End of Restlessness**

The commentators note the word *ḥiwalā* — a shifting, a turning away, a relocation — and have discussed its precise sense. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī read it as the desire to move on, to exchange one state for another.

The commentators have drawn the point that the verse denies not only departure but the *wish* for departure. The inhabitants do not merely stay; they do not want to be elsewhere. This is a stronger claim than permanence, and it is the chapter's definition of satisfaction.

The commentators have contrasted it with the condition of the world, where the desire for change is constant and is itself the engine of dissatisfaction. The person who is always contemplating an alternative is never fully where he is, and the verse presents the absence of that contemplation as a feature of the reward rather than a limitation of it.

The tradition has connected this to the state described at 89:27 — *al-nafs al-muṭmaʾinnah*, the soul at peace. Peace, on this reading, is not the satisfaction of desire but the end of its restlessness, and the verse is the chapter's only direct description of that state.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:109

> **Say, "If the sea were ink for the Words of my Lord, the sea would be exhausted before the Words of my Lord were exhausted, even if We brought the like thereof to replenish it."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Image**

*Qul law kāna al-baḥru midādan li-kalimāti rabbī la-nafida al-baḥru qabla an tanfada kalimātu rabbī wa law jiʾnā bi-mithlihi madadā* — the image is one of the Qur'an's most famous, and its parallel at 31:27 expands it:

> *"And if all the trees on earth were pens, and the sea [were ink], with seven more seas added to it, the Words of God would not be exhausted. Truly God is Mighty, Wise."* (Qur'an 31:27)

Al-Suyūṭī and al-Zamakhsharī note the components: the sea as *midād* — ink; the Words of the Lord as the text to be written; the exhaustion of the sea before the exhaustion of the Words; and the addition of a like quantity of sea, which changes nothing.

The commentators explain the logic: however vast the sea, it is finite; the Words of God are infinite and eternal (al-Jalālayn, al-Rāzī). The comparison is not between two quantities but between a quantity and a non-quantity.

**What "The Words of My Lord" Means**

The commentators offer a range of readings. Al-Suyūṭī and al-Zamakhsharī read it as God's knowledge and wisdom; al-Suyūṭī also reads it as the many spiritual meanings and benefits of the Qur'an and other divine revelation, rather than the literal words of the Qur'anic text. Al-Suyūṭī adds the reading that it refers to God's capacity to create and command whatever He wills, since God creates by the Word — 2:117:

> *"Originator of the heavens and the earth. And when He decrees a thing, He only says unto it, 'Be!' — and it is."* (Qur'an 2:117; see also 3:47, 6:73, 16:40, 19:35, 36:82, 40:68)

And 4:171, where Jesus's creation is described as God's Word committed to Mary. Al-Suyūṭī also reports the reading that the Words refer to the rewards promised and punishments threatened in the Hereafter, connecting to verses 102–108; al-Jalālayn reports the reading that they are the words God will speak to the people of the Garden.

The commentators preserve all of these without insisting on one, and the verse's force does not depend on the choice: whatever the Words are, they are inexhaustible.

**Why This Verse Comes at the End**

The placement is deliberate and the commentators have noted its function. The chapter has told four great stories, delivered a parable, described the Day, and stated its verdict. Verse 109 tells the reader that all of it — the cave, the garden, Khiḍr, Dhu'l-Qarnayn, the eschatology — is a fraction.

The effect is not to diminish what has been said but to relocate it. The reader who has worked through a hundred and eight verses might conclude that he has now grasped the chapter. Verse 109 denies the conclusion: what you have received is what the sea could not hold a fraction of.

There is a further connection to the chapter's theme of knowledge. Moses travelled to learn from Khiḍr and could not bear three acts. The Quraysh demanded answers as proof of prophethood. The garden owner claimed a knowledge of his own permanence. Verse 109 states the scale against which all such claims are measured, and it is the chapter's last word on the subject of what is known.

**The Practical Instruction**

The commentators have drawn an instruction that is characteristic of the Islamic tradition's relation to its own text: the Qur'an is inexhaustible, and no commentary — including this one — exhausts it. Al-Rāzī's massive commentary, al-Ṭabarī's, al-Qurṭubī's, Ibn Kathīr's, and every tafsīr written since are, in the verse's terms, a cup taken from the sea. The tradition's humility about its own scholarship is grounded here.

**The Second Sea**

The commentators note that the verse does not stop at the exhaustion of the sea. It adds: *"even if We brought the like of it as a supplement."* The image is doubled, and the commentators have drawn the significance of the doubling.

A single sea might be exhausted by a finite quantity of writing; the doubling forecloses the inference that the Words are merely very numerous. The commentators have read the second sea as a rhetorical device that converts a large quantity into an unbounded one — the Words are not greater than the sea by a margin but of a different order entirely.

The Qur'an makes the same point at 31:27:

> *"And if whatever trees are on the earth were pens, and the sea [were ink], reinforced by seven seas after it, the Words of God would not be exhausted."* (Qur'an 31:27)

The commentators have connected the two verses as the Qur'an's clearest statement of the inexhaustibility of divine knowledge, and have noted that the chapter places this statement immediately before its final verse, which returns to the human being: *"Say: I am only a human being like you."*

The juxtaposition is the chapter's last movement — from the boundlessness of God's Words to the limitedness of the one who conveys them, and then to the single thing asked of the reader: righteous action and hope in the meeting.

---

## Sūrah al-Kahf 18:110

> **Say, "I am only a human being like you. It is revealed unto me that your God is one God. So whosoever hopes for the meeting with his Lord, let him perform righteous deeds and make no one a partner unto his Lord in worship."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"I Am Only a Human Being Like You"**

*Qul innamā anā basharun mithlukum* — the chapter closes with the Prophet's ﷺ humanity. The commentators note the parallel assertions at 14:11, 17:93, and 41:6:

> *"Say: Had there been on the earth angels walking at ease, We would surely have sent down unto them an angel from the sky as a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:95)

And they note the objection the humanity provoked, recorded repeatedly: 6:91, 11:27, 14:10, 21:3, 23:24, 23:47, 26:154, 26:186, 36:15, 54:24, 64:6, 74:25. The Makkan objection to the Prophet ﷺ was substantially that he was ordinary — he ate food, walked in the markets, had no treasure, no angel accompanying him.

The verse does not answer the objection; it accepts the premise. *Basharun mithlukum* — a human being like you. The commentators have drawn the significance: the Qur'an's messenger is not a semi-divine intermediary but a man, and the message's authority rests on its content and its source rather than on the messenger's nature.

**"It Is Revealed Unto Me That Your God Is One God"**

*Yūḥā ilayya annamā ilāhukum ilāhun wāḥid* — the verse states the entirety of the prophetic commission in one clause. The commentators note the restrictive construction *innamā* — "only" — applied twice in the verse: I am *only* a human being; it is revealed to me *only* that your God is one God.

The double restriction is the verse's structure. The Prophet ﷺ claims nothing beyond humanity and nothing beyond the message. He does not claim knowledge of the Unseen (cf. 7:188: *"I do not possess the treasures of God, nor do I know the Unseen, nor do I say unto you that I am an angel"*), and he does not claim divinity or divinely-originated status for himself.

The commentators connect this to the chapter's occasion. The Quraysh tested the Prophet ﷺ with three questions. The chapter answered two, corrected him on the third occasion (vv. 23–24), and now closes by restating what he is: a human being to whom revelation comes. Not a miracle-worker on demand, not a soothsayer, not a king — a recipient.

**"Whosoever Hopes for the Meeting With His Lord"**

*Fa-man kāna yarjū liqāʾa rabbihi* — the verse turns from the Prophet ﷺ to the reader, and the word *liqāʾ* — the meeting — returns from verse 105, where disbelief in it was one of the two causes of the deeds coming to naught. The chapter's final instruction is addressed to the person who *hopes* for that meeting.

The verb *rajā* — to hope — is the same word verse 46 used: the enduring righteous deeds are *"better as a hope"* (*khayrun amalā*/*amalan*). The chapter closes by identifying the readership it has been addressing all along: not the disbeliever, not the mocker, but the one who hopes.

**The Two Requirements**

*Fa-l-yaʿmal ʿamalan ṣāliḥan wa lā yushrik bi-ʿibādati rabbihi aḥadā* — "let him do righteous work, and let him associate none with the worship of his Lord." The commentators note the pairing, which is the formula of verses 2, 30, 88, and 107 in a new form: righteous deeds, and the exclusion of *shirk*.

Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr record that because believers are by definition not committers of *shirk* in its outward sense, Ibn ʿAjībah and others cite the ḥadīth on the "minor shirk" in connection with this verse. Al-Tirmidhī records from Maḥmūd ibn Labīd:

> *"The thing I fear most for you is the lesser shirk." They said, "What is the lesser shirk, O Messenger of God?" He said, "Ostentation (riyāʾ)."* (al-Tirmidhī; graded ḥasan)

The instruction is thereby directed at the believer rather than at the idolater. The person who hopes for the meeting is told to do righteous work and to keep it free of association — including the subtle association of performing it to be seen.

**The Chapter's Final Word**

The verse is a remarkable closing. After a hundred and nine verses containing a cave of sleepers, a garden destroyed, a prophet humbled by a servant, a king who built a wall, a Day of gathering, and a sea that cannot write God's Words, the chapter ends with three plain sentences: I am a man like you; your God is one; do good and keep it pure.

The commentators have noted the proportion. The chapter's wonders are extensive and its conclusion is simple. The reader who has been carried through the marvels is set down at the ordinary — a human messenger, a single God, righteous work, sincerity — and told that this is what all of it was for.

The cave preserved seven young men. The garden destroyed a rich one. Khiḍr taught Moses that knowledge is not rank. Dhu'l-Qarnayn built a wall for people who could not pay. The Book will be set down and the mountains moved. And the instruction to the reader, at the end of all of it, is the same instruction the youths carried into the cave: believe, do good, and associate nothing with your Lord.

---
